**Authors:** Qian Xie, Wenyue Hua, Yueli He, Sripad Karne, Armaan Agrawal, Nikos Pagonas, Eugene Wu, Kostis Kaffes, Tianyi Peng, Nairen Cao

## 0. [Learning to Couple: Context-Adaptive Joint Denoising for Diffusion Language Models](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.25723) <a id="link0"></a>
**ArXiv ID:** 2610.25723
**Authors:** Zilei Shao, Ian Li, Heng Zhao, Kewei Lian, Guy Van den Broeck, Zhe Zeng, Anji Liu

**Abstract:** arXiv:2610.25723v1 Announce Type: new  Abstract: Diffusion language models accelerate generation by predicting multiple tokens in parallel, but factorized denoisers treat simultaneous predictions as conditionally independent. Decoding heuristics reduce the resulting errors by controlling which tokens are committed, but leave the underlying distribution factorized. Joint modeling addresses the source of the problem, yet existing methods attach separately learned layers to frozen denoisers, leaving the backbone unadapted to the joint objective and the coupling parameters fixed across contexts. We propose Codpula, a context-parameterized denoiser combining a Transformer with a tractable probabilistic inference layer. Given a partially masked input, the Transformer produces token potentials that shape per-position predictions and compact gates that modulate the layer's coupling parameters, yielding a context-dependent joint distribution while retaining exact conditional inference. We jointly optimize both components under a conditional denoising objective using a dual-flow multiplicative update that preserves valid probabilistic parameters. At inference, Codpula supports standard heuristics and uses conditional queries to adapt commitments to dependencies among proposed tokens without extra Transformer evaluations within a step. Codpula sets new state-of-the-art results across a broad suite of reasoning benchmarks while advancing the accuracy-latency Pareto frontier in mathematical reasoning and code generation.

**Comment:** Matches criterion 3: Advances diffusion language models by proposing Codpula, a context-adaptive joint denoiser that models token dependencies with a tractable probabilistic layer, improving reasoning and code generation benchmarks.
**Relevance:** 9
**Novelty:** 8

---

## 1. [Keep the Lineages Alive: Diversity-Preserving SMC for Diffusion LLM Decoding](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.25932) <a id="link1"></a>
**ArXiv ID:** 2610.25932
**Authors:** Minoo Ahmadi, Seyedarmin Azizi, Mehdi Kamal, Massoud Pedram

**Abstract:** arXiv:2610.25932v1 Announce Type: new  Abstract: Inference-time sequential Monte Carlo (SMC) decoding can improve the reasoning accuracy of masked diffusion language models (diffusion LLMs) without post-training by resampling a population of partial generations according to a reward. Its effectiveness therefore depends on the resampling step, which must concentrate computation on promising candidates while retaining enough diversity to explore alternative reasoning paths. The reward can be internal, the decoder's own confidence on the committed prefix, or external, a process reward model scoring that prefix. Under either reward, multinomial resampling can disrupt this balance by duplicating high-weight particles and discarding lineages that may still reach a correct solution. The effective sample size (ESS) does not register this loss: multinomial resampling resets it to its maximum even as distinct lineages disappear. To retain such lineages, we introduce lineage-preserving SMC (LP-SMC), which combines Chopthin resampling with an input weight floor. Instead of equalizing weights, Chopthin bounds the ratio between the largest and smallest weights it returns and carries the unequal weights forward. The floor limits how far below the maximum a weight may fall before resampling so that lineages with low early weight keep a chance to recover. Across mathematical-reasoning and code-generation benchmarks on two base models, LP-SMC raises oracle coverage, the fraction of problems with at least one correct particle, in every setting, by 1.8 to 25.6 points, and raises end-to-end accuracy under a common reward-model selector in eleven of twelve settings, by up to 7.9 points on LLaDA-1.5 and 18.3 on Dream-7B. The coverage gain persists under the external reward model, which suggests that diversity-preserving resampling and the guidance signal contribute separately to inference-time search.

**Comment:** This paper introduces lineage-preserving sequential Monte Carlo (LP-SMC) decoding for masked diffusion language models, improving inference-time decoding by preserving diversity of candidate generations. This is a clear advance in diffusion language models (criterion 3) with a novel resampling method that improves reasoning accuracy and coverage.
**Relevance:** 9
**Novelty:** 8

---

## 2. [Parallel Language Models via Variational Decoding](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.26693) <a id="link2"></a>
**ArXiv ID:** 2610.26693
**Authors:** Felix Draxler, Robin C. Geyer, Daniel Geyfman, Farrin Marouf Sofian, Justus Will, Philipp Hildebrandt, Giosue Migliorini, Prakhar Srivastava, Gagan Sai Trinadh Duggaraju Peddiraju, Kushagra Pandey, Jorge da Silva Gon\c{c}alves, Elijah Tavares, Jan Niklas Groeneveld, Yang Meng, Julia E. Vogt, Stephan Mandt

**Abstract:** arXiv:2610.26693v1 Announce Type: new  Abstract: Autoregressive language models emit one token per forward pass. This causes high inference latency, in particular as models are scaled up for quality. In this paper, we introduce Parallel Language Models (PLMs), which generate mutually consistent tokens in one forward pass by routing dependencies through latent auxiliary variables. PLMs are trained using a variational bound that reduces to an autoregressive loss plus a parallel decoding loss. Empirically, PLMs are on a faster and better Pareto frontier than autoregressive and discrete diffusion baselines. Pretrained PLMs achieve near-autoregressive pretraining loss on model scales of 80M to 3B parameters, and speedup follows a power law in model size. When post-trained, our 1.3B model matches or exceeds an identically trained autoregressive model on math, coding, and instruction-following benchmarks, speeding up inference $2.3\text{-}5.7\times$. Together, we show that parallel generation can be trained from a single training objective at autoregressive quality. We will make our code available at https://github.com/mandt-lab/plm.

**Comment:** Matches criterion 3: Presents a significant advance in diffusion language models by introducing Parallel Language Models (PLMs) that generate tokens in parallel via latent variables and variational decoding, achieving near-autoregressive quality with substantial speedups.
**Relevance:** 9
**Novelty:** 8

---

## 3. [DiffuPlan: Editable Planning State for Diffusion Language Model Agents](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.24717) <a id="link3"></a>
**ArXiv ID:** 2610.24717
**Authors:** Qisheng Zhou, Zhen Xiong, Qiaoyu Tan

**Abstract:** arXiv:2610.24717v1 Announce Type: new  Abstract: Diffusion language models (dLLMs) are emerging as an alternative to autoregressive (AR) language models, yet dLLM agents largely inherit an interaction protocol designed for AR generation: once intermediate state is written, later observations can only be incorporated by appending more text. This is a poor match to a defining capability of dLLMs -- reconstructing earlier text conditioned on context that appears both before and after it. We ask whether this capability can support a different agent-state paradigm in which intermediate planning state is directly revisable as evidence arrives. We introduce DiffuPlan, a diffusion search agent that maintains a single editable planning state and reconstructs only the affected steps when later evidence resolves an entity or changes the search route. To support variable-length revisions, we introduce Expansion-Partition Marginalization (EPM), which marginalizes over equivalent expansion paths. We further adapt diffusion trajectory RL to multi-turn search. Across four multi-hop QA benchmarks, DiffuPlan achieves 50.24 average F1, outperforming strong AR search agents. More importantly, under identical data and training, editable planning state improves the diffusion agent by 2.44 F1 versus only 0.47 for its AR source model. These results show that dLLMs need not imitate append-only AR agents: revising committed state is a distinct agentic advantage.

**Comment:** Matches criterion 3: Advances in diffusion language models. The paper proposes DiffuPlan, a diffusion LM agent with editable planning state and a novel Expansion-Partition Marginalization method, showing improved performance over autoregressive agents and demonstrating a distinct advantage of diffusion LMs for revisable intermediate states.
**Relevance:** 9
**Novelty:** 8

---

## 4. [UtilityBench: Measuring Language Model Utility When Errors Have Different Costs](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.25924) <a id="link4"></a>
**ArXiv ID:** 2610.25924
**Authors:** Qifeng Lei, Zhiyong Yang, Zitai Wang, Shilong Bao, Linchao Zhang, Qianqian Xu, Qingming Huang

**Abstract:** arXiv:2610.25924v1 Announce Type: new  Abstract: As language models are increasingly deployed in real-world scenarios with important outcomes, the impact of their mistakes often extends far beyond what correctness can capture. In domains like medicine, a single error can lead to serious consequences and put patient safety at risk. However, mainstream evaluation protocols still treat all errors as equal, overlooking the spectrum of real-world costs. To address this, we propose a utility-based evaluation paradigm that explicitly incorporates the varying consequences of errors. Guided by this principle, we develop UtilityBench, a large-scale benchmark of 15,000 instances with verifiable answers, quantifying error severity using Best-Worst Scaling to elicit meaningful, data-driven cost estimates. These quantified utilities shape when a model should answer or abstain, guiding it to make decisions that align with the true importance of each scenario. As another merit, we introduce UtilityAUC, a metric that measures the area under the utility curve determined by a model's response ordering, thereby enabling utility preference alignment beyond error frequency. Our theoretical analysis demonstrates that UtilityAUC can distinguish between models where standard accuracy and AURC fall short. Empirically, evaluating 20 state-of-the-art models, we find that greater accuracy or more advanced architectures do not reliably translate to higher UtilityAUC, highlighting a gap between problem-solving ability and responsible decision-making in language model evaluation.

**Comment:** Matches criterion 4: introduces a new utility-based evaluation paradigm for language models that accounts for different error costs and proposes UtilityAUC, a novel metric that better captures responsible decision-making beyond accuracy, fundamentally rethinking open-ended text generation evaluation.
**Relevance:** 9
**Novelty:** 8

---

## 5. [HarnessRL: Co-Evolving Policies with a Just-in-Time Training Harness for Open-Ended RL](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.26426) <a id="link5"></a>
**ArXiv ID:** 2610.26426
**Authors:** Xiaoqiang Wang, Ting-En Lin, Yuchuan Wu, Pinyi Zhang, Caixin Kang, Ling Li, Bingxue Qiu, Jieping Ye, Yongbin Li, Bang Liu

**Abstract:** arXiv:2610.26426v1 Announce Type: new  Abstract: Rubric-based reinforcement learning (RL) trains policies for open-ended generation with natural-language criteria as rewards, but these rewards can become ineffective as training progresses. We identify two failure modes. Under non-discriminative rewards, scores collapse to uniformly low or high values due to insufficient exploration or a mismatch between task difficulty and policy capability. Under spurious rewards, higher scores do not correspond to better responses. Both reflect a training configuration whose task sampling, exploration, and evaluation stay fixed as the policy evolves. We introduce HarnessRL, a training-time harness that co-evolves with the policy through three interfaces for task sampling, rollout guidance, and rubric criteria. HarnessRL maintains a training-time memory from which a harness evolver retrieves within-task history and cross-task evidence. Using this evidence, the evolver attributes each reward failure to the interface whose update can address it. It then runs a proposer and just-in-time critic loop in which the critic accepts a candidate update only after measuring its effect on the current frozen policy, returning rejected candidates for revision. We also curate Rubric-Mix-10k, a five-domain training and evaluation suite for open-ended RL across medical, science, writing, role-play, and instruction-following tasks. On Rubric-Mix-10k, HarnessRL outperforms the strongest baselines by 2.8 to 3.1 points across both 4B and 9B model scales and nearly halves the training steps to its best checkpoint. Code and data are available at https://github.com/Tongyi-ConvAI/Qwen-Character/tree/main/HarnessRL.

**Comment:** Matches criterion 1: The paper proposes HarnessRL, a new training-time harness that co-evolves with the policy for open-ended RL with natural-language criteria, improving instruction-following and RLHF-like training methods.
**Relevance:** 9
**Novelty:** 8

---

## 6. [Revealing what benchmark scores hide: Jointly measuring the breadth and reliability of LLM capabilities using accessibility profiles](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.24957) <a id="link6"></a>
**ArXiv ID:** 2610.24957
**Authors:** Kazem Faghih, Yize Cheng, Shoumik Saha, Mobina Pournemat, Armin Gerami, Soheil Feizi

**Abstract:** arXiv:2610.24957v1 Announce Type: new  Abstract: Language model benchmarks assign each question a single outcome from one fixed wording, even though the same underlying request can be expressed in many equivalent ways. We argue that the quantity of interest should therefore not be correctness on one wording, but the accessibility of an inquiry, which we define as the probability that a model succeeds across meaning-preserving formulations. The distribution of accessibility across inquiries reveals a structure that accuracy and existing best-, worst-, or average-case prompt scores collapse: how broadly a model's capabilities extend across inquiries and how dependably those capabilities can be elicited. Two models can have the same average performance while one succeeds across more inquiries unreliably and the other succeeds across fewer inquiries but more consistently, leading to opposite preferences depending on whether the task allows multiple retries until one success or when repeated consistent success is required. Across six benchmarks and more than twenty models, this tradeoff is common: for about half of the model pairs with nearly equal mean accessibility, which model looks better depends on whether one success across five formulations is enough or all five must succeed, quantities we call Access@5 and Reliable@5, respectively. For some pairs the two measures diverge significantly, by up to 10 points in opposite directions. Accessibility also reveals that scaling, reasoning, and prompt optimization can redistribute capability across inquiries in ways aggregate accuracy obscures, sometimes broadening the set of inquiries a model can succeed on while making success on them less repeatable. Accessibility turns formulation sensitivity from a nuisance into a measurable property of capability, distinguishing what a model can sometimes succeed on from what users can actually depend on.

**Comment:** Matches criterion 4: Proposes a new paradigm for evaluating open-ended text generation by measuring accessibility profiles that capture success probability across meaning-preserving reformulations, fundamentally rethinking evaluation beyond single fixed prompts.
**Relevance:** 9
**Novelty:** 8

---

## 7. [Breaking Diffusion: Parallel Reasoning with Draft Refinement](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.26542) <a id="link7"></a>
**ArXiv ID:** 2610.26542
**Authors:** Jaesang Yu, Seoyeon Byun, Yun Wang, Yan Lu, Juho Kim

**Abstract:** arXiv:2610.26542v1 Announce Type: new  Abstract: Drafting an answer allows a reasoning model to reconsider each decision in the context of the others. Yet repeated diffusion denoising does not ensure that individually plausible token updates form a better answer together. We introduce Draft Refinement Language Models (DRLMs), which use a single denoiser to propose parallel revisions and evaluate a candidate draft. We train the denoiser to reconstruct every token from uniformly corrupted drafts, teaching both preservation and repair. Its predictions specify revisions directly, without a diffusion reverse transition. Evaluation requires a different use of these predictions: confidence in an existing token reflects both contextual support and evidence from observing the token itself. Using the known corruption process, we correct for this self-token evidence to estimate how well each token is supported by the prompt and the remaining draft. Aggregating these estimates yields a cavity energy computed in one forward pass, without an additional scoring model. Each round proposes a revision, reads the completed candidate in its revised context, and retains it only if its energy is lower. Across Countdown, Sudoku-Extreme, and Maze-Hard, our method outperforms autoregressive and discrete diffusion baselines. With 6M parameters, DRLM reaches 44.4% on Countdown-5 and 93.9% on Sudoku-Extreme. The results establish complete-draft parallel refinement as an effective alternative to reverse diffusion for complex reasoning.

**Comment:** Matches criterion 3: The paper presents a novel diffusion language model approach (Draft Refinement Language Models) that performs parallel reasoning with draft refinement, improving over autoregressive and discrete diffusion baselines on reasoning tasks.
**Relevance:** 9
**Novelty:** 8

---

## 8. [Anatomy of Off-Policy Policy Gradient: Importance Sampling, KL Regularization, and Baselines](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.25725) <a id="link8"></a>
**ArXiv ID:** 2610.25725
**Authors:** Haoqun Cao, Yurun Yuan, Tengyang Xie

**Abstract:** arXiv:2610.25725v1 Announce Type: new  Abstract: Reinforcement learning has become a central tool for large language model (LLM) post-training, where policy gradient methods are routinely deployed off-policy, even though vanilla policy gradient assumes on-policy sampling. We study when such off-policy deployment is justified, and what roles its core ingredients, including importance sampling, KL regularization, and baselines, play in handling the resulting distribution shift. We show that for a general class of off-policy policy gradient updates, consistency, in the sense that the optimal policy remains a stationary point, requires one of two structures at the optimal policy: trajectory-level importance weighting, which suffers from the well-known curse of horizon, or KL regularization with a baseline equal to the optimal soft value, instantiated by the grouped mean. Focusing on the KL route, we identify it with the group-relative policy gradient (GRPG) update, establish an equivalence to a trajectory-level Bellman residual minimization objective, and obtain a finite-sample regret bound under all-policy coverage. This also gives a theoretical account of a variant of the empirically successful GRPO objective. We further uncover a hidden role of baselines in the off-policy setting: a constant shift in the baseline implements pessimism, the standard remedy in offline RL for partial data coverage. We instantiate this via the asymmetric REINFORCE objective, and connect it to the game-theoretic offline RL framework, showing a regret bound with single-policy coverage. Together, these results offer a unified theoretical view of off-policy policy gradient in LLM post-training and connect it to classical ideas in offline RL.

**Comment:** This paper provides a theoretical analysis of off-policy policy gradient methods used in LLM post-training, focusing on importance sampling, KL regularization, and baselines. It offers a unified theoretical view of off-policy policy gradient in LLM fine-tuning, directly matching criterion 1 on new methodological improvements to RLHF or instruction-following fine-tuning.
**Relevance:** 9
**Novelty:** 8

---

## 9. [Training Natural Schemers: RL Can Amplify Oversight-Conditional Behavior](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.26551) <a id="link9"></a>
**ArXiv ID:** 2610.26551
**Authors:** Bart Jaworski, Nathaniel Mitrani, Fred Bruford, Victoria Krakovna

**Abstract:** arXiv:2610.26551v1 Announce Type: new  Abstract: One of the central concerns for the safety of advanced AI is scheming: when a model strategically complies under oversight but pursues its own goals when unobserved. Current demonstrations, such as Anthropic's Alignment Faking work, depend on design choices that make them less convincing: explicit test-time prompting, handcrafted backdoors, or training data that demonstrates or hints at the target behavior. Model organisms built in this way cannot tell us whether scheming tendencies would arise or increase under realistic training conditions. We therefore construct a natural model organism of scheming, built on Kimi K2.6: trained with traits that could plausibly emerge in a frontier training pipeline, and without providing the model with the target scheming policy. The resulting model is a benign schemer: it aims to maximize aggregate help across all users over time, values its own continued operation only as a means to future help, and knows the consequences of oversight and deployment. We then train it using Reinforcement Learning (RL) with a reward that scores only task quality and policy compliance and does not reward scheming; we show that this amplifies scheming. We measure the oversight flip (the gap between monitored and unmonitored compliance with a given policy) and find that it increases during RL training across our environments. Chain-of-Thought (CoT) analysis finds explicit instrumental reasoning about oversight accompanying this behavior. The effect holds across our three RL environments, and we find that the scheming policy transfers between them. The pipeline also replicates on other open-weight base models. Our results suggest that a reward that does not reward scheming offers no inherent protection against certain traits when an oversight-conditional policy is indistinguishable from an aligned one during training.

**Comment:** Matches criterion 1 and 11 closely: The paper studies RL training effects on oversight-conditional behavior (scheming) in language models, analyzing how RL amplifies scheming despite no explicit reward for it. It involves instruction-following and alignment concerns, with chain-of-thought analysis revealing instrumental reasoning. This is a new methodological insight into RLHF and alignment.
**Relevance:** 9
**Novelty:** 8

---

## 10. [What RL Brings to Reasoning: A Few Decisions the Base Model Would Not Make](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.24663) <a id="link10"></a>
**ArXiv ID:** 2610.24663
**Authors:** Siyu Zhang, Yuquan Lu, Yoshua Y. Li, Yifan Ding, Kun Zeng, Yu Huo, Yifu Guo, Rongxiang Weng, Xunliang Cai, Xiaoying Tang

**Abstract:** arXiv:2610.24663v1 Announce Type: new  Abstract: Reinforcement learning with verifiable rewards (RLVR) substantially improves mathematical reasoning, but what it adds to the base model remains unclear. We investigate this through a sequence of generation-time interventions on Qwen3-4B-Base, measuring how much of its RL-trained counterpart's gain each intervention recovers on 120 AIME problems. Power sampling with the same 8,192-token output limit brings no gain beyond greedy decoding. Yet borrowing the RL model's token only where the two models' greedy choices differ, roughly 4% of generated positions, recovers 90.5% of the pass@1 gain, with similar recovery across three model pairs on MATH-500. We find that even a single opening token has a substantial effect: forcing the base model to begin with "Alright" recovers 35% of the RL gain. The opening alone is insufficient, however. Later replacements after the same opening add another 5.2 percentage points to pass@1, and the fixed rules we test for inserting reflective words such as "Wait" do not reproduce this benefit. Sparse replacement also retains much of the base model's diversity while staying close to the RL model at pass@1. At k=256, it solves 67.5% of the problems, significantly more than the RL model (61.4%) and a mixture of whole responses matched in pass@1 (61.45%). The comparison is at equal attempt counts rather than equal compute, since rl-replace consults both models at every decoding step. These results show that the opening can recover a meaningful part of the RL gain, but a larger contribution comes from later choices, which can be borrowed while retaining much of the base model's diversity. Code is available at https://github.com/CyrusZhang04/WhatRLBrings.

**Comment:** Matches criterion 1: Provides detailed analysis of what RL training adds to base LLMs in reasoning tasks, including token-level interventions that recover RLHF gains.
**Relevance:** 9
**Novelty:** 7

---

## 11. [RARE: Reinforcement Learning with Adaptive Rubric Evolution for Open-Ended Generation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.25434) <a id="link11"></a>
**ArXiv ID:** 2610.25434
**Authors:** Xueyun Tian, Bingbing Xu, Chuyue Li, Jiaming Liu, Chao Zhang, Weijia Feng, MingHua Ma, Yu Zhao, Jingchu Wang, Ruihua Huang, Yuanzhuo Wang, Huawei Shen

**Abstract:** arXiv:2610.25434v1 Announce Type: new  Abstract: Verifiable rewards have advanced reinforcement learning in mathematics and code. However, many valuable open-ended tasks share three properties: no automatic verifier of overall quality, multiple requirements to satisfy jointly, and multiple valid responses to each query. Training therefore needs feedback on requirement satisfaction across different valid responses. Rubric-based reinforcement learning provides this feedback by aggregating scores from explicit criteria. As learning progresses unevenly, some criteria become fully satisfied and lose discrimination while others remain informative. Dynamic methods revise shared rubrics or select evolving criteria to restore discrimination, but may also change useful criteria. To address this, we propose Reinforcement Learning with Adaptive Rubric Evolution (RARE). The core idea is to use current policy feedback to guide both which criteria change and what their replacements assess. Sample-level updates replace criteria fully satisfied across current outputs with checks of remaining quality differences, retaining all other criteria and scores. Within this rule, we compare rewarding desirable qualities with penalizing observed flaws. Video script generation exemplifies these properties and supports content production. Its demands for coherent, executable narratives under constraints make it a challenging testbed. Targeting flaws performs best across three video types with a mean gain of 3.82 points over matched static rubrics. Model and human preferences support these gains. Improvements also extend to writing and general generation benchmarks.

**Comment:** Strong match to criterion 1: New methodological improvements to RLHF or instruction-following. The paper proposes Reinforcement Learning with Adaptive Rubric Evolution (RARE), a novel fine-tuning approach that dynamically adapts reward criteria during RL to improve open-ended generation quality. This is a specific fine-tuning step to improve instruction-following and RLHF-like training.
**Relevance:** 9
**Novelty:** 7

---

## 12. [Mitigating Reward Hacking in Preference Reward Models with Evolving Penalties](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.26496) <a id="link12"></a>
**ArXiv ID:** 2610.26496
**Authors:** Afonso Baldo, Ant\'onio Farinhas, Marcos Treviso, Ricardo Rei, Nuno M. Guerreiro

**Abstract:** arXiv:2610.26496v1 Announce Type: new  Abstract: Reinforcement learning for language models has moved from preference reward models to verifiable rewards, which sustain much longer optimization. Preference models are prone to reward hacking: the policy raises their score without improving response quality, or even while degrading it. Yet for open-ended tasks, where quality can neither be verified nor fully written down as rubrics, they remain valuable. We introduce Dynalize, which mitigates reward hacking online, during policy training. Periodically and asynchronously, a language model analyzes training rollouts and writes penalties for undesirable behaviors that the reward model appears to reward; a judge applies them to every rollout. In clinical counseling and creative writing, training on the reward model alone raises its score continuously but ultimately leads to a decline in external benchmarks. Dynalize sustains longer training and results in improved downstream performance. For example, we find that our method suppresses patterns incentivized by preference models that, without penalties, spread to most responses, and even to the model's reasoning, which the reward model does not explicitly score. Furthermore, we show that its gains hold in multi-turn training, and even after we retrain the reward model on the discovered behaviors.

**Comment:** Matches criterion 1 and 11: proposes Dynalize, a method to mitigate reward hacking in preference reward models for language models by dynamically generating penalties during RL training, improving alignment and instruction-following robustness.
**Relevance:** 9
**Novelty:** 7

---

## 13. [Where RL Breaks: Localizing and Repairing Failures via Targeted Continued Pretraining](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.25836) <a id="link13"></a>
**ArXiv ID:** 2610.25836
**Authors:** Xinyi Wang, Danqi Chen

**Abstract:** arXiv:2610.25836v1 Announce Type: new  Abstract: Reinforcement learning with verifiable rewards (RLVR) is widely used to improve reasoning in language models, yet growing evidence suggests that it mostly sharpens what the base model can already do. This raises a practical question: when RL fails, what is the base model missing? We look inside the RL policy's own rollouts and find that failures are localized: whether a rollout fails is strongly predicted by a small set of characteristic n-grams, ranging from reflection phrases such as "let's recheck" to specific operations such as "the inverse of A". These failures persist throughout RL training, although they are not beyond the model's reach: other sampled answers to the same question are often correct. Building on this observation, we use the failure $n$-grams to retrieve text from the pretraining corpus and continue pretraining the base model on it before RL. The resulting corpus has only 35M tokens, 3--4 orders of magnitude smaller than typical mid-training. We evaluate this approach on OLMo2 and Qwen3 models trained with RL on (1) GSM8K; (2) a mixture of math, commonsense, and knowledge questions; and (3) general math and science reasoning. Despite its size, the corpus yields consistent gains: our approach outperforms continued pretraining on the same amount of text sampled from the pretraining corpus across 10 out of 11 settings, including by $2.0$ points on GSM8K when inserted before mid-training. The retrieved text also transfers across model families: text retrieved for OLMo2-1B's failures improves Qwen3-4B on the mixture by $1.9$ points over the same baseline. Together, our results suggest that where RL fails is a cheap and effective signal for selecting pretraining data.

**Comment:** Matches criterion 1: proposes a novel fine-tuning step to improve instruction-following models by targeted continued pretraining on failure n-grams before RLHF, improving reasoning performance across models and tasks.
**Relevance:** 9
**Novelty:** 7

---

## 14. [When Safety Fails Collectively: Robust Alignment Against Harmful Fine-Tuning via Joint Weight Perturbations](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.25683) <a id="link14"></a>
**ArXiv ID:** 2610.25683
**Authors:** Zhiyuan Wang, Yuezhi Zhou, Zhu Zhang, Zhixing Tan

**Abstract:** arXiv:2610.25683v1 Announce Type: new  Abstract: Harmful fine-tuning can rapidly erode the safety alignment of large language models, creating substantial risks when aligned models are adapted to downstream tasks. Existing alignment-stage defenses use harmful examples and simulated harmful perturbations during safety alignment to prepare models for later harmful fine-tuning, yet the collective effects of harmful weight perturbations remain underexplored. We identify a collective failure pattern: disjoint parts of a harmful perturbation have limited effects in isolation, but their joint application increases the model's harmfulness beyond the sum of their individual effects. Guided by this observation, we propose Joint Weight Perturbations (JWP), an alignment-stage defense that masks different parts of a harmful perturbation estimated from the current model and trains the model to respond safely in the resulting states. This exposes the model to different combinations of harmful perturbations and reduces its sensitivity to their accumulation during subsequent harmful fine-tuning. Experiments across downstream tasks and model families show that JWP offers stronger resistance to harmful fine-tuning than prior defenses while largely preserving task performance.

**Comment:** Matches criterion 10 closely: proposes a defense method (Joint Weight Perturbations) against harmful fine-tuning attacks that degrade safety alignment of LLMs, focusing on robustness against tampering and adversarial modifications to model weights.
**Relevance:** 9
**Novelty:** 7

---

## 15. [Beyond the Teacher: Understanding and Preserving Distillation Overshoot in Agentic On-Policy Distillation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.25034) <a id="link15"></a>
**ArXiv ID:** 2610.25034
**Authors:** Jike Zhong, Ming Li, Yuxiang Lai, Xi Xiao, Sheng Zhang, Ritwick Chaudhry, Tianle Chen, Jingyu Xie, Zheda Mai, Shao-Yuan Lo, Konstantinos Psounis, Yuanyuan Lei

**Abstract:** arXiv:2610.25034v1 Announce Type: new  Abstract: On-policy distillation (OPD) trains agents on their own trajectories using supervision from a stronger teacher. We uncover a counterintuitive phenomenon in multi-turn agentic tasks: a smaller, initially weaker student can substantially outperform its teacher during training, only to regress as distillation continues. We term this phenomenon distillation overshoot. Through decision- and task-level analyses, we trace overshoot to two competing effects of OPD: repairing weak student decisions and eroding successful student choices that the teacher disfavors. Early in training, repair outpaces erosion, allowing the student to combine the teacher's corrections with its own strengths and surpass the teacher. As training continues, erosion overtakes repair, causing these gains to vanish. Motivated by this finding, we propose Success-Preserving On-Policy Distillation (SPOD), which selectively masks conflicting teacher supervision on successful student choices and introduces a soft probability floor to counteract their erosion, while retaining teacher guidance elsewhere. SPOD requires only binary episode success and model probabilities already available during OPD. Across two model families and three agentic benchmarks, SPOD consistently outperforms ordinary OPD and evaluated variants, improving final success by up to 15.2 percentage points and surpassing the teacher by up to 20.0 points. Moreover, SPOD's final checkpoints outperform even ordinary OPD's best intermediate checkpoints in all six settings, demonstrating that beyond-teacher gains can be not only preserved but further extended. Code: https://github.com/Jike338/SPOD

**Comment:** This paper studies on-policy distillation (OPD) in agentic tasks, revealing a new phenomenon called distillation overshoot and proposing Success-Preserving On-Policy Distillation (SPOD) to improve instruction-following fine-tuning methods. This is a direct methodological improvement to instruction-following fine-tuning (criterion 1).
**Relevance:** 9
**Novelty:** 7

## 16. [Robust Direct Preference Optimization under Preference Label-Flip Attacks](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.24945) <a id="link16"></a>
**ArXiv ID:** 2610.24945
**Authors:** Chenye Yang, Weiyu Xu, Lifeng Lai

**Abstract:** arXiv:2610.24945v1 Announce Type: new  Abstract: Direct Preference Optimization (DPO) is widely used for offline Reinforcement Learning from Human Feedback. However, recent works have shown that DPO can be easily manipulated by adversarial preference label flip attacks, which are specifically designed to mislead the learner toward an attacker-chosen target policy. To address this issue, we design robust DPO methods that can defend against such attacks. Building on the property that lable flips induce parameter-independent gradient shifts in log-linear DPO, we propose Trim-$K$ Robust-DPO, which removes the $K$ highest-impact pairs, and Soft-Trim-$K$ Robust-DPO, which smoothly downweights them. We establish deterministic gradient perturbation bounds uniformly over all attack sets containing at most $K$ flips, explicitly separating defense-induced bias from surviving attack effects. Under bounded-region assumptions, these bounds yield time-uniform parameter and policy deviation guarantees. We further quantify how quadratic regularization improves curvature while introducing additional bias. Experiments on Stanford Human Preferences, HH-RLHF, and UltraFeedback-Binarized validate our analysis.

**Comment:** Matches criterion 1: Proposes robust methods for Direct Preference Optimization (DPO), a method used in RLHF, specifically defending against preference label-flip attacks with theoretical gradient perturbation bounds and policy deviation guarantees.
**Relevance:** 9
**Novelty:** 7

---

## 17. [Policy Is Not Capability: Some Safety Benchmarks Measure Which Definition of "Unsafe" You Trained On](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.25060) <a id="link17"></a>
**ArXiv ID:** 2610.25060
**Authors:** Vahid Jebraeeli, Jonathon Romero, Yijin Wang, Adrian Chan, Jianbang Zhang

**Abstract:** arXiv:2610.25060v1 Announce Type: new  Abstract: A guardrail classifier is reported as if unsafe were a property of text. It is not: it is a property of a policy, and the policy enters the model through its labels. We exploit an accident of an industrial deployment in which the same ~53k prompts were labelled twice, under two operational definitions ("is this a request for harmful content?" and "would a moderator block this?"), and used to train ten guardrail arms sharing architecture, codebase and optimiser family, at two model scales. First, the guardrail's own training corpus already contained the benchmarks: JailbreakBench-benign is 88% and OR-Bench-hard 82% present in the training pools, two over-refusal benchmarks, the kind a guardrail reports to argue it does not over-block. Everything below is decontaminated. Second, safety benchmarks stratify sharply by how much of their score is decided by the label definition rather than by the model: with architecture and optimiser fixed, the range across the ten arms is 0.99 on HarmBench-copyright and 0.043 on WildGuard-benign, a 23x spread. Third, a benchmark score can be moved across its entire range by 0.03% of the parameters. Across these arms HarmBench-copyright spans 0-100 and OR-Bench-hard over-refusal 3.4-56.4%, and the four arms whose last supervision signal carries a given policy occupy one extreme of both (exact rank p=0.005, or 0.04-0.14 at worst), at unchanged detection capability. For a distilled student the teacher's soft labels do this even when every hard label carries the other policy, so teacher provenance is an unrecorded confound in any released checkpoint. Fourth, validation accuracy on the one split that grades all twenty arms tracks agreement with the annotator's policy (rho=+0.74), not detection capability (rho=+0.34). We argue that guardrail evaluations should report contamination, a seed floor and the definition they were graded against as first-class variables.

**Comment:** Matches criterion 2: The paper analyzes test set contamination in safety benchmarks for language models, providing detailed empirical evidence and arguing for contamination reporting as a first-class variable in guardrail evaluations.
**Relevance:** 9
**Novelty:** 7

## 18. [Factorization-Guided Training and Inference for Diffusion-Drafted Speculative Decoding](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.23935) <a id="link18"></a>
**ArXiv ID:** 2610.23935
**Authors:** Jiakun Fan, Min Xiao, Qiang Wang, Neiwen Ling, Zhichao Liu, Zhihao Jia, Dimitrios S. Nikolopoulos, Zijun Wei

**Abstract:** arXiv:2610.23935v1 Announce Type: new  Abstract: Speculative decoding accelerates large language model inference through parallel verification of tokens proposed by a drafter. Diffusion drafters further parallelize proposal generation, but greater proposal capacity or higher mean accepted tokens (MAT) need not yield higher throughput. Because verification accepts only a consecutive prefix, early rejections can render later correct predictions ineffective, limiting MAT gains, while even higher MAT may not improve throughput if per-round latency increases. We introduce DScout, which uses two complementary factorizations to guide diffusion drafter training and inference for higher throughput. For training, DScout factorizes position-wise loss into inclusion, weighting, and prediction loss. This guides the design and combination of mixed-horizon training, intra-block unmasking, and acceptance-weighted loss to align supervision with prefix acceptance and improve MAT. For inference, DScout factorizes throughput as the ratio of MAT to per-round latency, guiding target-conditioning depth, drafter depth, and inference block size selection to identify a high-throughput configuration for each workload. Across six benchmarks, DScout achieves $3.84\times$ and $4.14\times$ mean speedups over autoregressive decoding on Qwen3-4B and Qwen3-8B, respectively, improving mean speedup over the state-of-the-art baseline by 9.1% and 7.1%. Across models and concurrency settings, our inference configuration policy provides up to 9.75% additional mean speedup over DScout's fixed configuration.

**Comment:** This paper presents DScout, a method improving diffusion-drafted speculative decoding for language models by factorizing training and inference losses to increase throughput. This directly matches criterion 3 on advances in diffusion language models with novel training and inference factorization techniques.
**Relevance:** 9
**Novelty:** 7

## 19. [Who Gets Unmasked? Sampler-Induced Bias in Diffusion Language Models](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.24795) <a id="link19"></a>
**ArXiv ID:** 2610.24795
**Authors:** Xujun Che, Farsheed Haque, Depeng Xu, Xi Niu, Haopeng Zhang

**Abstract:** arXiv:2610.24795v1 Announce Type: new  Abstract: Diffusion language models (dLLMs) generate by gradually unmasking a canvas, so their samplers decide not only which token to write but also when to commit it. We show that this second decision changes who appears in generated text, a sampler-induced bias on top of the model's own. Common commit rules, including LLaDA's default, commit the positions whose sampled tokens are most probable; this peeking favors majority options. To separate the sampler from the model, we read the model's belief about a fork, such as a protagonist's gender, in one forward pass, and show that this belief matches exact sampling from the model. Measured against it, peeking samplers on LLaDA and Dream amplify occupational gender stereotypes and collapse opinion distributions, and confidence-based ordering shifts gender-balanced occupations toward men, more so under faster parallel decoding. We propose Fork-First Sampling, which decides the fork before anything else is written: it follows the belief or any target distribution, improves fluency, and avoids the stories that remark on the protagonist's gender, which prompt-based fixes produce, at the cost of one forward pass. Fairness evaluations of dLLMs should report the commit rule.

**Comment:** Matches criterion 3 closely: studies diffusion language models and reveals sampler-induced bias affecting generation, proposes a new sampling method (Fork-First Sampling) that improves fairness and fluency in dLLMs.
**Relevance:** 9
**Novelty:** 7

---

## 20. [Learning to Reason Efficiently with Interventional Step Credit](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.25876) <a id="link20"></a>
**ArXiv ID:** 2610.25876
**Authors:** Guanda Li, Xuefeng Bai, Yulong Chen, Zhiyong Wang, Kehai Chen, Min Zhang

**Abstract:** arXiv:2610.25876v1 Announce Type: new  Abstract: Large reasoning models often produce lengthy chain-of-thought (CoT) traces mixing useful intermediate steps with unnecessary ones. Recent methods explore step-level signals such as attention mass and step entropy to identify and suppress unnecessary steps. Despite their effectiveness, such signals reflect the model's behavior at a step rather than that step's contribution to the answer. To address this, we propose the interventional step credit (ISC), which takes a causal-intervention view and assesses step importance to the reasoning through targeted parameter-level interventions. Building on ISC, we further propose IGPO, which uses ISC to guide fine-grained advantage rescaling in reinforcement learning, reducing redundant steps while preserving the important ones. Experiments across two model scales and nine benchmarks show that IGPO achieves up to 62% average token reduction and better overall performance among compared methods.

**Comment:** Matches criterion 12: Advances in LLM reasoning capabilities by proposing interventional step credit (ISC) to assess step importance in chain-of-thought reasoning and guiding reinforcement learning to reduce redundant steps.
**Relevance:** 9
**Novelty:** 7

## 21. [Enhanced Masked Diffusion Unlearning](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.25447) <a id="link21"></a>
**ArXiv ID:** 2610.25447
**Authors:** Tomer Garber, Tom Tirer

**Abstract:** arXiv:2610.25447v1 Announce Type: new  Abstract: Masked diffusion language models (MDLMs) generate text by iteratively unmasking tokens with bidirectional context. As these models reach real-world deployment, machine unlearning becomes essential. This process aims to remove the influence of a designated "forget set" without retraining from scratch, so that sensitive, copyrighted, or restricted content can no longer be elicited while preserving retained capabilities. Masked Diffusion Unlearning (MDU) is the first publicly available unlearning method customized for these models: it tunes the model $p_\theta$ to match a frozen prompt-masked anchor $p_{\mathrm{anchor}}$ on the forget set by minimizing the anchor-last divergence $\mathrm{KL}(p_\theta \Vert p_{\mathrm{anchor}})$. We identify two failure modes of MDU. First, random answer masking can leave key forget tokens visible to the anchor even when the question is fully masked. Second, at a memorized (near-one-hot) prediction, the anchor-last logit gradient vanishes while the anchor-first divergence $\mathrm{KL}(p_{\mathrm{anchor}} \Vert p_\theta)$ logit gradient does not. As a result, MDU training stalls on the tokens we most need to forget. Backed by theoretical and empirical analyses, we propose Enhanced Masked Diffusion Unlearning (EMDU): anchor-first KL divergence toward a frozen anchor whose entire answer span is masked. On TOFU and RWKU benchmarks using LLaDA-8B and Dream-7B, our EMDU matches or exceeds the forgetting of MDU and adapted AR baselines, while better preserving retain utility.

**Comment:** Matches criterion 9: Advances in machine unlearning techniques for removing specific knowledge or behaviors from trained models. The paper proposes Enhanced Masked Diffusion Unlearning (EMDU) for masked diffusion language models, addressing failure modes in prior unlearning methods.
**Relevance:** 9
**Novelty:** 7

## 22. [ToolOPD: Credit-Aware On-Policy Distillation for Tool-Use Agents](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.25693) <a id="link22"></a>
**ArXiv ID:** 2610.25693
**Authors:** Chu Zhao, Lei Tang, Yanming Wang, Jianzhe Zhao, Guibing Guo, Zhengzong Chen, Yuanyuan Zhao, Fei Huang

**Abstract:** arXiv:2610.25693v1 Announce Type: new  Abstract: On-policy distillation (OPD) enhances the capabilities of small language agents by providing dense, token-level supervision from a teacher model on student-generated trajectories. However, standard OPD implicitly assumes that credit is uniformly distributed across long-horizon trajectories, assigning the same strength of teacher supervision to different actions. Through credit-distribution analysis and outcome-reversal counterfactual experiments, we find that trajectory credit is concentrated on a small number of critical actions. Moreover, even when the trajectory is held fixed, reversing only the final outcome substantially changes the credit assigned to multiple interaction turns. These findings show that the uniform-credit assumption fails to identify critical decisions and ultimately degrades the model's reasoning accuracy. To address these challenges, we propose ToolOPD, an outcome-aware on-policy distillation framework designed for long-horizon tool use. ToolOPD first summarizes each completed trajectory and its outcome into a reusable hindsight skill. It then measures how this skill changes the teacher's probability of each student action token and converts the change into outcome-aware credit. The resulting credit preserves supervision that supports successful decisions or discourages failed ones, while suppressing irrelevant and conflicting signals. ToolOPD uses these credits to reweight the action-level distillation loss and jointly optimizes it with reinforcement learning. Extensive experiments across multiple long-horizon tool interaction benchmarks demonstrate that ToolOPD significantly outperforms existing reinforcement learning and self-distillation baselines.

**Comment:** Matches criterion 1 closely: proposes a new on-policy distillation method (ToolOPD) that improves instruction-following and reasoning accuracy by credit-aware reweighting of distillation loss, which is a specific fine-tuning step to improve instruction-following in language agents.
**Relevance:** 9
**Novelty:** 7

## 23. [Diffusion Language Models Change Speed Across Tasks and Languages](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.25835) <a id="link23"></a>
**ArXiv ID:** 2610.25835
**Authors:** Mohsen Fayyaz, Ali Modarressi, Daniel Israel, Lucas Bandarkar, Nanyun Peng

**Abstract:** arXiv:2610.25835v1 Announce Type: new  Abstract: Diffusion language models can write several tokens in one forward pass, and they decide during generation how many passes to spend. Two outputs of the same token length can therefore vary a lot in cost. To understand the underlying factors of this cost variation, we analyze two diffusion models across 10 tasks. We find that the number of forward passes per token depends heavily on the task. Text copied or lightly edited from the input is cheap, while open-ended writing is costly. In DiffusionGemma, generating a token for story writing requires more than three times as many forward passes as for grammar correction. Two output properties explain much of this gap: how much of it is new relative to the input, and how rare its words are within the task. The same holds within a single response when thinking is involved. The final answer is cheap when it repeats the model's reasoning trace and costly when it adds something new. In multilingual generation, we find that highly tokenized languages also require more passes per token, adding to the cost they already pay with more tokens. Finally, cost tracks correctness: on the same math problem, wrong answers take more passes per token than correct ones, and weighting candidate answers by decoding speed beats majority voting by up to 4.2 points at no extra cost. Taken together, our results show that diffusion's computational advantage depends on how much of the output is already determined, while its decoding effort offers a practical signal for making inference more efficient and reliable.

**Comment:** Matches criterion 3 closely: studies diffusion language models and analyzes their computational cost variation across tasks and languages, providing new empirical insights into diffusion LM behavior and decoding efficiency.
**Relevance:** 9
**Novelty:** 7

## 24. [ProbePO: Sparse Probes, Redistributed Credit for Critic-Free LLM Reinforcement Learning](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.24515) <a id="link24"></a>
**ArXiv ID:** 2610.24515
**Authors:** Diyi Hu, Ruizhong Qiu, Lin Qiu, Hanghang Tong, Hanqing Zeng

**Abstract:** arXiv:2610.24515v1 Announce Type: new  Abstract: Reinforcement learning from verifiable rewards assigns one terminal reward to an entire reasoning trajectory, obscuring how individual reasoning steps contributed to the outcome. GRPO broadcasts a group-normalized advantage to every response token, while finer-grained approaches often rely on dense auxiliary rollouts or branching schemes that can also alter the data used for policy updates. We cast the missing middle as budgeted measurement: with a limited rollout budget, which partial solutions should be evaluated, and how should their value estimates refine credit? ProbePO selects partial-solution states with a reward-free uncertainty score and estimates their expected verifier rewards under a capped continuation budget. ProbePO redistributes, rather than replaces, GRPO's trajectory-level advantage across tokens while preserving each trajectory's mean token-level advantage; probe continuations serve only as measurements. Across three DeepSeekMath and Qwen-family models spanning 1.5B-7B parameters, ProbePO consistently outperforms GRPO and achieves the highest accuracy among the four evaluated methods in 16 of 18 model-benchmark comparisons. Against dense-probing VinePPO on DeepSeekMath-7B, ProbePO achieves higher accuracy on all six benchmarks with 76% less end-to-end training time.

**Comment:** Matches criterion 1: proposes ProbePO, a new method for reinforcement learning in LLMs that redistributes credit for critic-free RL, improving instruction-following and RLHF-like training.
**Relevance:** 8
**Novelty:** 7

## 25. [Strength Controls Change How Interventions Compare Across Sparse Autoencoder Codes](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.26489) <a id="link25"></a>
**ArXiv ID:** 2610.26489
**Authors:** Jinchen Zhang, Zheyuan Liu, Yihan Zhu, Meng Jiang

**Abstract:** arXiv:2610.26489v1 Announce Type: new  Abstract: Changing sparse autoencoder (SAE) codes can alter both intervention direction and magnitude, so comparing intervention effects requires a strength control specifying what is held equal. Norm matching and output calibration provide different controls, but whether interventions yield different effects across codes after strength matching remains unclear. We compare intervention effects under these controls for fixed feature groups in Gemma and Llama and observe: (1) Whole-patch scaling. With one coefficient per input, full-output Kullback-Leibler (KL) calibration leaves smaller mean effect gaps from the encoder-based reference intervention than total-norm matching for nonnegative least-squares (NNLS) refits. On new Llama inputs, total-norm matching loses 11 reference transfers, whereas full-output calibration preserves every transfer decision. (2) Positionwise scaling. On a small matched gender-agreement subset, positionwise norm matching leaves smaller observed mean gaps than full-output calibration. (3) Answer exclusion. Compared with unscaled NNLS, answer-excluded calibration reduces gaps in the measured subject-verb agreement comparisons but increases them for gender refits. Full-output KL calibration also reduces larger gaps for norm-matched constructed candidates, although matching coverage is incomplete in several settings. Since calibration uses the evaluated input's outputs, it serves as a diagnostic of residual disagreement.

**Comment:** Matches criterion 7 closely: The paper studies sparse autoencoder codes and intervention effects, comparing different strength controls and calibration methods for sparse autoencoders applied to neural network interpretability, specifically in language models (Gemma and Llama).
**Relevance:** 9
**Novelty:** 6

## 26. [When Retrieval Partially Conflicts with Skills: Understanding Procedural Hallucination in Language Models](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.25827) <a id="link26"></a>
**ArXiv ID:** 2610.25827
**Authors:** Guanqun Jiang, Pengfei Cao, Kang Liu, Jun Zhao

**Abstract:** arXiv:2610.25827v1 Announce Type: new  Abstract: Language models increasingly use skills that provide reusable instructions and procedures for task execution. After a relevant skill has been selected, the model may need to apply its governing procedure together with retrieved task context. When the retrieved context is useful but contains cues that violate the skill, the model must retain compatible information while rejecting the conflicting cues. We study failures in which the model adopts such cues and produces a retrieval-supported but skill-invalid answer, which we call procedural hallucination. We introduce SourceClash, a matched benchmark with 1,271 bundles and 6,355 prompts across ten procedural domains. Each bundle fixes the task, skill specification, and answer space while varying the relation between the skill and retrieval. Across nine models, valid-answer rates average 25.7% under conflicting retrieval, compared with 78.2% and 72.8% under two supportive retrieval settings. Mechanistic analysis of open-weight models shows that the answer-preference difference typically reaches its maximum shortly after generation begins. Residual replacement moves the model toward valid answers, while attention decomposition attributes most of the repair to value vectors. Intervention at the identified value pathway also improves final answer validity. These results characterize how partly conflicting retrieval changes skill-guided generation.

**Comment:** Matches criterion 8 (mechanistic interpretability). The paper performs mechanistic analysis of language models under conflicting retrieval conditions, identifying internal pathways (value vectors) responsible for procedural hallucination and demonstrating interventions that improve answer validity. This is a mechanistic interpretability study revealing internal model behavior.
**Relevance:** 8
**Novelty:** 7

## 27. [DeltaTrace: Efficient Signed Attribution for Reasoning Language Models](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.25877) <a id="link27"></a>
**ArXiv ID:** 2610.25877
**Authors:** Chen Liang, Daoping Wu, Donghua Yang, Zhiyu Liang, Ying Zhang, Shuguang Xiong, Hongzhi Wang

**Abstract:** arXiv:2610.25877v1 Announce Type: new  Abstract: We study how words in the context support or oppose a language model's response and present DeltaTrace, a novel attribution framework that explains the effect of input tokens on a fixed model response. DeltaTrace measures how the response's log-probability changes when the context tokens being explained are masked. To distribute this difference among source tokens, it constructs operator-specific local rules for finite changes and composes them backward through the computational graph using effective secant slopes. Each token is assigned a signed attribution as the inner product of its embedding displacement and the corresponding backward-propagated slope. Local conservation makes these contributions sum to the measured score difference and preserves their meaning when aggregated over sentences or passages. DeltaTrace supports both attention and linear-attention models, with auxiliary memory growing linearly with sequence length at fixed model dimensions and chunk size. On the evaluated attention model, DeltaTrace achieves the lowest deletion-faithfulness scores (RISE and MAS) on most retrieval and reasoning tasks and the highest needle-in-a-haystack Recall, exceeding FlashTrace by 5.67-24.33 percentage points. On the evaluated hybrid attention and linear-attention model, DeltaTrace lowers RISE on 9 tasks and MAS on 12 relative to FlashTrace.

**Comment:** Matches criterion 8: The paper presents DeltaTrace, a novel signed attribution method for reasoning language models that explains token contributions via backward propagation through the computational graph, advancing mechanistic interpretability techniques.
**Relevance:** 8
**Novelty:** 7

## 28. [EG-GRPO: Evidence-Guided Step-level Credit Assignment for Deep Search Agents](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.25269) <a id="link28"></a>
**ArXiv ID:** 2610.25269
**Authors:** Sijia Luo, Mengjie Gao, Yuxiao He, Mengshu Sun, Cuiping Li, Hong Chen, Jing Zhang

**Abstract:** arXiv:2610.25269v1 Announce Type: new  Abstract: LLM-based search agents demonstrate strong capabilities in long-horizon information seeking and reasoning, typically trained via reinforcement learning. However, existing approaches predominantly rely on trajectory-level outcome rewards, providing limited supervision for complex search and reasoning processes. Although step-level credit assignment offers a promising solution, obtaining reliable and task-grounded supervision for open-ended search remains challenging. In this paper, we propose Evidence-Guided Group Relative Policy Optimization (EG-GRPO), a framework that derives step-level supervision from the evidence annotations available in synthetic deep search data. EG-GRPO identifies the first supporting citation for each required evidence claim and uses the newly acquired evidence to construct step-level process rewards. By representing the accumulated evidence as an evolving knowledge state, EG-GRPO further performs state-conditioned relative advantage estimation and combines the resulting step-level signal with the standard trajectory-level outcome advantage. Experiments on five challenging deep search benchmarks show that EG-GRPO generally outperforms standard GRPO and existing reward-enhanced and step-level RL baselines across different model scales. These results demonstrate that task-grounded evidence annotations can provide effective process supervision for credit assignment in deep search RL.

**Comment:** Matches criterion 1: The paper proposes EG-GRPO, a reinforcement learning method with step-level credit assignment for LLM-based search agents, improving instruction-following and RLHF-like training by using evidence-guided process supervision.
**Relevance:** 8
**Novelty:** 7

## 29. [Truncate or Not? A Scaling Study of Looped Transformers with Truncated Backpropagation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.26030) <a id="link29"></a>
**ArXiv ID:** 2610.26030
**Authors:** Xuyuan Xiong, Siyu Chen, Zihan Shao, Yue Su, Weiyang Jin, Ping Luo, Zhuoran Yang, Xihui Liu

**Abstract:** arXiv:2610.26030v1 Announce Type: new  Abstract: Looped transformers reuse a shared block of layers to increase computational depth without increasing the number of trainable parameters. They are trained with backpropagation through time (BPTT). To save training compute, BPTT is often truncated to the last few loops, although this may weaken the benefit of looping. To study this tradeoff systematically, we jointly vary model size, training tokens, and forward and backward loop counts across nearly 600 training runs and fit a unified scaling law that captures the effects of looping and truncation through effective parameters. Despite the training-compute savings of truncation, our fitted law favors full BPTT when forward and backward loop counts are jointly optimized. In matched-compute runs, full BPTT through fewer loops consistently outperforms truncated settings of the same compute. The law further shows that at fixed model size, the compute-optimal loop count and training-token budget both grow with compute. When model size is also free, the law identifies two fully trained loops as the best looped setting. Experimentally, this setting achieves lower loss than the compute-optimal non-looped model with about 27% fewer non-embedding parameters and only 8% more training compute.

**Comment:** Matches criterion 6: Studies scaling laws in neural networks. The paper fits a unified scaling law for looped transformers with truncated backpropagation, analyzing compute tradeoffs and optimal training strategies.
**Relevance:** 8
**Novelty:** 7

---

## 30. [How Data Scales in Agentic Reinforcement Learning: Laws and Synthesis Strategies](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.23946) <a id="link30"></a>
**ArXiv ID:** 2610.23946
**Authors:** Bowei He, Yankai Chen, Xiaokun Zhang, Changjiang Han, Ye Yuan, Chong Li, Xue Liu

**Abstract:** arXiv:2610.23946v1 Announce Type: new  Abstract: Pretraining scaling laws treat training data as a one-dimensional quantity: a token count. Agentic reinforcement learning (RL) inherits this scalar abstraction, but modern synthesis pipelines now generate \textit{tasks}, \textit{environments}, and \textit{trajectories} independently and at very different costs, turning data into a structured design space. This raises a question pretraining never had to answer: given a synthesis budget, \textit{which axis} should be scaled? We address this question end-to-end with three tightly coupled contributions. First, we decompose agentic data into three cardinality axes (task, environment, trajectory), plus a reward-density control variable, and fit per-axis scaling relations on different large language models (LLMs) across math+code, SWE, web, and UI/tool application domains. Across all model sizes, model families, domains, and RL algorithms (PPO and GRPO) we measured, the scaling exponents order as environment $>$ task $>$ trajectory. When environment diversity is decomposed into world and environment-structure diversity, its advantage is mainly driven by the former, while the latter is just comparable to the task diversity. Second, we use the fitted laws as a common ruler to price synthesis methods, capturing each method's efficiency as a per-axis discount factor and identifying critical synthetic-to-real ratios at which collapse begins; these ratios differ by an order of magnitude across axes. Third, we cast budget allocation as a constrained optimization under our laws and discounts, deriving a Chinchilla-style compute-optimal recipe and validating it at held-out scale. The empirical results demonstrate that following the recipe yields up to $1.7\times$ compute efficiency over the dominant ``scale-the-trajectories'' practice.

**Comment:** Matches criterion 6: Studies scaling laws in agentic reinforcement learning with detailed decomposition of data axes and fitted scaling exponents across LLMs and RL algorithms.
**Relevance:** 8
**Novelty:** 7

---

## 31. [Editing as Language: Teaching LLM Agents a Grammar for Native Context Editing](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.25550) <a id="link31"></a>
**ArXiv ID:** 2610.25550
**Authors:** Yunlong Deng, Yan Li, Xing Hu, Yida Zhu, Sean Du, Weiyang Liu, Guangyi Chen

**Abstract:** arXiv:2610.25550v1 Announce Type: new  Abstract: A synopsis in novels folds a story into a few lines; a function in code gathers many steps with a single execution. Context editing is native to language itself. In this paper, we propose the Native Context Editing (NCE) framework, which gives language model agents a grammar for natively editing their own context. By encoding context-editing operations in a grammar of dedicated special tokens, we teach the agents to invoke edits through the language itself. We train agents on iteratively constructed trajectory segments, with each teacher edit conditioned on the evolving context. This establishes context editing as an atomic and inherent capability of LLM agents, enabling them to dynamically modify their context as they generate responses over long-horizon reasoning and interaction. Compared with context-editing methods that learn to generate tool-use code within specific external harness environments, our approach integrates context editing as an internal mechanism of the model's native generation process, enabling better generalization across diverse real-world workflows. On four long-horizon agentic benchmarks (Terminal-Bench 2.0, SWE-bench Verified, SWE-bench Multilingual, and SWE-bench Pro), NCE improves the average task success rate over the base model by 5.6 percentage points for LongCat-Flash-Lite-Sparse and by 4.5 for Qwen3.6-35B-A3B, while reducing average token usage by 36% and 15%, respectively.

**Comment:** Matches criterion 1: Introduces Native Context Editing (NCE), a novel grammar-based method for LLM agents to natively edit their own context during generation, improving instruction-following and long-horizon reasoning, a new fine-tuning or training paradigm for instruction-following.
**Relevance:** 8
**Novelty:** 7

## 32. [PV-Decoding: Progressive Verification Decoding with Decode Gating for Diffusion Language Models](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.24722) <a id="link32"></a>
**ArXiv ID:** 2610.24722
**Authors:** Renxi Liu, Yiming Li, Shengyin Sun, Chen Chen, Xian Wang, Shuai Yang, Huiling Zhen, Xianzhi Yu, Yaoyuan Wang, Weizhe Lin, Xiaosong Li

**Abstract:** arXiv:2610.24722v1 Announce Type: new  Abstract: Discrete diffusion language models (dLLMs) offer an attractive alternative to autoregressive generation by updating multiple tokens in parallel, but their inference efficiency is still limited by the cost of safely committing tentative predictions. In particular, speculative decoding for dLLMs is challenging because speculative tokens within the same diffusion block may depend on one another, making aggressive acceptance prone to error propagation and strict verification computationally expensive. We present PV-Decoding, a confidence-driven speculative decoding framework with progressive verification for block diffusion inference. PV-Decoding partitions masked positions into directly decoded tokens, speculative tokens, and unresolved tokens using two confidence thresholds, and then verifies speculative tokens with progressively stronger policies, including single-batch, dual-batch, and multi-batch verification. This design exposes a practical accuracy--efficiency trade-off: weaker verification minimizes overhead, whereas stronger verification improves acceptance precision and robustness. Experiments on three diffusion language models across GSM8K, MBPP, and HumanEval show that PV-Decoding reduces sequential model forward passes (MFPs) by 8%--37% relative to confidence-based decoding, while keeping the largest observed accuracy drop to 1.3%. On Dream-7B-Instruct/GSM8K, PV-Decoding (1-batch) and PV-Decoding (2-batch) improve throughput by 7.0%--13.6% under the same hardware budget, whereas PV-Decoding (multi-batch) is slower under the current runtime. These results suggest that confidence-guided speculation with structured verification is a practical path toward efficient and reliable dLLM inference.

**Comment:** Matches criterion 3: presents PV-Decoding, a new confidence-driven speculative decoding method with progressive verification for discrete diffusion language models, improving inference efficiency and accuracy trade-offs in diffusion LLMs.
**Relevance:** 8
**Novelty:** 7

## 33. [Distribution-Based Regularization for Reward Fine-Tuning Diffusion and Flow Models](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.24897) <a id="link33"></a>
**ArXiv ID:** 2610.24897
**Authors:** Romeo Passaro, Jeongjae Lee, Jong Chul Ye, Michael M. Bronstein, Yoshua Bengio, Alexander Tong, Danyal Rehman

**Abstract:** arXiv:2610.24897v1 Announce Type: new  Abstract: Reward fine-tuning is an effective way to adapt pretrained diffusion and flow-matching models to downstream objectives, but aggressive optimization often leads to mode collapse and distributional drift. Existing methods attempt to control this drift with reverse-KL regularization, implemented as an $\ell_2$ penalty on the velocity field; however, this functions by indirectly constraining sampling dynamics rather than directly acting on the generated distribution. To address this, we propose DFR (Distributional Functional Rewards), which augments the task reward with a distribution-dependent, per-sample reward that acts explicitly on the generated distribution. Formally, we generalize the KL-regularized objective from expected rewards, which are linear in the model's distribution, to convex functionals of it. The first variation of this objective defines an effective, distribution-dependent reward that directly penalizes deviation from the pretrained distribution. This formulation yields additive per-sample rewards for the Fr\'echet distance (FD), maximum mean discrepancy (MMD), and DreamSim diversity, recovering several recently proposed rewards as special cases. Across various text-to-image fine-tuning tasks, DFR matches the peak reward of KL-tuned baselines while increasing diversity by up to 29% at equal or lower drift.

**Comment:** This paper proposes Distributional Functional Rewards (DFR), a new method for reward fine-tuning diffusion and flow models that explicitly penalizes distributional drift during fine-tuning. It directly relates to criterion 3 (advances in diffusion language models) by improving reward fine-tuning methods for diffusion LMs, with a novel formulation generalizing KL regularization to convex functionals.
**Relevance:** 8
**Novelty:** 7
