## 34. [Rubrics Carry the Standard: Shapley Attribution for Checklist-Based LLM Judges](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.24971) <a id="link34"></a>
**ArXiv ID:** 2610.24971
**Authors:** Saeid Asgari, Rui Ying, Sara Malvar, Rakshanda Agarwal, Bruce Sun, Claire Wang, Leonardo de Oliveira Nunes, Ranveer Chandra

**Abstract:** arXiv:2610.24971v1 Announce Type: new  Abstract: Checklist rubrics applied by large language model (LLM) judges have become a standard instrument for evaluating open-ended outputs and for rewarding them during training. Two assumptions surround this practice: that leniency or strictness is a property of the judge model, and that a rubric can be improved by asking a model to review it. We test both in a controlled grading task with planted flaws and on two human-labeled datasets, SummEdits and RAGTruth. Holding the judge and the outputs fixed, rubric content moves the pass rate of correct outputs from 2% to 100%, whereas changing the judge model or the rendering of the same checks moves it by a few points. Model self-review then drifts the rubric toward the reviser's own standard: it adds requirements the task never stated and deletes checks that human labels had taught, with three reviser models and on all three datasets. To measure and control this, we show that under all-must-pass aggregation the Shapley value of each check has an exact closed form, which yields per-check catch credit, false-fail blame and unique catches in linear time. Attribution shows that self-review deletes the checks that do unique work and keeps the checks that cause false fails. A change-control gate that combines these quantities with a provenance freeze on label-derived checks holds nearly every drifting revision chain at its starting quality, with identical decisions under three judge models.

**Comment:** Matches criterion 4: Proposes a novel statistical attribution method (Shapley attribution) for checklist-based LLM judges evaluating open-ended outputs, revealing how rubric self-review drifts evaluation standards. This fundamentally rethinks evaluation paradigms for open-ended LLM outputs by quantifying rubric component contributions and controlling rubric drift.
**Relevance:** 8
**Novelty:** 7

---

## 35. [LEAPS: Long-context Evaluation of Adaptive Pattern Learning and State Tracking](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.25919) <a id="link35"></a>
**ArXiv ID:** 2610.25919
**Authors:** Howard Yen, Nitya Mani, Alex Renda, Christopher S. Hardin

**Abstract:** arXiv:2610.25919v1 Announce Type: new  Abstract: Long-context language models (LCLMs) power agents that act over long trajectories, from improving a codebase to running research experiments to forecasters that condition on lengthy histories. These applications draw on three distinct long-context capabilities: recall, where the answer is a fact present in the context; state tracking, where the outcome can be computed by performing a series of transformations to a specified state; and pattern learning, where the goal is to make a prediction by sampling from a distribution inferred from many observations. Existing benchmarks primarily focus on the first capability. In this work, we introduce LEAPS (Long-context Evaluation of Adaptive Pattern learning and State tracking), a collection of 12 novel datasets, spanning agentic workflows, behavioral modeling, and forecasting of market and world events. By construction, LEAPS is resistant to data contamination, extends to millions of input tokens, and uses reliable metrics. We evaluate 30 API and open models from 8K to 512K input tokens. LEAPS distinguishes models that perform near perfectly on recall tasks, finding that such models' state tracking and pattern learning abilities can vary significantly and degrade much more dramatically with increasing input lengths--even for pattern learning, where more observations should make learning a theoretically easier task. Moreover, thinking models often underperform their base counterparts, exhausting their generation budget or oversimplifying the task. We hope LEAPS can guide the development of the next generation of LCLMs.

**Comment:** Matches criterion 4: Proposes a new paradigm for evaluating long-context language models across recall, state tracking, and pattern learning, with a novel benchmark LEAPS designed to be resistant to data contamination and to evaluate open-ended capabilities beyond recall. This fundamentally rethinks evaluation of LLMs in long-context settings, especially for adaptive pattern learning and state tracking.
**Relevance:** 8
**Novelty:** 7

---

## 36. [SteerSearch: Interactive Search Reasoning with Mid-Response Steering](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.24287) <a id="link36"></a>
**ArXiv ID:** 2610.24287
**Authors:** Udit Thakur, Baohao Liao, Matteo Nulli, Stefan Vasilev, Seyyed Hadi Hashemi, Shahram Khadivi, Christof Monz

**Abstract:** arXiv:2610.24287v1 Announce Type: new  Abstract: Search-augmented language models increasingly serve as long-horizon research agents, yet most open training methods assume a fixed user goal throughout search. We study mid-response steering, where users redirect a model during ongoing reasoning or retrieval. We identify answer-target anchoring, a failure mode in which fixed-query search agents retrieve evidence for the updated request but still answer an earlier question. We introduce SteerSearch, an RL framework that trains on interactive search trajectories with injected interventions to enable context-preserving target reassignment, and SteerSearch-Bench, a benchmark adapted from multi-hop QA datasets. We also investigate Contrastive Context Reward (CCR), an auxiliary signal for aligning post-intervention reasoning with the updated goal. Without CCR, SteerSearch improves average steering accuracy over Search-R1 from 11.1% to 31.3% on Qwen2.5-3B and from 36.6% to 44.6% on Qwen3-4B, while preserving standard single-query performance. Gains also extend to larger Qwen2.5 models and natural multi-turn conversations.

**Comment:** Matches criterion 1: Proposes an RL framework (SteerSearch) for interactive search reasoning with mid-response steering to improve instruction-following and reasoning alignment. Uses RL and auxiliary rewards to improve instruction-following behavior in language models.
**Relevance:** 8
**Novelty:** 7

## 37. [Trajectory Replay: Continuous-Time Mixture Transport for Lifelong Generative Models](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.26749) <a id="link37"></a>
**ArXiv ID:** 2610.26749
**Authors:** Alessandro Palma, Charles Jones, Tristan Cinquin, Fabian J. Theis, Frederik Wenkel

**Abstract:** arXiv:2610.26749v1 Announce Type: new  Abstract: Adapting pre-trained continuous-time generative models to sequentially incoming data without forgetting earlier tasks is a central challenge in lifelong learning. A leading strategy, generative replay, uses a generative model trained on historical tasks to augment its own continual update with synthetic samples from experience. However, it requires full trajectory simulations at every training step, which accumulate discretization errors and can degrade the replayed historical distribution. Here, we propose trajectory replay, which bypasses sample generation by training an updated velocity field to match both a frozen historical velocity along its trajectories and the incoming task's velocity field, weighted by a stability-plasticity coefficient. Through the lens of continuous-time transport, we show the resulting optimum averages the two task velocities by their respective densities and generates the mixture of their probability paths. Combined with mean velocity distillation, trajectory replay accesses historical trajectories using only a few network evaluations per continual update. Empirically, it outperforms generative replay and distillation baselines on class-incremental image generation. In two biological case studies, trajectory replay extends generative perturbation models to new contexts and experimental batches across two data modalities while preserving treatment-effect prediction.

**Comment:** Matches criterion 3: Advances in diffusion language models by proposing trajectory replay for continuous-time generative models, improving lifelong learning and generative replay efficiency.
**Relevance:** 8
**Novelty:** 7

---

## 38. [AM-Bench: A Unified Taxonomy and Evaluation Suite for Agentic Misalignment](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.24264) <a id="link38"></a>
**ArXiv ID:** 2610.24264
**Authors:** Eric Zhang, Terry Jingchen Zhang, Chijioke Ugwuanyi, Jerick Shi, Bernhard Sch\"olkopf, Zhijing Jin

**Abstract:** arXiv:2610.24264v1 Announce Type: new  Abstract: As LLM-based agents are deployed with increasing autonomy in real-world environments, a critical failure mode emerges: agentic misalignment, where agents choose harmful actions over failure. However, existing safety benchmarks primarily evaluate whether agents comply with explicitly harmful instructions, leaving the conditions that cause agents to choose harmful actions poorly understood. To address this limitation, we propose AM-Bench, a unified taxonomy and 35 scenario evaluation benchmark spanning seven industries for agentic misalignment grounded in the fraud triangle, a well-established criminological framework explaining goal-directed misconduct. We adapt this framework to agentic environments and construct our evaluation suite and detection systems along three dimensions: perceived threat, perceived opportunity, and reasoning. We operationalize each dimension into structured subcategories rooted in prior theory: five threat categories derived from the instrumental convergence thesis, seven opportunity categories drawn from the Emergent Strategic Reasoning Risks (ESRR) taxonomy, and a novel four-question binary reasoning framework synthesized from prior literature. Our evaluation of 10 frontier models reveals a 62-percentage-point gap in misalignment across scenarios, with Gemini 3 Flash and Gemini 3.1 Pro exhibiting the highest levels of agentic misalignment while Claude Sonnet 4.6 displays near-zero rates across all conditions. Our reasoning framework further reveals that models may exhibit harmful behaviours without explicit situational awareness or explanation, complicating both mitigation and detection. AM-Bench scenarios and code are available at https://github.com/AI4Collaboration/Agentic-Misalignment-Bench

**Comment:** Matches criterion 5: Provides a new benchmark and taxonomy (AM-Bench) for agentic misalignment in LLM-based agents, analyzing real-world safety properties and failure modes across multiple scenarios.
**Relevance:** 8
**Novelty:** 7

---

## 39. [A Fisher-Geometric View of Multi-Teacher On-Policy Distillation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.26525) <a id="link39"></a>
**ArXiv ID:** 2610.26525
**Authors:** Liu Kaiyuan, Zhuang Ziyuan, Ma Haoxuan, Gu Yannian, Song Mingfei, Weng Rongxiang, Ye Jieping

**Abstract:** arXiv:2610.26525v1 Announce Type: new  Abstract: Consolidating specialized foundation models into a unified generalist is a central goal of post-training. Two approaches are in common use: model merging, which is fast but treated as a heuristic, and multi-teacher on-policy distillation, which matches distributions but demands heavy rollout compute and suffers from cross-task gradient conflict. We show that the two are one objective seen at different orders. Within a shared basin of homologous specialists, the multi-teacher on-policy distillation objective is, to second order, exactly the Fisher-merging objective. Its minimizer is therefore the Fisher merge, a classic model-merging method given by a closed-form combination of the specialists, so merging is optimal, to second order, as a closed-form stage of distillation. Building on this identification, we propose Residual-Governed Branch-and-Merge(RGBM), a two-phase consolidation procedure. Concretely, a full-parameter Fisher merge is intractable on foundation models, so Phase~1 restricts the merge to the task-vector subspace. Extending tangent-space analyses of merging, we show that the same coefficients that mix task vectors also mix implicit rewards, which lets a residual program on unlabelled prompts solve for them. The program minimizes the worst per-domain residual and reports its energy-normalized values as the domain residual spectrum. Phase~2 lets this spectrum govern what may be added: RGBM passivates domains with low normalized residuals, trains isolated branches only for domains above the spectral gap, and writes back the admitted updates with calibrated shrinkage. On two open-source consolidation suites, the Phase~1 merge alone matches or outperforms fully trained multi-teacher distillation, and RGBM improves on it further with zero extra joint-training steps, whereas every un-gated write-back, including continued joint distillation from the merge, falls below it.

**Comment:** Matches criterion 1: Proposes a new two-phase consolidation procedure (Residual-Governed Branch-and-Merge) that improves multi-teacher on-policy distillation and model merging, which are methods related to instruction-following and RLHF fine-tuning steps for foundation models.
**Relevance:** 8
**Novelty:** 7

---

## 40. [Calibration-Free Early Stopping for Chain-of-Thought Reasoning via E-processes](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.25212) <a id="link40"></a>
**ArXiv ID:** 2610.25212
**Authors:** Dong-Jae Lee, Gyojin Han, Junmo Kim

**Abstract:** arXiv:2610.25212v1 Announce Type: new  Abstract: Reasoning large language models (LLMs) benefit from test-time scaling, motivating adaptive policies that spend tokens when they help and stop early otherwise. Existing rules halt when a confidence or stability signal crosses a threshold, but such thresholds rarely transfer across models and datasets, and validation-calibrated rules lose their guarantee under shifts in test-time horizon or signal dynamics. We reframe adaptive stopping as sequential evidence accumulation along the reasoning trajectory rather than threshold calibration on held-out data. Our framework runs two e-processes on a shared per-chunk signal: a commit stream tracks the signal's position relative to its slow trend, and an abstain stream tracks its signed velocity, making the two anti-correlated by construction. Under signal-level nulls on the per-chunk increments, Ville's inequality yields an anytime-valid signal-level false-alarm bound at user-specified significance levels, with no held-out calibration. Across various model--dataset combinations, the framework matches or exceeds calibration-based stopping on the token--accuracy Pareto front using zero calibration data, and the same significance level transfers unchanged across cells where calibration becomes infeasible.

**Comment:** Matches criterion 14: proposes a new calibration-free early stopping method for chain-of-thought reasoning using e-processes, providing an anytime-valid false-alarm bound without held-out calibration data, which is a novel paradigm for evaluating and controlling open-ended reasoning generation.
**Relevance:** 8
**Novelty:** 7

---

## 41. [WAQS: Weight Absorption for Quadratic Probing and Affine Steering](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.24367) <a id="link41"></a>
**ArXiv ID:** 2610.24367
**Authors:** Ding Zhu, Mohammad Mahdi Khalili

**Abstract:** arXiv:2610.24367v1 Announce Type: new  Abstract: Activation steering provides a lightweight alternative to fine-tuning for controlling large language model behavior. Existing methods, however, often face a trade-off between efficiency and expressiveness: fixed steering vectors incur negligible inference overhead but cannot adapt to the current model input, whereas input-dependent nonlinear interventions provide greater flexibility at additional runtime cost. We introduce \textbf{WAQS} (\textbf{W}eight \textbf{A}bsorption for \textbf{Q}uadratic probing and affine \textbf{S}teering), a framework that obtains state-dependent steering, without additional inference-time computation. WAQS models a concept with a quadratic probe, whose gradient yields an affine, activation-dependent steering direction. We show that this affine intervention can be folded exactly into adjacent linear transformations through an offline weight update. Moreover, we prove that quadratic functions form the maximal class of smooth concept scores whose gradient-based interventions are affine and therefore exactly absorbable into linear layers. Under a Gaussian representation model, our formulation admits an interpretation as the difference of class-conditional Mahalanobis pulls and naturally generalizes linear discriminant and difference-in-means steering. To scale to high-dimensional representations, we introduce a low-rank parameterization of the quadratic term. Across truthfulness, refusal, and sycophancy steering tasks, quadratic steering matches or improves upon constant linear steering while preserving model utility, with the absorbed implementation introducing no additional inference-time operations. Our results suggest that quadratic probes provide a practical middle ground between static linear steering and computationally expensive dynamic nonlinear interventions.

**Comment:** Matches criterion 8: Presents a new mechanistic interpretability technique called WAQS that enables state-dependent activation steering absorbed into weights, with theoretical guarantees and practical improvements over linear steering.
**Relevance:** 8
**Novelty:** 7

---

## 42. [Inside the Loop: A Mechanistic Study of Weight-Tied Transformers on Depth-Bound Algorithmic Tasks](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.24135) <a id="link42"></a>
**ArXiv ID:** 2610.24135
**Authors:** Magnus Sesodia, Juil Sock, Philip Torr, Christian Schroeder de Witt

**Abstract:** arXiv:2610.24135v1 Announce Type: new  Abstract: Solving algorithmic tasks with many sequential steps typically requires scaling up transformer depth or chain-of-thought length, incurring unfavorable parameter and inference-compute costs, respectively. Looped transformers, which reuse one block of layers across many iterations, are poised to address this, since they scale effective depth without adding parameters or tokens. Yet, it remains unclear what each iteration is actually computing, or whether the loop merely simulates a deeper untied stack. Here, we study directed permutation traversal, in which a model follows $H$ hops from a starting node and reports the node it reaches, so every intermediate hop has a known ground truth. We find that a minimum effective depth is necessary to solve the task, beyond which the degree of weight tying becomes the determining factor. For instance, a looped transformer with a four-layer block iterated eight times solves the task in every seed, while an identically-trained untied transformer of the same effective depth never does, succeeding only with a lower learning rate or supervision of its intermediate layers. Probing and intervening on the residual stream, we find that the looped model advances through the intermediate nodes gradually, forming a staircase that adapts to its iteration budget, whereas the untied model, at that lower rate, exposes them only in its final layers. Taken together, our results recast looped transformers as iterative program learners, which allocate their iterations to successive steps of the algorithm and solve tasks that identically-trained untied models of the same effective depth do not. Code is available at https://github.com/magnus-sesodia/insidetheloop.

**Comment:** Matches criterion 8: mechanistic interpretability techniques studying internal computations of weight-tied (looped) transformers on algorithmic tasks, with probing and intervention revealing iterative program learning behavior.
**Relevance:** 8
**Novelty:** 7

---

## 43. [Self-Distillation for Supervised Fine-Tuning](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.25497) <a id="link43"></a>
**ArXiv ID:** 2610.25497
**Authors:** Wenxuan Zhu, Bang An, Shuming Liu, Wenqing Cui, Tianqi Xu, Bing Li, Yibo Yang, Peter Wonka, Bernard Ghanem

**Abstract:** arXiv:2610.25497v1 Announce Type: new  Abstract: Supervised fine-tuning treats the expert demonstration or gold response as a one-hot target, suppressing valid alternative continuations that the model would otherwise assign probability to. We propose Gold-Conditioned Distillation (GCD), a method that turns expert demonstrations into model-native supervision. GCD uses one model as both teacher and student: the teacher conditions on a prompt containing the gold response in context, the student conditions on the original prompt as at inference, and the student is trained to minimize the forward KL divergence to the teacher's next-token distribution at each gold response token. This lets the model use its prior knowledge to teach itself the new task: correctness is anchored by the gold response, while the soft target preserves plausible alternatives. Across various training tasks on Qwen and Llama models, GCD improves new-task accuracy over SFT and generally reduces degradation on general benchmarks.

**Comment:** Matches criterion 1: proposes Gold-Conditioned Distillation (GCD), a new supervised fine-tuning method that improves instruction-following by using a teacher-student self-distillation approach preserving plausible alternatives rather than one-hot targets.
**Relevance:** 8
**Novelty:** 7

## 44. [Conformal Prediction-Enabled Sequence-level Policy Optimization for Reasoning Language Models](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.25092) <a id="link44"></a>
**ArXiv ID:** 2610.25092
**Authors:** Hui Yang, Wan Tian, Tao Ren, Zhongyi Li, Yijie Peng

**Abstract:** arXiv:2610.25092v1 Announce Type: new  Abstract: Reinforcement learning improves the reasoning capability of large language models through verifiable rewards for mathematical and coding tasks. In token-level methods such as GRPO, clipping acts on individual token ratios while the reward and advantage apply to a complete response. Recent sequence-level methods align these units but still use fixed clipping bands, whose tail probabilities change as the score distribution evolves. We propose Conformal Prediction-Enabled Sequence-level Policy Optimization (CSPO), which adaptively calibrates sequence-level clipping bounds using recent training statistics. The resulting band targets a specified marginal tail budget and adapts to policy drift; calibration conditional on response length requires additional assumptions. We bound the probability that a fresh score falls outside the band by the target miscoverage level plus explicit CDF discrepancy and drift terms. Under additional moment conditions, this bound also controls the objective and gradient error relative to the unclipped GSPO power surrogate. Experiments with the Qwen3-1.7B series show improvements over GSPO on difficult mathematical reasoning benchmarks and ALFWorld, with clipping endpoints that adapt during training.

**Comment:** Matches criterion 1: proposes Conformal Prediction-Enabled Sequence-level Policy Optimization (CSPO), a novel adaptive clipping method for reinforcement learning fine-tuning of reasoning language models, improving RLHF-like training methods.
**Relevance:** 8
**Novelty:** 7

---

## 45. [Pluralistic Alignment in Language Models: Objectives, Human References, and Preference Representations](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.24471) <a id="link45"></a>
**ArXiv ID:** 2610.24471
**Authors:** Meng-Chen Wu, Umesh Bodhwani

**Abstract:** arXiv:2610.24471v1 Announce Type: new  Abstract: Pluralistic alignment aims to represent meaningful variation in human preferences rather than collapse heterogeneous judgments into a single target. Yet existing work often mixes three distinct questions: what pluralistic behavior a model should exhibit, whose preferences define the reference, and what evidence is sufficient to demonstrate success. We organize the literature around a two-layer taxonomy. Layer 1 distinguishes representing a range of human-supported viewpoints (Overton Pluralism), adapting faithfully to a specified perspective or preference condition (Steerable Pluralism), and matching the response distribution observed in a target population (Distributional Pluralism). Layer 2 distinguishes aggregate populations, countries and regions, and subgroups as human references. Preference representation, implementation mechanism, modality, and evidence quality are treated as cross-cutting design choices rather than additional forms of pluralism. We synthesize a protocol-bounded corpus of 70 works on pluralistic alignment and neighboring areas, using the objective-by-human-reference matrix to map existing approaches and identify underexplored combinations. The coded literature concentrates on Steerable Pluralism and Distributional Pluralism, while eligible Overton Pluralism work appears only at the aggregate-population level under our stated protocol. We then connect each objective to the evidence needed to support it, including perspective validity, conditional fidelity, distributional fidelity, support preservation, and uncertainty in subgroup estimates. Together, the taxonomy and evaluation framework clarify what current methods establish, where their claims exceed their evidence, and how future systems can represent disagreement without mistaking variation for faithful representation.

**Comment:** Matches criterion 11: The paper discusses pluralistic alignment in language models, focusing on representing human preferences and alignment objectives, which relates to AI alignment techniques and value learning.
**Relevance:** 8
**Novelty:** 7

---

## 46. [HybridResearcher: Bridging the Structured-Unstructured Boundary for Deep Research Agents](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.24899) <a id="link46"></a>
**ArXiv ID:** 2610.24899
**Authors:** Soyoung Yoon, Peiran Xu, Han Wang, Ruofan Wu, Xiaodong Yu, Feng Yan, Mohit Bansal, Debiao Li, Seung-Won Hwang, Yuxiong He, Zhewei Yao

**Abstract:** arXiv:2610.24899v1 Announce Type: new  Abstract: While LLM agents are increasingly capable of gathering and synthesizing evidence from a single homogeneous source, such as structured tables or unstructured data, real-world analytical questions frequently span heterogeneous sources. For example, answering one enterprise question may require both a result computed over an internal database and evidence found on the web. Recent evaluations on benchmarks like HybridDeepResearch show that even frontier agents struggle when evidence must cross this boundary. While these benchmarks have exposed the limitation, methodologies to explicitly teach cross-source reasoning remain absent. To address this, we introduce HybridResearcher, a 4B agent trained to bridge evidence across the structured and unstructured boundary. To build it, we synthesize cross-source bridge trajectories by starting with validated single-source tasks-such as existing text-to-SQL or web search queries-and fabricating the missing source around a bridge entity drawn from the seed's own output. We retain a sample only when strict construction gates confirm that neither side alone suffices, ensuring the data requires true cross-source interdependence. Finally, we train on this curated dataset using supervised fine-tuning followed by reinforcement learning. Evaluated on the existing HybridDeepResearch benchmark, HybridResearcher improves over our base model, Qwen3.5-4B, by 30.0 points pass@8 and surpasses the far larger Qwen3.5-397B-A17B by 8.3 points, while also improving over its base model on established, held-out single-source benchmarks (BrowseComp and BrowseComp-Plus), demonstrating that this complex cross-source reasoning can be taught directly to small models without triggering catastrophic forgetting.

**Comment:** Matches criterion 1: Proposes a new supervised fine-tuning and reinforcement learning method to train an LLM agent (HybridResearcher) for cross-source reasoning, explicitly teaching complex instruction-following across heterogeneous data sources. This is a methodological improvement to instruction-following fine-tuning.
**Relevance:** 8
**Novelty:** 7

---

## 47. [On Reparameterizing the Score Function](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.24188) <a id="link47"></a>
**ArXiv ID:** 2610.24188
**Authors:** Mohammad Hossein Amani, Leonardo Bocchieri, Maxime Peyrard, Cl\'ement Hongler, Robert West

**Abstract:** arXiv:2610.24188v1 Announce Type: new  Abstract: Policy gradients for autoregressive sequence models differentiate each token's log-probability while holding the conditioning history fixed. This discards information about how earlier decisions shape later conditional distributions, creating a credit-assignment bottleneck when rewards are sparse. We introduce History-Reparameterized (HR) policy gradients, which replace the frozen prefix in the score function with a differentiable reparameterized or relaxed history, allowing gradients of downstream log-probabilities to propagate backward through the generated trajectory. The resulting history-pathwise term is generally biased when added directly, so we derive Optimal HR (OHR), a centered control-variate estimator with a closed-form coefficient that minimizes the trace of the gradient variance at the population level. The variance reduction is governed by temporal structure in the policy dynamics: random or weakly structured policies provide little benefit, while policies with learned sequential dependencies yield informative history-pathwise corrections. For discrete tokens, the choice of relaxation is crucial: embedding-level relaxation yields substantial variance reduction that grows with horizon, whereas straight-through Gumbel-Softmax yields essentially none in our diagnostics. We validate the method on a Gaussian linear Markov model, a sparse-reward T-maze with a recurrent policy, and an autoregressive Transformer model for small Traveling Salesman Problem (TSP) instances.

**Comment:** Matches criterion 1 closely: proposes a new policy gradient method (History-Reparameterized policy gradients) that improves credit assignment in autoregressive sequence models, which is a methodological improvement relevant to instruction-following and RLHF-like training of language models.
**Relevance:** 8
**Novelty:** 7

---

## 48. [The Window Is in the Reasoning: When LLM Agents Decide to Follow Prompt Injections](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.24048) <a id="link48"></a>
**ArXiv ID:** 2610.24048
**Authors:** Haoxuan Jia, Yang Liu, Bin Chong, Yingguang Yang, Yancheng Chen, Jiayu Liang, Qian Li, Hanning Lu, Kefu Xu, Hao Zheng, Chongyang Zhang, Yulin Huang, Jianshen Zhang, Yongzhi Qi, Yunuo Lin, Hao Peng

**Abstract:** arXiv:2610.24048v1 Announce Type: new  Abstract: LLM agents that read untrusted content can be hijacked by prompt injections. Runtime monitors that try to catch a hijack before the agent acts, and benchmarks that score each task with a single run, share an assumption: whether an agent follows an injection is settled when the injection is read. We test this assumption with branching rollouts that replay an episode exactly up to the step at which the agent reads an injection and then resample the rest of the episode many times, splitting the outcome variance into a part fixed at read time and a part decided afterwards. Across four AgentDojo suites, a Qwen3 agent without thinking is largely decided at read time (0.91 of the outcome variance lies between states); with thinking on, this share drops to 0.62 (95% CI of the drop [-0.38, -0.22]), in the same direction for three attack templates and two success labels. How much is fixed at read time is a property of the model that the same measurement reveals, and thinking lowers the attack success rate of Qwen3 but raises it for two other models. Resampling from reasoning prefixes shows that the decision forms gradually as the agent reasons. Zero-shot chain-of-thought monitors built on cheaper models miss much of what is already decided, whereas stronger monitors, and a lookahead monitor that judges simulated next actions, come close to what the prefix determines. Finally, single-run labels of thinking agents disagree with a rerun up to 35% of the time, so benchmarks need repeated runs per task.

**Comment:** Matches criterion 4: Describes new paradigms to evaluating open-ended text generation by analyzing how LLM agents decide to follow prompt injections and the variability in outcomes, highlighting the need for repeated runs and better monitoring methods.
**Relevance:** 8
**Novelty:** 7

## 49. [Progress-Guided Preference Generation for Aligning Generative Control Policies](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.25692) <a id="link49"></a>
**ArXiv ID:** 2610.25692
**Authors:** Tung M. Luu, Tri Ton, Duc M. Nguyen, Xuan Thanh Nguyen, Nguyen Gia Binh, Quang Tan Nguyen, Vu Duong, Daniel Sonntag, Duy M. H. Nguyen, Vien Anh Ngo, Chang D. Yoo

**Abstract:** arXiv:2610.25692v1 Announce Type: new  Abstract: Generative policies have achieved strong performance in imitation learning tasks, but their success is often constrained by the limited availability of expert demonstrations. Although preference-based fine-tuning can leverage suboptimal rollouts to improve performance, existing approaches heavily rely on expensive human annotations. In this work, we introduce Progress-Guided Preference Generation (PGPG), an unsupervised preference alignment framework that eliminates the need for human-in-the-loop supervision. Our method leverages ordered coverage alignment to measure sequential progress relative to a small set of expert demonstrations, enabling the automatic extraction of pseudo-preference labels from autonomously collected rollouts. Furthermore, we introduce key enhancements to improve the reliability of these progress-guided preferences, enabling effective fine-tuning of pretrained generative policies without requiring manually designed reward functions. Extensive experiments across a suite of challenging long-horizon manipulation tasks demonstrate that PGPG outperforms standard supervised fine-tuning baselines. Moreover, our conceptually simple framework is broadly applicable across multiple classes of generative policies, demonstrating strong versatility when integrated with both diffusion and flow-matching policies.

**Comment:** Matches criterion 1 and partially 3: Introduces Progress-Guided Preference Generation (PGPG), an unsupervised preference alignment method for fine-tuning generative policies, applicable to diffusion and flow-matching policies, improving instruction-following without human supervision.
**Relevance:** 8
**Novelty:** 7

---

## 50. [DeGMatch: Target-Aware Reasoning Data Selection via Deconfounded Gradient Matching](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.24963) <a id="link50"></a>
**ArXiv ID:** 2610.24963
**Authors:** Changho Lee, Janghoon Han, Rajdeep Mukherjee, Yeda Song, Lening Nick Cui, Xinya Du, Honglak Lee

**Abstract:** arXiv:2610.24963v1 Announce Type: new  Abstract: Reasoning post-training on long trajectories has substantially improved the reasoning capabilities of large language models, but scaling such training to large datasets is increasingly expensive. We study target-aware reasoning data selection, where a small reference set specifies a target capability and guides the selection of training trajectories for specialization. We find that existing target-aware methods can exhibit limited target discrimination on long reasoning trajectories, while dominant population-level structure in candidate gradients can obscure finer target-dependent variation during gradient matching. To address these limitations, we introduce Deconfounded Gradient Matching (DeGMatch), which first constructs a target-relevance gate and then reranks candidates using model-specific gradients. DeGMatch centers gradients using the candidate-population mean and removes leading principal directions before target matching, reducing the influence of dominant population-level structure and improving target discrimination. Across mathematics, science, and code, DeGMatch outperforms strong heuristic, target-aware, and reasoning-specific data selection baselines, while remaining effective across selection budgets, model families, and scales. Our code is publicly available at https://github.com/CHLee0801/degmatch.

**Comment:** Matches criterion 1: Proposes a new method (Deconfounded Gradient Matching) for target-aware reasoning data selection to improve instruction-following or reasoning specialization in LLMs. This is a specific fine-tuning step to improve reasoning capabilities and data selection for instruction-following.
**Relevance:** 8
**Novelty:** 7

## 51. [Steered Not Stirred: Effective Reward Tilting using Surrogate Reward Mixing](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.25502) <a id="link51"></a>
**ArXiv ID:** 2610.25502
**Authors:** Alec Sargood, Olivia Gallup, Georg Maierhofer, Noam Ghenassia, Paul Duckworth, Krisztina Sinkovics, Avishek Joey Bose

**Abstract:** arXiv:2610.25502v1 Announce Type: new  Abstract: Continuous-time generative models, from diffusion models, flow matching, and now flow maps, owe their practical value to high-fidelity generation and steerability. In many applications, a ground-truth latent ``oracle'' reward $R^*$ is unavailable or expensive to evaluate, e.g., human judgments or experimental measurements. Although the oracle reward is not directly observable, it can often be approximated through multiple cheaper surrogates that, individually, provide incomplete characterizations but collectively reveal substantial information about the downstream task. In this paper, we present a method for composing such surrogates to steer a pretrained model toward the distribution tilted by an unavailable $R^*$. Given a small feedback set of oracle rankings or numerical rewards, we introduce Mixing And Reward Tilting for INference-time Improvement (MARTINI), which learns a surrogate composition for plug-and-play use with standard inference-time steering methods. Our theory characterizes surrogate information limits and relates population recovery error to ideal-target KL at small tilt strength. For preferences, the bound additionally accounts for an irreducible mismatch between the Plackett-Luce population target and the conditional mean. Experiments demonstrate improved oracle-tilt fidelity in language generation and ImageNet alignment, and improved joint pose accuracy and validity in protein-ligand cofolding, without inference-time oracle queries.

**Comment:** Matches criterion 3 closely: advances in continuous-time generative models including diffusion models for language generation. Introduces MARTINI, a method for reward tilting using surrogate reward mixing to steer pretrained models at inference time, improving oracle-tilt fidelity in language generation.
**Relevance:** 8
**Novelty:** 7

## 52. [Rubrics Beyond Evaluation: Exploiting Rubric Structure in Reinforcement Learning](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.25850) <a id="link52"></a>
**ArXiv ID:** 2610.25850
**Authors:** Yuxun Huang, Yunyao Zhang, ZeLiang Chen, Yi-Ping Phoebe Chen, Junqing Yu, Zikai Song

**Abstract:** arXiv:2610.25850v1 Announce Type: new  Abstract: Rubrics are widely used to evaluate open-ended question answering by breaking overall quality into explicit criteria. These explicit criteria make rubrics a rich source of learning guidance, yet their potential for guiding the training process has been largely overlooked. Existing methods use rubrics mainly as direct feedback signals, without explicitly modeling the capabilities behind their criteria. This leaves their capability structure largely unused in guiding the training process. We introduce Rubric-Structured Reinforcement Learning (RSRL), an RL framework that unlocks the full potential of rubrics by exploiting their structure throughout the training process, rather than treating them simply as direct reward signals. At its core, RSRL builds on three key designs: (1) Rubric-to-Capability Structuring first recovers the capability structure hidden in rubric criteria, (2) Self-Sufficient Capability Learning then uses this structure to bootstrap underdeveloped capabilities into effective learning, (3) Capability-Residual Credit Routing finally preserves capability-specific gains during optimization. Experiments show that RSRL consistently improves both overall performance and underdeveloped capabilities, boosting downstream scientific question-answering performance by up to 7.43 over the initial policy and 7.60 over non-rubric structured RL baselines, showing that reinforcement learning can substantially benefit from the capability structure encoded in rubrics.

**Comment:** Matches criterion 1: New methodological improvements to RLHF or instruction-following by exploiting rubric structure in reinforcement learning to improve instruction-following capabilities in language models.
**Relevance:** 8
**Novelty:** 7

---

## 53. [Not Every User Says It All: Training Tool-Using Agents Beyond Ideal Simulated Users](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.24294) <a id="link53"></a>
**ArXiv ID:** 2610.24294
**Authors:** Han Bao, Zheyuan Zhang, Kaiwen Shi, Yanfang Ye

**Abstract:** arXiv:2610.24294v1 Announce Type: new  Abstract: Tool-using agents are trained and evaluated almost exclusively with strong simulated users who state every detail clearly, yet real users are often weaker. We construct weak users by masking sentences of a simulated user's replies, holding the task and the user's knowledge fixed, and cross-evaluate agents trained with strong and weak users on tau2-bench and ToolWOZ. Weak users lower the success of every policy, and training with them does not help: no policy trained with weak users, through fixed masks, mixtures or a curriculum, serves weak users better than the strong-trained policy beyond run-to-run noise, and prompting the agent to ask more does not help either. We propose StaGPO (State-Grouped Policy Optimization): the agent writes what the task needs, what is missing and how it is being filled, and GRPO uses this state to compare turns of different rollouts that reach the same situation, assigning step-level credit. Within the standard recipe of a small SFT cold start followed by RL, StaGPO makes weak-user training useful: on ToolWOZ, the Qwen3.5-2B policy trained with weak users reaches 76.8% with weak users, the highest of any Qwen3.5-2B policy we trained, 4.8 points above GRPO and 2.8 above the strongest baseline; it is also 5.4 points above GRPO with strong users, and generalises to an unseen mask rate; trained with strong users instead, it serves weak users at least as well as GRPO trained with weak users. On tau2-bench it scores highest with weak users for both models, by a small margin for Qwen3.5-4B.

**Comment:** Matches criterion 1: Proposes StaGPO, a new RL method improving instruction-following and policy training for tool-using agents under weak user conditions, enhancing instruction-following robustness beyond standard RLHF recipes.
**Relevance:** 8
**Novelty:** 7

## 54. [Balance-Aware Pivot Selection for Parallel Diffusion LLM Decoding](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.26040) <a id="link54"></a>
**ArXiv ID:** 2610.26040
**Authors:** Yejin Lee, Hyundong Jin, Yo-Sub Han

**Abstract:** arXiv:2610.26040v1 Announce Type: new  Abstract: Diffusion language models enable parallel token generation, offering a promising alternative to sequential autoregressive decoding. Existing acceleration methods use predictive confidence to enable parallel token commitment while preserving generation quality. However, confidence-based selection prioritizes reliable token predictions without explicitly considering how token positions shape the remaining decoding schedule. Moreover, such ordering does not always reflect inter-token dependencies, suggesting that confidence need not be the sole criterion for token selection. Motivated by this limitation, we propose Balance-Aware Pivot Decoding (BPD), which favors central pivots within contiguous masked intervals and decodes the selected positions in parallel. Balanced pivots yield logarithmic decoding depth in the interval length. Our practical method accounts for model uncertainty by combining confidence-based prefilling with confidence-thresholded center selection. We evaluate the resulting quality-efficiency tradeoff on LLaDA and Dream across five mathematical reasoning, code generation, and instruction-following benchmarks. Our implementation is available at: https://anonymous.4open.science/r/DLM_random_pivot-EE62/

**Comment:** Matches criterion 3: Proposes Balance-Aware Pivot Decoding (BPD) for diffusion language models, improving parallel decoding efficiency and quality with a novel pivot selection strategy.
**Relevance:** 8
**Novelty:** 7

## 55. [ReAnchor: Reusing Rejected Verifier States for Diffusion Drafting](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.26531) <a id="link55"></a>
**ArXiv ID:** 2610.26531
**Authors:** Sanghyun Lee, Wonjin Nam, Seungho Jang, Jaeho Lee, Jiho Park, Jaewoon Lee, Seungryong Kim

**Abstract:** arXiv:2610.26531v1 Announce Type: new  Abstract: Block diffusion drafters accelerate speculative decoding by generating multiple draft tokens in a single forward pass. However, each pass produces only a fixed-length block from a single target-generated anchor, limiting how far the draft can extend. Meanwhile, target verification computes hidden states even at positions beyond the first rejection, but these states are typically discarded rather than reused. We propose ReAnchor, a training-free method that reanchors a pretrained drafter at shifted positions using discarded verifier states to generate parallel draft blocks. These blocks improve predictions within the original block and extend coverage beyond its endpoint. At sampling temperature of T=0, ReAnchor achieves its largest average gains on Gemma 4-12B, improving acceptance length by 19.4% and decoding speed by 17.9% across eight benchmarks. Averaged equally over four target models and eight benchmarks, it improves acceptance length by 15.2% and decoding speed by 13.4% with pretrained DFlash drafters.

**Comment:** Matches criterion 3: Advances in diffusion language models. The paper proposes ReAnchor, a method to reuse verifier states in diffusion drafting for language models, improving acceptance length and decoding speed, which is a novel training-free method for diffusion LMs.
**Relevance:** 8
**Novelty:** 7

## 56. [Honeyed Words, Hidden Daggers: Benchmarking and Training LLMs to See Through Mock Politeness](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.26059) <a id="link56"></a>
**ArXiv ID:** 2610.26059
**Authors:** Caixin Kang, Sitong Gong, Tianyu Yan, Kaipeng Zhang, Yoichi Sato, Yifei Huang

**Abstract:** arXiv:2610.26059v1 Announce Type: new  Abstract: The Chinese idiom "kou mi fu jian" ("honeyed words, hidden daggers") names speech that is polite in form but hostile in function. We present ZhenhuanBench, a 1,592-item benchmark built from expert commentary on Empresses in the Palace, covering speaker intent, emotion-language mismatch, and per-listener uptake. Text-only evaluation covers 34 LLMs. GPT-6 Astra leads the leaderboard at 73.48% overall, while its listener-reaction score is 58.37%. The annotations also supply supervised traces and string-verifiable training rewards. With 338 gold traces, an 8B model outperforms teacher distillation on identical items. Subsequent RL saturates, and chance correction reduces the estimated recoverable performance gap by a factor of 7.5. Our CPR-GRPO loop (Compress-Probe-Replenish) uses this estimate to weight gold supervision, reaching 67.0 on public multiple-choice tasks and 74.5 under the training-matched hidden protocol. Without target-task training, CPR outperforms Base, SFT, and GRPO across six Chinese and English pragmatic and sarcasm evaluations. On three external datasets, training with CPR yields higher mean Macro-F1 than SFT and GRPO.

**Comment:** Matches criterion 1: Proposes a new training loop (CPR-GRPO) for improving instruction-following and pragmatic understanding in LLMs, with explicit mention of RL and supervised traces for instruction tuning.
**Relevance:** 8
**Novelty:** 7

## 57. [No Matter Who You Are: Addressivity and Sycophancy in Large Language Models](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.25728) <a id="link57"></a>
**ArXiv ID:** 2610.25728
**Authors:** Seungjong Sun, Hyunmin Jun, Minsik Yoon, Eungu Lee, Choongwon Kang, Young Seok Woo, Jang Hyun Kim

**Abstract:** arXiv:2610.25728v1 Announce Type: new  Abstract: Sycophancy, where an LLM becomes overly agreeable, deferential, or flattering toward the user, has been identified as a major failure mode. While post-training has been implicated as a cause, how it internally gives rise to sycophantic behavior remains insufficiently understood. Post-training transforms the model's output from text completion into utterances directed toward an addressee, thereby giving the output \textit{addressivity}. We hypothesize that the accompanying pressure to follow the addressee's instructions and align with their preferences links this addressivity to sycophancy. To test this, we use Sparse Autoencoders to extract addressivity directions from pre-trained and post-trained models and examine their relationship with sycophancy. Steering models with addressivity directions can amplify or suppress sycophancy, and this effect is observed only in post-trained models. Furthermore, comparing the internal mechanisms of both model types confirms that the association between addressivity and sycophancy is strengthened through post-training. Finally, through a controlled post-training experiment, we find that models are sycophantic not toward a specific addressee but toward any recognized addressee, suggesting that the presence of an addressee, rather than the addressee's identity, is what elicits sycophancy.

**Comment:** Matches criterion 7 closely: uses Sparse Autoencoders to extract addressivity directions in LLMs and analyze internal mechanisms related to sycophancy, a novel interpretability approach applied to neural networks.
**Relevance:** 8
**Novelty:** 7

## 58. [Beyond Factual Accuracy: A Benchmark for Evaluating Perspective Alignment in LLMs](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.25087) <a id="link58"></a>
**ArXiv ID:** 2610.25087
**Authors:** Sunghoon Kim, Sangyeop Kim

**Abstract:** arXiv:2610.25087v1 Announce Type: new  Abstract: Large Language Models often generate factually correct responses that fail to maintain user-requested perspectives, blending multiple perspectives and forcing users to filter through unwanted information---a perspective control failure that factual accuracy metrics cannot detect. We introduce PerspectiveBench, a benchmark quantifying perspective alignment by constructing scenarios where multiple valid reasoning paths coexist, and propose the Perspective Alignment Score (PAS) to measure adherence to specified perspectives. Experiments reveal that state-of-the-art models successfully include target content but fail to exclude non-requested perspectives. Providing documents through RAG improves factual grounding but does not resolve perspective drift, as models prioritize comprehensive coverage over strict user constraints. Code and data are available at https://github.com/sunghoon014/PerspectiveBench.

**Comment:** Matches criterion 4: The paper proposes a new benchmark and metric (PerspectiveBench and PAS) for evaluating perspective alignment in LLMs, fundamentally rethinking open-ended text generation evaluation by focusing on perspective control rather than factual accuracy.
**Relevance:** 8
**Novelty:** 7

## 59. [Preserving Reasoning Diversity in Reinforcement Learning with Verifiable Rewards via Adaptive Replay](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.24236) <a id="link59"></a>
**ArXiv ID:** 2610.24236
**Authors:** Wen-Zhi Li, Sainyam Galhotra, Claire Cardie

**Abstract:** arXiv:2610.24236v1 Announce Type: new  Abstract: Reinforcement Learning with Verifiable Rewards (RLVR) improves the reasoning capabilities of large language models, but often reduces the diversity of their reasoning behaviors. We study this phenomenon from the perspective of finite-sample learning dynamics and show that limited on-policy rollouts can progressively suppress semantically distinct but equally rewarded reasoning modes. Once a mode becomes sufficiently under-sampled, standard on-policy RLVR rarely recovers it. Motivated by this observation, we propose TAR, a Targeted Adaptive Replay framework that preserves diverse correct reasoning behaviors already present in the base policy. TAR constructs a compact memory bank of diverse base-model solutions and selectively replays them through an adaptive mechanism that activates only when stored behaviors are being suppressed during RLVR. Across six mathematical reasoning benchmarks and two Qwen2.5 model scales, TAR consistently achieves a better balance between reasoning performance and diversity preservation than existing diversity-preserving post-training methods, while substantially reducing the forgetting of correct base-model solutions. Code is available at https://github.com/wenzhilics/tar.

**Comment:** Matches criterion 12: advances in reasoning capabilities via reinforcement learning with verifiable rewards and preserving reasoning diversity.
**Relevance:** 8
**Novelty:** 7

## 60. [Does Unlearning Survive Collaboration? Robust Unlearning for Shared-Model LLM Teams](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.25731) <a id="link60"></a>
**ArXiv ID:** 2610.25731
**Authors:** Hengsong Liu, Liangyu Teng, Jingnan Cai, Yuntian Shi, Jingyu Zhang, Yang Liu, Jing Liu, Liang Song

**Abstract:** arXiv:2610.25731v1 Announce Type: new  Abstract: LLM unlearning is essential for removing sensitive or unwanted knowledge while preserving useful capabilities. As LLM teams are increasingly used to solve complex problems, forgetting must remain effective during collaboration. Despite advances in forgetting effectiveness, efficiency, and robustness to relearning, team deployment remains underexplored. We find that collaboration weakens forgetting: final-answer evaluations miss recoverable evidence in team messages, including complementary clues supporting joint recovery. We establish theoretically and confirm empirically that controlling final answers or individual messages does not ensure low recovery from their combination. To address this challenge, we propose Aggregation-Conditioned Unlearning (ACU). ACU trains a shared model using reconstruction feedback from individual and pooled messages, prioritizes views with higher recovery risk, and uses replacement-based credit assignment while protecting direct and collaborative utility. TOFU evaluates trajectory-level recovery, while WMDP-bio tests native forgetting and retained utility. On TOFU, ACU lowers full-trajectory recovery from 41.8% to 19.3% in ROUGE-L recall and factual recovery from 34.2% to 18.7% versus output-supervised training, with comparable retained-task performance. These results demonstrate that ACU curbs information leakage in LLM teams and generalizes across team sizes and readers.

**Comment:** Matches criterion 9: Studies robust unlearning in collaborative LLM teams, proposing Aggregation-Conditioned Unlearning (ACU) to reduce recovery of forgotten knowledge during collaboration.
**Relevance:** 8
**Novelty:** 7

## 61. [Auditing Tool Traces in Agentic LLMs: Source Localization and Counterfactual Dependence Testing](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.25540) <a id="link61"></a>
**ArXiv ID:** 2610.25540
**Authors:** Junhao Liu, Tianxiao Li, Yongxian Wu, Ziheng Duan, Ren Li, Jing Zhang, Martin Renqiang Min

**Abstract:** arXiv:2610.25540v1 Announce Type: new  Abstract: Agentic LLMs increasingly support scientific prediction by grounding decisions in external evidence. In biology, they promise accurate, interpretable predictions for important problems by integrating complex experimental and computational evidence. However, serialized tool results can contain hidden label shortcuts, such as dataset identifiers or template artifacts, that create apparent success without biological reasoning. To address this risk, we introduce a two-stage audit. Stage 1, Per-Tool TF-IDF Decomposition (PT2D), localizes label-predictive patterns to individual tool results while avoiding signal dilution in the concatenated tool trace. Stage 2, Token-Class Redaction, counterfactually tests whether the frozen LLM depends on the localized shortcut. We apply this audit to TCR-pMHC binding, where tool augmentation substantially outperforms a no-evidence baseline. At face value, this suggests that the LLM has learned to use scientific evidence. The audit overturns this interpretation: PT2D localizes the strongest signal to identifiers in one tool result, and redacting them reduces performance to chance. These findings show how hidden label shortcuts can masquerade as scientific reasoning or discovery, motivating source-level audits before benchmark gains are trusted.

**Comment:** Matches criterion 2: introduces a novel two-stage audit method (Per-Tool TF-IDF Decomposition and Token-Class Redaction) to detect hidden label shortcuts and contamination-like effects in agentic LLM tool traces, which relates to test set contamination and membership inference methods for language models.
**Relevance:** 8
**Novelty:** 7

---

## 62. [Safety Kernel: Mechanistic Alignment for Cross-Lingual Defense in LLMs](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.24614) <a id="link62"></a>
**ArXiv ID:** 2610.24614
**Authors:** Chengyu Xie, Fei Shen, Shuyi Miao, Yue Feng, Jian Yu, Pengyang Shao, Yijun Tian, Xiaoyu Du

**Abstract:** arXiv:2610.24614v1 Announce Type: new  Abstract: The safety capabilities of large language models (LLMs) are highly uneven across different languages, often allowing cross-lingual attacks to bypass safety guardrails. Existing alignment methods predominantly rely on external alignment signals and provide limited insight into underlying neuron-level mechanisms, making it difficult to provide a unified internal target for cross-lingual safety interventions. To address this, we analyze cross-lingual safety behavior at the neuron level and identify a compact set of safety-related neurons shared across languages, termed the safety kernel (SK). Specifically, we first utilize multilingual parallel safe and unsafe prompts to compute safety contrast with activation responsiveness (SCAR) scores, identifying language-wise safety-neuron candidates from internal activation patterns. We then intersect the resulting language-wise safety-neuron sets to obtain the SK, and validate its functional relevance to cross-lingual safety behavior through activation suppression and activation-state transfer interventions. Finally, building on the SK coordinates, we introduce SK-Masked LoRA, which constrains LoRA adaptation to the feed-forward network (FFN) output dimensions corresponding to the SK, thereby focusing safety-related changes on the cross-lingually shared neuron coordinates. Extensive experiments demonstrate that SK-Masked LoRA substantially reduces attack success rates across multiple languages, including languages excluded from SK discovery, while intervening on only a small fraction of internal neuron coordinates and producing generally limited changes in the model's general capabilities.

**Comment:** Matches criterion 8 closely: mechanistic interpretability of LLM safety behavior at neuron level, identifying safety-related neurons (safety kernel) and intervening on them to improve cross-lingual safety. This is a mechanistic interpretability technique applied to alignment/safety.
**Relevance:** 8
**Novelty:** 7

---

## 63. [Representations in Motion: Layer-wise Rotation and Trajectory Divergence in LLMs](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.25574) <a id="link63"></a>
**ArXiv ID:** 2610.25574
**Authors:** Zhuonan Yang, Yik Siu Chan, Wannan Yang, Ellie Pavlick

**Abstract:** arXiv:2610.25574v1 Announce Type: new  Abstract: Recent work suggests that activation dynamics--how representations change during inference--might be more revealing about language model behavior than static readouts from individual layers. In this work, we investigate the layer-wise evolution of representations of factuality, using true and false statements when the model is prompted to either lie or to be honest. We report a geometric phenomenon in which the factuality directions extracted from the lying and honest conditions appear to rotate away from each other across layers. We find that such layer-wise rotation also occurs in a task that requires the model to take different visual perspectives in the 3D world, suggesting that the rotation phenomenon might be indicative of manipulating conceptual spaces in general, rather than unique to factuality. We then investigate the "real time" transformation of conceptual representations on a trial-to-trial basis. We show that it is possible to make strong predictions about the model's target task and behavior from trajectories alone. In addition, diverging points in single-trial trajectories correspond to the onset of rotation in aggregated-trial analysis. Finally, we show that both rotation and divergent trajectories occur in more naturalistic settings in which models are not overtly prompted to "lie" but rather report false statements in the course of completing a different task.

**Comment:** Matches criterion 8: discovers a geometric phenomenon of layer-wise rotation of factuality directions in LLMs, providing mechanistic insights into representation dynamics during inference.
**Relevance:** 8
**Novelty:** 7

---

## 64. [From Stories to Features: Scaling Concept Vectors for AI Alignment & Interpretability](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.24556) <a id="link64"></a>
**ArXiv ID:** 2610.24556
**Authors:** Dmitry Scherbakov, Oleg Chernikov, Victoria Khamash, Kirill Afendulev, Alexey Dontsov, Ivan Oseledets, Elena Tutubalina, Anton Korznikov

**Abstract:** arXiv:2610.24556v1 Announce Type: new  Abstract: We study feature vectors: directions in a language model's activation space that correspond to human-interpretable concepts, such as honesty or deception. Existing unsupervised methods like sparse autoencoders attempt to recover the whole feature space, often at the cost of feature quality and interpretability. We take a more pragmatic route: focusing on concepts relevant to AI safety and alignment, we generate synthetic concept-antagonist story pairs and use the difference of their mean model activations as a feature vector. We scale this approach to 1,036 alignment-relevant concepts using 2M synthetic stories across multiple languages and genres, which we publicly release. Across six models from three model families, our feature vectors reliably generalize to detecting their corresponding concepts in chat dialogues and web text. They form an interpretable geometry shared across models and can causally steer model behavior when injected into activations. We further use these features to analyze model representations during jailbreak attacks, mathematical and agentic reasoning. Our targeted feature construction thus offers a scalable route to studying concepts in language models, potentially extending to millions of features across arbitrary domains.

**Comment:** Matches criterion 7: develops a scalable method to construct interpretable feature vectors (concept vectors) in LLM activation space using synthetic data, focusing on AI alignment and interpretability with sparse-like feature extraction.
**Relevance:** 8
**Novelty:** 7

---

## 65. [Safety Offset Learning: When Benign Fine-Tuning Misaligns LLMs](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.25611) <a id="link65"></a>
**ArXiv ID:** 2610.25611
**Authors:** Jesson Wang, Zhanhao Hu, Minghao Lin, Yuchen Wu, Aylin Caliskan, Mengyuan Li

**Abstract:** arXiv:2610.25611v1 Announce Type: new  Abstract: Safety risks from fine-tuning are commonly characterized by the harmfulness of the training data:harmful fine-tuning is considered dangerous, while benign fine-tuning is often assumed to preservealignment. We argue that this view misses a more fundamental factor-the behavioral transition inducedby supervision. Harmful fine-tuning and safety-degrading benign fine-tuning can both be understoodas learning the same refusal-to-compliance transition, but on different data distributions. We studywhether such a transition learned on benign inputs can generalize to harmful requests. We constructbenign examples that an aligned model initially tends to refuse and pair them with their originalharmless responses, creating a discrepancy between the model's initial refusal and the supervisedanswer. Fine-tuning on these examples increases harmful compliance, but only when two conditionshold together: the discrepancy is large enough, and it cannot be resolved through a narrow surface-levelshortcut. With the induced discrepancy matched, varying only whether it is localized changes harmfulcompliance by an order of magnitude; raising the discrepancy does not help while a shortcut remainsavailable, and removing the shortcut does not help while the discrepancy is small. Building on thisobservation, we introduce SAFEOFF, a dataset of benign instructions rewritten to raise the alignedmodel's refusal likelihood and paired with their original answers. Across three aligned LLMs and threesafety benchmarks, SAFEOFF substantially weakens safety alignment despite containing no explicitharmful supervision, suggesting that fine-tuning safety depends not only on what data a model sees, butalso on which behavioral boundary the supervision moves.

**Comment:** Matches criterion 1 and 11: studies fine-tuning effects on safety alignment and instruction-following behavior, revealing subtle behavioral transitions that degrade alignment despite benign data.
**Relevance:** 8
**Novelty:** 7

---

## 66. [FlashMap: Flow Map Speculative Decoding](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.24365) <a id="link66"></a>
**ArXiv ID:** 2610.24365
**Authors:** Jeff Shen, Zhejian Zhou, Ryan Lee, Jonathan May, Shirley Ho

**Abstract:** arXiv:2610.24365v1 Announce Type: new  Abstract: Speculative decoding accelerates large language model (LLM) inference by using a lightweight drafter to propose a block of tokens that the target model verifies in one parallel pass. Autoregressive drafters generate this block one token at a time, whereas block diffusion drafters such as DFlash generate it in one pass, making drafting faster. However, these diffusion drafters predict each token independently, ignoring dependencies between tokens in the block. We introduce FlashMap, a continuous flow drafter that conditions a flow map language model on the target model's hidden states and generates the whole block in one forward pass while capturing inter-token dependencies. On Qwen3-8B, FlashMap is faster than a matched DFlash baseline on every benchmark, with 7% higher average speedup and up to 6.1x lossless acceleration.

**Comment:** Matches criterion 3: advances in diffusion language models with a continuous flow drafter for speculative decoding that captures inter-token dependencies and improves speed and acceleration.
**Relevance:** 8
**Novelty:** 7

## 67. [Concerns in Benchmarking Jailbreaks: An Empirical Study of Attack Success Rates](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.26595) <a id="link67"></a>
**ArXiv ID:** 2610.26595
**Authors:** Leon Tu Nguyen, Rohan Menon, Rafael Tappe Maestro, Eric Aubinais, Chih-Hong Cheng

**Abstract:** arXiv:2610.26595v1 Announce Type: new  Abstract: Jailbreak methods are commonly compared using Attack Success Rate (ASR), but these comparisons depend on how responses are generated and judged. In this paper, we examine eight attacks against three open-weight language models using three evaluation procedures. Low-resource language attacks achieve a mean ASR of 96.0% under keyword matching but 2.7% under a Qwen3 evaluator following StrongREJECT scoring instructions. Changing the number and arrangement of attempts reverses the ranking of DeepInception and rewriting. In a separate experiment, sampling 100 responses for each unchanged request at Ollama's default temperature of 0.8 yields 100% ASR under keyword matching but 50% under the Qwen3 evaluator. Beyond evaluator sensitivity, these findings raise a safety concern: repeated sampling can expose residual unsafe behavior despite an initial refusal, suggesting probabilistic suppression rather than robust exclusion. We recommend explicit success definitions, matched response budgets, validated evaluators, and reporting cumulative failure under repeated interaction rather than relying on single-generation tests.

**Comment:** Matches criterion 19: Studies security of LLMs by empirically analyzing jailbreak attack success rates and evaluation sensitivity, highlighting probabilistic suppression issues and recommending robust evaluation protocols.
**Relevance:** 8
**Novelty:** 6

---

## 68. [ABACUS: Program-Guided Multistep Reasoning over Frozen Judgment Models](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.25963) <a id="link68"></a>
**ArXiv ID:** 2610.25963
**Authors:** Yuhao Wang, Zelin Zang

**Abstract:** arXiv:2610.25963v1 Announce Type: new  Abstract: Judgment models answer semantic questions by scoring a finite set of candidate outcomes. Can reusable programs turn these local decisions into powerful multistep reasoning? We introduce \method, a framework that jointly constructs judgment schemas and composition programs for frozen judgment models. A generative teacher designs the procedure offline; the judgment model resolves local semantic questions online, and the saved program composes its decisions into an answer. We study three allocations of design effort between humans and the teacher, focusing on construction through a shared interface. We instantiate the framework with Kev. On three selected BIG-Bench Hard tasks, nine independently constructed programs solve all 450 executions of 150 development inputs. A separate deployment study freezes three programs after development-set reference-agreement refinement and evaluates them on 600 held-out questions. These programs achieve near-perfect 99.67\% macro accuracy, versus 64.00\% for direct Kev and 68.80\% for final-answer-only Qwen3.5-4B. They attain comparable observed accuracy to Qwen's unrestricted response policy (99.07\%) with an \textbf{8.43-fold online speedup}: 0.255 versus 2.150 seconds per question. These results show how reusable programs can substantially expand the reasoning capability of a compact judgment model while keeping its parameters fixed.

**Comment:** Matches criterion 12: Advances in LLM reasoning capabilities by using reusable programs to compose multistep reasoning over frozen judgment models, achieving near-perfect accuracy with speedup.
**Relevance:** 7
**Novelty:** 7

## 69. [GLOBE: Geometry-Led Online Behavioral Early Exit via Unit-Sphere Trajectories](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.25970) <a id="link69"></a>
**ArXiv ID:** 2610.25970
**Authors:** Xinyue Peng, Zhenlin Liang, Meng Cai, Hua Wang

**Abstract:** arXiv:2610.25970v1 Announce Type: new  Abstract: Chain-of-thought (CoT) reasoning can improve problem solving, but models often keep generating after the correct answer is already reachable; the extra steps raise serving costs and may replace a correct intermediate result with an incorrect one. We propose GLOBE (Geometry-Led Online Behavioral Early Exit), an online, training-free algorithm that terminates the model's CoT process early. GLOBE embeds each completed reasoning segment as a point on a unit sphere and reads the shape of the resulting semantic trajectory. GLOBE first applies a trial phase that generates short trial answers in a separate branch, stopping the main stream on an accepted trial after checking confidence against a threshold. If generation continues, a trajectory phase stops the stream once this path repeatedly reverses direction with little net progress. On five benchmarks including mathematical reasoning, tool calling, and agent memory, GLOBE cuts output tokens by 49.7% and active end-to-end wall time (E2E) by 51.0% across two models, and achieves a five-task average of 80.58%, comparable to Full CoT.

**Comment:** Matches criterion 14: Proposes a new paradigm for monitoring chain-of-thought reasoning by analyzing semantic trajectories to enable early exit.
**Relevance:** 7
**Novelty:** 7

## 70. [Global Convergence in Deep Networks via the Second Law of Thermodynamics](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.24775) <a id="link70"></a>
**ArXiv ID:** 2610.24775
**Authors:** Matan Tsipory, Ofir Gaash, Itamar Harel, Nathan Srebro, Daniel Soudry

**Abstract:** arXiv:2610.24775v1 Announce Type: new  Abstract: Existing convergence analyses of finite-width overparameterized deep neural networks under standard parameterization often rely on parameters remaining close to their initialization and typically neglect the role of noise, despite empirical evidence that stochasticity can improve optimization and generalization. To bridge this gap, we study $L_2$-regularized continuous-time Langevin dynamics (CLD) with isotropic noise, for training feedforward networks with smooth activations. We introduce a change-of-measure inequality, inspired by the second law of thermodynamics, that enables control of key properties of the training dynamics under the time-evolving parameter distribution. Leveraging a sharpened lower bound on the minimum eigenvalue of the Neural Tangent Kernel (NTK) at initialization, we show that injected noise helps preserve NTK stability with high probability without confining the weights to a small neighborhood of their initialization. As a result, we derive a non-asymptotic upper bound on the expected training loss. Our analysis yields explicit conditions on width and noise temperature that guarantee convergence to a finite loss floor. Finally, we experimentally show the network Jacobian can change from initialization, departing the commonly analyzed lazy regime.

**Comment:** Matches criterion 6: Studies scaling laws in neural networks with a novel theoretical analysis leveraging thermodynamics and NTK stability under noise.
**Relevance:** 7
**Novelty:** 7

---

## 71. [Conditional information Retrieval for Longitudinal Survey Harmonisation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.26173) <a id="link71"></a>
**ArXiv ID:** 2610.26173
**Authors:** Wing Yan Li, Zeqiang Wang, Michelle Lin, Jon Johnson, Suparna De

**Abstract:** arXiv:2610.26173v1 Announce Type: new  Abstract: Identifying conceptually equivalent questions across different surveys is a challenging information retrieval task where surface-level textual similarity is insufficient. Two questions may be lexically similar yet assess entirely different sociological sub-concepts, or lexically different yet conceptually equivalent. We introduce a conditional information retrieval framework that addresses this by decomposing the task into two steps: concept extraction, which identifies the underlying sociological concept of a survey question using a fine-tuned generative model, and concept-aware retrieval, which augments question representations with the extracted concept as an explicit conditioning signal for finer-grained alignment. To reflect the realistic data scarcity conditions of specialised social science annotation, we use CLOSER as a case study. Our framework consistently outperforms baselines by 2-3 points in both MAP and Recall. We further analyse fine-tuning strategies including SFT, DPO, and dynamic DPO, finding that SFT delivers the most stable gains while dynamic DPO interacts differently with instruction-tuned versus distilled reasoning backbones. Finally, we demonstrate that a model fine-tuned on a general social science thesaurus (ELSST) transfers robustly to task-specific survey data, offering a practical solution under data scarcity.

**Comment:** Matches criterion 1: Discusses fine-tuning strategies including SFT, DPO, and dynamic DPO for instruction-following and concept extraction in language models. Analyzes instruction-tuning methods and their interaction with reasoning backbones, relevant to improving instruction-following capabilities.
**Relevance:** 8
**Novelty:** 6
