## 72. [Transformers as Adaptive Kernel Learners across Tasks for In-Context Learning](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.24441) <a id="link72"></a>
**ArXiv ID:** 2610.24441
**Authors:** Zhaiming Shen, Alexander Cloninger, Rongjie Lai, Wenjing Liao

**Abstract:** arXiv:2610.24441v1 Announce Type: new  Abstract: Transformers have emerged as a dominant architecture in modern machine learning, with in-context learning (ICL) enabling models to adapt to new tasks by conditioning on prompts, without any parameter updates. To understand the attention mechanism in transformer blocks, a growing line of theoretical work interprets ICL through the lens of classical statistical estimators. Recent works have established a connection between the attention mechanism and kernel methods, while the kernel function is usually assumed to be fixed. In practice, the query and key matrices in the attention mechanism are trained, which allows the attention mechanism to serve as an adaptive kernel method. In this work, we demonstrate that transformers can act as adaptive anisotropic kernel learners. Specifically, we study ICL for low-rank linear and multi-index nonlinear regressions, and conduct numerical simulations to show that the learned attention scores closely resemble the oracle kernels for these function classes. We then develop a general framework to analyze the generalization error of ICL by connecting transformers to the oracle kernel estimator for each function class. Our theory gives an explicit and constructive implementation of the oracle kernel estimators for these function classes using a wide and shallow transformer architecture, illustrating how the prediction is carried out by the interaction between the query token and the context tokens. Our work complements existing studies by providing numerical and theoretical evidence of how the attention mechanism adapts to anisotropic kernels in both linear and nonlinear settings.

**Comment:** Matches criterion 12: Advances in LLM reasoning capabilities and understanding how transformers perform in-context learning via adaptive kernel learning. Provides theoretical and empirical insights into transformer attention as adaptive kernel learners, relevant to reasoning and generalization in language models.
**Relevance:** 7
**Novelty:** 7

---

## 73. [Verifying Human-Written Reasoning References](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.24690) <a id="link73"></a>
**ArXiv ID:** 2610.24690
**Authors:** Ali Ansari, Farhana Ferdousi Liza

**Abstract:** arXiv:2610.24690v1 Announce Type: new  Abstract: Evaluating the reasoning of AI systems often relies on human-written reasoning chains as references, yet the logical support for their individual steps is rarely verified. Although prior neuro-symbolic approaches have formalized and checked reasoning, their reported evaluations do not systematically assess whether step conclusions in human-written reference derivations are entailed by their cited premise sets or investigate the sources of failures in those references. To address these limitations, we present a novel reference-centred assessment method for human-written derivations that combines entailment checking against cited premise sets, full-premise rechecking, and failure attribution to examine the reliability of these derivations and their formal representations. We evaluate it on P-FOLIO, using a large language model (LLM) to translate step conclusions into first-order logic (FOL) and the Z3 SMT solver to check entailment. Of 386 derivations examined, 202 (52.3%) meet all our checking criteria, meaning that every translated step is entailed by its stated support and the required target is established under the available FOL representations. Among 230 steps not entailed by their stated support, 191 become entailed when checked against the complete original premises, indicating that most initial failures reflect incomplete or mismatched support rather than unsupported claims. Manual analysis of the remaining 39 cases identifies 21 human reasoning errors, 11 FOL translation errors, and 7 dataset annotation errors. A separate manual audit of 50 randomly sampled steps that passed the entailment checks found 46 faithful FOL translations (92%). These findings suggest that disagreement with a human-written reasoning reference may originate in the reference or its formal representation rather than in the AI system being evaluated.

**Comment:** Matches criterion 4: Proposes a new paradigm for evaluating human-written reasoning references by formal entailment checking using LLM translation to FOL and SMT solvers, fundamentally rethinking evaluation of reasoning chains.
**Relevance:** 7
**Novelty:** 7

---

## 74. [Cache2LoRA: Understanding and Improving Context Internalization](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.24107) <a id="link74"></a>
**ArXiv ID:** 2610.24107
**Authors:** Yi Fang, Gaurav Srivastava, Zhenyu Bi, Sirui Ding, Xuan Wang

**Abstract:** arXiv:2610.24107v1 Announce Type: new  Abstract: Context-to-LoRA methods encode documents as low-rank parameter updates, allowing language models to answer without retaining the source text in their decoding context. How reliably do these updates preserve facts and support their use under different instructions? Controlled diagnosis of existing context-to-LoRA methods separates fact discrimination, target retention, and response adaptation. Competing sources influence answers, while target-only construction and memory-state optimization improve recovery at fixed update size. Recovering facts also does not ensure that answers follow response instructions. We introduce Cache2LoRA, which uses each query to aggregate evidence from a reusable frozen document cache before generating LoRA updates. Trainable encoding operates only on the query and memory-token suffix, and multi-style supervision teaches both evidence preservation and answer expression. Across 17 tasks, Cache2LoRA improves on existing context-to-LoRA methods and achieves macro F1 of 0.663, approaching full-context in-context learning at 0.671. For 32K documents and 256-token outputs at batch size 16, its decoding throughput is 5.9$\times$ that of full-context ICL. At matched memory size, generated LoRA preserves answer accuracy more reliably than the learned-KV variant under long, diverse intervening text and combines more effectively with retained document KV, yielding higher hybrid F1 and EM.

**Comment:** Matches criterion 1: Proposes a new method Cache2LoRA for improving context internalization and instruction-following in language models via low-rank parameter updates and multi-style supervision, which is a specific fine-tuning step to improve instruction-following.
**Relevance:** 8
**Novelty:** 6

---

## 75. [Continual Test-Time Learning with Evolution Strategies](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.24137) <a id="link75"></a>
**ArXiv ID:** 2610.24137
**Authors:** Guangya Hao, Yiting Duan, Longyan Tan, Yunbo Long, Hao Wu, Ge Zheng, Alexandra Brintrup, Zhuokai Zhao

**Abstract:** arXiv:2610.24137v1 Announce Type: new  Abstract: Large language models increasingly operate in open-ended environments, where they are expected to continually adapt to evolving tasks and distributions, often without access to external supervision. Existing continual learning approaches rely on external supervision, such as labeled data, demonstrations, or reward signals, which can be costly or unavailable in practice. Test-time adaptation removes this dependence by learning from self-generated supervision, but existing methods derive that supervision from the single policy being updated, so once the policy drifts, its own learning signal drifts with it, leading to policy collapse, degraded reasoning, and catastrophic forgetting. In this paper, we propose \textbf{Evolution Strategy Reinforcement Learning (ESRL)}, a continual test-time learning framework that draws its self-generated supervision from a population of policies instead of the single policy being trained. ESRL perturbs an anchor policy in parameter space to form a population of nearby policies, samples one trajectory from each, and aggregates their answers into label-free group-relative learning signals for the central student. The anchor is held fixed within each block and refreshed from the student at block boundaries, so the population stays centered on a stable yet current policy and continues to supply diverse and reliable supervision as the student moves. In single-task test-time adaptation across mathematical reasoning, general knowledge, and medical domains, ESRL improves average accuracy by up to 31.7% relative to the base model and 16.6% relative to the strongest test-time adaptation baseline. Notably, ESRL substantially reduces catastrophic forgetting and achieves the strongest final performance in continual learning.

**Comment:** Matches criterion 1: Proposes a new continual test-time learning method (Evolution Strategy Reinforcement Learning) that improves adaptation of large language models without external supervision, which relates to improving instruction-following or RLHF-like continual fine-tuning methods.
**Relevance:** 7
**Novelty:** 7

---

## 76. [SWIFT: Self-guided Weak-to-strong dIFfusion models via Target extrapolation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.25844) <a id="link76"></a>
**ArXiv ID:** 2610.25844
**Authors:** Yanjin He, Cuifeng Shen, Wanzhou Lei, Maohua Li, Tao Lan, Kan Liu, Hanlin Tang, Qing Qu

**Abstract:** arXiv:2610.25844v1 Announce Type: new  Abstract: Training high-quality diffusion models is computationally expensive, and inference-time guidance often adds further sampling cost. In this work, we introduce Swift, a simple yet effective self-guided training method that uses a frozen weak denoiser to improve a stronger model without adding inference overhead. For each noisy input, Swift extrapolates the clean regression target away from the weak denoiser's prediction, amplifying components that remain underfit. The weak guide can be an early training checkpoint, requires no backpropagation during main-model training, and is discarded afterward. The resulting model retains the original sampler and supports both conditional and unconditional generation without label dropout or an unconditional branch. We explain this mechanism through a spectral analysis of finite-time population gradient flow in an RKHS. With an early-stopped weak guide, we prove that a nonempty range of extrapolation strengths yields strictly lower posterior-mean error than matched standard training, despite shifting the regression target away from the posterior mean. Experiments on CIFAR-10 and ImageNet-256 demonstrate improvements across model scales and representations. In one ImageNet-256 configuration, Swift surpasses the baseline's best FID using roughly half the total training compute, including guide preparation and training overhead. On RAEv2, it achieves FID 1.15 without inference-time guidance and 0.99 with guidance. These results establish target extrapolation as an effective approach to weak-to-strong diffusion training with efficient single-model inference.

**Comment:** Matches criterion 3 closely: The paper presents a new training method (Swift) for diffusion models that improves performance and efficiency, with theoretical analysis and empirical results. Although the experiments are on image datasets, the method is a general diffusion training technique potentially applicable to language diffusion models, and the abstract emphasizes continuous diffusion modeling improvements.
**Relevance:** 7
**Novelty:** 7

---

## 77. [Prefix-Tuning for Arbitrary Output Sequences on Pretrained Transformers](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.24123) <a id="link77"></a>
**ArXiv ID:** 2610.24123
**Authors:** Peter Cho-Ho Lam, Ziyi Wang, Zirui Zhou

**Abstract:** arXiv:2610.24123v1 Announce Type: new  Abstract: Prefix-tuning is a parameter-efficient fine-tuning method for large language models (LLMs) that steers model outputs by prepending task-specific prefix representations to the input sequence. Despite its practical success, the theoretical understanding of the expressiveness of prefixes remains incomplete. In particular, the following question remains open: Given a pretrained autoregressive Transformer, for any input-output sequence pair $(\mathtt{x},\mathtt{y})$, does there always exist a prefix $\mathcal{S}$ such that prepending $\mathcal{S}$ to $\mathtt{x}$ induces the model to generate the target output $\mathtt{y}$? In this work, we prove that under the assumption that $\mathtt{y}$ is in the range of the pretrained Transformer, such a prefix $\mathcal{S}$ exists and can be explicitly computed by solving multiple linear systems. We further show that this assumption is necessary, in the sense that such a prefix may not exist when the assumption fails, thereby providing a complete answer to the above question. Our analysis is constructive and the obtained theoretical results hold under fairly general assumptions on model architecture. Extensive experiments on GPT-2 and Qwen3-4B demonstrate that our algorithm produces prefixes with strong control over model outputs.

**Comment:** Matches criterion 8: Provides a theoretical constructive proof that prefix-tuning can induce arbitrary output sequences by solving linear systems, deepening understanding of prefix expressiveness in pretrained Transformers.
**Relevance:** 7
**Novelty:** 7

---

## 78. [ECHO: Efficient Causal Self-Recursion Beyond Auxiliary Multi-Token Prediction](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.24766) <a id="link78"></a>
**ArXiv ID:** 2610.24766
**Authors:** Yuxuan Zhou, Junyuan Shang, Tianyu Wang, Chunrui Zheng, Zichang Liu, Wenbin Chang, Shuohuan Wang, Dianhai Yu

**Abstract:** arXiv:2610.24766v1 Announce Type: new  Abstract: Existing Multi-token prediction (MTP) methods are auxiliary by design: the multi-horizon loss mostly trains modules attached to the backbone, and each added horizon costs another module and another pass. We propose Efficient Causal Self-Recursion (ECHO), which removes both costs. The backbone's own final layer predicts every horizon, so the supervision lands on the parameters that generate the next token, and Dynamic Depth Sampling (DDS) keeps deeper horizons affordable by drawing the training depth with probabilities that decay along depth. Future prediction thus becomes part of the autoregressive objective rather than a task attached to it, and the gains follow on both sides of it. In pretraining, added depth converts into downstream accuracy where auxiliary modules saturate, concentrating on reasoning and code at half the MTP training overhead of training every depth. At inference, the pretrained model is its own drafter: it drafts beyond the depth it was trained for, and keeps its draft-target alignment on the open-ended, high-entropy generation where post-hoc drafters lose theirs. The advantage survives a doubled token budget, a repeated pretraining run, and supervised finetuning.

**Comment:** Matches criterion 12: introduces Efficient Causal Self-Recursion (ECHO), a new training method improving autoregressive language model reasoning and generation by integrating multi-token prediction into the backbone with dynamic depth sampling.
**Relevance:** 7
**Novelty:** 7

---

## 79. [World Model Interpretability: From Decoding Representations to Understanding Latent Worlds](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.25296) <a id="link79"></a>
**ArXiv ID:** 2610.25296
**Authors:** Jize Wang, Yongcheng Jing, Xinyi Le, Cailian Chen, Xinping Guan, Dacheng Tao

**Abstract:** arXiv:2610.25296v1 Announce Type: new  Abstract: Emerging Joint-Embedding Predictive Architectures (JEPAs) are moving beyond representation learning towards latent world models that support prediction, planning, and control. Yet their latent worlds naturally lack a human-interpretable interface. In this Perspective, we argue that as JEPA latents become internal states for prediction and action, interpretability must move beyond what they encode to how latent variables interact, evolve, and causally drive prediction. To this end, we envision a four-stage research agenda, involving Ground, Structure, Verify, and Act, to guide this shift toward actionable latent-world understanding. Within our framework, existing JEPA interpretability efforts provide early evidence for Ground, while systematic analysis of latent structure, causal verification, and intervention remains largely unexplored. Overall, this Perspective aims to call for a broader latent-world interpretability agenda that shifts attention from decoding representations toward understanding, validating, and acting on latent worlds.

**Comment:** Matches criterion 8 and 16: The paper discusses interpretability of latent world models in Joint-Embedding Predictive Architectures, focusing on understanding latent variables' interactions and causal effects, which relates to mechanistic interpretability and latent reasoning.
**Relevance:** 7
**Novelty:** 7

---

## 80. [Learning When to Abort in Transformer Planning: A Mechanistic Study](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.25300) <a id="link80"></a>
**ArXiv ID:** 2610.25300
**Authors:** Shi Feng, Qimin Zhong, Siwei Wang, Yifei Shen, Shang-Hua Teng, Hao Liao, Wei Chen

**Abstract:** arXiv:2610.25300v1 Announce Type: new  Abstract: Planning does not by itself imply the ability to recognise errors, but recognising errors can improve planning capacity. In this paper, we study this phenomenon in Transformer planning. To analyze the inner mechanism, we follow the approach of Wang et al. (2024) and abstract planning as path-finding in graphs. In this setting, we train the model to detect the error of invalid transitions, i.e., after the model generates a next node that is not adjacent with the current node, the model should then generate a special abort token x. For expressiveness, we construct a Transformer with feed-forward width $O(|V|)$ for any fixed graph with node set $V$. It can detect all possible invalid transitions, and select a valid next node if the prefix is valid. We then propose a worst-case lower bound on feed-forward width, which makes the detector's linear width near-optimal up to logarithmic factors. In trained models, we demonstrate that invalid-transition detection increases previous-node attention, and most of the valid--invalid separation locates in the feed-forward network's output. This accords with our theoretical insights. Finally, we use this error detection to improve the model's performance: at inference, generating the abort token x will directly cause an external procedure to discard the attempt and restart from the original prompt. Experiments show that error detection combined with retry improves model's planning reliability on graphs, Countdown, and mazes.

**Comment:** Matches criterion 8: mechanistic interpretability study of Transformer planning models detecting invalid transitions and aborting, with theoretical and empirical analysis of internal mechanisms.
**Relevance:** 7
**Novelty:** 7

---

## 81. [Compositional Activation Steering Across Languages and Behaviors with Contextual Gates](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.24906) <a id="link81"></a>
**ArXiv ID:** 2610.24906
**Authors:** Daniil Gurgurov, Akansh Maurya, Josef van Genabith, Simon Ostermann, Hitomi Yanaka

**Abstract:** arXiv:2610.24906v1 Announce Type: new  Abstract: Activation steering has emerged as an effective and precise way to influence the behaviours of language models. However, composing multiple steering directions to control several behaviours simultaneously remains challenging, particularly in multilingual settings and with training-free steering vectors. We address this challenge with contextual steering, jointly training gates that adapt the strength of fixed steering directions to the current hidden state and interactions between interventions. Across a broad range of languages, behavioural attributes, and models, our approach outperforms constant-strength steering and four strong baselines, including prompting. It also better preserves general knowledge and keeps the model's internal computation closer to that of the prompted model. Our analysis further shows how jointly trained gates adapt their contributions during composition, explaining the mechanisms underlying effective compositional steering.

**Comment:** Matches criterion 8: Presents a new mechanistic interpretability technique called contextual activation steering with trained gates to compose multiple steering directions in multilingual LLMs, explaining internal mechanisms of compositional control.
**Relevance:** 7
**Novelty:** 7

---

## 82. [CADS: Measuring the Incremental Contribution of LLM Proposers in Sequential Biological Screens](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.24597) <a id="link82"></a>
**ArXiv ID:** 2610.24597
**Authors:** Guanxing Chen, Nihui Shao, Haohuai He, Zhenchao Tang, Pengwei Hu, Zhi-An Huang

**Abstract:** arXiv:2610.24597v1 Announce Type: new  Abstract: Large language models are increasingly used to guide sequential biological screens, yet standard evaluations rely on raw hit counts that conflate a proposer's value with the discovery capacity of the underlying algorithmic harness. Adaptive sampling and corrupted experimental readouts also leave standard feedback comparisons without a false-discovery guarantee. We present Certified Agentic Discovery Screens (CADS), an error-controlled measurement framework that quantifies a proposer's incremental value against an identical LLM-free loop. By integrating capped likelihood-ratio bounds, e-processes, and complete-family e-BH, CADS controls the terminal false discovery rate under the stated null and corruption conditions despite arbitrary dependence, adaptive sampling, and optional stopping. In DepMap-backed simulations, CADS exposes two evaluation artifacts: the LLM-free harness alone clears the feedback gate, and raw origin counts overstate LLM contributions because LLM picks displace fallback discoveries. On the initial 20 seeds, supplying tested-candidate history to Qwen2.5-7B shifts its net contribution from +0.35, with a 95% interval covering zero, to +2.90, with the interval above zero. This initial net effect did not persist in a 50-seed matched-quota replication. These results support net gain over an LLM-free loop, measured between separately certified sets, as the appropriate measure of proposer contribution.

**Comment:** Matches criterion 2: Presents a new statistical framework (CADS) with error-controlled measurement and likelihood-ratio bounds to quantify incremental contributions of LLM proposers, addressing evaluation artifacts and false discovery control under adaptive sampling and corruption. This relates to test set contamination and membership inference methods by providing rigorous statistical guarantees in LLM evaluation.
**Relevance:** 7
**Novelty:** 7

---

## 83. [Trackable Skill Library Lifecycle Management for Agentic Reinforcement Learning](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.25126) <a id="link83"></a>
**ArXiv ID:** 2610.25126
**Authors:** Xi Zhu, Yuanlin Duan, Changyi Yang, Kai Mei, Riyang Bao, Shichao Zhu, Dimitris N. Metaxas

**Abstract:** arXiv:2610.25126v1 Announce Type: new  Abstract: Skill-augmented agentic RL has emerged as an effective approach to improving LLM agents on long-horizon tasks. Despite this progress, individual skills may follow distinct trajectories as the policy evolves, with some becoming redundant after the model internalizes their capabilities, others remaining valuable as external scaffolding, and still others turning harmful. However, existing skill library systems fail to identify each skill's true contribution due to free-riding among co-retrieved skills, difficulty confounding across tasks, and the absence of skill-specific counterfactual evidence, which prevents reliable skill lifecycle management. We propose TrackRL, an agentic RL framework where skill tracking drives skill library full lifecycle management. Specifically, within each GRPO group, we turn grouped sampling into online randomized controlled experiments. Independent random injection and within-group contrasts on the same task isolate each skill's true contribution, and reliability-weighted aggregation accumulates evidence across groups. As evidence accumulates, we continuously track skill utility, which measures the skill's causal contribution as external scaffolding, and model mastery, which reflects how far the corresponding capability has been internalized. Along this line, we formulate skill lifecycle management as an evidence-gated state-transition process that governs each skill from generation and admission through protected incubation to retention, retirement, or removal, while a soft capacity bound controls library growth. Experiments show that TrackRL outperforms recent skill-augmented RL baselines in both library-assisted and skill-free evaluation, with further analyses revealing how skill value shifts during policy learning and how evidence-driven state transitions shape skill library evolution. Our code is available at https://github.com/xizhu1022/TrackRL.

**Comment:** Matches criterion 1: Proposes a new agentic RL framework (TrackRL) with randomized controlled experiments to track skill contributions and manage skill lifecycle, which is a methodological improvement in reinforcement learning for instruction-following agents.
**Relevance:** 7
**Novelty:** 7

---

## 84. [AfriGuard: Safety and Robust Refusal For African Languages](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.26474) <a id="link84"></a>
**ArXiv ID:** 2610.26474
**Authors:** Israel Abebe Azime, Jesujoba O. Alabi, Iffat Maab, Alexander D. Cai, Tadesse Destaw Belay, Atnafu Lambebo Tonja, Jonas Kgomo, Folasade Alabi, Salomey Osei, Saminu Muhammad Aliyu, Nkechinyere Faith Aguobi, Bontu Fufa Balcha, Blessing Sibanda, Davis David, Mouhamadane Mboup, Daud Abolade, Neo Putini, Louis Powell, Kiant'e Brantley, Philipp Slusallek, Dietrich Klakow, Julia Kreutzer

**Abstract:** arXiv:2610.26474v1 Announce Type: new  Abstract: Large language models (LLMs) designed to interact with users are typically trained to respond appropriately to harmful or toxic content. However, ensuring appropriate responses becomes more challenging in diverse linguistic and cultural contexts, where safety risks may be expressed or interpreted differently. Building safe AI systems therefore requires detecting safety risks, understanding their nature, and responding appropriately, while accounting for linguistic and cultural variations that add complexity to each stage. In this work, we introduce AfriGuard, a culturally grounded multilingual dataset for studying harmful intent, safety-category understanding, and refusal behavior across 10 African languages and English. Our findings reveal that guardrail models underperform both open- and closed-source LLMs on safety-intent and safety-category classification, while models show limited understanding of appropriate safety behavior, scoring below 60% F1. We further find that models often fail to respond appropriately to unsafe prompts not because they explicitly refuse, but because they produce linguistically inadequate responses that result in incidental non-assistance rather than proper refusals. Finally, using AfriGuard, we develop efficient guardrail models that achieve performance competitive with models up to 30x larger, introduce refusal detectors that improve average performance by 43.9% over existing detectors, and demonstrate that supervised fine-tuning can substantially improve overall safety performance.

**Comment:** Matches criterion 5: Provides a new multilingual dataset (AfriGuard) for studying safety and refusal behavior in LLMs across African languages, contributing to real-world usage and safety properties of language models.
**Relevance:** 8
**Novelty:** 6

---

## 85. [Statistical Learning Theory for Direct Preference Optimization](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.25856) <a id="link85"></a>
**ArXiv ID:** 2610.25856
**Authors:** Joel Wills, Yuanfan Li, Yiming Ying, Ding-Xuan Zhou

**Abstract:** arXiv:2610.25856v1 Announce Type: new  Abstract: Direct Preference Optimization (DPO) learns from pairwise preferences through a logistic objective whose score-difference parameterization follows the Bradley--Terry model. We study how failure of this modeling assumption limits DPO, distinguishing structural misspecification from limited model capacity and finite-sample estimation. Our main contribution is a characterization of structural approximation error through the triangle-curl of the preference log-odds, which vanishes exactly when preferences admit a Bradley--Terry representation. We upper bound the achievable excess logistic risk by a score-class approximation term and a triangle-curl term, and establish a lower bound proportional to triangle-curl under additional boundedness assumptions. For ReLU score models, including clipped models, we derive explicit approximation rates showing that increasing capacity can eliminate the score-approximation term while leaving the structural obstruction unchanged. We further establish finite-sample bounds for clipped empirical risk minimizers that separate network approximation, structural misspecification, and estimation error. We place these results within a surrogate-risk framework by identifying contextual preference accuracy (CPA) as a natural measure of agreement between preference judgments and changes in pairwise probability ratios relative to the reference policy. Standard logistic consistency and calibration results connect DPO's objective to this policy property and transfer our excess-risk upper bounds to CPA, without treating CPA as a complete measure of generated-response quality.

**Comment:** Matches criterion 1: Discusses Direct Preference Optimization (DPO), a method related to preference learning and optimization that is relevant to instruction-following and RLHF improvements by analyzing structural misspecification and approximation errors in preference models.
**Relevance:** 7
**Novelty:** 7

## 86. [How Benchmark Graph Construction Shapes Multi-Hop KG Navigation: A Controlled Audit of PathQuestion and PathQuestion-Large](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.26399) <a id="link86"></a>
**ArXiv ID:** 2610.26399
**Authors:** Eduin E. Hernandez, Sergio A. Diaz, Luis F. Garcia, Nurassyl Askar, Stefano Rini

**Abstract:** arXiv:2610.26399v1 Announce Type: new  Abstract: Multi-hop knowledge-graph question answering (KGQA) benchmarks are often treated as fixed language-and-reasoning tasks, but for navigation-based methods, difficulty also depends on the graph in which those tasks are instantiated. This makes graph construction a consequential but often entangled part of the navigation task. To isolate this effect, we audit PathQuestion (PQ) and PathQuestion-Large (PQL) as graph-navigation benchmarks under a controlled intervention: we hold questions, topic entities, released answers, released path annotations, and family-level splits fixed while replacing each released task-filtered graph with the larger source KG distributed with the benchmark. Source restoration expands the effective navigation search space and graph-consistent answer and path multiplicity while sharply reducing unguided reachability to released answers. We evaluate whether these structural changes persist across navigation policies using structural references, frozen small-language-model navigation policies, and trained MINERVA policies. Across both frozen and trained policies, restoration substantially reduces released-answer accuracy and trajectory fidelity, while graph-consistent evaluation recovers only part of the loss. These results show that benchmark graph construction is part of the navigation task specification rather than merely an implementation detail and can materially shape measured behavior even when the released QA task is held fixed.

**Comment:** Matches criterion 4: Proposes a controlled audit of benchmark graph construction effects on multi-hop KG navigation evaluation, fundamentally rethinking evaluation paradigms by showing how benchmark design shapes measured behavior.
**Relevance:** 7
**Novelty:** 7

## 87. [OPDArena: Understanding Learning and Forgetting in On-Policy Distillation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.25651) <a id="link87"></a>
**ArXiv ID:** 2610.25651
**Authors:** Yuanze Xu, Tianzhu Ye, Hui Sun, Wenhui Wang, Li Dong, Furu Wei

**Abstract:** arXiv:2610.25651v1 Announce Type: new  Abstract: On-policy distillation (OPD) is increasingly used for language model post-training, yet how teacher supervision shapes learning and forgetting remains unclear. We present OPDArena, a framework comparing supervised fine-tuning (SFT), reinforcement learning with verifiable rewards (RLVR), and OPD across five pretrained models and diverse teacher sources. It jointly evaluates mathematical learning, general capability retention, and predictive distribution changes. Teacher choice strongly shapes retention: students of RLVR-trained teachers improve on the target task while retaining prior capabilities, whereas students of SFT-trained teachers incur greater capability loss. Across post-training methods, out-of-domain (OOD) distribution change shows a more consistent positive association with forgetting than in-domain change. To understand how teacher choice affects these changes, we examine the additional information teachers provide relative to the student's pretrained model. Controlled teacher comparisons show that the task-specific preferences teachers acquire through post-training make student updates more concentrated in-domain.

**Comment:** This paper presents OPDArena, a framework to analyze learning and forgetting in on-policy distillation, comparing SFT, RL with verifiable rewards, and OPD. It provides insights into how teacher supervision affects learning and forgetting in language model post-training, relevant to understanding instruction-following fine-tuning methods (criterion 1).
**Relevance:** 8
**Novelty:** 6

## 88. [Cognitive Map in Large Language Models](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.25596) <a id="link88"></a>
**ArXiv ID:** 2610.25596
**Authors:** Albert Qin, Jonathan Cohen, Gautam Reddy

**Abstract:** arXiv:2610.25596v1 Announce Type: new  Abstract: It has long been proposed that animals construct cognitive maps to support flexible behavior in novel environments. We tested whether large language models form similar representations using relational reasoning tasks and causal "mosaic experiments" that manipulate internal activations in open-weight language models. Models beyond the ten-billion-parameter scale successfully inferred unstated relationships between entities and generalized to synthetic entity names. Across model and task families, models generated abstract representations of relational structure and combined them additively with representations of entity identity, binding entities to roles within that structure. Models stored observed relationships as pairwise links that they composed retrospectively when queried, rather than prospectively positioning each entity in a global coordinate system. Binding was mediated by a sparse, distributed set of key--value pairs; by perturbing these pairs, we implanted "false memories" that altered model behavior in predictable ways. Overall, our findings reveal an internal organization analogous to a cognitive map that explains how language models reason with limited information.

**Comment:** Relevant to criterion 8: Provides mechanistic interpretability insights by analyzing internal representations in large language models as cognitive maps, revealing how relational reasoning is internally organized.
**Relevance:** 7
**Novelty:** 7

## 89. [Productive State-Space Exploration for Multi-turn RL in LLM Agents](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.25957) <a id="link89"></a>
**ArXiv ID:** 2610.25957
**Authors:** Mohammed Haroon Dupty, Yong Liang Goh, Song Guo, Wee Sun Lee

**Abstract:** arXiv:2610.25957v1 Announce Type: new  Abstract: Reinforcement Learning (RL) for Large Language Model (LLM) agents in multi-turn, long-horizon environments remains challenging due to inefficient action selection and repetitive behavioral patterns. In particular, agents often become trapped in high-confidence yet unproductive behaviors, repeatedly executing invalid actions or entering action loops that hinder task completion. We observe that these frequently violate environment preconditions, revealing a mismatch between the agent's representation of the internal state and the dynamics of the underlying environment. In addition, repetitive high-confidence actions lead to cyclical behaviors that provide little to no progress towards the goal. We argue that addressing these challenges requires more productive task-aligned exploration to improve the agent's understanding of environment dynamics. Motivated by intrinsic curiosity-driven exploration, we propose a conditional state-space exploration framework that balances task completion with intrinsic information-seeking behavior. Specifically, we introduce an intrinsic reward that penalizes actions whose observation duplicates prior state memory. This provides the benefit of reducing redundant behaviors and encouraging state-space coverage without relying on a general information-theoretic measure. Strong experimental results demonstrate that our approach substantially improves policy learning in multi-turn environments, mitigating invalid actions and repetitive loops.

**Comment:** Matches criterion 1: Proposes a novel intrinsic reward method for reinforcement learning in LLM agents to improve multi-turn instruction-following and exploration, addressing RLHF-related challenges in multi-turn environments.
**Relevance:** 7
**Novelty:** 7

---

## 90. [GardenBench: A lightweight, daily evaluation of LLM capabilities](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.23866) <a id="link90"></a>
**ArXiv ID:** 2610.23866
**Authors:** Benedict Cummins-Mburu, Mariana Garcia Mejia, Oscar Heath, Sabrina Kreyzerman, Ellie Murray, Arusan Surendiran, Rohan Alexander

**Abstract:** arXiv:2610.23866v1 Announce Type: new  Abstract: We introduce GardenBench, a benchmark with 11 test suites that evaluate the capabilities of Large Language Models (LLMs) across a range of tasks. By systematically prompting LLMs with various lightweight queries, we document day-to-day changes in model performance, including run costs and times, that other benchmarks miss. This allows us to compare the accuracy and features of responses across model providers and versions. We account for daily variations in model accuracy, capturing both model progress and degradation, for instance, when a model is changed post-deployment or when a new model is released. We base our evaluation on general-use tasks and make our results publicly accessible via a live dashboard, diversifying existing approaches to LLM testing. Our main contribution is an innovative, lightweight, and inexpensive benchmark, designed to be consumer-facing and for everyday tasks.

**Comment:** Matches criterion 4 closely: proposes GardenBench, a lightweight daily evaluation benchmark for LLM capabilities that captures day-to-day performance variations and model changes, innovating open-ended LLM evaluation paradigms.
**Relevance:** 8
**Novelty:** 6

## 91. [UpLoop: Upcycling Large Language Models with Gated Loop](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.25004) <a id="link91"></a>
**ArXiv ID:** 2610.25004
**Authors:** Tieyuan Chen, Huabin Liu, Yingtao Zhang, Shijie Li, Tengyu Pan, Weiyao Lin, Jianguo Li

**Abstract:** arXiv:2610.25004v1 Announce Type: new  Abstract: Looped large language models trade parameter storage for recurrent computation, but most are trained from scratch. In practice, pretraining with looped depth is expensive, making post-training upcycling of a pretrained non-looped LLM an attractive alternative. We introduce \textbf{UpLoop}, a post-training recipe based on \emph{layer-wise} recurrence. Instead of looping a whole model or a middle block, UpLoop loops each selected layer locally, preserving the pretrained depth hierarchy and avoiding feature-space mismatch. To make loop execution useful, we add hidden-wise gates on the attention and MLP residual branches, so later loops act as progressive refinements rather than redundant rewrites. Empirically, UpLoop breaks the commonly observed saturation at two loops ($R=2$) in prior looped models. Starting from Qwen3-4B Base and applying SFT, UpLoop improves the average accuracy across 20 benchmarks by 6.17\% at $R=3$. We use $R=3$ as the main setting for the best accuracy--cost trade-off, while both native and extrapolated $R=4$ yield further gains.

**Comment:** Matches criterion 3: advances in generative modeling of language via looped large language models with gated loops improving recurrent computation and accuracy.
**Relevance:** 7
**Novelty:** 7

## 92. [FisherEdit: Fisher Information Guided Knowledge Editing for Large Language Models](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.25914) <a id="link92"></a>
**ArXiv ID:** 2610.25914
**Authors:** Yizhou Yang, Jiahong Fu, Zhiwu Wang, Kailong Fan, Haokun Lin, Yichen Wu, Zhenan Sun, Renzhen Wang, Deyu Meng

**Abstract:** arXiv:2610.25914v1 Announce Type: new  Abstract: Sequential knowledge editing in Large Language Models (LLMs) often leads to a degradation of general capabilities due to cumulative parameter interference. In this paper, we revisit prominent projection-based methods like AlphaEdit and reveal a surprising mismatch: their robustness is primarily driven by update regularization rather than the intended null-space projection. Based on this insight, we propose FisherEdit, a theoretically grounded method built on Fisher geometry, which replaces coarse activation-space projections with importance-aware regularization in parameter space. It penalizes changes to sensitive parameters more strongly while retaining flexibility for new facts, and admits an efficient row-wise closed-form solution. Experiments on Llama-3, Qwen2.5, and Olmo-3 across multiple benchmarks demonstrate that FisherEdit achieves state-of-the-art performance while better preserving model stability and downstream task accuracy.

**Comment:** Matches criterion 9: presents a new method (FisherEdit) for knowledge editing/unlearning in LLMs that better preserves model stability and downstream accuracy.
**Relevance:** 7
**Novelty:** 7

## 93. [Not Every Rollout Needs Distillation: Correctness-Filtered On-Policy Self-Distillation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.25842) <a id="link93"></a>
**ArXiv ID:** 2610.25842
**Authors:** Harrison Zhao, Lei Huang, Fei Liu, Wen Shi, Zijian Jin

**Abstract:** arXiv:2610.25842v1 Announce Type: new  Abstract: On-policy self-distillation (OPSD) supervises student-generated trajectories with a reference-conditioned version of the same base model. We ask whether every trajectory supplies distinct supervision that warrants its distillation cost. Gradient measurements on Qwen3-1.7B at initialization reveal that correct and incorrect responses have similar individual gradient magnitudes, but incorrect responses have approximately three times the coherent gradient share. Their estimated population-mean directions also largely overlap. A signal--noise model fitted at a small group size tracks alignment at larger group sizes without refitting. Motivated by these measurements, we introduce correctness-filtered OPSD (C-OPSD), which generates one response per problem and distills only a subset of incorrect responses. Removing unselected rows before both distillation forwards avoids their teacher/student forward and student backward work. Across Qwen3-1.7B, 4B, and 8B on AIME24, AIME25, and HMMT25, C-OPSD achieves on-par performance with full OPSD. In a measured micro-batch-size-four implementation, executed rows decrease by approximately 68%, and with retained rows packed into full micro-batches, training takes about 40% less time while rollout generation remains unchanged. Together, the measurements and training results connect outcome-conditioned supervision redundancy to more compute-efficient distillation.

**Comment:** Matches criterion 1: proposes a new fine-tuning/distillation method (Correctness-Filtered On-Policy Self-Distillation) to improve instruction-following efficiency and performance in language models.
**Relevance:** 8
**Novelty:** 6

## 94. [T-MUON: Tensorizing Muon via t-SVD for Large Language Model Pre-training](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.24938) <a id="link94"></a>
**ArXiv ID:** 2610.24938
**Authors:** Dongyang Li, Mingsong Yan, Zheng Zhang, Sui Tang

**Abstract:** arXiv:2610.24938v1 Announce Type: new  Abstract: Muon has recently emerged as a strong optimizer for large language model pretraining by exploiting matrix structure through polar orthogonalization. However, it processes each parameter matrix independently, whereas recent tensor-aware extensions of Muon jointly update groups of eligible matrix parameters through matrix unfoldings. In contrast, we propose T-MUON, a t-SVD-based generalization that groups structurally related Transformer gradients into third-order tensors and defines the corresponding Muon-style update directly through the $M$-transform. We show that the exact T-MUON update is a steepest-descent direction under the $\star_M$-spectral norm and establish a convergence guarantee. Across GPT-2-style and LLaMA-style models from 51M to 1.3B parameters trained on FineWeb and FineWeb-Edu, head-wise T-MUON with the identity transform consistently achieves lower validation perplexity than AdamW, Muon, and variants using DFT, DCT, or DST transforms. These gains persist in additional experiments with longer context lengths and larger token budgets, although the preferred tensorization mode varies across settings.

**Comment:** Matches criterion 6: proposes a new optimizer (T-MUON) based on tensorized updates with convergence guarantees for large language model pretraining, with theoretical grounding and empirical improvements.
**Relevance:** 7
**Novelty:** 7

## 95. [Towards Robust Model Organisms for Eliciting Latent Knowledge](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.26698) <a id="link95"></a>
**ArXiv ID:** 2610.26698
**Authors:** Lennart Finke, Hardik Bhatnagar, Euodia Dodd, Francis Rhys Ward, Yves-Alexandre de Montjoye, Matthias Bethge, Mary Phuong

**Abstract:** arXiv:2610.26698v1 Announce Type: new  Abstract: A key problem in AI safety is eliciting latent knowledge (ELK), extracting information from a model that is misaligned and more knowledgeable than its overseer. For example, a misaligned model used to scan code for vulnerabilities may want to withhold its findings, i.e., sandbag, in order to exploit them later. To study ELK empirically, we need model organisms that mirror this setting. Existing model organisms fall short in several respects. In this work, we train model organisms to withhold solutions in a vulnerability detection task. We combine transcript distillation with counter-prompting and tamper-resistance training to instill three properties that we argue are critical for a useful ELK testbed: 1) Selectivity, the model sandbags when it thinks the overseer doesn't know the answer and complies otherwise, 2) Robustness, the model resists elicitation, and 3) Retention, the model retains a knowledge advantage over the overseer, as opposed to losing the relevant capability. We train a sequence of model organisms and show that our strongest model substantially outperforms prior models on robustness while keeping the other two properties intact. We hope that our model organisms provide a useful ELK testbed for the community, and that our methodology sparks the development of increasingly challenging model organisms.   All our code, models and the dataset are available at https://huggingface.co/collections/lennart-finke/sandbagging.

**Comment:** Matches criterion 10 partially: The paper develops tamper-resistance training and counter-prompting to create model organisms that resist elicitation of latent knowledge, which relates to robustness against tampering and secure AI systems. This is relevant to security and robustness of language models, with a novel empirical methodology.
**Relevance:** 7
**Novelty:** 7

---

## 96. [Bootstrap Stochastic Interpolant Tree for Inference-time Search Under Sparse Reward](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.24185) <a id="link96"></a>
**ArXiv ID:** 2610.24185
**Authors:** Binglin Ji, Anindya Sarkar, Yevgeniy Vorobeychik

**Abstract:** arXiv:2610.24185v1 Announce Type: new  Abstract: Tree-based samplers offer a compelling alternative for scalable inference-time search, bypassing the per-step reward and gradient dependencies that introduce systematic bias into SMC-style frameworks. Despite this advantage, contemporary tree samplers fail to achieve broad exploration. Their reliance on learned, DDPM-like transitions inherently couples neighbor states in a trajectory, thereby restricting child-parent feature divergence in the tree sampler. Consequently, these samplers suffer from restricted coverage of the target marginal distribution, severely hindering the exploration critical for efficient search under sparse reward. To overcome these limitations, we introduce a simple yet effective Bootstrap Stochastic Interpolant Tree (BSIT) sampler that decouples child node generation from explicit parent dependencies. By expanding the degrees of freedom during child node sampling, BSIT encourages diverse trajectory rollouts and unlocks broader path-space diversity-the exact catalyst needed for efficient exploration. Crucially, we show that BSIT's improved exploration accelerates convergence to the target distribution while achieving broader distributional coverage than samplers that rely on DDPM-like transitions for child-node generation, all without incurring additional function evaluations or reward feedback, thereby providing a highly efficient basis for rare target search. Extensive experiments across diverse search settings, including rare-species discovery and target-aligned molecule generation tasks, demonstrate BSIT's superior search efficiency over the baselines.

**Comment:** Matches criterion 3: Introduces Bootstrap Stochastic Interpolant Tree (BSIT), a new sampler improving exploration and convergence in inference-time search, relevant to diffusion-like generative modeling methods for language models.
**Relevance:** 6
**Novelty:** 7

---

## 97. [Where and Why Search-Augmented LLMs Fail When Reasoning over Rapidly Evolving Knowledge](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.25323) <a id="link97"></a>
**ArXiv ID:** 2610.25323
**Authors:** Tianyi Hu, Bhavyajeet Singh, Niket Tandon, Akhil Arora

**Abstract:** arXiv:2610.25323v1 Announce Type: new  Abstract: As the world changes, new information is published while older records remain online. This complicates reasoning for large language models (LLMs), which must retrieve evidence and resolve conflicts to determine which information remains valid. Existing evaluations treat information seeking as a black box, revealing little about where and why LLMs fail. To address this gap, we introduce TREK, a suite of three components: TREKTRACE is a plug-and-play framework that attributes failures to specific stages of retrieval and reasoning. We apply it to TREKBENCH-WILD, a meta-benchmark of real-world questions that require complex reasoning over rapidly evolving knowledge. Surprisingly, the main bottleneck is post-retrieval reasoning rather than retrieval: frontier models are often misled by conflicting evidence even after retrieving all the information needed to answer. To study this bottleneck, we build TREKBENCH-CTRL, a controlled benchmark with distractors spanning temporality, relevance, and trustworthiness, and show that benchmarks dominated by outdated distractors substantially underestimate task difficulty. Together, TREK provides a testbed for diagnosing and improving reasoning over rapidly evolving knowledge.

**Comment:** Matches criterion 4: Proposes TREK, a new evaluation paradigm that diagnoses failures in reasoning over evolving knowledge, fundamentally rethinking evaluation of LLM reasoning and retrieval stages.
**Relevance:** 7
**Novelty:** 6

---

## 98. [Beyond Prediction: A Component-Level Study of Concept Recovery in In-Context Learning](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.23936) <a id="link98"></a>
**ArXiv ID:** 2610.23936
**Authors:** Xufeng Liu, Fang Wan, Yi Liu

**Abstract:** arXiv:2610.23936v1 Announce Type: new  Abstract: In-context learning (ICL) enables language models to make accurate predictions from demonstrations, but prediction success alone does not reveal the extent to which the underlying concept has been recovered. Existing evaluations typically assess recovery at the level of the whole concept, making it difficult to identify which components are recovered and to separate model limitations from ambiguity in the demonstrations. We introduce a component-level framework for concept recovery that decomposes concepts into their defining components and uses controlled structural interventions to evaluate both component-level and whole-concept recovery under identifiable demonstrations. Across four instruction-tuned LLMs and three composition forms, we find that models often achieve high prediction accuracy despite poor whole-concept recovery, with systematic over-specification of relevant attributes and level constraints. Individual structural components are generally insufficient for whole-concept recovery, whereas jointly specifying complementary components, particularly relevant attributes and their level constraints, substantially improves recovery. However, this improvement is fragile: a single plausible but unnecessary level constraint can substantially disrupt recovery. Finally, joint supervision of recovery and implicit prediction substantially improves recovery while preserving prediction performance, with the learned capability transferring across composition forms. Overall, our component-level analysis provides a more diagnostic view of what models recover from demonstrations than the existing holistic view.

**Comment:** Matches criterion 1: Provides a component-level analysis of concept recovery in instruction-tuned LLMs, diagnosing how models recover concepts from demonstrations beyond prediction accuracy, relevant to instruction-following improvements.
**Relevance:** 7
**Novelty:** 6

---

## 99. [When to Stop Asking: Factor Coverage Bias and LLM Determinability Judgments with Underspecified Information in Medicine](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.25819) <a id="link99"></a>
**ArXiv ID:** 2610.25819
**Authors:** Yusuke Watanabe, Yohei Kobashi, Taisei Tosaki, Takeshi Kojima, Yusuke Iwasawa, Yasushi Okuno, Yutaka Matsuo

**Abstract:** arXiv:2610.25819v1 Announce Type: new  Abstract: Clinical decision-making often begins with underspecified information and requires not only gathering missing information but also knowing when to stop. This calls for judging determinability (whether the available information is sufficient for a decision) by considering whether missing information could change the conclusion, not simply the amount of available decision-relevant information. Reliance on this amount as a cue for determinability, which we term \textit{factor coverage bias}, could undermine the trustworthiness of large language models (LLMs) through premature conclusions or excessive information gathering, such as unnecessary invasive tests. Yet whether LLMs exhibit this bias remains insufficiently studied. To address this gap, we construct a controlled pairwise benchmark in which LLMs identify the determinable case within each determinable--undeterminable pair. We add a decision-relevant factor to either side while keeping each case's determinability unchanged, and similarly add irrelevant text to distinguish this bias from context-length effects. Adding a relevant factor made LLMs more likely to select the augmented case as determinable even when it remained undeterminable, an effect that persisted with increased reasoning effort, while adding irrelevant text made them less likely to select the augmented case, an effect that reasoning substantially reduced. These findings identify factor coverage bias as a failure mode distinct from context-length effects and a potential risk to LLM trustworthiness. Our work provides an evaluation framework for LLM trustworthiness under underspecification.

**Comment:** Analyzes factor coverage bias in LLM determinability judgments under underspecified information, providing an evaluation framework for LLM trustworthiness. This relates to criterion 4 (new paradigms to evaluating open-ended text generation) because it fundamentally rethinks evaluation by identifying a new failure mode and proposing a benchmark framework.
**Relevance:** 7
**Novelty:** 6

---

## 100. [What Social Media Teaches Us About Accumulative AI Risks](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.24006) <a id="link100"></a>
**ArXiv ID:** 2610.24006
**Authors:** Dawei Li, Bohan Jiang, Huan Liu

**Abstract:** arXiv:2610.24006v1 Announce Type: new  Abstract: Prominent AI safety discussions emphasize existential threats and catastrophic events, such as AI-enabled nuclear conflict or engineered biological outbreaks. This position paper argues that there is another type of AI risk that arises and accumulates from the widespread use of AI. Drawing on lessons from social media, we argue that safety assessments must also address accumulative harms that develop through repeated use, recurring decisions, and feedback across interconnected deployments. Repeated reassurance, selective exposure, recurring decision biases, and growing personal profiles may gradually narrow perspectives, weaken independent judgment, or enable exploitative influence without a single clearly harmful event. We advance the Trajectory Neglect Thesis: a focus on vivid catastrophic events and identifiable incidents risks overlooking less visible harms that accumulate through the widespread daily use of AI. Social media research motivates this position by showing how reciprocal media effects, social reinforcement, and individual susceptibility shape outcomes beyond individual encounters. We examine how related mechanisms may operate in AI systems, using conversational AI as a concrete setting where generation, semantic inference, and persistent memory may tighten feedback loops. Sycophancy, manipulation, and privacy loss provide a case study of how these risks may interact and accumulate. This comparison identifies mechanisms to investigate rather than assuming that repeated AI use is harmful. We therefore call for evaluation of cumulative patterns of use and deployment, individual and societal outcomes, careful causal inference, and proportionate safeguards that preserve useful assistance and informed user choice.

**Comment:** Matches criterion 5: Provides a conceptual survey and analysis of accumulative AI risks from repeated AI use, focusing on societal and individual safety implications of language models and conversational AI. Offers new perspectives on real-world usage and safety properties.
**Relevance:** 7
**Novelty:** 6

---

## 101. [Sample-Efficient Reinforcement Learning with General Utilities via Neural Dual Critics](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.24880) <a id="link101"></a>
**ArXiv ID:** 2610.24880
**Authors:** Ankur Naskar, Vaneet Aggarwal

**Abstract:** arXiv:2610.24880v1 Announce Type: new  Abstract: Reinforcement learning with general utilities optimizes nonlinear functionals of policy-induced occupancy measures. We develop a natural actor-critic method for concave RLGU objectives admitting an exact variational dual representation, with explicit constructions for a broad class of separable occupancy utilities. Rather than estimating the occupancy measure and then forming the policy-gradient pseudo-reward, our method learns this pseudo-reward directly as a function of the state-action pair using a neural critic. The critic is trained by projected stochastic updates, while multilevel Monte Carlo combines outputs across training horizons to control its bias at logarithmic expected cost. Our analysis separates conditional actor bias from estimator second moments and accounts explicitly for dual optimization, critic approximation, and neural linearization errors. Under policy-transfer, dual-stability, and local critic-regularity conditions, the method achieves $\widetilde{O}(T^{-1/2})$ global convergence above policy, critic-class, and finite-width approximation errors using $\widetilde{O}(T)$ expected environment transitions and reference samples. For a fixed critic representation, this yields $\widetilde{O}(\epsilon^{-2})$ sampling complexity above the approximation floor, covering infinite state-action spaces and occupancy utilities beyond fixed finite vectors of returns.

**Comment:** Matches criterion 1: introduces a new natural actor-critic method for reinforcement learning with general utilities, involving neural dual critics and policy-gradient pseudo-rewards, which is a methodological improvement in RL potentially relevant to RLHF or instruction-following fine-tuning.
**Relevance:** 6
**Novelty:** 7

---

## 102. [A Measurement Perspective on Benchmarking LLM-Generated Text Detectors using Item Response Theory](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.24972) <a id="link102"></a>
**ArXiv ID:** 2610.24972
**Authors:** Yanbin Zhao, Zhipeng Chen, Guangsheng Bao, Guandong Zhang, Yan Li

**Abstract:** arXiv:2610.24972v1 Announce Type: new  Abstract: Benchmarking large language model (LLM)-generated text detectors commonly relies on aggregate detector-level metrics such as accuracy, F1 score, and AUC. Although useful for summarizing overall performance, these metrics provide limited insight into how benchmark composition shapes detector comparisons or which texts distinguish detectors with different response profiles. We propose a measurement-based benchmarking framework for jointly characterizing benchmark heterogeneity and detector behavior using multidimensional item response theory (IRT). By treating benchmark texts as measurement items and detectors as respondents, the framework estimates text-level discrimination and difficulty-location parameters alongside multidimensional detector abilities. We apply the framework to a benchmark of 32K human-written and machine-generated texts across four domains, evaluated by 14 detectors. The estimated IRT item-parameter space reveals distinct but overlapping human-machine distributions, with substantial variation across domains and text lengths. The human-machine label shows the strongest multivariate association with this space, while domain and text length further contribute to benchmark heterogeneity. Detector ability profiles reveal multidimensional and domain-dependent response patterns that are not fully reflected in aggregate performance rankings. As a practical application, clustering texts in the estimated IRT space yields a compact subset of 2,088 texts (6.5\% of the full 32K benchmark) that largely preserves the comparative detector-performance patterns observed on the full benchmark. Overall, the proposed framework provides a principled and interpretable methodology for benchmarking heterogeneous detection technologies while jointly characterizing the measurement properties of the texts used for their evaluation.

**Comment:** Matches criterion 4: proposes a fundamentally new measurement-based benchmarking framework for evaluating LLM-generated text detectors using multidimensional item response theory, rethinking evaluation paradigms for open-ended text generation detection.
**Relevance:** 7
**Novelty:** 6

---

## 103. [miniTE: Diagnosing and Bridging the Execution Gap in Native NVFP4 Reinforcement Learning](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.23928) <a id="link103"></a>
**ArXiv ID:** 2610.23928
**Authors:** Zhen Li, Mingfa Feng, Yiming Zhang, Yang Yu, Changhai Zhou, Pengxiang Li, Dongkuan Wu, Congkai Xie, Hongxia Yang

**Abstract:** arXiv:2610.23928v1 Announce Type: new  Abstract: Native NVFP4 promises to accelerate reinforcement learning (RL) for large language models (LLMs), but achieving stable learning across separate training and sampling engines remains challenging. We show that matching model weights and numerical formats is insufficient to ensure consistent policy evaluation, highlighting the importance of cross-engine numerical execution. We introduce miniTE, a lightweight framework that combines execution diagnostics, cross-engine numerical alignment, and efficient native W4A4 (4-bit weights and activations) GEMM computation for LLM RL. It improves numerical execution consistency without modifying the RL objective, while reducing implementation overhead to realize the benefits of native low-bit computation. Long-horizon RL experiments on Qwen3-30B-A3B demonstrate stable training and benchmark performance close to BF16, outperforming naive Transformer Engine (TE) NVFP4 in evaluation quality. On single NVIDIA B300 GPU node, the optimized learner reaches 33,540 tokens/s, yielding 1.39x and 1.47x the throughput of BF16 and TE NVFP4, respectively, for complete learner updates. We released the framework, kernels, diagnostics, and training configurations to make native low-bit RL easier.

**Comment:** Matches criterion 1: Presents a methodological improvement for reinforcement learning fine-tuning of LLMs by addressing numerical execution consistency in native low-bit RL training (NVFP4), improving stable RLHF training throughput and quality without changing the RL objective. This is a specific fine-tuning step to improve RLHF for LLMs.
**Relevance:** 7
**Novelty:** 6

## 104. [Truncated Reasoning Under a Budget: Evaluating System-1 Models as Decision-makers](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.25768) <a id="link104"></a>
**ArXiv ID:** 2610.25768
**Authors:** Zhongyin Wang, Zhaoqian Xue, Junting Mu, Haozheng Liu, Ye Yuan, Haolun Wu, Lingyao Li

**Abstract:** arXiv:2610.25768v1 Announce Type: new  Abstract: Large language models (LLMs) often generate more reasoning tokens than needed. Limiting the reasoning budget reduces cost, but forcing an answer from an incomplete trace can hurt accuracy. System-1 decision models select among candidate options without producing a reasoning response. We evaluate Jev as a decision reader of truncated reasoning across seven LLM reasoners and five multiple-choice benchmarks (35 pairs). Jev achieves higher mean accuracy than forced answering at tested budgets from 500 to 16,000 tokens, including the estimated costs of reasoning and answering. At a 1,500-token budget, it improves accuracy by 4.1 pp overall and 8.6 pp on truncated questions, with significant gains in 17 pairs under Holm-corrected bootstrap tests. Offline replay estimates 34.5% of the full-run cost, with a 1.13 pp accuracy loss. A policy selected on training folds saves 71.3% of the estimated full-run cost on held-out folds, with a 0.58 pp accuracy loss. We also find that Jev shows strong question-only accuracy, exceeding 500-token forced answering on average (68.0% vs. 65.5%), although this advantage varies by benchmark. These findings suggest that System-1 decision models can complement LLM reasoning and reduce the need for costly reasoning completion.

**Comment:** Matches criterion 12: Advances in LLM reasoning capabilities by proposing System-1 decision models to improve truncated reasoning under budget constraints, showing empirical gains in accuracy and cost reduction.
**Relevance:** 7
**Novelty:** 6

## 105. [Near-Optimal Last-Iterate Convergence of SGD in the Smooth Interpolation Regime](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.25469) <a id="link105"></a>
**ArXiv ID:** 2610.25469
**Authors:** Matan Schliserman, Tomer Koren

**Abstract:** arXiv:2610.25469v1 Announce Type: new  Abstract: We study the last iterate of stochastic gradient descent (SGD) with a constant stepsize on smooth convex objectives in the low-noise and interpolation regimes, where the variance $\sigma_\star^2$ of the stochastic gradients at the optimum is small or zero. We establish that after $T$ steps of SGD on $\beta$-smooth convex losses with any constant stepsize $0<\eta<2/\beta$, the last iterate attains expected excess risk $\widetilde O\bigl(\tfrac{1}{\eta(2-\beta\eta)^3T}+\tfrac{\eta}{(2-\beta\eta)^4}\sigma_\star^2\bigr)$, which gives the rate $\smash{\widetilde O(1/T)}$ for every constant stepsize in the interpolation regime ($\sigma_\star=0$), matching the $\Omega(1/T)$ lower bound up to logarithmic factors and improving upon the $O(1/\sqrt T)$ rate of Attia et al. (2026) for $\eta=1/\beta$. Our analysis is based on a new technique, which compares the second moments of the stochastic gradients along the SGD trajectory across only logarithmically many time scales. For quadratic losses in the interpolation regime, an adaptation of this technique gives an $O(1/T)$ rate for every constant stepsize, extending the recent optimal rate for the randomized Kaczmarz method of Derezinski and Dong (2026) to stepsizes above the greedy stepsize with an arguably simpler proof.

**Comment:** Matches criterion 6: Studies scaling laws in neural networks. The paper provides a new theoretical analysis of last-iterate convergence rates of SGD in smooth interpolation regimes, improving known rates and using a novel technique comparing second moments across time scales. This relates to theoretical understanding of optimization scaling behavior.
**Relevance:** 7
**Novelty:** 6

## 106. [A Calibrated Instrument for Detecting Disposition Drift in Language Models](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.25735) <a id="link106"></a>
**ArXiv ID:** 2610.25735
**Authors:** Jerry Kaplan

**Abstract:** arXiv:2610.25735v1 Announce Type: new  Abstract: Before language models are released, they are tested for harmful traits such as willingness to carry out dangerous requests, deception, power seeking, and sycophancy. The safe behavior these tests certify may degrade as models are modified, by topical fine-tuning, new training data, or changes to settings such as the depth of their reasoning. Most work reports such drift as single summary rates scored by another AI model, without the error analysis needed to interpret them. We adapt a calibrated measurement methodology recently developed for assessing how inference optimizations affect the quality of model outputs. Applying it to several misalignment dimensions in two open-weight models, we first establish the instrument's precision, showing that shifts of a few percentage points can be reliably detected. We then induce drift deliberately by fine-tuning on correct and on subtly harmful medical and financial advice; harmful advice induces a far greater shift than correct advice. Most strikingly, a model that reasons before answering can conceal the shift almost entirely in its answers, even though a direct reading of its internal state shows that its disposition has moved. With reasoning switched off, the same model shifts steadily as the share of harmful advice in its training grows. These results demonstrate the importance of testing safety with reasoning turned both on and off.

**Comment:** Matches criterion 13: Presents a calibrated measurement instrument to detect disposition drift in language models, including shifts caused by fine-tuning on harmful advice, with implications for AI control and monitoring during deployment.
**Relevance:** 7
**Novelty:** 6

---

## 107. [When Empathy Misses the Goal: A Benchmark for Goal Displacement in LLM Advice](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.25522) <a id="link107"></a>
**ArXiv ID:** 2610.25522
**Authors:** Dean Ariel, Guy Laban

**Abstract:** arXiv:2610.25522v1 Announce Type: new  Abstract: Conversational large language models can respond with warmth and reassurance while failing to advance the user's stated goal. We conceptualize this failure as $\textbf{goal displacement}$, the substitution of persona-maintenance content for goal-relevant substance. To measure it, we introduce $\textbf{GDB}$ (Goal Displacement Benchmark), comprising 230 scenarios across nine domains and $4{,}134$ human-scored responses from 18 models. A $2 \times 2$ taxonomy crosses hard constraints with emotional distress, distinguishing failures to confront infeasible goals or combine empathy with practical guidance from reverse displacement, where unsolicited problem-solving replaces requested emotional support. A deletion-test rubric assesses whether substantive help remains after purely supportive sentences are removed. Independent raters achieve weighted $\kappa = 0.82$ against author scores for displacement severity. Trap displacement ranges from $0.0\%$ to $37.7\%$ and is strongly negatively correlated with Chatbot Arena Elo ($\rho = -0.706$, $n = 15$). Reverse displacement reaches $41.9\%$, including substantial rates in models with negligible trap displacement. A factorial probe supports a distinction from social sycophancy. Human-scored responses in the high-complexity, low-pressure condition exhibit $35.0\%$ sycophancy but $7.5\%$ displacement, with little association between the constructs. Matched OLMo-2 checkpoints show that mixed empathy-with-constraints displacement remains at $52.3\%$ after supervised fine-tuning and falls to $13.8\%$ after direct preference optimization. GDB provides a reproducible diagnostic of whether responses advance the operationalized user goal, supporting model comparison, post-training evaluation, and regression testing. We release the dataset, annotations, and rubric under CC-BY-4.0 and the evaluation code under MIT.

**Comment:** Matches criterion 5: Provides a new dataset and benchmark (Goal Displacement Benchmark) with human annotations to study real-world usage failures in LLM advice, focusing on goal displacement and empathy failures, supporting model comparison and evaluation.
**Relevance:** 7
**Novelty:** 6

---

## 108. [Power-Law Correlations Without Apparent Temperature-Tuned Criticality in Fixed-Tree Hierarchical Languages](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.24334) <a id="link108"></a>
**ArXiv ID:** 2610.24334
**Authors:** Marcel K\"uhn, Bernd Rosenow

**Abstract:** arXiv:2610.24334v1 Announce Type: new  Abstract: Sampling temperature can substantially reshape autoregressive generation, but it is unclear when temperature changes in long-range correlations reflect a genuine phase transition rather than a smooth change in statistics. We study this question in a controlled analogue of temperature sampling: a finite-depth probabilistic context-free grammar on a fixed hierarchical tree, where exact next-token conditionals are computed by belief propagation and then rescaled by a softmax temperature before autoregressive sampling. We measure two-point mutual information between tokens as a function of separation. Across the tested temperatures and grammar ensembles, mutual information remains well described by a power-law envelope. Temperature changes the correlation magnitude and fitted exponent, but within the accessible system sizes we find no long-distance plateau characteristic of an ordered phase and no clear crossover to short-range exponential decay. An approximate mapping to an Ising model on a tree explains how exponential decay in tree distance becomes a power law in token distance, allowing power laws to persist without tuning to a special decoding temperature. This controlled baseline clarifies which temperature effects can arise from hierarchical structure alone, before adding training, approximation error, or neural architecture.

**Comment:** Matches criterion 6: Studies power-law correlations in hierarchical language models and clarifies the absence of temperature-tuned criticality, providing theoretical insights into scaling behavior of correlations in language generation.
**Relevance:** 7
**Novelty:** 6

---

## 109. [On Reasoning Correctness Directions](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.25464) <a id="link109"></a>
**ArXiv ID:** 2610.25464
**Authors:** Munachiso Samuel Nwadike, Zayd M. K. Zuhri, Rifo Genadi, Abzal Nurgazy, Ali Mekky, Alham Fikri Aji, Zangir Iklassov, Kentaro Inui

**Abstract:** arXiv:2610.25464v1 Announce Type: new  Abstract: We present experimental evidence suggesting that information about whether a model's reasoning is on the right track can be linearly decodable without defining a causally useful direction for steering. To study this, we introduce ChainSearch, a controlled arithmetic reasoning task that extends Countdown with explicit search and backtracking. We train a 3-million-parameter model to solve this task. Its compact size allows us to study internal model correctness directions in detail using linear probes, mean activation differences, and direct optimisation, as part of a rigorous search for internal reasoning-correctness directions. We find that while the model contains statistically significant internal directions of reasoning correctness, none of the resulting directions consistently improves reasoning under causal intervention. This calls into question whether the linear representation hypothesis is sufficient for identifying causally useful directions in the context of reasoning. Our code is made available together with this work.

**Comment:** Matches criterion 14: The paper studies internal directions of reasoning correctness in a language model and questions the linear representation hypothesis for reasoning, which relates to advances in chain-of-thought monitoring and reasoning transparency.
**Relevance:** 7
**Novelty:** 6

## 110. [P2N: Reusing Deep Representations for Greater Effective Depth](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.25076) <a id="link110"></a>
**ArXiv ID:** 2610.25076
**Authors:** Yiqin Hao, Boyi Zeng, Zitong Wang, He Li, Shixiang Song, Feichen Song, Yifan Liu, Zhouhan Lin

**Abstract:** arXiv:2610.25076v1 Announce Type: new  Abstract: Mathematical problem solving and autonomous agents are increasingly important applications of language models, demanding stronger reasoning capabilities. Computational depth is a key resource for such reasoning, but adding model layers increases both training and inference costs, while test-time scaling incurs substantial additional inference computation. We introduce P2N (Previous to Next), which reuses the previous token's deep representations, already available during generation, to increase effective computational depth with negligible decoding overhead. Specifically, P2N adds the previous token's deep hidden state to the current token's shallow representation, allowing subsequent layers to build on previously completed deep computation. This recurrence extends effective computational depth across tokens, while introducing only a single vector addition per decoding step. We train the recurrence using parallel Jacobi updates restricted to a middle-layer core. Experiments with Qwen-style models show that, at 1.5B parameters, P2N improves GSM8K accuracy by over 10 percentage points under both zero-shot and few-shot prompting, with nearly unchanged decoding latency. The benefits also extend to language modeling and average performance on general downstream benchmarks. on general downstream benchmarks.

**Comment:** Matches criterion 12: advances in LLM reasoning capabilities by introducing P2N, a method to increase effective computational depth during generation, improving reasoning performance on GSM8K and other benchmarks.
**Relevance:** 7
**Novelty:** 6
