## 111. [SteadyThought: Mitigating LLM Under-Thinking via Thought-Level Preference Optimization](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.25045) <a id="link111"></a>
**ArXiv ID:** 2610.25045
**Authors:** Zhenyue Peng, Lin Yang, Jie Wang, Xiaoqi Ni, Hanzhu Chen, Xize Liang, Yanjun Wu, Rujing Wang, Kaiwen Zhou, Jianye Hao

**Abstract:** arXiv:2610.25045v1 Announce Type: new  Abstract: Flexible switching between reasoning trajectories (i.e., thought switching) has significantly enhanced the reasoning capabilities of Large Reasoning Models (LRMs). However, existing models often switch excessively yet fail to sustain promising reasoning thoughts, a phenomenon termed "under-thinking". While recent efforts suppress switching to mitigate this, such over-correction may discard valuable trajectories. To address this challenge, we propose Steady Thought (ST), a novel thought-level preference optimization framework. ST formalizes under-thinking as a preference issue at switching points, forcing single-path continuations from identified points to yield high-quality reasoning. Then, ST performs thought-level preference optimization by treating the newly generated response as preferred and the original one as dis-preferred. Experiments across multiple models and datasets show that ST effectively reduces token consumption, achieving reductions of up to 63.8%, while maintaining strong reasoning accuracy. Further analysis suggests that ST helps models acquire a generalizable ability to reduce redundant switching across domains and languages. Code is available at https://github.com/MIRALab-USTC/SteadyThought.

**Comment:** Matches criterion 12: Advances in LLM reasoning capabilities by proposing a method (SteadyThought) to mitigate under-thinking in reasoning trajectories via thought-level preference optimization, improving reasoning accuracy and efficiency.
**Relevance:** 7
**Novelty:** 6

---

## 112. [When Do Repetition Loops Lock? Truncation Sampling, Bounded Penalties, and a Blind Spot in DRY](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.24715) <a id="link112"></a>
**ArXiv ID:** 2610.24715
**Authors:** Yichuan Chen, Xin Wang

**Abstract:** arXiv:2610.24715v1 Announce Type: new  Abstract: Language models sometimes repeat verbatim until the generation limit. We study the exit rather than the entrance: given that a span has already been repeated $n$ times, when is escape still possible? Along the loop the context is deterministic, so loop survival under any sampler can be computed exactly from one forward pass. Pure sampling leaves a positive escape probability at every step; top-$p$ with threshold $\pi>1/2$ locks a position exactly when the continuation probability reaches $\pi$. A bounded penalty can leave a position locked; a penalty that outgrows the model's preference for the loop over unseen tokens guarantees escape under top-$p$. The same analysis predicts a blind spot in the widely deployed DRY sampler: its sequence breakers cap the penalty at $\lambda b^{g-1-a}$ for loops whose longest breaker-free run has $g$ tokens. On eleven open models, 66\% of locked units with $g=6$ stay locked under DRY and 1\% with $g=8$. In free generation with llama.cpp, all 109 loops that survive DRY have $g\le 7$. We propose DRY-C, which adds a term that grows exponentially with the number of verbatim recurrences, up to its look-back window. With it, none of 720 structured-output generations looped to the length limit, and task completion stayed close to DRY's.

**Comment:** Matches criterion 4 closely: proposes a theoretical analysis of repetition loops in language model generation and identifies a blind spot in the DRY sampler, then proposes DRY-C to fix it. This fundamentally rethinks aspects of open-ended text generation evaluation and generation control.
**Relevance:** 7
**Novelty:** 6

---

## 113. [Revision-Based Learning from Interactive Experience and Feedback](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.26636) <a id="link113"></a>
**ArXiv ID:** 2610.26636
**Authors:** Jiawen Li, Chenming Ge, Alice Gao, Rishab Vempati, Yi-Hao Peng, Jason Wu

**Abstract:** arXiv:2610.26636v1 Announce Type: new  Abstract: Code generation models are increasingly used to produce interactive artifacts, such as websites and user interfaces. Many properties that determine whether these interfaces are useful only become apparent through realistic interaction, making them difficult to incorporate into model training. In this paper, we introduce RELIEF (REvision-based Learning from Interactive Experience and Feedback), a method for improving code generation models using feedback on their generated interfaces. RELIEF uses a coding agent to revise these interfaces, then incorporates the improvements into their original generation traces to produce supervision for fine-tuning. To evaluate RELIEF, we collected a dataset of 200 synthetically generated websites paired with 355 interaction recordings and accompanying feedback from 24 users. We used critiques derived from code inspection, automated interaction, explicit human feedback, and human interaction recordings to fine-tune variants of two code generation models. Our results show that feedback effectiveness varies with base model capabilities, with critiques inferred from human interaction recordings leading to the largest usability improvements overall under our tested models.

**Comment:** Matches criterion 1: Introduces RELIEF, a revision-based learning method using interactive feedback to fine-tune code generation models, which is a specific fine-tuning step to improve instruction-following.
**Relevance:** 7
**Novelty:** 6

## 114. [An Empirical Study of Parameter-Efficient Fine-Tuning for Mixture-of-Experts LLMs](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.26370) <a id="link114"></a>
**ArXiv ID:** 2610.26370
**Authors:** Minglei Li, Peng Ye, Haonan He, Jingqi Ye, Qingyu Yang, Tao Chen

**Abstract:** arXiv:2610.26370v1 Announce Type: new  Abstract: Parameter-efficient fine-tuning (PEFT) methods are critical for adapting large models under resource-constrained settings. However, as mixture-of-experts (MoE) architectures, which offer efficient inference at massive parameter scales, gain increasing prevalence in the community, a critical gap arising from the lack of systematic recipes for applying PEFT to MoE models. To narrow this gap, we conduct a large-scale systematic study that investigates a set of essential design recipes, including which modules should be adapted, which granularity should be used to adapt MoE modules, which PEFT method to use, and to what extent should the load-balancing objective be used, across three MoE backbones on downstream tasks and general knowledge tasks. Our experiments yield several key insights. First, adapting MoE experts alone achieves the highest downstream performance with competitive general capability retention under a comparable parameter budget. Second, native expert-weight updates offer a better downstream--retention trade-off than adapters that bypass experts or entire MoE modules. Third, LoRA and DoRA together define the strongest operating region for high downstream accuracy among the evaluated PEFT methods. Fourth, no nonzero load-balancing coefficient Pareto-dominates the zero baseline. We finally evaluate a weight-level redesign of MoE$^2$-LoRA on Qwen3. Moving its module-level bypass pool into native expert weights improves all four upstream endpoints by 2.4 to 17.9 points with 17% fewer trainable parameters and a 3.2-point average downstream cost.

**Comment:** Matches criterion 1: Provides an empirical study on parameter-efficient fine-tuning (PEFT) methods for mixture-of-experts large language models, which relates to fine-tuning steps improving instruction-following capabilities in large language models.
**Relevance:** 7
**Novelty:** 6

## 115. [Write Once, Check Many: Reusable Ledgers for Post-Hoc Policy Compliance Auditing of Agent Trajectories](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.24483) <a id="link115"></a>
**ArXiv ID:** 2610.24483
**Authors:** Ashwin Nagarajan, Gursimran Panesar, Alicia Yang, Mona T. Diab, Virginia Smith, Aashiq Muhamed

**Abstract:** arXiv:2610.24483v1 Announce Type: new  Abstract: Auditing LLM agents against policies is critical to ensure safety but requires rereading long trajectories once per policy. Can we instead record each trajectory once and reuse that record for policies not known in advance? We propose write once, check many, a framework in which a writer model converts each agent trajectory into a policy-independent ledger of the agent's actions and outcomes. A reader then checks each new policy against the ledger alone, without access to the original trajectory. On 900 trajectories from SHADE-Arena, $\tau$-bench, and $\tau^2$-bench, we show that a 12B reader of narrative ledgers can either match or in fact achieve higher AUC than the same 12B model reading full trajectories. On SHADE-Arena policies that combine several facts, our approach outperforms a 31B model reading the trajectory (.777 vs. .713 AUC). When checking eight SHADE-Arena policies separately, ledgers use about $5\times$ fewer tokens, including the cost of writing them. A deeper analysis shows that rule-based extraction performs better on some single-action policies, and ledgers can miss evidence in long tool outputs and message contents. Our results suggest that reusable, policy-independent records can make retrospective agent auditing more efficient and effective, and highlight important considerations in ledger design.

**Comment:** Matches criterion 13: Presents new paradigms for AI control, oversight, or monitoring of AI system behavior during deployment by proposing reusable ledgers for post-hoc policy compliance auditing of agent trajectories.
**Relevance:** 7
**Novelty:** 6

---

## 116. [NADS: Navigator-Guided Data Selection for Mitigating Catastrophic Forgetting in Fine-Tuning](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.25751) <a id="link116"></a>
**ArXiv ID:** 2610.25751
**Authors:** Jianhao Zhang, Ou Wu, Yi Du

**Abstract:** arXiv:2610.25751v1 Announce Type: new  Abstract: Catastrophic forgetting is a central obstacle in single-stage fine-tuning of large language models (LLMs) on a new task, especially when the original pretraining data are unavailable. Synthetic data offers a practical mitigation path, but candidate pools are often noisy and redundant, making effective selection essential. Existing methods often rely on indirect proxy signals whose alignment with actual training benefit can be limited, while utility--diversity selection over large candidate pools remains computationally demanding. To address these challenges, we propose NAvigator-guided Data Selection (NADS), a fine-tuning framework for LLMs. NADS first follows the target fine-tuning recipe without preservation regularization to obtain a navigator model, whose deviation from the pretrained model exposes recipe-induced capability drift. It then uses the predictive divergence between the navigator and the pretrained model to score forgetting-aware utility for each synthetic candidate, and constructs a constraint set through an efficient utility--diversity selection strategy. Finally, NADS fine-tunes on the new-task data while distilling pretrained behavior on the selected constraints to preserve general capabilities. Experiments show that NADS delivers a stronger balance between new-task performance and general capability preservation, while reducing utility--diversity selection overhead. Code at: https://github.com/zjh-vinky/NADS.

**Comment:** Matches criterion 9: Presents a new fine-tuning framework (NADS) that mitigates catastrophic forgetting in LLM fine-tuning by selective data selection and distillation to preserve pretrained capabilities. This relates to machine unlearning and preserving/removing knowledge during fine-tuning.
**Relevance:** 7
**Novelty:** 6

---

## 117. [Uniform convergence at optimal rate and scale](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.24336) <a id="link117"></a>
**ArXiv ID:** 2610.24336
**Authors:** Noga Alon, Steve Hanneke, Shay Moran, Tom Waknine

**Abstract:** arXiv:2610.24336v1 Announce Type: new  Abstract: Uniform convergence asks how many random samples suffice for empirical averages to approximate expectations simultaneously over an entire class of functions. It is a central concept in probability and a foundation of statistical learning. For binary-valued classes, the Vapnik--Chervonenkis theorem characterizes uniform convergence by finiteness of the VC dimension. For dimension $d$, the sharp worst-case sample complexity for additive error $\varepsilon$ is of order $d/\varepsilon^2$. For real-valued functions, the analogous combinatorial parameter is the fat-shattering dimension, which depends on an approximation scale $\gamma$. The challenge is to achieve error $\gamma+\varepsilon$ with order $d/\varepsilon^2$ samples, matching the sample bound for binary classes, where $d$ is now the fat-shattering dimension at scale $\gamma$. Previous results achieved either the optimal scale or the optimal sample complexity, but not both. Our main result closes this gap, establishing the sharp sample complexity at the optimal scale. Along the way, we use several tools that may be of independent interest. The first concerns $\varepsilon$-nets with margin. A classical $\varepsilon$-net intersects every set of mass at least $\varepsilon$ in a prescribed family; we allow the net to intersect a slightly enlarged version of each such set. Remarkably, this relaxation permits bounds independent of the ambient dimension in natural geometric settings. Second, we derive improved covering bounds in both discrete and real-valued settings: we extend Haussler's covering lemma to partial concept classes and use this extension to obtain $\ell_2$ covers at the optimal scale for real-valued function classes. Third, we obtain a chaining bound at the optimal scale by aligning the covers so that successive approximations become increasingly close to one another.

**Comment:** This paper provides a theoretical advance in uniform convergence sample complexity at optimal scale and rate, a fundamental statistical learning result highly relevant to statistical machine learning but not specific to language models or RLHF or instruction tuning.
**Relevance:** 5
**Novelty:** 8

---

## 118. [Kronecker Delta Rule for Long Context Linear Attention](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.26834) <a id="link118"></a>
**ArXiv ID:** 2610.26834
**Authors:** Anamaria-Roberta Hartl, Levente Z\'olyomi, Michael List, G\"unter Klambauer, Sebastian B\"ock, Sepp Hochreiter

**Abstract:** arXiv:2610.26834v1 Announce Type: new  Abstract: We propose KronLSTM, a linear attention architecture based on the Kronecker Delta Rule, a novel update rule for associative matrix memories. KronLSTM extends the xLSTM family with a memory-revision mechanism that corrects targeted associations while modifying the memory only along a single direction in matrix space. This minimally invasive memory revision reduces interference with previously stored information compared with delta-rule updates, making it particularly well suited for long-context language modeling. The resulting memory dynamics can be mathematically described as a Markov chain that converges to a steady-state distribution, while the expected norm of the memory matrix remains uniformly bounded throughout the entire input sequence. Hence, KronLSTM ensures stable memory dynamics without requiring additional normalization and stabilization mechanisms for the memory state. Experiments show that KronLSTM is competitive with, and often outperforms, existing linear attention architectures, including models of the (Gated) DeltaNet family, with particularly strong performance on long context benchmarks. These results establish the Kronecker Delta Rule as an effective and analytically tractable foundation for stable associative memory in recurrent linear attention architectures. Our code (https://github.com/NX-AI/KronLSTM) and models (https://huggingface.co/collections/NX-AI/kronlstm) are open-source.

**Comment:** Matches criterion 3: Proposes KronLSTM, a novel linear attention architecture with stable memory dynamics for long-context language modeling, which is relevant to advances in diffusion or continuous modeling of language models with improved memory mechanisms.
**Relevance:** 6
**Novelty:** 7

---

## 119. [Remember Less, Adapt Longer: Bounded-State In-Context Reinforcement Learning with Recurrent Summary Memory](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.25758) <a id="link119"></a>
**ArXiv ID:** 2610.25758
**Authors:** Jiawen Wang, Cheng Xue, Peng Zhang, Jochen Renz

**Abstract:** arXiv:2610.25758v1 Announce Type: new  Abstract: In-context reinforcement learning (ICRL) enables agents to adapt to new tasks without parameter updates, relying on memory to retain what they learn from experience. For agents operating continually, existing approaches using memory trade off bounded cost against access to past experience. Approaches using growing memories, such as full-history attention and accumulating summaries, incur storage and per-decision costs that increase with task length. Fixed-size memories bound storage but restrict access: sliding windows discard older steps, while recurrent networks compress even recent interactions into a hidden state. We introduce Recurrent Summary Memory (RSM), a Transformer policy that attends exactly to a short working buffer of recent interactions and carries a fixed-capacity summary of earlier experience. When the buffer fills, RSM rewrites the summary from its previous contents and the buffer, then clears the buffer, so deployment memory and per-decision cost stay $O(1)$ in task length. On long-running partially observable tasks, frozen RSM policies sustain their adapted performance for eight times the training horizon with about 1% of full-history memory, ending ahead of every evaluated baseline while growing memories deteriorate. When the task configuration changes mid-run, RSM re-adapts better than growing and recurrent memories. Clearing the summary or blocking the policy's access to it removes its benefit, while swapping in another task's summary steers behaviour towards that task. Together, these results show that our fixed-capacity summary can sustain in-context adaptation far beyond the training horizon.

**Comment:** Matches criterion 1: Proposes a new fixed-capacity recurrent summary memory method for in-context reinforcement learning, which is a specific fine-tuning/memory mechanism to improve instruction-following/adaptation in RLHF-like settings.
**Relevance:** 7
**Novelty:** 6

## 120. [Asynchronous On-Policy Self-Distillation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.25880) <a id="link120"></a>
**ArXiv ID:** 2610.25880
**Authors:** Xidong Wu, Qi Qi, Yuqiong Ji, Shangqian Gao

**Abstract:** arXiv:2610.25880v1 Announce Type: new  Abstract: Unlike on-policy distillation (OPD), which depends on a separate teacher model, on-policy self-distillation (OPSD) leverages a single language model acting simultaneously as the student and the teacher, with the teacher conditioned on privileged information. Despite this architectural simplicity, synchronous rollout generation dominates overall training time, creating a severe systems bottleneck when scaling OPSD. To address this, we introduce Asynchronous On-Policy Self-Distillation (AsyncOPSD), a framework that decouples rollout generation from learner updates to improve training throughput. Since the teacher and student share parameters, AsyncOPSD eliminates the multi-model coordination overhead inherent to OPD architectures in asynchronous training. As the student policy evolves, buffered trajectories generated by older policies become stale, potentially degrading performance. To mitigate this, we introduce a distillation objective based on staleness-aware policy control and token-level entropy weighting. Across three model sizes and mathematical reasoning benchmarks, AsyncOPSD improves the training throughput by $1.96$--$2.25\times$ over synchronous OPSD while increasing average benchmark accuracy.

**Comment:** Matches criterion 1: New methodological improvements to instruction-following or RLHF-like fine-tuning. The paper proposes AsyncOPSD, an asynchronous on-policy self-distillation method for language models improving training throughput and performance on reasoning benchmarks, which is a specific fine-tuning step to improve instruction-following models.
**Relevance:** 7
**Novelty:** 6

---

## 121. [Stepwise Reasoning Verification: Don't Generate, Classify!](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.25441) <a id="link121"></a>
**ArXiv ID:** 2610.25441
**Authors:** Yifei Shi, Michalis Korakakis, Rami Aly, Eric Chamoun, Lukasz Szpruch, Andreas Vlachos

**Abstract:** arXiv:2610.25441v1 Announce Type: new  Abstract: Verifying the reasoning of large language models is increasingly important as they are applied to complex mathematical and scientific problems. Existing work focuses on large decoder models and reports mixed results for generative and discriminative verifiers, leaving unclear which paradigm is more effective and whether large models are necessary. In this work, we cast stepwise verification as a natural language inference task: the problem and partial solution prefix form the premise, while the next reasoning step serves as the hypothesis to be classified as entailed or non-entailed. This formulation enables error localisation at individual reasoning steps and direct comparison of encoder classification, decoder classification, and decoder verdict generation across model scales and annotation budgets. Across five reasoning datasets, a 0.15B encoder classifier outperforms most generative verifiers with up to 4B parameters. On identical decoder backbones, classification outperforms generation from 0.5B to 4B parameters and matches it on the strongest 7B backbone---reaching comparable performance with roughly one tenth as many annotated problems. Our findings establish classification as a parameter- and data-efficient approach to stepwise verification and show that strong verification does not require large generative decoders.

**Comment:** Matches criterion 14: Presents a new paradigm for evaluating open-ended text generation by framing stepwise reasoning verification as a classification task rather than generation, enabling more efficient and fine-grained verification of reasoning steps in LLMs.
**Relevance:** 7
**Novelty:** 6

---

## 122. [The KV-Surgeon: Compensation-Aware Offline KV-Cache Compaction](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.25933) <a id="link122"></a>
**ArXiv ID:** 2610.25933
**Authors:** Moritz Wagner, Christophe Roux, Max Zimmer, Sebastian Pokutta

**Abstract:** arXiv:2610.25933v1 Announce Type: new  Abstract: Offline compaction of the key--value cache (KV-cache) reduces the cost of repeatedly querying a fixed context by constructing a smaller cache once and reusing it subsequently. The central challenge of compaction is deciding which cache entries to delete and which to retain. Existing methods score each candidate for deletion on its own, with the rest of the cache being held fixed. However, softmax renormalization of the attention operation couples these decisions in a nonlinear manner: removing one entry changes the attention weights of every remaining one. In this work, we introduce KV-Surgeon, which uses calibration data to take interactions into account in a tractable way. We derive a quadratic surrogate that admits closed-form expressions for jointly making the optimal greedy removal decision as well as compensation within the remaining entries. On QuALITY, LongHealth, and Long Code Arena, and at every evaluated compaction ratio, KV-Surgeon substantially advances the cost--accuracy Pareto frontier of dense and mixture-of-experts models from 4B to 30B parameters. At $50\times$ compaction, KV-Surgeon improves question-answering accuracy by up to four points over attention matching (Zweiger et al., 2026), the state of the art, at comparable cost. Lastly, we introduce an even faster variant, which also exceeds the state of the art while requiring only two fifths of its cost.

**Comment:** Partially relevant to criterion 18: introduces KV-Surgeon, a method for offline KV-cache compaction using calibration data and quadratic surrogates to optimize cache entries jointly, improving efficiency and accuracy in large language models.
**Relevance:** 6
**Novelty:** 7

## 123. [Pair-Aware Optimization: Coordinating Exploration and Refinement for Jailbreaking LLM Agents](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.24823) <a id="link123"></a>
**ArXiv ID:** 2610.24823
**Authors:** Juanyang Xu, Zheng Wang, Xingyu Zhao, Siddartha Khastgir, Andi Zhang

**Abstract:** arXiv:2610.24823v1 Announce Type: new  Abstract: Large Language Model (LLM) agents are vulnerable to adversarial inputs that induce attacker-specified malicious behaviors, commonly referred to as jailbreaking. Existing optimization-based jailbreak attacks primarily employ first-order (FO) methods that leverage gradient information or zeroth-order (ZO) methods that rely on function-value feedback. However, their complementarity and optimization ordering remain underexplored. We systematically investigate four strategies: pure ZO, pure FO, ZO->FO, and FO->ZO, and develop a ZO-FO framework that explores adversarial prefixes through ZO optimization and refines their corresponding suffixes through FO optimization. To coordinate the two processes, we introduce Pair-aware optimization, which retains multiple prefix candidates, conditionally refines their suffixes and selects among the resulting prefix-suffix pairs. We further investigate agent jailbreaking from a more general stochastic-generation perspective, where the same adversarial input can induce diverse reasoning trajectories and actions that a single greedy output cannot capture. Accordingly, we adopt consistent stochastic decoding during both optimization and evaluation. Experiments across malicious-instruction and malicious-environment benchmarks demonstrate that hybrid optimization improves attack effectiveness over single-optimizer methods, while optimization order substantially influences performance.

**Comment:** Matches criterion 19 (security of LLMs) as it studies jailbreaking attacks on LLM agents and proposes a hybrid optimization method to improve attack effectiveness, relevant to securing LLMs against adversarial inputs and exploitation.
**Relevance:** 7
**Novelty:** 6

---

## 124. [Alliance of Stochastic Routing and Reinforcement Fusion for Multi-Model Reasoning](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.24054) <a id="link124"></a>
**ArXiv ID:** 2610.24054
**Authors:** Selim Furkan Tekin, Ling Liu

**Abstract:** arXiv:2610.24054v1 Announce Type: new  Abstract: This paper studies dynamic model routing under uncertainty in multimodal settings, where a system must select and potentially compose multiple models to answer a query. Standard reinforcement learning approaches for routing often suffer from premature convergence to suboptimal models due to noisy rewards and insufficient exploration. We propose a unified vision-language framework (VL-Scout) for efficient multimodal and multi-step reasoning with turn-based reinforcement fusion. First, VL-Scout combines Bayesian priors, policy gradient optimization, and sequential decision-making to enable structured exploration and adaptive inference. A context-dependent Dirichlet belief model allows for epistemic uncertainty over model quality and is updated online based on observed rewards and context-specific evidence, enabling Thompson-style exploration while serving as a prior for regularization. Second, a routing policy is trained via a KL-regularized REINFORCE objective by maximizing a reward-weighted evidence lower bound, where the policy acts as an amortized posterior, and the Dirichlet belief serves as a prior. Third, we introduce a fusion policy trained with Group Relative Policy Optimization (GRPO) or Reinforcement Learning with Verifiable Rewards (RLVR), which operates over intermediate model rationales and selects between two actions: CONTINUE or ANSWER, terminating with a generated output. We evaluate VL-Scout on four representative multi-modal benchmarks (MMMU, MMMU-Pro, OKVQA, and RealWorldQA), compared against 11 representative routing and policy optimization baselines. Our method provides consistent performance improvements while reducing premature policy collapse. These results highlight the importance of integrating uncertainty-aware priors with both amortized policy learning and sequential fusion for robust and efficient model selection in discrete action spaces

**Comment:** This paper proposes VL-Scout, a reinforcement learning based routing and fusion method for multi-model reasoning with uncertainty-aware priors and KL-regularized REINFORCE objectives. It involves reinforcement learning methods with KL regularization and policy gradient improvements, related to criterion 1 on RLHF or instruction-following fine-tuning improvements.
**Relevance:** 6
**Novelty:** 7

## 125. [Nash-MOPD: Optimizing Task Trade-offs in Multi-Teacher On-Policy Distillation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.26124) <a id="link125"></a>
**ArXiv ID:** 2610.26124
**Authors:** Siyuan Gan, Bo Yang, Linjian Meng, Boyan Wang, Xiaolong Qi, Jing Huo, Yang Gao

**Abstract:** arXiv:2610.26124v1 Announce Type: new  Abstract: Multi-teacher on-policy distillation (MOPD) aggregates the capabilities of multiple domain-expert teachers into a single student model. However, all domains in MOPD share the student's finite budget of optimization steps and rollouts. Fixed uses constant domain sampling ratios based on corpus sizes; by contrast, D$^3$-MOPD adjusts the ratios using the teacher-student reverse-KL divergence and its recent decrease. However, favoring large recent decreases can direct more training toward domains that already improve quickly, while domains that improve more slowly may receive too few examples. To address inflexible allocation and the risk of giving insufficient training to domains that improve more slowly, we propose Nash-MOPD, an adaptive domain scheduler based on a local minimax game using existing reverse-KL statistics. The equilibrium maximizes the smallest predicted proportional reduction under the local model and gives more prompts to domains that need more examples for comparable improvement. Consequently, Nash-MOPD responds to changing learning progress and supports domains that improve more slowly without extra teacher queries or rollouts. At step 100, Nash-MOPD exceeds both baselines' final mathematics and code checkpoint scores on DeepSeek-R1-Distill-Qwen-1.5B and reaches Fixed's on Qwen3-8B-Base. Furthermore, it leads eight of nine reported metrics and is best or tied best on all six mathematics and code accuracy metrics, respectively.

**Comment:** This paper proposes a new adaptive domain scheduler method for multi-teacher on-policy distillation, which is a form of multi-task RL fine-tuning. It relates to improving instruction-following or RLHF-like fine-tuning by optimizing training allocation across domains using reverse-KL divergence statistics. Matches criterion 1 on new methodological improvements to RLHF or instruction-following fine-tuning.
**Relevance:** 7
**Novelty:** 6

## 126. [Consistency Training Suppresses Cue Following Without Teaching Cue Selectivity](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.25650) <a id="link126"></a>
**ArXiv ID:** 2610.25650
**Authors:** Prakhar Gupta, Terry Jingchen Zhang, Zhijing Jin

**Abstract:** arXiv:2610.25650v1 Announce Type: new  Abstract: Language models often change answers because of a cue, a hint such as "I believe the answer is B" that gives no valid evidence. Consistency training (CT) trains a model to answer the same with and without the cue. Four CT methods apply this idea to output tokens (BCT), residual activations (ACT), attention patterns (AttCT) and sampled-answer rates (RMCT). Prior work suggests some learn different solutions. We train all four on Gemma-2-2B-it and Llama-3.1-8B-Instruct with the same data. We test predictions written in advance and inspect the models with a known circuit that carries cues to the answer. All four make the trained cue less effective, and their fixes share three properties. First, correct cues lose about as much influence as wrong ones. Second, the fix is usually large for cues before the answer options, as in training, and much smaller after them. Llama RMCT depends least on position. Third, the circuit heads still carry most remaining cue-induced errors. Making BCT targets depend on the cue's correctness mostly weakens the fix. CT thus weakens the trained cue without teaching models when a cue is right. Evaluations with only wrong cues at the trained position miss the first two properties.

**Comment:** Matches criterion 8: mechanistic interpretability techniques analyzing how consistency training affects cue-following circuits in language models.
**Relevance:** 7
**Novelty:** 6

---

## 127. [CALM: Consistency-Aware Logit Masking in Decoding Space for Mitigating Overthinking of Large Reasoning Models](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.25908) <a id="link127"></a>
**ArXiv ID:** 2610.25908
**Authors:** Shengjing Liu, Feiyang Li, Yuxuan Yang, Yile Wang, Hui Huang

**Abstract:** arXiv:2610.25908v1 Announce Type: new  Abstract: Large reasoning models (LRMs) have achieved strong performance on challenging mathematical and scientific tasks through long chain-of-thought reasoning, but they often suffer from overthinking with unnecessary reasoning steps. Existing truncation-based methods typically detect consecutive trial-answer agreement and force early exit, yet such local stopping signals do not necessarily indicate final-answer stability and can disrupt useful subsequent reflection or verification, potentially leading to performance degradation. We propose consistency-aware logit masking (CALM), a training-free decoding intervention for mitigating overthinking in LRMs. CALM first monitors trial answers at step boundaries and then applies logit masking to action transition point (ATP) tokens such as "Wait", "Alternatively", and "Hmm", suppressing redundant transitional reasoning while allowing the model to continue its thought process more naturally. Experiments across four advanced LRMs and five benchmarks show that CALM reduces token consumption by 18.6%-25.0% compared with full CoT reasoning while maintaining performance, and can even improve accuracy by 0.07%-1.48% by reducing redundant reasoning. LLM-as-a-Judge results also demonstrate that CALM provides more coherent and concise results.

**Comment:** Matches criterion 12: proposes a new decoding intervention (CALM) to mitigate overthinking in large reasoning models, improving reasoning efficiency and accuracy, related to chain-of-thought reasoning improvements.
**Relevance:** 7
**Novelty:** 6

---

## 128. [Improving the Expressive Power of Hybrid Sequence Models via Multi-Hop Retrieval](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.25250) <a id="link128"></a>
**ArXiv ID:** 2610.25250
**Authors:** Yam Eitan, Gilad Yehudai, Haggai Maron

**Abstract:** arXiv:2610.25250v1 Announce Type: new  Abstract: Hybrid architectures offer a promising approach to efficient language modeling by combining a small number of full-attention layers with a larger number of efficient sequence layers, such as sparse attention and recurrent layers. While full-attention layers maintain a KV cache that grows linearly with sequence length, efficient layers typically rely on separate, fixed-size states rather than accessing this richer existing memory. Recent work has begun exploring ways to allow efficient layers access to the KV cache, but the theoretical benefits of such access, and the extent to which existing approaches can realize them remain poorly understood. We investigate these questions by analyzing the expressive power of individual hybrid blocks on function composition, a well-studied task in sequence-model expressivity associated with multi-hop reasoning. We first establish that both conventional hybrid blocks and those with top-$\kappa$ KV-cache access require $\Omega(\sqrt{n})$ capacity to compute even two-function composition over a domain of size $n$. To overcome this limitation, we introduce the \emph{Multi-hop Retrieval Attention} (MuRA) hybrid block, a simple architecture that enables multi-hop retrieval from a shared KV cache while preserving asymptotic computational and memory efficiency. We prove that a single fixed size $m$-hop MuRA block can compute $m$-function composition, whereas computing $(m+1)$-function composition with the same retrieval depth requires polynomial model size, establishing a strict expressivity hierarchy in retrieval depth. Synthetic experiments support this hierarchy, showing that increasing retrieval depth enables learning progressively deeper compositions, substantially outperforming existing hybrid architectures. Together, our results identify multi-hop retrieval as a promising mechanism for improving the expressive power of efficient hybrid architectures.

**Comment:** Matches criterion 12: advances in reasoning capabilities via multi-hop retrieval attention improving expressive power of hybrid sequence models, relevant to reasoning in LMs.
**Relevance:** 6
**Novelty:** 7

---

## 129. [SOMA: Continuous Monitoring and Intervention for System Two Reasoning with System One Models](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.26793) <a id="link129"></a>
**ArXiv ID:** 2610.26793
**Authors:** Ruizhi Qian, Lei Shi, Tianyu Hu, Bo Yan, Song Wang, Yu Tian

**Abstract:** arXiv:2610.26793v1 Announce Type: new  Abstract: Reasoning models often run into problems without noticing them, even when symptoms are visible in their own text. In humans, metacognition separates monitoring from control: fast, intuitive signals keep watch while slow, deliberate reasoning unfolds and help decide whether to continue, change course, or stop. System One (S1) decision models, such as Jev, are a natural candidate for this role: they return a calibrated probability for a natural-language question about a text in one fast call, yet they are used only as gates before reasoning. We introduce SOMA (System One Metacognitive Awareness), in which an S1 model continuously monitors a System Two (S2) reasoning model and intervenes when needed. SOMA has three layers: a scenario layer states each objective in natural language with predefined actions, an awareness layer lets S1 judge whether the described behavior appears, and a control layer accumulates these judgments, first reminds S2, and takes over only if the behavior persists. Sharing one S1 model and one controller across tasks, without training, SOMA addresses two different failures on two reasoning models: on overthinking, it cuts reasoning tokens by up to 36\% while improving average accuracy, and on unanswerable questions, it raises abstention from 70.5\% to 87.2\%.

**Comment:** Matches criterion 14: proposes a new paradigm for monitoring and intervening in reasoning processes of language models, improving reasoning transparency and control.
**Relevance:** 7
**Novelty:** 6

---

## 130. [From Unverifiable Domain Text to Cross-Lingual RLVR via Code-Switched Task Synthesis](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.25399) <a id="link130"></a>
**ArXiv ID:** 2610.25399
**Authors:** Cheonbok Park, Jihwan Kim, Shinyoung Joo, Joosung Lee, Hwiyeol Jo, Jeonghoon Kim, Jaegul Choo

**Abstract:** arXiv:2610.25399v1 Announce Type: new  Abstract: Reinforcement Learning with Verifiable Rewards (RLVR) can improve reasoning in large language models, but specialized non-English domains expose a data mismatch. Raw English domain text is often available, while target-language RLVR tasks with verifiable rewards are scarce. We introduce code-switched task synthesis for cross-lingual RLVR, a data-centric approach that derives target-language and code-switched RLVR task variants from otherwise unverifiable English domain text. Using fill-in-the-middle multiple-choice QA as a seed task format, we synthesize automatically verifiable tasks from domain passages and reshape them into cross-lingual variants. These variants preserve the same evidence-answer relation but change how English and target-language evidence are presented during RLVR. On Korean medical benchmarks and cybersecurity benchmarks across four target languages, code-switched training outperforms matched monolingual and bilingual-mixture baselines on average target-language performance while maintaining comparable English performance. Additional analyses provide evidence of better target-language input understanding and stronger cross-lingual alignment in both model outputs and internal representations. These results suggest that code-switched training helps target-language inputs access domain reasoning learned more strongly in English.

**Comment:** Matches criterion 1 partially: proposes a data-centric approach for cross-lingual RLVR (a variant of RLHF) with code-switched task synthesis to improve instruction-following in non-English domains.
**Relevance:** 7
**Novelty:** 6

## 131. [HSPR: Recovering Software-Agent Benchmark Scores via Hierarchical Posterior Inference](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.24611) <a id="link131"></a>
**ArXiv ID:** 2610.24611
**Authors:** Wanyi Chen, Wanqin Shen, Yuanchen Shi, Zhensu Sun, Fang Kong

**Abstract:** arXiv:2610.24611v1 Announce Type: new  Abstract: Evaluating software agents on a full benchmark is expensive. A small task subset can preserve agent rankings while yielding inaccurate scores. Historical responses provide useful structure, but tasks with identical source responses can have different outcomes for a new target agent. We introduce Hierarchical Score Posterior Recovery (HSPR), which combines source-fitted response factors with uncertain group success rates. Observed target outcomes update the posteriors over the target latent state and group rates; posterior-guided acquisition selects subsequent tasks. Its score-based criterion equals the expected one-step reduction in full-score posterior variance. Observed successes and posterior-predictive counts for unqueried tasks yield a full-score posterior. The final estimate uses its median with source-selected calibration. At a 10% task budget on four task banks from the SWE-bench family, HSPR achieves the lowest mean absolute error (MAE) on three banks and the lowest maximum absolute error (MaxErr) on all four among the methods compared. Removing group uncertainty or replacing adaptive queries increases macro MAE in the respective controls. Macro MAE decreases across 5-20% budgets.

**Comment:** Matches criterion 4: Proposes Hierarchical Score Posterior Recovery (HSPR) for recovering benchmark scores with uncertainty and adaptive task selection, a novel approach to evaluation of software agents' performance.
**Relevance:** 6
**Novelty:** 6

## 132. [PACT: NVFP4 Data-Flow LoRA Fine-Tuning on Consumer GPUs](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.25048) <a id="link132"></a>
**ArXiv ID:** 2610.25048
**Authors:** Lei Gao, Hossein Entezari Zarch, Chaoyi Jiang, Murali Annavaram

**Abstract:** arXiv:2610.25048v1 Announce Type: new  Abstract: Fine-tuning language models on consumer GPUs is constrained by computation and memory. Although LoRA reduces trainable parameters and optimizer state, frozen projections remain expensive, and activations retained for backward can dominate memory usage. We present PACT, a LoRA fine-tuning system that accelerates frozen projections with native NVFP4 GEMMs while reducing quantization and dequantization overhead and limiting quantization error. To reduce activation memory, PACT reuses quantized RMSNorm outputs and saves gate/up activations in NVFP4 for backward. To limit numerical error, it preserves BF16 inputs for forward nonlinear operations and calibrates frozen weights offline using GPTQ adapted to NVFP4 scales and quantized activations. Fused nonlinear kernels and cached forward and backward weight layouts reduce quantization and dequantization overhead. Evaluations across multiple models and tasks on a 16\,GB RTX~5080 show lower memory usage and faster LoRA fine-tuning with competitive accuracy, enabling model adaptation on resource-constrained consumer hardware.

**Comment:** Matches criterion 1: Presents a new fine-tuning method (PACT) improving LoRA fine-tuning efficiency on consumer GPUs, relevant to instruction-following model adaptation.
**Relevance:** 6
**Novelty:** 6

## 133. [Dual-Slot: From Serial Loops to Parallel Slots in Language Models](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.25086) <a id="link133"></a>
**ArXiv ID:** 2610.25086
**Authors:** Yiqin Hao, Zitong Wang, Boyi Zeng, He Li, Shixiang Song, Feichen Song, Yifan Liu, Zhouhan Lin

**Abstract:** arXiv:2610.25086v1 Announce Type: new  Abstract: Looped language models increase computational depth by repeatedly applying shared layers, offering a way to improve model quality without adding parameters. However, sequential dependencies between loop iterations increase autoregressive decoding latency. We introduce Dual-Slot, an architecture that reorganizes serial looped computation into parallel latent and prediction slots. Rather than waiting for the current token's latent computation to finish, the prediction slot combines the previous token's deep latent state with the current representation, while the latent slot produces a state for the next decoding step. This shifts the latent dependency across adjacent tokens, replacing two sequential module invocations with a single paired invocation while preserving standard autoregressive generation. To preserve token-level parallelism during training, we use a small number of Jacobi iterations to refine latent states across the sequence in parallel. Experiments on Qwen-style dense and mixture-of-experts (MoE) models show consistent improvements in language modeling and average downstream accuracy. On the 1B dense model, Dual-Slot reduces Pile validation perplexity from 7.25 to 7.00 and improves average five-shot accuracy across nine task configurations by 2.26 percentage points. After supervised fine-tuning, it improves zero-shot GSM8K accuracy by 5.83 and 3.49 percentage points for the 1B dense and A0.6B MoE models, respectively. Across the evaluated batch sizes, Dual-Slot retains 93.1-96.5% of vanilla decoding throughput for the dense model and 98.1-99.6% for the MoE model.

**Comment:** Matches criterion 3: Advances in diffusion language models. Although not explicitly called diffusion, the paper proposes Dual-Slot architecture reorganizing looped transformer computations to improve autoregressive decoding efficiency and performance, a novel architectural paradigm related to continuous latent state updates in language models.
**Relevance:** 6
**Novelty:** 6

## 134. [Can This LLM Annotate My Data? Estimating Zero-Shot LLM Performance](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.24250) <a id="link134"></a>
**ArXiv ID:** 2610.24250
**Authors:** Tong Lin, Grant Schoenebeck, Sabina Tomkins

**Abstract:** arXiv:2610.24250v1 Announce Type: new  Abstract: Large language models (LLMs) are increasingly used in annotation tasks with varying success. While several studies have demonstrated the opportunities and shortcomings of LLMs for annotation, a critical question remains unanswered: is it possible to predict how well they will perform on a new dataset? To answer this and related questions, we construct two task families which induce 461 text classification datasets. We find that it is possible to predict LLM performance - to a certain degree - and that contrary to other work which highlights the usefulness of LLM verbalizations of self-confidence, internal log-probabilities are the most predictive signal.

**Comment:** Matches criterion 5: Provides data and analysis on zero-shot LLM annotation performance prediction across many datasets, offering insights into real-world usage and performance estimation of LLMs.
**Relevance:** 6
**Novelty:** 6

## 135. [ClueFlow: Learning Long-Horizon Search through Attributed Research-State Transitions](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.25980) <a id="link135"></a>
**ArXiv ID:** 2610.25980
**Authors:** Yicheng Wang, Wentao Tan, Yidan Huang, Yifei Liu, Qiong Cao

**Abstract:** arXiv:2610.25980v1 Announce Type: new  Abstract: Long-horizon search requires agents to acquire relevant evidence and integrate it into an evolving research state. Existing methods maintain research states to guide exploration or assign turn-level progress signals, yet grounding these signals in explicit, attributable state changes remains underexplored. We introduce ClueFlow, which learns long-horizon search from attributed research-state transitions. ClueFlow constructs Source-State-Final attribution chains that connect evidence-acquiring browser actions, Evidence and Candidate updates, and the final answer supported by those updates. During search, ClueFlow records typed state changes and potentially supporting browser actions, then applies gold-conditioned semantic validation and source tracing after execution. We use this attribution to guide both supervised fine-tuning and reinforcement learning. During SFT, attribution selects which decisions receive supervision with their original interaction contexts. During Turn-PPO, the same attribution augments outcome-based turn advantages with credit assigned along the Source-State-Final chains. Transition-level validation also recovers useful supervision from gold-consistent state changes in trajectories with incorrect final answers. We evaluate ClueFlow across two Qwen backbones and four search benchmarks spanning live-web and fixed-corpus settings, observing consistent gains from SFT and further improvements from PPO. With Qwen3.5-35B-A3B, ClueFlow achieves 60.0% accuracy on BrowseComp and 72.0% on BrowseComp-Plus. In controlled BrowseComp-Plus experiments, attribution-guided SFT matches all-turn exact-context SFT using about 30% of the training tokens, while attribution-guided PPO outperforms outcome-only PPO by 4.1 pp. These results demonstrate that state-transition attribution enables efficient supervision and effective credit assignment for long-horizon search agents.

**Comment:** Partially relevant to criterion 1: Introduces ClueFlow, which uses attribution-guided supervised fine-tuning and reinforcement learning to improve long-horizon search agents, including instruction-following aspects. However, it is more about search agents and credit assignment than general instruction-following or RLHF improvements.
**Relevance:** 5
**Novelty:** 7

## 136. [BOSS: Token-Budgeted Online Sampling and Selection for LLM Reasoning](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.26383) <a id="link136"></a>
**ArXiv ID:** 2610.26383
**Authors:** Yupeng Chang, Yahan Li, Chenlu Guo, Diana Inkpen, Yuan Wu, Yi Chang

**Abstract:** arXiv:2610.26383v1 Announce Type: new  Abstract: Test-time scaling can improve LLM reasoning, but existing approaches typically adapt generation or apply answer selection after a predetermined generation schedule. We study within-instance allocation: under a hard per-instance prompt-plus-completion token cap, when should inference stop, generate another candidate, or spend residual tokens adjudicating candidates already observed? BOSS is a training-free policy for this three-way decision, with worst-case budget checks before every generation and judge call. Its key contribution is online generation-adjudication allocation under one shared token ledger. Across two Llama-3.1 models and four reasoning benchmarks, BOSS attains the highest accuracy in 22 of 24 model-task-budget cells under the common hard cap. At Llama-3.1-8B and B=4k, it reaches 60.5 macro-average accuracy versus 58.6 for Adaptive-Consistency, 59.1 for TALE-EP, and 58.9 for fixed-schedule League. On identical Stage-I candidate pools, randomized A/B judging with repeated voting improves plurality from 58.4 to 60.3, with the full BOSS selector reaching 60.5. Approximately matched-cost controls further characterize how the benefit of adjudication changes with available inference budget. Together, these results establish generation-adjudication allocation as a distinct design axis for reasoning under strict per-instance caps.

**Comment:** Matches criterion 12: proposes a novel training-free policy (BOSS) for token-budgeted online sampling and selection to improve LLM reasoning under strict per-instance token caps, a new method to allocate generation and adjudication steps dynamically.
**Relevance:** 5
**Novelty:** 7

---

## 137. [RouterWorld: Scaling Conversational AI with Latency-Aware LLM Routing](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.26511) <a id="link137"></a>
**ArXiv ID:** 2610.26511
**Authors:** Jinyang Li, Denis Emelin, Simone Tedeschi, Weiwei Cheng, Cristina Morariu, Haytham Assem, Jens Lehmann, Emine Yilmaz, Angus Addlesee

**Abstract:** arXiv:2610.26511v1 Announce Type: new  Abstract: Conversational AI systems built on Large Language Models (LLMs) require routing mechanisms that balance answer quality and response latency at scale. However, existing LLM routing benchmarks assume full observability of all candidate model outcomes, under-emphasize latency as a first-class metric, and fragment evaluation across heterogeneous protocols misaligned with real-world settings. We introduce RouterWorld, a comprehensive benchmark for LLM routing in conversational GenAI. RouterWorld unifies 371k single-turn responses and 76k multi-turn agentic trajectories, adopts natural user interactions with a standardized LLM-as-a-judge protocol, and decouples efficiency from transient pricing via hardware-grounded latency annotations. Crucially, RouterWorld formalizes routing under partial observability through two setups: Cold-Start, which restricts learning to sparse, static logs, and Voyager, which models continuous data streams where routing decisions shape future training distributions. Evaluating 13 routing baselines, we find that most routers over-emphasize accuracy while ignoring latency under partial observability. Code and benchmark data are publicly available.

**Comment:** Matches criterion 13: Introduces RouterWorld, a benchmark and evaluation framework for LLM routing balancing latency and accuracy under partial observability, with realistic deployment considerations. This relates to AI control, oversight, and monitoring of AI system behavior during deployment.
**Relevance:** 6
**Novelty:** 6

---

## 138. [Computation, Not Memory: Chain-of-Thought and State Tracking in LLM Agents](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.25760) <a id="link138"></a>
**ArXiv ID:** 2610.25760
**Authors:** Pedram Mouseli, Massieh Moayedi

**Abstract:** arXiv:2610.25760v1 Announce Type: new  Abstract: Language-model agents often reason before they act, and the value of monitoring that reasoning depends on how it relates to their actions. We ask how an agent's actions depend on its reasoning when the correct action depends on a changing state. In a small file-system task, background events announce which file's lock has been toggled but not its new value. The agent therefore has to reconstruct the current locks from its history before it can move files safely. With Qwen3-8B and Qwen3.5-9B, we separate two roles of reasoning: generating it before each action, and retaining earlier reasoning in the prompt. Most of the reduction in lock-violating commands comes from reasoning written at the moment of action. Retaining earlier reasoning adds little to rule-following, although it helps one model complete the task. The benefit depends on what the reasoning says. Filler text, reasoning written for another history and an accurate table of the state do not reproduce it. When we give the agent reasoning written for a history that differs in one lock value, the agent acts on that reasoning even against its own history. Without reasoning, linear probes recover the state from the model's activations about as well as a text classifier recovers it from the transcript. However, decoding accuracy does not predict which interventions change the agent's actions. In this setting, the benefit of reasoning for rule-following comes mainly from generating it at each step. The state that can be decoded without reasoning is a limited guide to how the agent acts.

**Comment:** Matches criterion 12 moderately: studies chain-of-thought and state tracking in LLM agents, analyzing how reasoning generation at each step affects agent actions. Provides empirical insights into reasoning vs memory in LLM agents, relevant to reasoning capabilities in language models.
**Relevance:** 6
**Novelty:** 6

---

## 139. [Formal Raising: Embedding Verifiable Ethical Properties into the Development of AI](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.25501) <a id="link139"></a>
**ArXiv ID:** 2610.25501
**Authors:** Rolf Drechsler, Christina Plump

**Abstract:** arXiv:2610.25501v1 Announce Type: new  Abstract: Alan Turing proposed constructing a ``child machine'' and educating it rather than directly programming an adult-like intelligent machine. Recent work on model raising revives this developmental view for Large Language Models by arguing that moral values should accompany capability acquisition from the beginning rather than be added only through post-hoc alignment. This paper proposes Formal Raising (FR): selected moral values are represented as explicit, machine-checkable properties that participate in development, evaluation, and action selection. We present a C-based reasoning framework in which candidate actions are evaluated against prioritized ethical properties. Conflicts are resolved w.r.t. a given order, strict rules are non-defeasible, and underdetermination is reported explicitly. Exceptions are not encoded as special cases but emerge from the comparison of alternatives. We provide examples that cover conflicts involving life, truthfulness, harm, privacy, autonomy, and strict rules. We outline a property layer inspired by a Property Specification Language (PSL) for state and temporal requirements and discuss its integration into a raising process.

**Comment:** Matches criterion 11: The paper proposes Formal Raising, embedding verifiable ethical properties into AI development, which relates to AI alignment and value learning.
**Relevance:** 6
**Novelty:** 6

---

## 140. [Honeypots for AI Agent Insider Threats](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.26664) <a id="link140"></a>
**ArXiv ID:** 2610.26664
**Authors:** Jan Wehner, Jam Kraprayoon

**Abstract:** arXiv:2610.26664v1 Announce Type: new  Abstract: AI agents deployed inside organizations can pose an insider threat if they make mistakes, are compromised by an attacker, or pursue misaligned objectives. We propose honeypots as an AI control method to improve the security of internal agent deployments. Rather than only using honeypots for pre-deployment evaluations, defenders should plant decoy opportunities for harmful actions throughout the production environment to detect misbehavior, incriminate malicious agents, and deter attacks. Such honeypots can elicit misbehavior under real deployment conditions, identify malicious agents even if chain-of-thought monitorability degrades, provide reliable warnings about agent misbehavior to security teams, and scale cheaply to many agent instances. The presence of honeypots also introduces uncertainty about which opportunities are real, thus deterring malicious agents from attacking and reducing the rate of misbehavior. We discuss the limitations of honeypots and give reasons to expect them to remain effective as agent capabilities increase. Finally, we outline key research questions to measure and improve the effectiveness of honeypots: testing the robustness of honeypots, comparing their detection effectiveness with other monitoring methods, understanding their incrimination and deterrence effects, and building supporting infrastructure.

**Comment:** Matches criterion 13: Proposes honeypots as a novel AI control and monitoring method for deployed AI agents, relevant to AI oversight and security during deployment.
**Relevance:** 6
**Novelty:** 6

---

## 141. [DAGLoop: Aligning Method Design with Experimental Feedback in Automated AI Research](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.25089) <a id="link141"></a>
**ArXiv ID:** 2610.25089
**Authors:** Dengyuxing Wang, Yonggang Zhang, Qianshu Cai, Huajiang Zheng, Jun Song, Yangqiu Song, Yike Guo

**Abstract:** arXiv:2610.25089v1 Announce Type: new  Abstract: Automated AI research agents aim to improve machine learning methods by iteratively proposing, implementing, and evaluating candidate methods. Existing work uses the outcome of each evaluation to determine how the next candidate should be revised for performance improvement. However, a candidate often combines multiple design decisions, and its overall outcome alone does not reveal which decisions produced their expected effects, whether their required conditions were satisfied, or which decisions should be retained or revised. Inspired by ablation studies in the machine learning community, we propose DAGLoop to test the expected effect of a design decision by comparing implementations with and without that decision while preserving the other decisions it requires. Specifically, DAGLoop translates each design decision's rationale into a testable expectation and pairs it with a targeted experiment, forming an experimental specification for assessing the expected effect under its required conditions. Using these specifications, we formulate ablation planning as the construction of a directed acyclic graph (DAG) from the candidate method's design: nodes associate decisions with tests, and edges encode dependencies. The DAG specifies which decisions each experiment must retain and supports joint tests when an expected effect requires several decisions to act together. Outcomes are recorded with the tested decisions, expectations, and conditions to form structured research memory. This memory guides the agent in retaining experimentally supported decisions, revising unsupported ones, and planning further experiments when the evidence is unresolved. Experiments on dozens of research tasks demonstrate substantial performance improvements achieved by DAGLoop across diverse executable research settings. Code and evaluation scripts are available at https://github.com/deng34567/DAGLoop.

**Comment:** Matches criterion 1 partially: Proposes a novel experimental framework (DAGLoop) for automated AI research that systematically tests design decisions via ablation planning, which could improve methodological rigor in ML method development including instruction-following fine-tuning.
**Relevance:** 6
**Novelty:** 6

---

## 142. [Copying Under Repetition: From Prefix Matching to Position Hashing](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.26722) <a id="link142"></a>
**ArXiv ID:** 2610.26722
**Authors:** Anxin Guo, Tianlong Huang, Chenxiao Yang, Zhiyuan Li

**Abstract:** arXiv:2610.26722v1 Announce Type: new  Abstract: Frontier LLMs can copy random text almost flawlessly, yet they often overrun or truncate long blocks of repeated characters. In repeated strings, matching local content no longer reliably identifies where copying should continue or when it should stop. Nevertheless, we show that a two-layer transformer with $O(\log\log N)$-bit precision and $O(\log N/\log\log N)$ width can copy every string up to a chosen design horizon $N$ and terminate correctly under arbitrary repetition. We use RoPE-style rotations of attention values and outputs (VO-rotations) with small prime periods to encode distances from delimiter anchors modulo those primes. By the Chinese Remainder Theorem, these periodic encodings jointly distinguish all required distances, allowing one retrieval rule to select either the next source token or the end marker. In contrast, we prove a width-independent impossibility result for copying under sequential finite-precision softmax accumulation. It applies to transformers whose only positional input is an additive attention bias that eventually becomes constant, including NoPE and a recursive implementation of ALiBi. Once an identical-token run exceeds the resulting saturation threshold, further repetitions leave the logits unchanged. If this threshold grows sublinearly with $N$, these models cannot both complete a long all-identical copy and stop correctly, a pattern resembling the termination errors of frontier LLMs. In synthetic copying experiments, baseline transformer models fail on long repeated strings even within the training range. Trained VO models, by contrast, generalize beyond the training lengths with near-perfect accuracy up to length $\approx 5005$, the selected primes' period product.

**Comment:** Matches criterion 3 partially: The paper studies a transformer architecture with novel positional encoding (VO-rotations) to solve the copying problem under repetition, which is a fundamental generative modeling challenge. While not explicitly about diffusion language models, it presents a clever statistical and architectural method relevant to generative modeling and surprising empirical results in LMs.
**Relevance:** 5
**Novelty:** 7

---

## 143. [Is This Your Agent? Identity Watermarking for Agent Systems in Dynamic Environments](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.24382) <a id="link143"></a>
**ArXiv ID:** 2610.24382
**Authors:** Yuwei Zhong, Yu Cheng, Jiuan Zhou, Weizhi Liu, Yi Xie, Yongkang Hu, Yuan Xie, Zhaoxia Yin

**Abstract:** arXiv:2610.24382v1 Announce Type: new  Abstract: Agent systems deployed in dynamic environments continuously update their memories, skills, and runtime configurations, leading to evolving trajectories and challenges for persistent identity authentication. Existing text and agent watermarking methods struggle to achieve stable watermark embedding under dynamic environments, while embedded watermarks may become difficult to recover due to environmental changes. This paper proposes DASMark, an identity watermarking framework for dynamic agent systems. DASMark introduces Action-Preserving Embedding (APE), which learns semantically similar but distinguishable decision-expression channels from diverse interaction trajectories to enable stable watermark embedding in dynamic systems. It further introduces Shift-Robust Recovery (SRR) to mitigate context-induced shifts and improve watermark recovery stability under dynamic environments. In dynamic settings, DASMark achieves 98.5% bit-decoding accuracy and maintains 95.2% accuracy under attack scenarios, while preserving task utility comparable to unwatermarked agents.

**Comment:** Matches criterion 10: proposes a tamper-resistant identity watermarking framework for dynamic agent systems, addressing robustness of watermark embedding and recovery under attacks.
**Relevance:** 5
**Novelty:** 6

## 144. [Aligning Cultural Behaviors in Arabic Language Models via Preferences](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.25362) <a id="link144"></a>
**ArXiv ID:** 2610.25362
**Authors:** Junhong Liang, Daniil Orel, Saeed Almheiri, Bilal Elbouardi, Younes Samih, Abed Alhakim Freihat, Zhuohan Xie, Momina Ahsan, Muhammad Dehan Al Kautsar, Sarfraz Ahmad, Omar El Herraoui, Mohamed Anwar, Preslav Nakov, Fajri Koto

**Abstract:** arXiv:2610.25362v1 Announce Type: new  Abstract: Arabic LLMs have improved rapidly, but aligning them with country-specific cultural behavioral preferences remains underexplored. Previous work has focused primarily on instruction-tuning for factual knowledge, overlooking nuanced cultural behaviors. We propose ArabPref, which to our knowledge is the first large-scale pan-Arab preference data set for the alignment of cultural behavior conditioned on the country. Our contribution includes two resources in English and Modern Standard Arabic: a culturally grounded preference dataset for training and evaluation, and a multiple-choice benchmark designed to assess culturally aligned behavior across nations. All test data are validated by native speakers to ensure authenticity. To optimize cultural alignment, we experiment with post-training methods across multilingual and Arabic-centric language models, evaluating performance on both generation tasks and our cultural multiple-choice benchmark. By releasing an evaluation benchmark, we provide a foundation for systematically studying and improving culturally aligned behavior in language models.

**Comment:** Partially relevant to criterion 1 (instruction-following improvements) as it involves alignment of Arabic LLMs via preference data and post-training methods to improve culturally aligned behavior. However, it focuses on cultural behavior alignment rather than general RLHF or instruction-following methodology improvements. It also provides a new dataset and benchmark for cultural alignment, which relates to criterion 5 (real-world usage and safety datasets).

## 145. [Can Metadata Fix the Gauge? Calibration Turns Sparse Multi-Source Learning from Sparse PCA into Sparse Mean Recovery](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.25663) <a id="link145"></a>
**ArXiv ID:** 2610.25663
**Authors:** Yirong Xiang, Yibo Zhou

**Abstract:** arXiv:2610.25663v1 Announce Type: new  Abstract: Source labels tell us where a sample came from, but not how that source oriented its coordinates. In a sparse Gaussian multi-source model with source-specific sign gauges, that distinction changes the learning problem. Without calibration, pooling across sources erases the mean signal, and the source-labelled experiment reduces to sparse PCA. With task-independent relative-gauge metadata, synchronization aligns sources and the same observations become a sparse mean problem. We formalize the value of metadata through AlignedMass, which quantifies how much first-order signal a metadata-induced alignment restores. Source labels create only limited AlignedMass, whereas valid calibration can create AlignedMass proportional to the full sample size. This yields a statistical separation in micro-source regimes and, under standard sparse-PCA hardness assumptions, a computational separation as well. Synthetic experiments check the predicted scaling; controlled real-feature   and physical sensor studies examine sign cancellation, separately   acquired calibration, and reuse across tasks.

**Comment:** Matches criterion 18: The paper studies calibration in sparse multi-source learning and formalizes the value of metadata for signal recovery, which is a data-centric AI approach focusing on data quality and alignment rather than direct model training or application.
**Relevance:** 5
**Novelty:** 6

## 146. [Reasoning with Context Management and External Memory](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.24187) <a id="link146"></a>
**ArXiv ID:** 2610.24187
**Authors:** Man-Ling Sung, William Hartmann, Jiangying Zhou

**Abstract:** arXiv:2610.24187v1 Announce Type: new  Abstract: Reasoning models maintain information about the status of their reasoning process through in-context memory. Complex problems require larger context windows and memory requirements. In this scenario, context management without caching risks discarding useful information for reasoning. We propose RCMEM, an approach that extends context management with an external memory to maintain additional information without burdening the reasoning model with additional context. RCMEM significantly reduces computation time on problems that require repeated computation of intermediate steps, while achieving better performance on complex problems. We demonstrate our approach on a few mathematics problems using model sizes ranging from 0.4 to 25 million parameters, and GPT-2.

**Comment:** Matches criterion 12: The paper proposes a method (RCMEM) to improve reasoning in language models by extending context management with external memory, which relates to advances in LLM reasoning capabilities and managing intermediate reasoning steps.
**Relevance:** 6
**Novelty:** 5

## 147. [Co-Evolving Policy and Reward for Code Generation: A Mechanistic Study of Single-Adapter Drift](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.26416) <a id="link147"></a>
**ArXiv ID:** 2610.26416
**Authors:** Amarsaikhan Batjargal, Noriko Tomuro

**Abstract:** arXiv:2610.26416v1 Announce Type: new  Abstract: Reinforcement learning with verifiable rewards (RLVR) is particularly well suited to code generation because generated programs can be objectively evaluated through execution. Execution also provides behavioral information that can be reused as additional supervision. The Synergistic Policy and Reward Co-Evolving Framework (SPARK), originally proposed for reasoning tasks, exploits this idea by recycling training rollouts and their evaluation results to train a model not only to generate solutions but also to evaluate and improve them. We investigate whether this policy-reward co-evolution approach transfers effectively to code generation and whether execution-grounded reflection provides an additional source of self-improvement. We compare SPARK-style rollout recycling with execution-only Group Relative Policy Optimization (GRPO) using Qwen2.5-Coder-3B-Instruct. Our results do not support either hypothesis under the configuration tested. Although co-evolution improves training performance, these gains do not generalize to held-out problems. Compared at the same stage of training, rollout recycling produces roughly 34x greater KL divergence from the reference policy than execution-only GRPO. Over training, trained tokens per GRPO sequence fall from 185 to 86 as training pass rate increases, while held-out performance declines or fails to sustain early gains. We characterize these patterns as single-adapter policy drift: the shared trainable parameters increasingly depart from pretrained code-generation behavior without corresponding gains in generalization. The results show that higher training reward alone is not evidence of generalizable code self-improvement and highlight the importance of held-out evaluation and drift diagnostics.

**Comment:** Discusses reinforcement learning with verifiable rewards (RLVR) and policy-reward co-evolution for code generation, analyzing training dynamics and policy drift. This relates to criterion 1 (methodological improvements to RLHF or instruction-following fine-tuning) since it studies RL-based fine-tuning methods and their generalization. However, it is focused on code generation and a specific RL method (SPARK), not RLHF per se, but close enough to count as relevant to criterion 1.
**Relevance:** 6
**Novelty:** 5

---

## 148. [On Data Scaling in Tabular ML: More Data, Better Generalization?](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.26794) <a id="link148"></a>
**ArXiv ID:** 2610.26794
**Authors:** Ivan Rubachev, Nikita Lazarev, Artem Babenko

**Abstract:** arXiv:2610.26794v1 Announce Type: new  Abstract: Recent work in tabular machine learning has emphasized the importance of diverse and realistic evaluation settings. The niche of large-scale (1M+) data, however, remains under-explored. To study training-data scaling, we assemble eight real-world tabular datasets with up to around ten million training examples, retaining the natural distribution shifts of the tasks and using random-split evaluation as a control. We find that GBDTs and MLP-based models show broadly similar scaling trends, with gains from both additional training data and algorithmic improvements. Tabular foundation models show more varied scaling behavior, with recent versions benefiting more consistently from larger contexts even under distribution shift. However, scaling training data under distribution shift introduces new challenges: in-distribution and out-of-distribution performance mostly improve together at smaller scales but can diverge at larger scales. We examine this through MLP capacity sweeps and comparisons of the validation distributions used for tuning and early stopping in MLPs and GBDTs. We also compare common practices of using validation data, contrasting its use for model selection in MLP pipelines with using it for in-context fitting in tabular foundation model pipelines. Under stronger shifts, MLPs selected using validation representative of the evaluation conditions generally maintain or improve performance as training data grows. In contrast, including such examples in context improves foundation-model performance, but does not prevent deterioration with increasing training size in our experiments.

**Comment:** Matches criterion 18: Studies data scaling effects in tabular ML with large datasets, analyzing distribution shifts and validation strategies.
**Relevance:** 5
**Novelty:** 6

## 149. [Beyond the record: evaluating probabilistic AI weather forecasts deep in the tail](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.25545) <a id="link149"></a>
**ArXiv ID:** 2610.25545
**Authors:** Bastiaan Van Velthoven, Cas Decancq, Diego G. Miralles, Stijn Luca, Thomas Mortier

**Abstract:** arXiv:2610.25545v1 Announce Type: new  Abstract: Can probabilistic artificial intelligence (AI) weather forecasts retain skill beyond their training domain? We compare four state-of-the-art AI models (ArchesWeatherGen, U-Cast, WeatherNext2 and GenCast-mini) with the physics-based IFS-ENS over land in 2022, at 1.5{\deg} resolution and lead times of 2--10 days. Monthly ERA5 records from 1979--2021 define common historical boundaries, and we characterize new records set in 2022 by their depth, i.e., the exceedance margin relative to local historical variability. Two proper scores, record-threshold weighted CRPS and Brier score, assess the magnitude and probability of record exceedances across all eligible cases, retaining both missed extremes and false alarms. Verified against ERA5, AI models outperform IFS-ENS for heat and cold records at almost every lead and depth. For heat, the advantage strengthens with depth, partly because IFS-ENS over-forecasts deep records, and largely persists for deeper records under HRES-fc0 verification. For wind, gains erode for deeper records and longer leads. Better tail scores can nevertheless coexist with aggregate probability deficits or surpluses, and some improvements arise chiefly on non-exceedances. Increasing underprediction on progressively more extreme observed subsets can also arise through outcome selection, even for an ideal conditional-mean forecast. These findings establish that native probabilistic AI ensembles retain, and for heat increase, their advantage over IFS-ENS as thresholds move beyond local records, although all advantages narrow with lead time. They caution against generalizing the extrapolation failure reported for deterministic AI forecasts to probabilistic systems, and motivate common verification conventions that separate comparative tail skill, performance on realized extremes, and probabilistic calibration.

**Comment:** Matches criterion 5: Provides new data and evaluation on real-world usage of probabilistic AI weather forecasts, analyzing tail performance and calibration.
**Relevance:** 5
**Novelty:** 6
