## 150. [CoEvo-RSI: When Harnesses Evolve, Which Models Should Follow?](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.25078) <a id="link150"></a>
**ArXiv ID:** 2610.25078
**Authors:** Jiaqi Su, Guibin Zhang, Jie-Jing Shao, Cong Pang, Tiankuo Yao, Ivor Tsang, Haiyan Yin, Shuicheng Yan

**Abstract:** arXiv:2610.25078v1 Announce Type: new  Abstract: Model--harness co-evolution has emerged as a prominent paradigm for recursive self-improving (RSI) agents, jointly evolving their execution scaffolds and intrinsic capabilities. We study the persistent yet often overlooked brain--body mismatch problem in this paradigm, where an evolving harness (body) demands behaviors its model (brain) has not learned or restricts capabilities it already possesses. We introduce CoEvo-RSI, a RSI framework that advances model--harness co-evolution through agent-driven, bidirectional historical inheritance. Drawing on a shared evolutionary archive and execution feedback, a meta-agent coordinates harness development from historical designs with model checkpoint selection or merging and subsequent adaptation under the selected harness, aligning inherited capabilities with evolving execution requirements to reduce brain--body mismatch. Using only supervised fine-tuning (SFT) on its own rejection-sampled trajectories, CoEvo-RSI makes Qwen3.5-9B competitive with larger models across diverse vertical applications, including multi-agent failure attribution, role-aware text-to-SQL, and smart-home instruction following. It surpasses DeepSeek-V4-Pro on TraceElephant (+14.16 agent accuracy) and GLM-5.3 on HomeBench VM (+4.90 success rate), underscoring the need for brain--body alignment and the potential of bidirectional historical inheritance to advance agent co-evolution.

**Comment:** Partially relevant to criterion 1: Discusses supervised fine-tuning and model-harness co-evolution for instruction following and agent capabilities, but focuses more on recursive self-improving agents and brain-body mismatch rather than direct RLHF or instruction-tuning improvements.
**Relevance:** 5
**Novelty:** 6

## 151. [VibeTraining-Bench: Data-Trainer-Harness Co-Design for Iterative System Improvement](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.26342) <a id="link151"></a>
**ArXiv ID:** 2610.26342
**Authors:** Ruotian Peng, Yuanyi Ren, Shuaiqi Liu, Xin Guo, Fang Zhu, Yangyi Huang, Yue Wu, Zheng Wang, Yandong Wen, Zhihang Fu

**Abstract:** arXiv:2610.26342v1 Announce Type: new  Abstract: Recursive self-improvement (RSI) requires agents to diagnose system bottlenecks and coordinate improvements across training and inference. We refer to the agents' capability to jointly optimize training data, the trainer, and the inference harness as cross-component co-design. We introduce VibeTraining-Bench, a benchmark for evaluating this capability across tasks spanning terminal operation, software engineering, multi-turn tool use, and mathematical reasoning. Within each task, research agents start from an identical initial agent system under a fixed resource budget. Guided by validation feedback, they iteratively refine data, trainers, and inference harnesses, then select and submit a final agent system for evaluation on the test set. To prevent reward hacking, we enforce three safeguards: test data isolation, separate research and grading containers, and network restrictions for research agents. Our evaluation results show that frontier research agents substantially improve target agent systems. For example, on SWE-bench Verified, GPT-6-Astra raises the target agent system's performance from 32.9% to 53.7%, comparable to the human-developed instruct model's 55.4%. However, there is still room to improve their ability to co-design and iteratively refine data, trainer, and harness to achieve cumulative performance gains. We hope that VibeTraining-Bench will serve as a unified platform for advancing RSI, deepening our understanding of cross-component optimization for agent systems. Code and data are available at https://github.com/D2I-ai/VibeTrainingBench.

**Comment:** Partially relevant to criterion 1 in that it involves iterative system improvement including training data and trainers, but focuses on multi-component co-design and benchmarking rather than specific RLHF or instruction-following fine-tuning methods.
**Relevance:** 5
**Novelty:** 6

## 152. [Hidden Cartographer: Hidden State Guided Data Selection for Dual Budgeted Instruction Tuning](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.25215) <a id="link152"></a>
**ArXiv ID:** 2610.25215
**Authors:** Zixuan Qin, Qi Qin, Erbo Li, Ting Wei, Yifan Sun, Zhaoxin Fan

**Abstract:** arXiv:2610.25215v1 Announce Type: new  Abstract: Given the high cost of expert annotation and limited fine-tuning resources, effective data selection from large unlabeled pools under strict budgets has become a crucial step in adapting LLMs to specialized domains. Existing label-dependent methods risk distorted selection when using pseudo-labels, while traditional active learning methods do not explicitly characterize instruction-tuning dynamics. To address these limitations, we propose Hidden Cartographer (HC), a two-stage framework that uses hidden states to guide annotation and exact training dynamics to select the final training subset. The first stage is Progressive Candidate Expansion, which utilizes the proxy to iteratively select top unlabeled candidates for expert annotation, merging them to recompute training dynamics and update the proxy until the annotation budget is exhausted; the second stage is Exact Cartography Pruning, which ranks these annotated candidates by their exact dynamics scores to isolate a compact subset under the final training budget. Experiments across the medical, legal, and financial domains demonstrate that HC achieves the highest average accuracy among all budgeted baselines, requiring annotation for only 20--30% of the original pool and fine-tuning on just 10--15%.

**Comment:** Matches criterion 18: proposes a novel data selection method for instruction tuning under budget constraints, focusing on data-centric AI improvements for instruction tuning datasets.
**Relevance:** 5
**Novelty:** 6

## 153. [StebBench: Diagnosing Real-World Procedural Understanding in AI Agents](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.26679) <a id="link153"></a>
**ArXiv ID:** 2610.26679
**Authors:** Hossein A. Rahmani, Bin Wu, Chaeeun Lee, Fanghua Ye, Sahan Bulathwela, Sujay Kumar Jauhar, Marzieh Fadaee, Nick Craswell, Emine Yilmaz

**Abstract:** arXiv:2610.26679v1 Announce Type: new  Abstract: Evaluating an agent's planning ability remains challenging for open-domain tasks, where verifying procedures through execution is often impractical. Existing evaluations typically assess only the final plan and do not distinguish procedural errors such as missing, incorrect, or misordered actions. We introduce STEPBENCH, a benchmark of 6,885 verified procedures crawled from wikiHow across 81 open-domain categories. From each procedure, we derive 5 diagnostic tasks: four with deterministic answers involving withheld, incorrect, or shuffled steps, and one requiring next-step generation. We additionally evaluate full-plan generation to examine how diagnostic performance correlates with end-to-end plan validity. Using the same procedures across tasks lets us directly compare different planning capabilities. For generation, STEPEVAL identifies critical failures that prevent goal completion. Across 14 configurations of 12 models, we find substantial differences across planning capabilities, with step ordering proving the most difficult: the strongest model correctly orders only 63.0% of procedures, dropping to 33.1% for those with nine or more steps. Incorrect-step detection also predicts judged plan validity across models, suggesting a lower-cost alternative for evaluating generation quality. On STEPBENCH-Val, comprising 214 procedures published in 2026, comprehension scores and rankings remain stable, suggesting limited influence from memorisation.

**Comment:** Partially relevant to criterion 4: proposes STEPBENCH, a new benchmark for diagnosing procedural understanding and planning in AI agents, which includes diagnostic tasks and evaluation of generation quality, touching on new paradigms for evaluating open-ended generation.
**Relevance:** 5
**Novelty:** 6

## 154. [Adaptive Decoding Methods for Token-Efficient Reinforcement Learning](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.25860) <a id="link154"></a>
**ArXiv ID:** 2610.25860
**Authors:** Zhaonan Li, Zelei Cheng, Amritansh Mishra, Chia-Hsuan Lee, Mann Patel, Sambit Sahu, Ben Zhou, William M. Campbell

**Abstract:** arXiv:2610.25860v1 Announce Type: new  Abstract: Rollout decoding affects both which trajectories are used for reinforcement learning with verifiable rewards (RLVR) and how many completion tokens are spent collecting them. We study whether candidate-token support should remain fixed or widen during training in the context of rollout sampling efficiency. Our main schedule moves from top-K=10 to K=20 and then to full support as the attempted-token budget increases. In a 4B code-RLVR setting, the schedule has a higher interpolated 150M-token validation value than its matched fixed-K=10 continuation in all three runs, with a mean gain of $1.80{\pm}0.42$ points. The schedule also performs comparably to a separately trained fixed-K=20 baseline, the strongest fixed-support setting in our sweep. Follow-up experiments suggest that late widening to full support is the most promising variant, although the evidence remains noisy, and mixed-reward-group diagnostics do not clearly explain the gain. An exploratory single-run test replay yields identical point estimates for the schedule and fixed $K=10$. Overall, our results show that rollout support is an important control for sampling efficiency in RLVR and motivate further study of adaptive support schedules.

**Comment:** Matches criterion 1: proposes adaptive decoding methods for token-efficient reinforcement learning with verifiable rewards (RLVR), related to reinforcement learning fine-tuning methods improving instruction-following efficiency.
**Relevance:** 6
**Novelty:** 5

## 155. [Mixture-of-Memories: Low-Overhead Language Modeling with Modular Knowledge Injection](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.25425) <a id="link155"></a>
**ArXiv ID:** 2610.25425
**Authors:** Yifan Lu, Rubin Wei, Ziyun Zou, Junhong Liang, Haolin Yang, Xinlin Zhuang, Abhijit Das, Adinath Madhavrao Dukre, Shadab Khan, Zhouhan Lin, Imran Razzak

**Abstract:** arXiv:2610.25425v1 Announce Type: new  Abstract: Large language models store substantial factual knowledge in their parameters, yet this knowledge is difficult to control, update, and reliably elicit at inference time. Existing memory-augmented approaches typically rely on a single auxiliary mechanism, such as retrieval, parameter updates, or neural memory, and therefore apply the same form of correction to tokens with very different knowledge demands. We propose Mixture-of-Memories (MoM), a memory-augmented language modeling framework that separates routed neural memory from writable factual memory. MoM combines a frozen base LM with two neural memory experts: a latent memory that provides low-latency hidden-state corrections and a lexical memory that provides a token-space knowledge pathway. A lightweight Budget Router sets per-token mixture weights from uncertainty and disagreement features, replacing the fixed interpolation of prior memory modules. For post-hoc updates, an external explicit memory stays outside the router, because a router trained before facts are written learns to down-weight it; new facts are appended without retraining any component and used only when an answer-level gate accepts the retrieved fact. Experiments across language modeling, factual QA, update analysis, and efficiency show that MoM improves both predictive accuracy and updateability while retaining low inference overhead.

**Comment:** Related to criterion 9 (machine unlearning) in that it proposes a modular memory-augmented LM framework allowing post-hoc updates without retraining, which can be seen as a form of selective knowledge update/unlearning. However, it does not explicitly frame this as unlearning or forgetting harmful knowledge, so relevance is moderate.
**Relevance:** 5
**Novelty:** 6

## 156. [What Remains Without Test Labels? Re-evaluating a Graph-Level Anomaly Detection Benchmark](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.24045) <a id="link156"></a>
**ArXiv ID:** 2610.24045
**Authors:** Yujie Lu, Da Yan

**Abstract:** arXiv:2610.24045v1 Announce Type: new  Abstract: Graph-level anomaly detectors are trained only on normal graphs, yet benchmarks may use test labels for model selection. We examine whether UB-GOLD results reused by later papers support label-free detection. We audit UB-GOLD and 14 GLAD code bases, re-run 21 detectors on UB-GOLD's folds under selection rules with and without test labels, and evaluate sensitivity to known structural changes. Test labels select checkpoints in UB-GOLD's shared training loop and checkpoints or hyperparameters in 8 of the 14 code bases. UB-GOLD also evaluates its kernel baselines against misaligned labels. Across nine datasets, selecting checkpoints by test AUROC adds an average of 5.12 AUROC points [3.97, 6.24] for the 12 UB-GOLD detectors relative to the final checkpoint and 4.13 [3.32, 4.79] relative to held-out normal graphs. In a post hoc permutation analysis with no truly better checkpoint, picking the best of noisy test evaluations still yields a gain more than half as large as the one observed against the final checkpoint. Configurations tuned on test labels score a further 1.53 points [0.64, 2.42] above repository defaults, a gap that mixes the value of test labels with that of tuning itself. With tuned hyperparameters and label-free checkpoint selection, the two strongest detectors retain their lead, while comparisons below them are less stable. Six of eight detectors evaluated out of sample miss size-preserving composition changes that graphlet counts detect. We recommend reporting results selected without test labels as the main comparisons and release per-run outputs that allow verification without retraining.

**Comment:** This paper audits a graph-level anomaly detection benchmark and reveals issues with test label leakage and checkpoint selection bias. It studies test label contamination effects and recommends label-free evaluation. Although not about language models, it addresses test set contamination and benchmark leakage with statistical analysis, matching criterion 2 partially.
**Relevance:** 5
**Novelty:** 6

## 157. [Training-Free Prompt Selection for Efficient Reinforcement Learning With Partial Rollouts](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.24229) <a id="link157"></a>
**ArXiv ID:** 2610.24229
**Authors:** Lei Jiang, Honghui Bao, Yue Zhou, Zhengyao Gu, Langzhou He, Natalie Parde

**Abstract:** arXiv:2610.24229v1 Announce Type: new  Abstract: Reinforcement learning with verifiable rewards (RLVR) has emerged as a prominent paradigm for training reasoning models, but its computational cost makes efficient data selection increasingly important. Since training examples contribute unequally to learning, selecting a small, informative subset can offer a more efficient alternative to using the entire data pool. The training-free selection method estimates prompt value with hidden states from complete greedy rollouts. However, in our datasets, $26$--$32\%$ of these rollouts fail to terminate within the generation budget. We introduce LIFT, a training-free data selector that identifies impactful prompts using only partial reasoning rollouts. At its core, LIFT uses Partial Rollout Context Lift (PRCL), a score inspired by pointwise $\mathcal{V}$-information that accumulates token-level log-likelihood ratios between a question-conditioned context and a null context with the question removed. This enables prompt scoring from a short rollout prefix rather than a complete trajectory. Across Qwen2.5-Math-1.5B and DeepSeek-R1-Distill-1.5B, prefixes retaining at least 90\% and 95\% of the full-rollout PRCL score, respectively, require only 26\% and 4.7\% of the tokens generated by full rollouts. Compared with SHIFT, LIFT reduces selection cost by 3.7--9.1$\times$ while achieving competitive downstream RLVR performance, with the highest six-benchmark average in three of six settings.

**Comment:** Partially relevant to criterion 1: Proposes a training-free prompt selection method to improve efficiency in reinforcement learning with verifiable rewards, related to instruction-following and RLHF efficiency but not a direct fine-tuning or instruction-tuning method.
**Relevance:** 5
**Novelty:** 6

## 158. [How Does Muon Work in Private Deep Learning? Scalable Differentially Private Training via Orthogonalization](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.24736) <a id="link158"></a>
**ArXiv ID:** 2610.24736
**Authors:** Alexander Gaponov, Grigory Malinovsky, Egor Shulgin, Peter Richtarik

**Abstract:** arXiv:2610.24736v1 Announce Type: new  Abstract: Training deep neural networks requires optimizers that balance computational efficiency with formal data privacy. While orthogonalized update rules, such as the recently introduced Muon optimizer, have shown strong empirical performance and scalability, their behavior under Differential Privacy (DP) is not yet understood. In this paper, we propose DP-Muon, a differentially private variant of the Muon optimizer. By analyzing Muon's orthogonalized updates through the lens of a Linear Minimization Oracle (LMO), we show that the leading effect of per-example clipping is a positive rescaling of the gradient, to which the LMO is blind, so that only a small directional residual controlled by the centered gradient variance enters the optimization error. This yields an $\mathcal O(K^{-1/4})$ convergence rate, up to logarithmic factors, to a clipping-dependent neighborhood at a fixed noise scale and, with noise calibrated to a fixed $(\varepsilon,\delta)$ budget, the standard $(\sqrt d/(n\varepsilon))^{1/2}$ privacy--utility bound of DP-SGD, without assuming bounded gradients. Furthermore, we investigate the hyperparameter landscape of DP-Muon and document how the optimal learning rate, momentum, and batch size change under privacy. Empirically, on image classification DP-Muon matches or improves on DP-SGD and DP-Adam, whereas on language modeling the best optimizer depends on the batch size, with DP-Muon best at small batches and DP-Adam best near the privacy-optimal batch size.

**Comment:** Relevant to criterion 17: proposes DP-Muon, a differentially private optimizer variant with theoretical convergence and privacy-utility bounds, advancing private training methods for deep learning including language models.
**Relevance:** 5
**Novelty:** 6

## 159. [AdamPM: Memory-Efficient LLM Optimization via Moment Pooling](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.24598) <a id="link159"></a>
**ArXiv ID:** 2610.24598
**Authors:** Xiaowei Ji, Lishan Xu, Jianing An, Jie Luo, Wenjun Wu, Lei Huang

**Abstract:** arXiv:2610.24598v1 Announce Type: new  Abstract: Adam is widely used for training large language models (LLMs) owing to its strong optimization performance and training stability, but its element-wise estimation for the first and second moments incur substantial memory overhead. Although numerous memory-efficient optimizers have been proposed, many incur additional computational overhead or require additional tuning of hyperparameters. Consequently, Adam remains a dominant and reliable choice for LLM pre-training. We propose AdamPM (Adam with Pooled Moments), a simple and memory-efficient variant of Adam that groups adjacent moment entries along the row or column dimension and represents each group by a single shared mean. AdamPM can well inherit Adam's hyperparameter settings and is readily integrated into existing Adam optimizer implementations, with less memory overhead. We systematically investigate pooling of both first and second moment estimates and identify second moment pooling as the effective design. With a group size of $s$, AdamPM reduces second moment memory to $1/s$ of the original requirement. Extensive experiments on language model pretraining, fine-tuning and multimodal learning demonstrate that AdamPM maintains model performance and training speed while substantially reducing optimizer-state memory. Our code is available at \url{https://github.com/jixiaowei95/AdamPM}.

**Comment:** Matches criterion 1 partially: proposes a new optimizer variant (AdamPM) for memory-efficient LLM optimization, which is a training method improvement but not specifically about RLHF or instruction-following fine-tuning steps. It is relevant to training large language models but not directly to RLHF or instruction tuning.
**Relevance:** 5
**Novelty:** 6

## 160. [Selective Transfer through Sequence Distillation: From Benchmark Knowledge to Backdoor Behaviour](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.26311) <a id="link160"></a>
**ArXiv ID:** 2610.26311
**Authors:** Jonibek Mansurov, Sholpan Bolatzhanova, Filbert Aurelian Tjiaranta, Vallerie Alexandara Putra, Alham Fikri Aji

**Abstract:** arXiv:2610.26311v1 Announce Type: new  Abstract: Knowledge distillation is widely used to transfer capabilities from teacher models to student models, but recent work shows that it can also transfer unintended teacher properties. We study whether different hidden teacher properties survive the same distillation channels. Specifically, we compare two properties: benchmark-associated knowledge and refusal backdoors, and show that distillation treats them very differently. Teachers fine-tuned on benchmark data successfully pass that knowledge to students through all four distillation channels we test, including when students are trained only on teacher-generated number sequences containing no natural language or domain content whatsoever. Refusal backdoors tell the opposite story: backdoored teachers achieve near-perfect triggered refusal, yet sequence-distilled students inherit essentially none of this behaviour. Logit-based distillation transfers it substantially, reaching up to 85.5\% triggered refusal in students that saw zero backdoor examples. Distillation is therefore selective, and the selection criterion is not what data the student sees, but what structure the teacher's property has. The code is available at https://github.com/MJonibek/kd-leakage.

**Comment:** Matches criterion 2 partially: studies knowledge transfer and backdoor behavior via distillation, touching on membership inference-like concerns about what knowledge transfers. However, it does not explicitly address test set contamination detection or membership inference methods for language models in a general sense.
**Relevance:** 5
**Novelty:** 6

## 161. [When Does Better Acceptance Pay? A Controlled Study of Speculative Decoding](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.24993) <a id="link161"></a>
**ArXiv ID:** 2610.24993
**Authors:** Yongchao Long, Chunrui Zheng, Yuxuan Zhou, Guangkun Nie, Tian-Chi Ma, Chao Pang, Shuohuan Wang, Yuchen Ding, Xianbin Wen, Dianhai Yu, Junyuan Shang, Yuxi Zhou, Shenda Hong

**Abstract:** arXiv:2610.24993v1 Announce Type: new  Abstract: The throughput gains of speculative decoding depend on the trade-off between acceptance length and execution latency. We ask when additional acceptance is worth the cost of obtaining it. We establish a comparison protocol spanning training, inference, and evaluation, remeasuring released checkpoints and retraining six drafter recipes on shared target-generated data across ten benchmarks and two target models. Alignment changes the picture: gaps that appear large in published comparisons shrink substantially under shared training and inference conditions. This narrowing motivates two deeper questions: how is acceptance improved at different costs, and when do these gains improve throughput? We find that acceptance can be improved through nearly free objective and context changes or more expensive token-to-token conditioning. DCore illustrates the former route as a strong baseline with little additional measured drafting overhead. The payoff is conditional: task uncertainty and serving conditions affect acceptance returns, execution costs, and the preferred drafter and budget. These findings inform future drafter design and deployment choices.

**Comment:** Matches criterion 1: Discusses acceptance improvements in speculative decoding related to drafting and inference in language models, which relates to methodological improvements in instruction-following or decoding strategies for language models.
**Relevance:** 5
**Novelty:** 5

## 162. [Budgeted Bayesian Bandits for Future Utilization](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.24156) <a id="link162"></a>
**ArXiv ID:** 2610.24156
**Authors:** Sudipto Guha

**Abstract:** arXiv:2610.24156v1 Announce Type: new  Abstract: The multi-armed bandit problem models the trade-off between exploration and exploitation. In this paper, we study the variant where exploration has a hard budget and precedes exploitation. Prior guarantees include a 1/3 approximation by Guha and Munagala using Lagrangian duality, and a 4/27 approximation by Ma against a primal linear programming relaxation for a more general model. We show that the analyses of both algorithms are tight, even when their relaxations are asymptotically exact. We provide a different efficient algorithm that achieves a 0.43 approximation. We then provide an efficient algorithm whose guarantee approaches 1-e^{-1} approx 0.63 for bounded exchangeable reward observations in a non-vanishing regime, which is asymptotically tight against natural linear programming relaxations. The technique also gives certificates for stopping sequential model evaluation.

**Comment:** No direct match to any specific criterion. The paper studies budgeted Bayesian bandits with improved approximation guarantees, relevant to statistical machine learning but not specifically about language models, RLHF, or instruction-following.

## 163. [Deep Fusion Memory: A Chunk-Level Parametric Memory for Large Language Models](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.25100) <a id="link163"></a>
**ArXiv ID:** 2610.25100
**Authors:** Rubin Wei, Siyuan Sun, Hao Sun, Yuliang Liu, Xiang Shi, Jiarui Wang, Junming Zhang, Zhouhan Lin

**Abstract:** arXiv:2610.25100v1 Announce Type: new  Abstract: Large language models need effective ways to acquire and use external knowledge beyond what they retain from pretraining. Continued pretraining risks catastrophic forgetting, while retrieval at inference time can add substantial overhead. External parametric memory avoids online retrieval, but constructing its training data requires extensive offline search, and output-level interpolation provides only shallow fusion. We introduce Deep Fusion Memory (DFM), a parametric memory architecture that reduces this construction workload and incorporates learned memory into intermediate hidden states. A causal memory predictor learns to generate memory from the preceding context, using retrieved chunk embeddings from the corpus as supervision. The predicted memory is shared across tokens within each chunk and fused into their hidden states through gated cross-attention. Memory prediction and fusion are trained end to end with the backbone frozen, and inference requires no explicit retrieval. Compared with existing parametric memory approaches, which often construct token-level memory targets, DFM requires $32\times$ fewer retrieval queries and $2048\times$ fewer retrieved items to construct its training data. Across six QA tasks, DFM performs better over the strongest baseline by 12.71% on Mistral-7B-v0.3 and 11.19% on Qwen3-8B-Base. Beyond QA, domain adaptation yields average gains of 6.51 and 4.38 points over Qwen3-4B-Base and Qwen3-8B-Base, respectively.

**Comment:** This paper proposes Deep Fusion Memory (DFM), a chunk-level parametric memory architecture for large language models that reduces retrieval overhead by predicting memory from context and fusing it into hidden states. While it relates to improving LLM capabilities, it does not specifically address RLHF, instruction-following fine-tuning, test contamination, diffusion LMs, or evaluation paradigms.
**Relevance:** 4
**Novelty:** 6

## 164. [Which Long Documents Matter? A Controlled Study of Data Selection for Context Extension](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.24469) <a id="link164"></a>
**ArXiv ID:** 2610.24469
**Authors:** Junlong Jia, Jiang Zhou, Xing Wu, Chaochen Gao Tinghao Yu, Feng Zhang, Lilin Wang

**Abstract:** arXiv:2610.24469v1 Announce Type: new  Abstract: Natural long documents are widely used for context extension, but which selection pipelines are effective remains unclear. We conduct a controlled comparison on Qwen2.5-3B at 128K under matched 6B-token continued-training budgets. Random sampling of natural long documents (random-long) outperforms repacking at longer evaluation lengths. Within the same candidate pool, selecting documents with high LongPPL key-token density yields further gains, unlike the evaluated generic-quality and LLM-judge selectors. We introduce truncLongPPL to reduce scoring cost by computing key-token density over a truncated prefix. At 128K, scoring the first half of the reference span yields downstream performance close to full-span scoring with a measured $2.28\times$ end-to-end scoring speedup. Across three training seeds, truncLongPPL achieves the highest mean scores among evaluated pipelines, exceeding random-long by 4.54 points on validation HELMET and 3.28 on held-out RULER, with gains most consistent in retrieval. At a 256K target context, it gains 2.82 average RULER points over random-long on Qwen2.5-3B. In short/long mixed training across Qwen2.5-3B, Qwen3-8B, and OLMo~3-7B, it gains 1.47--7.31 average RULER points over random-long mixing, with seven-task short-context averages within 0.23 points of the corresponding baselines. After supervised fine-tuning (SFT), Qwen3-8B and OLMo~3-7B models trained with selected long documents outperform their random-long counterparts by 1.13--1.67 points on LongBench v2 and 0.62--1.44 points on MRCR.

**Comment:** Related to criterion 18 (data-centric AI) as it studies data selection methods for long document context extension, proposing a scoring method truncLongPPL for efficient data selection. However, it is more about data selection for training rather than dataset curation or quality assessment broadly, so moderate relevance.
**Relevance:** 5
**Novelty:** 5

## 165. [CoMem: Learning to Anticipate Failures from Retrieved Experience](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.25790) <a id="link165"></a>
**ArXiv ID:** 2610.25790
**Authors:** Yun Shen, Ming Shen, Tianjun Yao

**Abstract:** arXiv:2610.25790v1 Announce Type: new  Abstract: Reflection lets LLM agents improve from their own attempts, but the memory it builds is local to a single task, so an agent reflecting on its own tends to revisit the same explanation for failure. Cross-task reflective memory addresses this in two forms: retrieved memory keeps past experience explicit and looks up related cases, which is specific but bounded by the bank, and parametric memory internalizes experience into parameters, which generalizes but is grounded in nothing beyond the task description. When both are used, existing agents combine them additively, feeding their outputs to the solver side by side, so retrieved experience never changes what the learned memory produces. We introduce CoMem (Compounding Memory), which makes retrieved experience an input to the learned memory itself, at training and at inference. The agent builds a bank of its own successful and failed reflections, learns from retrieved cases to write failure hypotheses, candidate explanations of how an attempt is likely to go wrong, and at test time reasons from newly retrieved cases to guide each attempt. Varying the retrieved cases gives the memory different perspectives, yielding reflective diversity that is not merely sampled but also grounded in retrieved experience. Across two backbones and five benchmarks in math, code, and multi-hop QA, CoMem achieves strong overall performance. Additional evaluations show that it generalizes to out-of-distribution tasks and enables smaller models to provide effective guidance to stronger solvers.

**Comment:** This paper introduces CoMem, a method combining retrieved and parametric memory to improve LLM agents' reflection and failure anticipation. It relates to instruction-following improvements but does not explicitly discuss RLHF or instruction tuning datasets, so it partially touches criterion 1.
**Relevance:** 4
**Novelty:** 6

## 166. [Toward Online Robust Zero-Sum Markov Games with Function Approximation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.26286) <a id="link166"></a>
**ArXiv ID:** 2610.26286
**Authors:** Chenhao Zhou, Jiyu Wei

**Abstract:** arXiv:2610.26286v1 Announce Type: new  Abstract: Online robust zero-sum Markov games pose a coupled operator-learning and game-solving problem: from nominal interaction data, the learner must recover a worst-case minimax Bellman operator while maintaining strategic coverage and controlling stage-game approximation error. Existing robust reinforcement-learning theory does not yet provide such an operator-learning perspective beyond specialized multi-agent settings. We develop an optimistic dual robust Bellman framework that combines dual robust Bellman fitting, optimistic minimax planning, backward-validated confidence sets, and exploiter-mix data collection. Our analysis is organized around the robust Bellman residuals induced by the candidate value class and yields a regret reduction that separates exploration complexity, robust operator-estimation error, stage-game solution error, and misspecification. Under explicit interpretable assumptions, we obtain conditional theorem interfaces for general and projected function approximation, together with closed results in several structured regimes, including tabular, epochwise cross-fit linear, self-normalized pure-online linear / finite-rank kernel, and projected RKHS truncation. Small-scale numerical experiments illustrate robustness calibration, strategic coverage, and the return-gap trade-off predicted by the framework.

**Comment:** No direct match to any criteria. The paper is about online robust zero-sum Markov games with function approximation, a reinforcement learning topic but not about RLHF, instruction-following fine-tuning, or language models specifically. It focuses on robust RL theory and operator learning, not language model instruction tuning or contamination.
**Relevance:** 3
**Novelty:** 6

## 167. [Toward Collective Model Intelligence: Characterizing and Coordinating Models](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.24739) <a id="link167"></a>
**ArXiv ID:** 2610.24739
**Authors:** SinapisAI Team, Jian-Dong Liu, Qun Dai, Zhendong Du, Guannan Lai, Hao-Yu Liu, Yi-Fan Ma, Zi-Yu Mao, Tengzhong Sun, Xin-Yi Zhang, Zi-Chen Zhao, Gelin Bian, Zongyuan Cai, Ju Cang, Zhangqin Chen, Shilei Gu, Guoding Ji, Taojian Jiang, Hao-Xuan Ma, Fan Wu, Lin-Xing Wu, Tao Yu, Xiao Zhang, Tianyi Zhou, Rong Gu, Ming Li, Zhi-Hao Tan, Han-Jia Ye, Yang Yu, De-Chuan Zhan

**Abstract:** arXiv:2610.24739v1 Announce Type: new  Abstract: Independently developed AI models accumulate diverse and complementary capabilities, but a growing model collection does not automatically realize Collective AI. Addressing this gap requires understanding model capabilities with limited evaluation, organizing complementary models to collaborate on diverse tasks, and executing model calls efficiently under resource constraints. We present a systematic approach to pursuing Collective Model Intelligence through three connected components. First, Model Eval constructs evidence-based capability profiles by combining query-level performance estimation with cross-domain prediction, reducing the need for exhaustive evaluation. Second, Mio (Models in One) organizes complementary model candidates and supports task-conditioned, cost-aware selection and collaboration as task requirements evolve. Third, Mos (Model Serving OS) separates model selection from execution and provides endpoint scheduling and shared model deployment and distribution. Experiments show lower capability-estimation errors under limited evaluation. Across nine public benchmarks, Mio 1.0 Lite achieves the highest average score and lowest average cost among compared methods, with per-request costs 73.7-97.5% lower than those of the best-scoring alternatives. A prewarmed shared cache accelerates model-file retrieval by up to 23.3 times. This work lays a foundation for Collective Model Intelligence by characterizing and coordinating the diverse capabilities of an expanding model universe.

**Comment:** No direct match to any specific criterion. The paper proposes a framework for collective intelligence by coordinating multiple AI models, focusing on evaluation and deployment efficiency rather than RLHF, test contamination, or interpretability.
**Relevance:** 3
**Novelty:** 6

## 168. [Memories That Outlive Their Reasons: Dependency-Tethered Memory for Tool-Using LLM Agents](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.25123) <a id="link168"></a>
**ArXiv ID:** 2610.25123
**Authors:** Yiqi Wang, Jinqian Ju, Jiaqi Zhang, Zhangkai Wu, Yiqun Duan, Zequn Sun, Taotao Cai, Mingkai Zheng

**Abstract:** arXiv:2610.25123v1 Announce Type: new  Abstract: Agents now act on the Web, publishing packages, models and datasets through registry APIs, and remembering past verifications spares them the work. But a verdict rests on premises. When a signing key is revoked, everything built on it stops passing, yet the advisory names only the key, and the old verdicts of its dependents may never share a retrieval window with it. This non-locality of invalidation turns memory into a liability: in long simulated chats, an agent reading a memory of atomic facts publishes 62% of the objects whose failure lies upstream, each backed by a record that was true when written. Yet derivation is local even when invalidation is not. Tether records, at write time, the exact versions each verdict derives from, and at read time withdraws any verdict whose support has failed anywhere in the store; the agent reads the same fields as before. On Fallout, a generator of versioned worlds with hypothesis-blind simulated histories shared by every memory, Tether publishes 0.3% of upstream-failing objects across three domains when relations are stated with their verdicts, with 37% fewer verification calls than checking everything. A-Mem and Graphiti re-verify most candidates (0.83-0.86 checks per candidate), which limits their savings, yet publish 9% of upstream-failing objects; Mem0 relies on memory more and publishes 42%. Flushing every verdict on any failure is as safe as Tether but costs 32% more checks. The gain over fact memory holds with three open-weight backbones and against a whole-store window. These gains depend on capturing dependencies: when relations arrive in sessions apart from their verdicts, Tether's downstream unsafe publication rate rises to 9.4-22.1%, and a weak writer that misses edges loses safety in the same way.

**Comment:** No direct match to any specific criterion. The paper addresses memory invalidation and dependency tracking in LLM agents, which is a systems-level improvement but not related to RLHF, test contamination, diffusion LMs, or interpretability.
**Relevance:** 3
**Novelty:** 6

## 169. [Measuring Empowerment from Offline Data for Intrinsically Motivated Exploration](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.24461) <a id="link169"></a>
**ArXiv ID:** 2610.24461
**Authors:** Ishir Garg, Vivek Myers, Qiyang Li, Catherine Ji, Benjamin Eysenbach, Sergey Levine

**Abstract:** arXiv:2610.24461v1 Announce Type: new  Abstract: Large, unlabeled datasets of prior experience have become increasingly available for agentic tasks in domains such as robotics and coding. A common recipe for these tasks is to train a policy using the prior data, and then further improve it with online rollouts. But while much work has studied how to use both the offline and online data for policy extraction, far less work has been done to understand how the prior data can be used to efficiently \textit{explore} during the online learning phase. Previous work has attempted to formalize this idea by defining the \textit{empowerment}, a measure of how many future states an agent can realize; states with higher empowerment require more exploration as there are more futures to explore. However, existing methods for estimating empowerment require online policy rollouts in the environment. We propose the first method for estimating empowerment from unlabeled prior data, without any environment interaction. By jointly learning an empowerment-maximizing policy together with its discounted state-occupancy measure, our estimator can be trained on unlabeled trajectories generated from any unknown policy. We demonstrate that our empowerment estimator qualitatively gives intuitive empowerment measures and show that the pretrained estimator can be used to improve exploration in the online goal-conditioned reinforcement learning setting. Code is available at \url{https://github.com/ishirgarg/offline-empowerment}.

**Comment:** No direct match to any specific criterion. The paper proposes a method to estimate empowerment from offline data for exploration in RL, which is related to reinforcement learning but not specifically about RLHF or instruction-following fine-tuning.
**Relevance:** 3
**Novelty:** 6

## 170. [A "feature ODE" describing the learning behavior of shallow MLPs on simple functions](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.25652) <a id="link170"></a>
**ArXiv ID:** 2610.25652
**Authors:** Joseph Turnbull, Berkan Ottlik, Jamie Simon

**Abstract:** arXiv:2610.25652v1 Announce Type: new  Abstract: We study the gradient flow training dynamics of two-layer multi-layer perceptrons (MLPs) on anisotropic Gaussian data. Even in this simple setting, solving the dynamics in full is notoriously difficult, so we try something different. We propose a "feature ODE" that might be intuitively expected to describe the learning dynamics of a shallow MLP. We find that MLPs trained to learn simple target functions (low-order Hermite polynomials and staircase functions) actually roughly follow the loss trajectories from this feature ODE. This agreement is a surprise because of the size of the simplification and the fact that it does not come from a rigorous chain of approximations. We discuss this surprise and speculate on its reasons and uses.

**Comment:** This paper proposes a 'feature ODE' to describe learning dynamics of shallow MLPs on simple functions, showing surprising empirical agreement. It is a theoretical study of training dynamics but does not address RLHF, instruction-following, contamination, diffusion LMs, or evaluation paradigms.
**Relevance:** 3
**Novelty:** 6

## 171. [Weighted State Souping for Efficient Long-Context Retrieval](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.25621) <a id="link171"></a>
**ArXiv ID:** 2610.25621
**Authors:** William L. Tong, Aryo Lotfi, Emmanuel Abbe, Josh Susskind, Cengiz Pehlevan, Eran Malach

**Abstract:** arXiv:2610.25621v1 Announce Type: new  Abstract: State space models (SSMs) combine fast inference and fixed state size with competitive language-modeling performance. Recently, state souping has been proposed as a procedure whereby an SSM independently encodes chunks of tokens like documents, tool definitions, or memory records. The resulting state encodings may be cached and recombined to cheaply construct a context for downstream queries. However, both state souping and vanilla SSMs deteriorate on long, crowded contexts, limiting their utility for long-horizon domains. To address this issue, we propose weighted state souping, a simple method that learns query-dependent weights for composing cached states. The resulting models improve long-context performance substantially across both single- and multi-hop document question answering, needle-in-a-haystack retrieval, variable tracking, and tool use. Our method can be further adapted into an efficient sparse variant, yielding near-constant encoding cost with respect to context length while preserving (and sometimes improving) performance. We also present simple theoretical arguments that illustrate why souping works and when it may fail, and discuss informally the advantages of weighted souping. Weighted state souping thus combines reusable context representations with selective retrieval, offering an efficient approach to language modeling over large collections of documents, tools, and other forms of structured context that increasingly dominate modern agentic workloads.

**Comment:** This paper proposes weighted state souping, a method to improve long-context retrieval in state space models for language modeling. It includes theoretical arguments about why souping works. However, it does not address RLHF, instruction-following fine-tuning, contamination detection, diffusion LMs, or evaluation paradigms.

## 172. [Adversarial Post-Training of Foundation Models for High-Frequency Trading](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.25604) <a id="link172"></a>
**ArXiv ID:** 2610.25604
**Authors:** Alexandre Bismuth, Kang Li, Aramis Marti-Shahandeh, Valentin Mohl, Jakob Foerster, Anisoara Calinescu

**Abstract:** arXiv:2610.25604v1 Announce Type: new  Abstract: While autoregressive foundation models trained on limit order book (LOB) data show state-of-the-art performance for market simulation, pre-training with cross-entropy neither penalizes the compounding of sampling errors over free-running rollouts nor targets desired market properties. We introduce a post-training method for LOB foundation models using a Wasserstein generative adversarial network (GAN) objective. A critic, trained online on rollouts through the true, non-differentiable matching engine, guides updates from evolution strategies over rank-4 LoRA perturbations of the pre-trained generator, under a KL trust region. For efficiency, we use EGGROLL (Evolution Guided GeneRal Optimisation via Low-rank Learning), a memory-efficient variant of evolution strategies. On a held-out panel, adversarial post-training improves LOB-Bench realism by 17.6% (WS-21) and 17.0% (Mean-$L_1$), while selection-matched random-perturbation and compute-matched continued-pre-training controls yield no improvement. Post-training is thus a necessary stage that more pre-training does not replace. Per-position divergence measurements show that it slows error accumulation over 500-message rollouts from $3.1\times$ to $2.3\times$. Adversarial post-training is also inexpensive, requiring under 50M training tokens and 12 GPU-hours per seed, three orders of magnitude below the 48B-token pre-training budget. Ablations over critic inputs, optimizers, and the population-rollout trade-off, together with an analysis of reward hacking, yield practical design guidelines for adversarial post-training.

**Comment:** Related to generative modeling with foundation models and adversarial post-training, but does not address RLHF, instruction-following fine-tuning, test contamination, diffusion LMs, or evaluation paradigms. Focus is on adversarial post-training for market simulation models, not language models specifically.

## 173. [Representations Have Trajectories: Characterizing Temporal Representation Dynamics in Neural Networks](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.24571) <a id="link173"></a>
**ArXiv ID:** 2610.24571
**Authors:** Shivaprasad B. Gowda, Sparsh Mittal

**Abstract:** arXiv:2610.24571v1 Announce Type: new  Abstract: Recent work has shown that deep neural networks exhibit an implicit low-rank simplicity bias. In this paper, we investigate the questions that naturally arise from this observation. What are the temporal consequences of low-rank simplicity bias during optimization? How does representation geometry evolve throughout optimization rather than only at convergence? Do different neural architectures exhibit distinct geometric optimization behaviors? To answer these questions, we conduct a large-scale empirical study tracking representational geometry throughout training. We find that neural networks follow three recurring geometric trajectories: persistent high-rank, collapse-and-recovery, and persistent low-rank. We further identify Destructive Representation Collapse (DRC) as a pathological regime in which representations collapse to rank-one and fail to recover, leading to a sustained loss of representational capacity. While collapse-and-recovery is consistently associated with successful optimization in controlled settings, experiments on real-world models show that the relationship between geometric trajectories and optimization outcomes is configuration dependent. Overall, our findings suggest that representation geometry evolves dynamically during training and provide a new perspective on how deep neural networks learn.

**Comment:** No criteria matched. This paper studies temporal dynamics of representation geometry in neural networks, which is interesting for understanding training dynamics but does not directly address mechanistic interpretability of language models or sparse autoencoders or scaling laws.
**Relevance:** 3
**Novelty:** 6

## 174. [Constructive Universal Approximation by Infinite-Dimensional Neural Networks via Gaussian-Weighted Ridgelet Transform](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.24293) <a id="link174"></a>
**ArXiv ID:** 2610.24293
**Authors:** Sho Sonoda, Luca Galimberti, Yuanchao Xu, Isao Ishikawa, Masahiro Ikeda

**Abstract:** arXiv:2610.24293v1 Announce Type: new  Abstract: Neural networks whose inputs are functions, as in operator learning, are usually shown to be universal by existence arguments, either by reduction to finite-dimensional networks or by Stone--Weierstrass-type density; these prove that approximants exist but say little about how the parameters depend on the target. We take a constructive route. We extend the ridgelet transform, an integral transform related to the Fourier, Radon, and wavelet transforms, to continuous maps on a separable infinite-dimensional Hilbert space, replacing the Lebesgue measure that is unavailable in infinite dimensions by Gaussian weights with a homogeneous mixture on the direction space. The resulting transform has a Plancherel identity and reconstruction formulas incorporating the frame operator, the correction induced by the weights, and it assigns to each target an explicit coefficient measure, one for every admissible analysis filter. From these representations we obtain uniform approximation on compact sets for all continuous non-polynomial activations of polynomial growth, including ReLU, and, by Monte Carlo sampling of the coefficient, finite-width networks with a dimension-free $O(N^{-1/2})$ error in the width $N$ for globally Lipschitz activations and coefficients with finite variation and second moments. Explicit examples include Gaussian targets and neural-operator layers. A companion Lean~4 formalization provides machine-checked proofs, with its correspondence to the manuscript documented at https://shosonoda.github.io/lean-operator-ridgelet/ .

**Comment:** No direct match to any criterion. The paper is about universal approximation by infinite-dimensional neural networks via Gaussian-weighted ridgelet transform, a theoretical contribution in neural operator learning, not specifically about language models, RLHF, or evaluation paradigms.
**Relevance:** 3
**Novelty:** 6

## 175. [Uncovering Semantic Hierarchies in Text-Attributed Graphs via Variational EM-based LLM-GNN Synergy](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.24624) <a id="link175"></a>
**ArXiv ID:** 2610.24624
**Authors:** Yunhui Liu, Xudong Jin, Qizhuo Xie, Chunhui Zhao, Xiao Luo, Tao Zheng, Bin Chong, Tieke He

**Abstract:** arXiv:2610.24624v1 Announce Type: new  Abstract: While hierarchical structures are intrinsic to organizing human knowledge, current representation learning on text-attributed graphs predominantly operates on flat semantic spaces, overlooking the rich coarse-to-fine granularity inherent in real-world data. To bridge this gap, we propose SHiFT, which synergizes the reasoning power of LLMs with the structural representation capability of GNNs within an Expectation-Maximization (EM) paradigm. In the E-step, we harness LLMs as expert taxonomists to induce an explicit semantic tree, introducing a lightweight Mapping & Diagnosis strategy that reduces inference costs by surgically updating the hierarchy only when anomalies are detected. In the M-step, we align the GNN encoder with this induced hierarchy from complementary perspectives, including clustering partition, topological skeleton, and semantic concepts. Theoretical analysis interprets SHiFT as a generalized Variational EM algorithm. Extensive experiments demonstrate that SHiFT not only achieves state-of-the-art performance but also uncovers high-quality, human-readable taxonomies. Our code can be found at https://github.com/Cloudy1225/SHiFT.

## 176. [Means-Ends Analysis Helps LLMs Plan in Continuous-State Domains](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.24928) <a id="link176"></a>
**ArXiv ID:** 2610.24928
**Authors:** Jiayi Li, Carl Kingsford

**Abstract:** arXiv:2610.24928v1 Announce Type: new  Abstract: LLM planning research has traditionally focused on discrete-state domains, where actions form a finite, enumerable set. Existing LLM planning frameworks largely assume actions are enumerable, modeling planning as a search process over candidate actions. However, in continuous-state domains, where actions form a continuum and cannot be enumerated, this assumption breaks down. We find a remedy in Newell and Simon's Means-Ends Analysis (MEA), a general problem-solving technique rooted in heuristics from human psychology. We develop Medaka, an MEA-inspired structured prompting framework that encourages recursive goal decomposition, algebraic derivation, and per-step state tracking. To evaluate native LLM planning abilities in continuous-state domains, we develop Liquidworld, a systematic testbed on liquid mixing. On Liquidworld, Medaka can improve performance across model families, suggesting that classical means-ends analysis elicits LLM-native reasoning abilities that are helpful for continuous-state planning.

**Comment:** Focuses on LLM planning in continuous-state domains using Means-Ends Analysis, improving reasoning and planning capabilities but does not match criteria on RLHF, contamination, diffusion models, or evaluation paradigms.

## 177. [WeAct: Dynamic Weight Switching as Agent Actions](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.26847) <a id="link177"></a>
**ArXiv ID:** 2610.26847
**Authors:** Rui Shi, Xunjian Yin, Jiacheng Sang, Zichuan Liu, Shuyan Zhou

**Abstract:** arXiv:2610.26847v1 Announce Type: new  Abstract: Long-horizon agents must learn heterogeneous capabilities from interleaved trajectories. Updating shared parameters to learn new capabilities can degrade previously acquired ones, resulting in catastrophic forgetting. As a trajectory unfolds, different stages may require different capabilities, yet conventional joint training and static adapter merging maintain a fixed parameter configuration throughout execution. We introduce WeAct (Weight-level Internal Actions), which trains independent capability-specific LoRA adapters over a frozen backbone and adds adapter selection to the agent's action space. These internal actions use the model's native tool-calling interface for on-demand capability composition in a shared context. Across eight deep-research benchmarks, Qwen3-32B with WeAct improves average accuracy by 6.2% relative to full fine-tuning counterparts despite using less compute, and by 27.5% relative to the strongest static LoRA-merging method. WeAct also supports modular continual adaptation, by adding a new code computation adapter to expand the agent's capabilities and updating the existing writing adapter to improve report quality. Both updates leave the backbone and other adapters unchanged, supporting modular continual adaptation in agents.

**Comment:** Related to continual adaptation and modular fine-tuning of language models via LoRA adapters, but does not explicitly address RLHF, instruction-following improvements, or any other specific criteria.

## 178. [A Statistical Viewpoint on Adam, Shampoo and Muon](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.24839) <a id="link178"></a>
**ArXiv ID:** 2610.24839
**Authors:** Tetiana Parshakova, Robert M. Gower, Paulo Orenstein

**Abstract:** arXiv:2610.24839v1 Announce Type: new  Abstract: We provide new statistical insights into designing stochastic optimization methods. By modeling the gradient via a normal distribution, we argue that the stochastic gradient should be whitened using its covariance. We give an online method for estimating the mean and variance of the gradients based on regularized likelihood or, equivalently, through a maximum a posteriori estimate. We then show how to use structured approximations to the covariance within this same framework, which includes diagonal and Kronecker-factored approximations. The diagonal approximation results in a method that is very closely related to Adam and performs as well in practice. The Kronecker approximation results in the new MN method, which is a variant of Shampoo and KL-Shampoo. We also recover Muon by applying this same statistical framework to the gradient direction rather than the full gradient. By combining this with a whitening step, we arrive at MN-Muon, a novel preconditioned version of Muon. Our experiments on GPT-2-type models at Chinchilla scale indicate that MND, the diagonal implementation of MN, outperforms both the Distributed Shampoo and the KL-Shampoo implementations in terms of stability, final validation loss and time taken. MND-Muon, the diagonal implementation of MN-Muon, improves on Muon in 21 of 24 paired GPT-small runs and achieves a lower best validation loss on GPT-medium, where it remains stable over the six tested learning rates. However, we found that NorMuon was comparable to MND-Muon and achieved a slightly lower validation loss on GPT-medium and at GPT-small batch 512 on FineWeb.

**Comment:** Matches general interest in statistical machine learning and optimization for language models but does not match any specific criterion related to RLHF, contamination, diffusion, interpretability, or evaluation paradigms.

## 179. [Predictive Bandit Curriculum Reinforcement Learning via Gradient-Based Sample Group Discovery](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.25704) <a id="link179"></a>
**ArXiv ID:** 2610.25704
**Authors:** Liang Xu, Shufan Shen, Zhongni Hou, Chao Bi, Yufei Zhang, Wei Lin, Guojun Yin, Shuhui Wang

**Abstract:** arXiv:2610.25704v1 Announce Type: new  Abstract: Curriculum learning, which organizes training samples according to their learning priority, has been widely adopted in reinforcement learning to improve training efficiency. However, existing methods commonly construct curriculum units using predefined data properties and estimate their utility by simply aggregating a single proxy signal from recent training history.Such designs may group samples with different optimization effects and fail to anticipate how their utility evolves in relation to the policy. We propose Predictive Gradient-based Bandit (PGB) Curriculum, which first discovers optimization-aware learning units by clustering projected sample-level gradients. PGB then employs a lightweight temporal predictor to forecast the future utility of each unit and converts the predictions into a Boltzmann sampling distribution for adaptive data allocation. Experiments across multiple model scales and reasoning benchmarks show that PGB consistently outperforms standard GRPO, difficulty-based curricula, and history-based scheduling strategies, demonstrating improved data utilization and training efficiency.

**Comment:** Partially relevant to criterion 1 in that it improves reinforcement learning training efficiency via curriculum learning, but does not specifically address RLHF or instruction-following fine-tuning methods for language models; more about sample scheduling in RL generally.

## 180. [KEEP-Bench: Evaluating Evidence Preservation in Context Compression](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.26827) <a id="link180"></a>
**ArXiv ID:** 2610.26827
**Authors:** Ziqing Man, Di Wu, Jia-Chen Gu, Junyi Zhang, Kai-Wei Chang, Nanyun Peng

**Abstract:** arXiv:2610.26827v1 Announce Type: new  Abstract: Context compression improves long-context efficiency but may introduce faithfulness issues through an information bottleneck. Existing evaluations rely on QA metrics, but a reader can still answer correctly from an unfaithful compression, so these metrics underestimate such loss. To bridge this gap, we introduce KEEP-Bench, a diagnostic benchmark that directly assesses compression faithfulness with 4,102 cases across three challenging settings that require keeping evidence for plausible reference resolutions, explicit qualifiers, and minority perspectives. Experiments show that even with tailored prompting, the strongest models remain unreliable: despite context windows exceeding 100K tokens and inputs averaging only 3.1K tokens, the best open-weight model (31B) preserves all required evidence in only 66.8% of cases, and the best proprietary model in only 61.6%. Performance also varies sharply across evidence structures, so conclusions from limited structures may be biased. We further introduce a metric, Evidence Preservation Return (EPR), to relate evidence recall to inference FLOPs, and find that it peaks at moderate scales (4B-8B), where recall remains low. Additional output budget mainly benefits already strong models and widens capability gaps, yet the stronger preservation of these models comes at a steep computational cost. These findings highlight the importance for future work to investigate compressors with strong information preservation under a small computation budget, rather than relying on a large output budget or model scaling.

**Comment:** Partially relevant to criterion 18 (data-centric AI) as it introduces a benchmark and metric for evidence preservation in context compression, but does not focus on instruction-following or RLHF or contamination or diffusion LMs.
**Relevance:** 4
**Novelty:** 5

## 181. [ATEST: Adaptive Resource-Aware Test-Time Scaling for LLM Inference](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.25519) <a id="link181"></a>
**ArXiv ID:** 2610.25519
**Authors:** Mobina Kashaniyan, Ali Jannesari

**Abstract:** arXiv:2610.25519v1 Announce Type: new  Abstract: Test-time scaling (TTS) improves large language model (LLM) reasoning by sampling multiple answers, but fixed sampling budgets ignore both query difficulty and the changing energy cost of inference under GPU contention. We measure up to a 4.1-fold increase in energy for the same four-sample query as GPU load rises. We present ATEST, a resource-aware TTS framework that jointly considers the expected value of additional samples and their current GPU cost. ATEST-Online, its default mode, requires no offline calibration: it incrementally generates samples, estimates the stability of the plurality answer, monitors live GPU conditions, and learns step energy cost online to decide when to stop. ATEST-Calibrated instead selects a per-query budget using offline accuracy and cost models, favoring deployments with stable loads. We evaluate both modes on four reasoning and question-answering datasets using Phi-3-mini on an NVIDIA A100 under controlled varying load, and test transfer to an NVIDIA L40S and Qwen2.5-3B. Across the four A100 datasets, ATEST-Online answers every query without exceeding the energy budget, while achieving the highest or statistically tied accuracy and reducing sampling as load increases. On GSM8K at high load, it achieves 60.0% accuracy with no deferrals, versus 44.0% accuracy and 42.4% deferrals for ATEST-Calibrated. Ablations show that online cost learning drives adaptation to load. Including offline profiling, ATEST-Online uses 42 times less GPU time and 48 times less energy than ATEST-Calibrated for the evaluated workload. Online control trades higher tail latency and lower throughput on short tasks for robustness under varying load; offline calibration remains competitive under stable conditions.

**Comment:** Proposes ATEST, a resource-aware test-time scaling method for LLM inference that adapts sampling based on query difficulty and GPU load. While it relates to inference efficiency and reasoning accuracy, it does not address RLHF, instruction-following fine-tuning, test contamination, diffusion LMs, or new evaluation paradigms.

**Novelty:** 6

## 182. [The Scheduler Is a Hidden Retriever: How Asynchrony Shapes Evidence Acquisition in Search Agents](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.26310) <a id="link182"></a>
**ArXiv ID:** 2610.26310
**Authors:** Ruiyang Ren, Yuhao Wang, Yinan Zhang, Chunyan Miao

**Abstract:** arXiv:2610.26310v1 Announce Type: new  Abstract: Language-model agents increasingly solve complex tasks through parallel reasoning and asynchronous information gathering. Controlled replay across two model families and three benchmarks shows that delivery timing redirects search decisions made before all results arrive, despite identical initial evidence. Acquisition disagreement reaches up to 24 percentage points above same-order repeat variation. This dependence persists after all initial evidence arrives and through a second search, turning temporary differences in what agents have seen into lasting differences in what they acquire. An acquisition-regret bound with a matching construction shows that invariance at a fixed early arrival count can limit the use of evidence already received. We introduce Confluent Evidence Acquisition (CEA), which instead releases local reasoning when each subquestion's evidence is complete and allocates shared search independently of completion order. For a fixed plan and stable conditional response law, we prove joint invariance of decisions, acquisitions, and answers. With question-only plans across three benchmarks, CEA brings acquisition disagreement to within 1.0 percentage point of repeat variation and achieves accuracy comparable to complete-input controls, improving over early commitment by up to 12.6 percentage points. Replacing CEA's fixed allocation with first-ready allocation under contention adds 21.6 percentage points of acquisition disagreement, while local query-target disagreement remains near repeat variation. Timed comparisons show that CEA reaches decisions earlier and responds faster than the same program waiting for all initial evidence.

**Comment:** No criteria matched. This paper studies asynchronous evidence acquisition in language-model agents and proposes Confluent Evidence Acquisition to reduce acquisition disagreement. While interesting for agent design, it does not match criteria related to RLHF, contamination, diffusion LMs, or evaluation paradigms.
**Relevance:** 3
**Novelty:** 6

## 183. [MELD: Recursive Self-Improvement via Hierarchical Coordination for Multi-Agent Scientific Discovery](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.24454) <a id="link183"></a>
**ArXiv ID:** 2610.24454
**Authors:** Sizhe Tang, Zuyuan Zhang, Zeyu Fang, Tian Lan

**Abstract:** arXiv:2610.24454v1 Announce Type: new  Abstract: Multi-agent scientific discovery with large language models raises a coupled learning problem: research assignments determine both which experiments are pursued and the experiences from which agents adapt. We introduce MELD, a unified hierarchical test-time training framework that jointly adapts a team-level Coordinator policy and agent-level individual Scientist policies within a single discovery process. The Coordinator generates a joint portfolio of search branches and research directives over a shared derivation tree, while Scientists develop hypotheses and execution plans implemented by a shared frozen Executor. Both levels receive alternating GRPO-style parameter updates under a shared smooth best-of-budget objective, allowing team organization and individual research behavior to co-evolve in a recursive self-improvement loop. Our analysis establishes objective alignment for the hierarchy, characterizes conditions for coordination gains over independent parallel discovery and the first-order benefit of private adaptation, and derives a finite-budget regret bound for adapted models. On 22 MLGym and MLE-bench tasks, MELD is best on 20 of them and achieves the highest aggregate normalized scores on MLGym, using a 4B backbone for coordination and research. Most baselines use proprietary Scientists, and all systems share one frozen Executor.

**Comment:** No direct match to any criterion. The paper proposes a hierarchical multi-agent training framework for scientific discovery with LLMs, focusing on coordination and recursive self-improvement, but not on RLHF, contamination, diffusion LMs, or evaluation paradigms.

## 184. [RESIN: Multi-Chain Reasoning with Entity-Structured Indexing](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.26782) <a id="link184"></a>
**ArXiv ID:** 2610.26782
**Authors:** Xuanyu Meng, Jash Rajesh Parekh, Jiawei Han

**Abstract:** arXiv:2610.26782v1 Announce Type: new  Abstract: Multi-chain reasoning is a challenging task not only because the multi-chain evidence is dispersed, but also because different parts of reasoning may require distinct, interacting chains of support. Flat retrieval can flood a model with loosely related text, whereas early selection of a single reasoning path can discard evidence that becomes useful only after other facts are considered. We introduce RESIN (Reasoning with Entity Structured Indexing), which uses structure to locate evidence while keeping the original source as the basis for reasoning. RESIN decomposes a question into focused one-hop needs, retrieves a small set of source-grounded units for each hop, and places only this relevant evidence in a compact reasoning buffer. The buffer preserves alternative supports until final composition, making intermediate decisions explicit without forcing a brittle path. On the full HolisQA benchmark, RESIN achieves 82.92 context recall and 76.70 answer relevancy over all questions, with higher reported CR and AR across five domains and higher F1 in four domains than the published baselines. Ablations show that both typed evidence diversity and hop-wise decomposition are essential, supporting a clear conclusion: Precise evidence organization and delayed commitment make multi-chain reasoning effective and auditable. Our code is available at https://github.com/RamonMeng/RESIN.

**Comment:** This paper introduces RESIN, a method for multi-chain reasoning with entity-structured indexing to improve retrieval and reasoning in LLMs. While it improves reasoning and evidence organization, it does not explicitly discuss RLHF, instruction-following fine-tuning, test contamination, diffusion LMs, or new evaluation paradigms.
**Relevance:** 3

## 185. [Revisiting Smoothed Online Learning: Competitive Ratio, Dynamic Regret with Switching Cost, and Best of Both Worlds](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.24370) <a id="link185"></a>
**ArXiv ID:** 2610.24370
**Authors:** Lijun Zhang, Linzhe He, Wei Jiang, Shiyin Lu

**Abstract:** arXiv:2610.24370v1 Announce Type: new  Abstract: We revisit smoothed online learning and develop algorithms with improved competitive ratios, optimal dynamic regret with switching cost, and best-of-both-worlds guarantees. Surprisingly, minimizing the hitting cost alone is $\max(1,\frac{2}{\alpha})$-competitive for $\alpha$-polyhedral functions under the $\ell_2$-norm switching cost and $1+\frac{4}{\lambda}$-competitive for $\lambda$-quadratic growth functions under the squared $\ell_2$-norm switching cost, significantly improving existing guarantees. The polyhedral bound is tight for this naive algorithm, and we establish a matching lower bound for $\alpha\geq1$. For convex $\lambda$-quadratic growth hitting costs, minimizing a suitably weighted sum of hitting and squared $\ell_2$-norm switching costs yields a competitive ratio of $1+\frac{2}{\sqrt{\lambda}}$. For dynamic regret with the $\ell_2$-norm switching cost, we develop Smoothed Ader (SAder), which achieves the optimal $O(\sqrt{T(1+P_T)})$ rate in the standard online convex optimization setting, where $P_T$ is the comparator's path-length. Its lookahead version removes the bounded-gradient assumption, and a matching lower bound establishes optimality in this setting. Finally, for convex hitting costs on a bounded convex domain, we develop UDNP to aggregate any two experts in the lookahead setting under both switching costs. Its additional cumulative cost is at most $O(1)$ relative to the first expert and $O(\sqrt{T\log T})$ relative to the second, yielding best-of-both-worlds guarantees without bounded gradients.

**Comment:** This paper revisits smoothed online learning with improved competitive ratios and regret bounds. It is a theoretical contribution in online learning but does not address language models, RLHF, instruction-following, test contamination, diffusion LMs, or evaluation paradigms for LLMs.
**Relevance:** 3
**Novelty:** 6

## 186. [GraphEvoResearcher: Graph-Aware Training-Free Self-Reflective Reasoning for Cost-Bounded Multi-Hop Question Answering](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.25978) <a id="link186"></a>
**ArXiv ID:** 2610.25978
**Authors:** Suxing Liu

**Abstract:** arXiv:2610.25978v1 Announce Type: new  Abstract: Multi-hop question answering (QA) requires models to chain intermediate conclusions while remaining cost-aware. Existing training-free self-reflective protocols (generate -> critique -> revise) improve accuracy but operate on a flat token stream: revisions cannot identify which prior step is unreliable, and early stopping cannot exploit structural convergence. We introduce GraphEvoResearcher, a training-free protocol that lifts EvoResearcher's self-reflective meta-reward onto an explicit reasoning graph - nodes are intermediate conclusions/evidence and edges encode support, contradict, and depends_on. A graph-aware meta-reward combines correctness, efficiency, reflection, diversity, and four structural signals (connectivity, acyclicity, branch coverage, path convergence) to decide whether to revise, backtrack, or stop. A graph-convergence early-stop terminates when evidence branches converge on a single conclusion. On a real LLM (DeepSeek), over GSM8K, MATH, and BBH (n=50 each), GraphEvoResearcher reaches 98%, 92%, and 82% respectively, the highest of all baselines, with the largest gains on the hardest set (MATH: +24 pp over single-shot, +12 pp over flat reflection), while keeping construction cost bounded at ~2 steps / 4.5-5 nodes. Our results indicate the structural signal of a reasoning graph is most valuable exactly where single-step reasoning is unreliable.

**Comment:** This paper proposes a new training-free self-reflective reasoning protocol using graph-aware meta-rewards for multi-hop question answering. While it involves reasoning and LLMs, it does not explicitly discuss RLHF, instruction-following fine-tuning, or instruction-tuning datasets, nor test set contamination or diffusion LMs. It is closest to reasoning improvements but does not match criterion 12 explicitly.

## 187. [Train An Imaginative Agent](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.26845) <a id="link187"></a>
**ArXiv ID:** 2610.26845
**Authors:** Jiaxin Ge, Junyi Zhang, Tianning Chai, Hanjun Yoo, Chenyang Wang, Xiuyu Li, Weijia Shi, XuDong Wang, Sewon Min, Luke Zettlemoyer, Trevor Darrell, Haiwen Feng

**Abstract:** arXiv:2610.26845v1 Announce Type: new  Abstract: A seemingly reasonable push in Sokoban can make the puzzle unsolvable. To avoid such mistakes, an agent has to anticipate the consequences of its actions before execution, especially when feedback from the real environment is sparse or actions are irreversible. We introduce Agentic Imagination (Agima), a training framework that trains an agent to imagine before acting, i.e. to be imaginative. Agima trains a single omni model to propose actions, generate images of their outcomes, and then reason over these imagined futures to decide what to execute in the real environment. Rather than committing to a single plan, the agent can simulate a sequence of actions, explore alternatives, and compare possible futures. We first use supervised fine-tuning (SFT) to unify policy and world modeling within a single omni model. This enables the agent to predict future outcomes and perform basic forms of imagination, such as forward thinking and counterfactual branching. We then use agentic reinforcement learning (RL) to teach the agent to reason with its imagined futures and use them effectively for planning. Across four environments spanning Maze2D, Sokoban, Pacman, and PushT, Agima generalizes to harder settings unseen during training. Trained with SFT alone on two-box Sokoban, Agima solves 40% of four-box puzzles while seeing the real board only once every five moves, compared with an action-only planner that only solves 2%. After RL, new behaviors emerge in how Agima uses its imagination: it learns a new unified format that was unseen during the SFT stage, learns to conduct forward thinking only when necessary, and branches more frequently at critical decision points. An agent that can picture where its actions lead can learn when and where to look ahead, that is, to plan. Project page: https://agima.net/.

**Comment:** No direct match to any criterion. The paper proposes a training framework for agents to imagine future outcomes before acting, combining supervised fine-tuning and reinforcement learning, but this is focused on embodied agents and planning rather than instruction-following or language model methodology.
**Relevance:** 3
**Novelty:** 6

## 188. [LatComm: Reusable Context Compression for Latent Communication between Large Language Models](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.26840) <a id="link188"></a>
**ArXiv ID:** 2610.26840
**Authors:** Huawei Lin, Jinting Liu, Tan Yu, Weijie Zhao

**Abstract:** arXiv:2610.26840v1 Announce Type: new  Abstract: Systems that combine several large language models (LLMs) often use one context for many questions. When the context is passed between models as text, the receiving model processes all of its tokens again for every question. Avoiding this cost requires a context representation that is compact, reusable across questions, and readable by a different LLM. Existing compression and latent communication methods each provide only some of these properties. We introduce LatComm, a Latent Communication interface that provides all three by separating encoding from reading: the context is encoded once without the question, and the receiver selects what to read for each question. A frozen context encoder LLM compresses the context into a fixed-size packet and sends it to a different frozen receiver LLM, which reads the packet through cross-attention, with gates computed from its prompt. On 12 multiple-choice question-answering datasets, LatComm improves average accuracy over single-vector baselines by up to 10.2 points. On QuALITY, it reduces the receiver's input by 57x and answers the questions of an article 3x faster.

**Comment:** LatComm proposes a latent communication interface for reusable context compression between LLMs, improving efficiency in multi-LLM systems. This is relevant to generative modeling and efficiency but does not match any specific criteria related to RLHF, contamination, diffusion LMs, or evaluation paradigms.
**Relevance:** 3
**Novelty:** 6
