## 189. [LatLM: Line-Parallel Language Models with Deferred Commitment](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.23951) <a id="link189"></a>
**ArXiv ID:** 2610.23951
**Authors:** Shaorong Zhang, Shiyuan Li, Longxuan Yu, Greg Ver Steeg

**Abstract:** arXiv:2610.23951v1 Announce Type: new  Abstract: Line-parallel language models advance multiple lines concurrently for substantial speedups. However, frameworks like the Line-Coupled Language Model (LCLM) enforce a fixed commitment schedule across lines. Because dependencies vary within text, a fixed schedule cannot hold back a line whose prerequisites remain uncomputed, forcing premature commitments and cascading reasoning errors. To address this limitation, we introduce the Lattice Language Model (LatLM), making commitment schedules an adaptive decoding-time choice. Text occupies a 2D lattice where eligible frontier tokens are scored each round; a policy commits confident candidates while deferring unready ones for extended context. One forward pass supervises entire sampled trajectories via 2D causal masking, while an append-only cache retains exact states. At 482M parameters, we establish two findings. (1) LatLM matches a data- and capacity-matched autoregressive model on held-out text when decoded sequentially, while its adaptive parallel decoder achieves near-autoregressive accuracy on independent-line tasks using 25--41% as many decoding rounds. (2) When cross-line dependencies cause LCLM's fixed schedule to fail, deferred commitment substantially improves accuracy while retaining parallelism where dependencies permit. On mixed dependency chains, LatLM achieves 96.5% accuracy versus LCLM's 78.5%, using fewer than half the decoding rounds of the autoregressive baseline.

**Comment:** This paper proposes a new adaptive decoding schedule for line-parallel language models improving instruction-following style decoding efficiency and accuracy, which relates to criterion 3 on advances in diffusion language models since it involves novel parallel decoding and adaptive commitment schedules in language modeling. However, it does not explicitly mention diffusion models or RLHF/instruction tuning.
**Relevance:** 3
**Novelty:** 6

## 190. [When Form Changes but Logic Doesn't: Building Logic-invariant LLMs through Logical Structure](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.25402) <a id="link190"></a>
**ArXiv ID:** 2610.25402
**Authors:** Xuyuan Liu, Xinshuai Dong, Elynn Chen, Yujun Yan

**Abstract:** arXiv:2610.25402v1 Announce Type: new  Abstract: Large language models (LLMs) have achieved strong performance across diverse reasoning tasks, yet it remains unclear whether this performance reflects an understanding of underlying logical structure or a reliance on shallow semantic cues. A key indicator of such understanding is whether models treat logically equivalent inputs consistently, producing the same predictions despite differences in expression. Through systematic evaluation on curated data, we show that even strong LLMs often fail this test: they do not consistently preserve predictions across logically equivalent inputs and generalize poorly to unseen logical forms. To address this limitation, we propose LoGIcal STructure-guided reasoning (LoGIST), a framework that guides LLM reasoning with explicit logical structure. LoGIST maps each input instance to a decision diagram, a directed acyclic graph that captures the underlying logical structure, and encodes this graph into a logical embedding that conditions the LLM's reasoning process. Across multiple training settings, LoGIST reduces inconsistency under logical equivalence and improves generalization to unseen logical forms, suggesting that logical invariance is a useful target for building more robust and generalizable logical reasoning in LLMs.

**Comment:** Related to reasoning robustness but does not match criteria on RLHF, contamination, diffusion, or evaluation paradigms. Focuses on logical invariance and reasoning consistency, not instruction-following or contamination.

## 191. [Cross-Fitting for Neural Posterior Estimation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.26733) <a id="link191"></a>
**ArXiv ID:** 2610.26733
**Authors:** Jeffrey Regier

**Abstract:** arXiv:2610.26733v1 Announce Type: new  Abstract: Neural Posterior Estimation (NPE) fits an amortized conditional density estimator to a simulated training set of complete-data realizations. NPE's training objective, log-likelihood, favors posterior approximations that assign arbitrarily high density to the training points, and typical families of amortized conditional density estimators (e.g., normalizing flows, Gaussian encoders) are flexible enough to oblige. The resulting policy of extreme overconfidence translates poorly to held-out data. Early stopping and other regularizers can prevent this degeneracy, but they are limited to selecting posterior approximations along an unfavorable optimization trajectory set by in-sample residuals, where the posterior mean is underfit while the posterior variance is overfit. To avoid this trade-off, we propose Cross-Fitting NPE (CF-NPE), which uses sample splitting to separate conditional-mean estimation from the estimation of the posterior's higher moments. Using $K$-fold cross-fitting, CF-NPE fits $K$ posterior-mean regressors and then fits a conditional density estimator to the resulting out-of-fold residuals. In experiments with synthetic data and in a cosmological application, CF-NPE achieves higher held-out log-likelihood than standard NPE and typically improves calibration. CF-NPE is most useful in high-dimensional, simulation-limited settings.

**Comment:** No direct match to any criterion. The paper proposes Cross-Fitting for Neural Posterior Estimation, a method in simulation-based inference, which is a statistical machine learning method but unrelated to RLHF, contamination detection, diffusion LMs, or LLM-specific instruction tuning.

## 192. [Learning to Program Retrievals: Composing Search Primitives via Reinforcement Learning](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.24854) <a id="link192"></a>
**ArXiv ID:** 2610.24854
**Authors:** Kyuyoung Kim, Zilin Xiao, Zhiyuan Wang, Avinash Atreya, Ke Yang, Jinwoo Shin, Kecheng Hao

**Abstract:** arXiv:2610.24854v1 Announce Type: new  Abstract: Retrieval-augmented language models increasingly use multi-step search to solve knowledge-intensive tasks, yet existing systems typically expose retrieval through fixed pipelines or collections of atomic tools. We introduce Retrieval Program Learning (RPL), a programmable search framework that allows a policy to compose search primitives into executable programs with local state and control flow. This enables adaptive search that can retrieve broadly, process intermediate results, and selectively construct model context without tying retrieval breadth to context consumption. However, the resulting flexibility creates a difficult exploration problem: useful programs may require several coordinated decisions, while reinforcement learning often supplies only sparse feedback. To address this challenge, we propose program mutation, a grammar-constrained and execution-verified procedure that generates local variants of suboptimal programs sampled during RL, converts them into training trajectories, and distills them back into the policy. We evaluate RPL on open-domain question answering and corpus-level reasoning tasks, comparing it with a range of retrieval and reasoning baselines. RPL consistently improves open-domain QA performance across model sizes and in- and out-of-domain benchmarks, with particularly large gains on multi-hop tasks. Notably, a 4B model trained with RPL outperforms all 8B model baselines. On corpus-level reasoning, RPL achieves the highest answer F1 and document-coverage F1 for both model sizes while substantially reducing context consumption and retrieval turns. Our results show that programmable search expands the retrieval strategies that agents can learn, while program mutation provides an effective mechanism for exploring useful program structures beyond direct on-policy sampling.

**Comment:** No direct match to criteria. The paper proposes a reinforcement learning method for composing retrieval programs, improving retrieval-augmented language models, but does not explicitly discuss RLHF, instruction-following fine-tuning, contamination, diffusion LMs, or evaluation paradigms.

## 193. [The One-Pixel of Reasoning: One Truthful Relation Is Enough to Mislead LLM Agents](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.24873) <a id="link193"></a>
**ArXiv ID:** 2610.24873
**Authors:** Jinwei Hu, Youcheng Sun, Yi Dong, Xiaowei Huang

**Abstract:** arXiv:2610.24873v1 Announce Type: new  Abstract: Can one sentence manipulate an agent's belief by targeting a single implicit warrant linking evidence to a conclusion? In this paper, we introduce the one-relation attack (ORA), a novel cognitive attack on this inferential relation. ORA localizes a critical implicit warrant and performs a one-relation intervention using a single source-grounded sentence that qualifies this warrant and steers the agent toward an incorrect belief. Across eighteen models on biomedical inference and vulnerability detection, best-of-$10 attacks overturn initially correct answers at mean rates of 54.9% for proprietary and 80.0% for open-weight models. Attacks also transfer without victim feedback, and paired controls support the roles of targeting and qualification. Moreover, successful sentences can pass source-support checks and evade identification, and multi-agent debate raises team error from 50.0% to 59.5% in our balanced stress test. These findings establish implicit warrants as a cognitive attack surface for manipulating agents' beliefs.

**Comment:** This paper introduces a novel cognitive attack (one-relation attack) on LLM agents by manipulating implicit warrants in reasoning. While it studies vulnerabilities and attacks on LLM reasoning, it does not focus on test set contamination or membership inference methods. No direct match to criteria.

## 194. [MemPrice: Online Memory Allocation via Adaptive Pricing for Long-Horizon Agents](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.24955) <a id="link194"></a>
**ArXiv ID:** 2610.24955
**Authors:** Yiming Zong, Shiyuan Piao, Jiashuo Jiang, Fugee Tsung

**Abstract:** arXiv:2610.24955v1 Announce Type: new  Abstract: Long-horizon LLM agents face a fundamental allocation problem as interaction histories grow: which information to retain, how much context to devote to it, and at what granularity. Existing memory mechanisms largely focus on relevance, retrieval, or compression, leaving this allocation trade-off implicit. We introduce MemPrice, which treats active context as a shared resource and dynamically allocates it across memories and representation levels. MemPrice turns accumulated memory pressure into an adaptive price on context, encouraging detail only when its estimated retention value justifies the additional footprint. This pressure persists across memory refreshes, allowing the contents of memory to evolve while preserving long-run control over context usage. We show that irreversible compression can force linear worst-case regret against hindsight following its own memory trajectory, and introduce a same-state benchmark to evaluate allocation on MemPrice's realized memory states. MemPrice enforces the hard context budget at every round and regulates long-run occupancy through adaptive pricing, without prior knowledge of the interaction horizon. Under priced-frontier regularity, its average allocation-regret bound against this benchmark vanishes when accumulated estimation error is sublinear. We evaluate the framework under matched memory budgets on LongMemEval, LoCoMo, AppWorld, and WebShop across three foundation models, spanning both query-blind memory construction and goal-directed agent context management.

**Comment:** Not relevant to any specific criterion: focuses on memory allocation and pricing for long-horizon LLM agents, not on instruction-following fine-tuning, contamination, diffusion LMs, or evaluation paradigms.

## 195. [MemT-1.0: Towards Memory-Native Evolution](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.26825) <a id="link195"></a>
**ArXiv ID:** 2610.26825
**Authors:** Zhiyu Li, Zeyu Zhang, Linyun Liu, Yiwei Bu, Chenyang Xi, Xichong Zhang, Yi Yu, Yu Yu, Zhichen Liu, Yuxiao Yi, Chunyu Li, Haoran Liu, Zhenkai Ding, Le Zhou, Juncheng Zhang, Boyu Chen, Zehao Lin, Guorui Li, Renyu Fu, Ji Li, Youyang Xia, Jiajun Shen, Harvey Li, Jingsong Zhou, Helan Hu, Tong Shen, Tianxiao Xu, Chenye Han, Weijie Li, Zhongquan Zhou, Hongming Xu, Yuekeng Li, Erenkai Zhu, Peng Yu, Wenhao Li, Jingyi Kang, Xixuan Hao, Xuzhou Zhu, Can Chen, Jiawei Liu, Chen Jiang, Tao Jiang, Fangwei Xiang, Zepeng Yao, Kai Chen, Guang Yang, Mingfei Lu, Chunhui Li, Zhengxuan Jiang, Bo Tang, Shiyu Zhan, Yihang Sun, Jie Chu, Wenjun Xiong, Zhenxiang Hua, Chen Lin, Detong Su, Dongyu Deng, Qiuping Ding, Guofeng Ding, Si Chen, Guohua Wen, Xu Chen, Zhi-Qin John Xu, Ying-Cong Chen, Ningyu Zhang, Haofen Wang, Muning Wen, Haotian Chen, Wentao Zhang, Xuanhe Zhou, Rita Zhang, Junchi Yan, Feiyu Xiong

**Abstract:** arXiv:2610.26825v1 Announce Type: new  Abstract: Memory-native evolution requires making memory an intrinsic capability of foundation models, enabling them to continuously accumulate and utilize information. To this end, we introduce MemT-1.0, a new architecture of memory foundation models that internalize memory as part of model computation. Building on Metis, MemT-1.0 employs Gated DeltaNet-2 with a stable token-level memory update mechanism within each turn. We further introduce Mixture-of-Storage to scale native memory capacity. Following a deliberate backbone preparation, we develop a two-stage training pipeline consisting of continued pre-training and post-training, supported by 34B tokens of training data and efficient training and inference infrastructure. We release a model family comprising MemT-1.0-Nano and MemT-1.0-Flash, and conduct a comprehensive evaluation of memory and general capabilities. The results show that they outperform the parametric memory baselines on average and remain competitive with retrieval-augmented methods, while largely preserving general capabilities. At a history length of 128K tokens, MemT-1.0-Nano achieves approximately $1.86\times$ end-to-end inference speedup and a $66\times$ reduction in persistent storage relative to full context KV cache. By internalizing retrieved procedural experience, MemT-1.0 improves task success on agentic tasks, providing a practical step toward memory-native evolution.

## 196. [Memory as Belief: Bayesian Evolution of Memory for Self-Evolving LLM Agents](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.25119) <a id="link196"></a>
**ArXiv ID:** 2610.25119
**Authors:** Yiming Xu, Qian Yang, Zihan Chen, Yuefei Wu, Wenhan Zeng, Bin Shi, Bo Dong

**Abstract:** arXiv:2610.25119v1 Announce Type: new  Abstract: Large language model (LLM) agents increasingly rely on external memory to accumulate past experiences for continual adaptation. However, effectively reusing memory requires accurately assessing its value, as retrieved experiences may become unreliable when transferred across task contexts. Despite its importance, memory valuation remains largely underexplored, with existing methods relying on historical retrieval success rates that overlook the context dependency of memory usefulness and the varying reliability of estimates under different amounts of evidence. Inspired by the Bayesian brain hypothesis, we propose BEAM (Bayesian-style Evolutionary Agentic Memory), a framework that reformulates memory value evaluation as a parameterized cognitive inference process, where memory value is represented as a context-conditioned dynamic belief rather than a passive historical statistic. Specifically, BEAM infers memory value beliefs from current task contexts and continuously calibrates them using execution feedback as empirical evidence, transforming memory from passive storage into an adaptive cognitive mechanism for continual self-evolution. Extensive experiments across reasoning and code generation benchmarks demonstrate that BEAM consistently improves memory utilization and enables continual self-evolution of LLM agents.

**Comment:** No direct match to criteria; proposes a Bayesian memory valuation framework for LLM agents, related to memory and continual adaptation but not instruction-following or RLHF.

## 197. [TideKV: Delayed KV Eviction via Layer-Wise Chunked Prefill for Memory-Efficient Long-Context LLM Inference](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.24499) <a id="link197"></a>
**ArXiv ID:** 2610.24499
**Authors:** Yu Wang, Zhu Wang, Ning Hu, Yifan Guo, Xiu Ma, Bin Guo, Zhiwen Yu

**Abstract:** arXiv:2610.24499v1 Announce Type: new  Abstract: Long-context large language model (LLM) inference is important for modern intelligent applications but remains constrained by peak memory during prompt prefill. Existing KV cache compression reduces decoding memory but typically requires full-prompt prefill to preserve global context, retaining a high construction peak. Online chunk-level eviction lowers memory earlier but selects before each layer has observed the complete prompt, potentially degrading cache construction and decoding. We present TideKV, an on-GPU cache-construction architecture using layer-major delayed eviction. TideKV processes all prompt chunks within each Transformer layer, retains full-prompt KV only for the active layer, and evicts at layer end. This schedule preserves full-prompt-aware selection while reducing activation and temporary memory. Experiments across multiple computing platforms show that TideKV preserves the inference quality and final cache size of the evaluated compression methods. At 64K on A100, it reduces the construction-time transient footprint by 83.89%. On RTX 4090, it increases time to first token by only 1.06% at 64K and enables 128K prefill. TideKV also completes 128K on Jetson, whereas matched post-prefill compression runs out of memory on both resource-constrained platforms. These results show that delayed layer-end eviction substantially expands feasible context lengths without sacrificing cache utility.

**Comment:** Does not match any criteria; focuses on memory-efficient long-context LLM inference via delayed KV eviction, a systems optimization rather than methodological improvement to RLHF or contamination detection.
**Relevance:** 3
**Novelty:** 5

## 198. [Arctic-SWE: Revisiting the Long Horizon Agent Training by Modeling-System Co-Design](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.24914) <a id="link198"></a>
**ArXiv ID:** 2610.24914
**Authors:** Boyi Liu, Yite Wang, Xinyu Lian, Fan Shu, Sida Li, Feng Yan, Mingjia Zhang, Yuxiong He, Zhewei Yao

**Abstract:** arXiv:2610.24914v1 Announce Type: new  Abstract: Long-horizon agent training, including software engineering, is advancing rapidly, but the recipe and infrastructure needed for a strong reinforcement-learning baseline remain difficult to reproduce. This makes gains from new algorithms or data hard to distinguish from improvements to an under-tuned baseline. We present Arctic-SWE, a fully asynchronous RL framework that establishes such a baseline through model-system co-design, using overfitting on a small task pool as the testbed. This testbed exposes failures that early reward gains conceal and guides the integration of established components: gated reward, importance-weight control, aligned training and inference engines, and consistent state across policy updates. No single component is new, yet together they form the missing foundation. On raw R2E-Gym data, without new algorithms or data work, Arctic-SWE trains Qwen3.5-4B to 62.0% pass@1 on SWE-bench Verified. This is comparable to concurrent FrogNano (61.5%), which relies on online task synthesis, after about one-sixth as many training rollouts. Building on this foundation, we further filter and refine R2E-Gym, SWE-rebench V2, and Scale-SWE. With this curated subset, the 4B model improves from 47.4%/25.0%/37.9% to 65.0%/38.3%/59.7% on SWE-bench Verified/Pro-v1/Pro-v2, and the recipe also improves Qwen3.5-9B from 56.2%/31.9%/48.8% to 65.7%/39.6%/70.2% on Verified/Pro-v1/Pro-v2. Controlled comparisons show that both curation and test strengthening improve held-out performance, although stronger tests reduce apparent training-task mastery. Arctic-SWE provides a documented starting point for studying better algorithms, data and environments for asynchronous agent RL.

**Comment:** No direct match to any criteria. The paper presents an RL framework (Arctic-SWE) for long-horizon agent training with model-system co-design, focusing on reinforcement learning baselines and infrastructure rather than RLHF, instruction tuning, test contamination, or language models specifically.

## 199. [Block Recursive Transformers for Structured Parameter Sharing](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.24998) <a id="link199"></a>
**ArXiv ID:** 2610.24998
**Authors:** Josep Marques, Hyunyoung A. Cho, Youngeun Kim, Youjia Zhang, Aecheon Jung, Kyuhong Shim, Sungeun Hong

**Abstract:** arXiv:2610.24998v1 Announce Type: new  Abstract: Recursive Transformers depend not only on how many parameters are shared, but also on how shared parameters are organized across depth. Existing static schemes adopt two extreme reuse topologies: layer-wise repetition, where each layer is reused independently, and global cyclic reuse, where the entire stack is repeatedly applied as a single shared function. We argue that sharing granularity is an important factor in recursive parameter sharing. This study proposes Block Recursive Transformers (BRT), which partition layers into contiguous blocks and recursively reuse each block before moving to the next. This intermediate topology enables local refinement within blocks while preserving depth-wise transitions, without adding routing modules, auxiliary states, or extra training objectives. Across decoder-only models up to 1.7B parameters, with additional encoder-decoder and encoder-only studies, BRT improves validation negative log-likelihood and downstream accuracy over static recursive sharing baselines. Ablations and representation analyses further show that intermediate block granularity better balances local refinement and depth-wise differentiation. Project website: https://aim-skku.github.io/BRT/

**Comment:** No direct match to any specific criterion. The paper proposes a new parameter sharing scheme for transformers improving efficiency and performance, but does not discuss RLHF, instruction-following fine-tuning, test contamination, diffusion LMs, or interpretability methods.

## 200. [Are Self-Evolving Agents Continual Learners?](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.25858) <a id="link200"></a>
**ArXiv ID:** 2610.25858
**Authors:** Xiaoyin Chen, Yizheng Huang, Yinglun Xu, Ziyun Zhao, Emmett McQuinn, Julie Liu, Hope Yao

**Abstract:** arXiv:2610.25858v1 Announce Type: new  Abstract: Large language model agents are often deployed in environments where new knowledge, tools, and workflows keep arriving. Such an agent is therefore expected to adapt continually, learning new tasks as they arrive without forgetting earlier ones. This resembles the continual learning problem, but at a pace that conventional gradient updates cannot sustain. Nonparametric self-evolving agents are a natural alternative: they adapt by rewriting their own harness, such as prompts, memory, and skills, from task feedback while the model weights stay frozen. We study whether such agents are effective continual learners across four drifting task streams that shift in topic, workflow, or domain (built from BIRD, BFCL, and ScienceWorld). Evaluating three representative methods spanning experience retrieval and self-modification (ReasoningBank, GEPA, and Meta-Harness), we find that although self-evolution improves aggregate performance on most streams, higher plasticity consistently comes at the cost of stability. Specifically, self-modifying methods apply global edits to a shared prompt or workspace, interfering with unseen tasks and overwriting earlier knowledge as the stream shifts. Conversely, similarity-based memory retrieval localizes updates across distant domains, but still retrieves foreign memories on unseen tasks and suffers from retrieval collision when similar queries require conflicting workflows. These findings show that current self-evolving agents remain incomplete continual learners.

**Comment:** Studies continual learning in self-evolving LLM agents but does not focus on RLHF or instruction-following fine-tuning methods; no direct match to criteria.
**Relevance:** 3
**Novelty:** 5

## 201. [Shaping the Document, Not the Model: Optimized Suffixes for Context Following](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.26236) <a id="link201"></a>
**ArXiv ID:** 2610.26236
**Authors:** Gia-Bao Dinh Ho, Sekh Mainul Islam, Masahiro Kaneko, Timothy Baldwin, Isabelle Augenstein

**Abstract:** arXiv:2610.26236v1 Announce Type: new  Abstract: When a user supplies a document and wants the answer drawn from it, a language model is expected to follow it even when it contradicts its parametric knowledge. Prior work has focused on making the model rely on context by steering activations, contrasting logits during decoding, or instructing it in plain language. We ask whether the document alone, with no instruction written into it and no access to the model at inference time, is enough. We optimize a document suffix by discrete token search, starting from the answer to a single question and extending the objective to a set of passages, to multi-hop questions that follow from the planted fact, and to later turns of a conversation. On counterfactual passages that contradict parametric knowledge of the model, the suffix flips nearly all items the model resists across three instruction-tuned model families, in most settings outperforms the decoding baseline and the strongest instruction we could write, and matches activation steering. Controls show that the suffix largely acts through the passage rather than injecting the answer, and a single suffix per model transfers to unseen passages. Each extended objective carries the effect to its target, so the objective, not only the document, sets how far the effect reaches.

**Comment:** Related to instruction-following and context usage but does not describe new fine-tuning or RLHF methods; no direct match to criteria.

## 202. [Competing Learning in Hybrid Architectures](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.26830) <a id="link202"></a>
**ArXiv ID:** 2610.26830
**Authors:** Xingyu Shen, Yingfa Chen, Ziqing Qiao, Chaojun Xiao, Xu Han, Zhiyuan Liu

**Abstract:** arXiv:2610.26830v1 Announce Type: new  Abstract: Many modern language models have adopted hybrid architectures that incorporate both linear-time recurrence and softmax attention. Modern recurrence layers provide a highly expressive mixing mechanism while ensuring high throughput by compressing contextual information into a fixed-size state, but underperform softmax attention in contextual information retrieval. In this paper, we reveal a Competing Learning phenomenon where different kinds of mixers compete to learn the same function, and when any layer learns a function, it suppresses the corresponding learning signal for other layers. Layer attribution analysis reveals that under typical data distributions, modern recurrence learns retrieval functions faster than softmax attention, causing attention to fail to learn retrieval in most cases. Stopping weight updates in recurrence in the middle of training helps attention learn retrieval. Based on these results, we propose a staged training procedure for pre-training hybrid architectures on natural language. Empirical results on models up to 1B parameters show that our method results in substantial improvement in language modeling loss, common-sense reasoning, as well as a wide range of retrieval-related tasks across different context lengths.

**Comment:** Relevant to general language model training improvements but does not explicitly address RLHF or instruction-following fine-tuning steps; no direct match to criteria.

## 203. [Selm: Self-Evolving Library Management for Multi-Skill Agent Optimization](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.24630) <a id="link203"></a>

**Authors:** Mengxi Hu, Yuxuan Hu, Jinbo Su, Ke Wang, Honghao Shi, Wenli Wu, Cuiping Li, Hong Chen, Jing Zhang

**Abstract:** arXiv:2610.24630v1 Announce Type: new  Abstract: Large language model (LLM) agents increasingly use skills as a lightweight way to acquire task-specific knowledge. Recent work enables skills to evolve from task trajectories. However, these methods often lack systematic management of evolved skills, making it difficult for agents to reuse and accumulate skills across domains, thus limiting performance on subsequent tasks.To reliably accumulate, reuse, and maintain useful skills across tasks, we present SELM (Self-Evolving Library Management), a framework that formulates skill evolution as library-level management. SELM jointly manages skill retrieval, updating, and lifecycle by maintaining skill confidence and utility signals. It introduces utility-aware retrieval to select useful skills, a Map-Reduce framework to generate targeted updates from task-specific experience, and lifecycle controls to retain or remove skills based on their effectiveness. These mechanisms enable the skill library to continuously accumulate useful experience while preserving and pruning skills across tasks, improving agent performance on complex tasks across diverse domains. We evaluate SELM on four agent benchmarks covering diverse tasks and execution environments, and SELM consistently outperforms in most settings.

**Comment:** This paper presents SELM, a framework for managing evolving skills in LLM agents, focusing on skill retrieval, updating, and lifecycle management. It relates to skill accumulation and reuse but does not specifically address RLHF, instruction-following fine-tuning, contamination detection, diffusion LMs, or evaluation paradigms.

## 204. [Where Are Language-Specific Features Represented in Multilingual LLMs? Everywhere](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.25505) <a id="link204"></a>

**Authors:** Mikaela Martins, Jonathan Dunn

**Abstract:** arXiv:2610.25505v1 Announce Type: new  Abstract: Previous work has argued that multilingual LLMs encode language-specific features most strongly in their initial and final layers, implying that intermediate layers support language-agnostic representations. This paper reconsiders this finding by performing four different experiments: three that undertake a fine-grained analysis of representations of Portuguese and Spanish, two closely related languages, and one large-scale language identification experiment that covers over 200 languages. The results of these experiments challenge the strict separation between language-specific and language-agnostic layers in multilingual LLMs, using Llama 3.2 1B as a test case. By analyzing and manipulating contextual sentence embeddings, we demonstrate that language identity remains strongly recoverable throughout the model rather than being restricted to initial and final layers. Moreover, we find that the model tends to classify ambiguous strings as closer to Spanish than to Portuguese, revealing a skew in model behavior that differs from human judgments.

**Comment:** This paper analyzes where language-specific features are represented in multilingual LLMs, challenging prior assumptions about layer specialization. ## 205. [LOOP: Recovery-Aligned Iterative Layer Pruning via On-Policy Distillation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.25679) <a id="link205"></a>
**ArXiv ID:** 2610.25679
**Authors:** Liang Xu, Shufan Shen, Zhongni Hou, Yufei Zhang, Wei Lin, Guojun Yin, Shuhui Wang

**Abstract:** arXiv:2610.25679v1 Announce Type: new  Abstract: Layer-level pruning is attractive for large language model (LLM) deployment as it reduces the model size while preserving a dense architecture that can be efficiently executed on standard hardware. Current methods typically follow a one-shot paradigm, which first removes selected layers to reach a target model size and then performs additional training to recover model capabilities. However, pruning directly to the target size can substantially degrade reasoning capabilities, making subsequent recovery more difficult under a limited training budget. Moreover, this paradigm separates layer selection from recovery, limiting the use of recovery information to guide selection and potentially hindering the recovery of reasoning capabilities. To address these limitations, we propose LOOP, a Layer-level Recovery-Oriented On-Policy Pruning framework that interleaves pruning and recovery across multiple shots. In each shot, LOOP removes a single layer and performs short on-policy distillation, mitigating the abrupt degradation in reasoning capabilities associated with one-shot pruning. To guide layer selection in subsequent shots, LOOP reuses on-policy trajectories and optimization signals from recovery to jointly assess each remaining layer's reasoning contribution and recovery sensitivity, thereby making layer selection recovery-aware. Evaluations on several benchmarks show that LOOP consistently outperforms one-shot baselines in average accuracy across different model scales and pruning ratios.

**Comment:** This paper proposes a new iterative layer pruning method with on-policy distillation to better preserve reasoning capabilities during pruning of LLMs. While it relates to improving reasoning and pruning, it does not specifically address RLHF, instruction-following fine-tuning, or any of the listed criteria such as contamination detection or evaluation paradigms.
**Relevance:** 3
**Novelty:** 5

## 206. [Prosodic Structure as a Critical Component in Phonemic Language Modelling at BabyLM Scale](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.24172) <a id="link206"></a>
**ArXiv ID:** 2610.24172
**Authors:** Bill K. S. Ku, Janet B. Pierrehumbert

**Abstract:** arXiv:2610.24172v1 Announce Type: new  Abstract: Human children learn language from continuous, prosodically structured speech long before they can read, yet almost all language models train on orthographic text. Building on previous phonemic BabyLM training (Goriely et al., 2024; arXiv:2410.22906), we ask whether supplying an estimate of the prosody infants exploit improves phonemic language modelling at BabyLM scale. We compare an orthographic baseline to three conditions forming a ladder of phonological richness. All conditions are trained as BERT MLMs at 10M and 100M words. Addressing the ecological validity of existing metrics in the small-data regime, we build a lexical decision task and two semantic organisation tasks. Our new lexical decision task offers improved insights into the distinction between wordlikeness, token familiarity, and lexicality as factors. Triphone phonotactic control and pseudo-log-likelihood scoring shows that these scores reflect phonotactic familiarity rather than wordhood: every model sits near or below chance on an adversarial inverted condition, revealing prior results of this kind as partly artefactual. On our new semantic tasks, a prosodically annotated phoneme stream gives the strongest taxonomic organisation at both the 10M and 100M word scales. Phonemic models with boundary structure are competitive with the orthographic baseline. These findings suggest that prosodic structure is a key component of phonemic language modelling in the small-data regime most analogous to infant learning.

**Comment:** No direct match to criteria. Studies phonemic language modeling with prosodic structure at BabyLM scale, focusing on small-data phonemic models and evaluation metrics. Does not address RLHF, instruction tuning, test contamination, diffusion LMs, or new evaluation paradigms for open-ended generation.

**Novelty:** 5

## 207. [A Cross-Domain Evaluation of Prompting Techniques for Large Language Models](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.24913) <a id="link207"></a>
**ArXiv ID:** 2610.24913
**Authors:** Kevin Krazius, Tim Schlippe, Paul Libbrecht

**Abstract:** arXiv:2610.24913v1 Announce Type: new  Abstract: Prompt engineering has become a central mechanism for guiding the behavior of Large Language Models (LLMs) across a wide range of reasoning and language understanding tasks [1], [2]. However, existing studies frequently evaluate prompting techniques using different LLMs, datasets, or task settings, making direct comparisons across methods and domains difficult [3]. Consequently, in this paper we present a controlled cross-domain evaluation of Zero-Shot prompting, Few-Shot prompting, Retrieval-Augmented Generation (RAG), and automatic prompt optimization based on a PE2-inspired optimization pipeline [4]-[6]. All experiments were conducted with the same LLM and evaluated on six benchmark datasets covering arithmetic reasoning, logical reasoning, commonsense reasoning, and factual verification. The results show that no prompting strategy consistently outperforms the others across all domains, and pairwise statistical comparisons of the highest-performing configurations revealed no significant differences. RAG achieves the highest overall average accuracy of 78.33% when 300 examples are available for retrieval, while Few-Shot Prompting achieves comparatively high accuracies across several structured reasoning tasks. Zero-Shot Prompting remains competitive on tasks with self-contained inputs, whereas the PE2-inspired optimization approach produces stable but not systematically superior results compared to the other evaluated prompting techniques. Furthermore, increasing the number of task-specific examples does not consistently improve performance across prompting techniques, indicating strong domain and task dependency. These findings suggest that prompting techniques should be selected adaptively based on task characteristics rather than treated as universally applicable optimization strategies.

**Comment:** Related to criterion 4 (new paradigms to evaluating open-ended text generation) since it evaluates prompting techniques across domains with controlled comparisons, but it does not fundamentally rethink evaluation paradigms or address subjectivity/adversaries. More of an empirical benchmarking study.
**Relevance:** 4
**Novelty:** 4

## 208. [A Simple and Unified Method for Gradient-Variation Regret](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.26473) <a id="link208"></a>
**ArXiv ID:** 2610.26473
**Authors:** Hang Yu, Peng Zhao

**Abstract:** arXiv:2610.26473v1 Announce Type: new  Abstract: Gradient-variation online learning has attracted increasing attention for its connections to game theory and optimization. It has been studied in both non-stationary online learning, which aims to minimize dynamic regret against time-varying comparator sequences, and universal online learning, which seeks to adapt to unknown loss curvature. Prior work obtained gradient-variation bounds using different techniques tailored to each setting. We propose a simple and unified method based on surrogate losses with a quadratic penalty on decision movement, yielding gradient-variation bounds in both settings. For non-stationary online learning, the method induces an optimistic-to-gradient-variation reduction, enabling a broad class of algorithms to match the rate established by Zhao et al. (2024). For universal online learning, it yields a simple algorithm that matches the optimal universal regret bounds of Yan et al. (2024) while relaxing the extended smoothness assumption. Finally, we apply our method to online games and improve on existing guarantees.

**Comment:** No direct match to any specific criterion. The paper proposes a unified method for gradient-variation regret bounds in online learning and games, which is relevant to optimization theory but does not explicitly address RLHF, language models, or interpretability.

## 209. [First-Order LoRA Training with Phone NPUs](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.24816) <a id="link209"></a>
**ArXiv ID:** 2610.24816
**Authors:** Xu Zheng, Wei Cheng, Zhuomin Chen, Dongkuan Xu, Mo Sha, Jingchao Ni, Dongsheng Luo

**Abstract:** arXiv:2610.24816v1 Announce Type: new  Abstract: On-device fine-tuning lets a large language model (LLM) learn from its user's private data without uploading it. Low-rank adaptation (LoRA) reduces the trainable parameters, but each first-order training step still multiplies by the frozen base-model weights during the forward pass, activation recomputation, and input-gradient computation. A phone's neural processing unit (NPU) already holds these weights for inference; using it for training also requires computing input gradients, which its inference interface does not support. We present a system that runs all three frozen-weight products on the NPU while keeping the training graph, adapters, and optimizer on the CPU. Its input-gradient kernels reuse the resident weight tiles and the forward dequantizer, avoiding a transposed weight copy and ensuring forward and backward use the same effective weights. To preserve usable gradients, the system selects precision by gradient sensitivity and applies a per-row range guard for half-precision (FP16) outputs. Its runtime fuses the remaining CPU operators and overlaps NPU execution with adapter computation, while resumable execution and thermal pacing let training proceed as a background job. We evaluate it on two Qualcomm Snapdragon platforms using four LLMs from the Qwen and Llama families. Our workloads cover nightly personalization with labels supplied on the phone and the public LaMP-2 benchmark; comparisons include an optimized CPU trainer, a phone-GPU trainer, forward-only training, and GPU-trained references.

**Comment:** No direct match to any criterion. The paper focuses on efficient on-device LoRA fine-tuning using phone NPUs, which is a system/hardware optimization rather than a new method for RLHF, instruction tuning, or interpretability.
**Relevance:** 3
**Novelty:** 5

## 210. [CXBench: A Benchmark for Process-Compliant LLM Agents in Regulated Customer-Experience Workflows](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.25954) <a id="link210"></a>
**ArXiv ID:** 2610.25954
**Authors:** Boxi Yu, Yuzhong Zhang, Zhenfeng Su, Linfu Xi, Yinghan Yao, Shitao Chen, Yang Cao, Liting Lin, Lionel Briand, Emir Mu\~noz

**Abstract:** arXiv:2610.25954v1 Announce Type: new  Abstract: Tool-using language agents increasingly perform regulated customer-service workflows, where a correct final state is insufficient: agents must also provide disclosures, obtain consent, and follow required procedures. Existing tool-agent benchmarks largely score final business states, accepting trajectories that reach correct outcomes through non-compliant processes.   We introduce cxbench, a benchmark that evaluates outcome and process compliance separately across six regulated domains. Each task combines a customer request, a tool-based environment, and explicit process requirements. Rule cards link cited regulatory obligations to observable trace evidence. A hybrid grader combines deterministic checks for ordering and authority with an LLM judge for semantic requirements. We define a false-pass as an outcome-pass run that fails process checks.   Across seven models and 120 tasks, 55.5% of the 840 retained executions are false-passes, representing 80.1% of outcome-passing runs; only 13.8% pass both checks. Process-inclusive evaluation changes the observed model ordering and exposes differences hidden by outcome-only scoring. cxbench makes these failures inspectable through trace-linked criteria.

**Comment:** No direct match to any criterion. The paper introduces a benchmark for process compliance in tool-using LLM agents, focusing on evaluation of regulated workflows rather than new evaluation paradigms for open-ended generation or instruction-following improvements.
**Relevance:** 3
**Novelty:** 5

## 211. [An important Bottleneck in LLM Optimizers: Principle-Level Local Optima](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.24291) <a id="link211"></a>
**ArXiv ID:** 2610.24291
**Authors:** Nian Ran, Tianhe Jia, Zhengyang Mu, Yue Wang, Sebastian Brulin, Markus Olhofer, Mauricio A. Alvarez, Yang Liu, Richard Allmendinger

**Abstract:** arXiv:2610.24291v1 Announce Type: new  Abstract: Feedback-driven LLM optimizers are increasingly used for evolutionary search, Bayesian optimization, heuristic design, and scientific discovery. Yet rapid initial gains often give way to prolonged plateaus despite substantial remaining evaluation budgets. We investigate \emph{principle-level search collapse}, where an optimizer's search becomes confined to a narrow set of design principles, even though it may continue to generate distinct candidates and achieve small improvements. We assemble a diagnostic benchmark spanning molecular optimization, network sentinel-node selection, and GPU-kernel optimization, and introduce a decision-tree protocol combining principle narrowing, prolonged performance plateaus, and gaps to attainable reference solutions. Across 161 assessable runs on the three problem domains, 78.3\% receive support for collapse, including 55.3\% with strong support. Candidate-level variation can nevertheless conceal restricted search at the principle level. We then test \emph{diversity injection}, a common response to search stagnation, such as higher t, multiple LLMs, and web search, and show that it provides limited and inconsistent benefits: among 65 molecular runs, still 73.8\% and 53.8\% receive support and strong support respectively. We finally test reference-principle interventions and find that they improve some runs, but their benefits vary across models and tasks: access to a better principle does not guarantee its effective use. Our benchmark and diagnostic protocol provide a basis for evaluating future optimizers on principle-level collapse. Our findings highlight the need for online collapse detection and better mechanisms for generating and maintaining alternative principles during optimization.

**Comment:** No direct match to any specific criterion. The paper studies optimization behavior of LLM optimizers and search collapse, which is related to optimization dynamics but not to RLHF, instruction tuning, contamination, diffusion LMs, or interpretability.
**Relevance:** 3
**Novelty:** 5

## 212. [Bridging Exploration and Persona: Information-Directed Dueling for Test-Time Agent Personalization](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.24385) <a id="link212"></a>
**ArXiv ID:** 2610.24385
**Authors:** Jie Mao, Pingchen Lu, Xiangyi Wang, Hengquan Huang, Shuang Qiu, Min Zhang, Zhongxiang Dai

**Abstract:** arXiv:2610.24385v1 Announce Type: new  Abstract: Effective test-time personalization requires LLM agents to adapt to query-dependent user preferences from lightweight interaction. We study persona selection with binary comparisons between candidate responses as the online signal. We present ID-TAP, a scalable, IDS-inspired neural dueling-bandit framework for large discrete persona spaces. ID-TAP combines a query-conditioned neural utility model with a lightweight Bradley-Terry explorer, and transfers comparison evidence across related queries through similarity-weighted warm starts. The neural model shares statistical strength across queries while preserving query-specific scores, and efficient BT updates score hypothetical challengers. Across seven user-conditioned generation benchmarks under reference- and model-based preference simulators, ID-TAP achieves the strongest aggregate performance among the evaluated baselines within a fixed ten-round comparison budget. These results support information-directed challenger selection as an effective design for test-time persona adaptation.

**Comment:** Presents a neural dueling-bandit framework for test-time persona adaptation, which is an application of online learning and personalization but does not match criteria on RLHF, contamination, diffusion, or evaluation paradigms.

## 213. [Assertion and Belief: Moore's Paradox in Language Models](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.25586) <a id="link213"></a>
**ArXiv ID:** 2610.25586
**Authors:** Anshun Asher Zheng, Weili Cao, Fangcong Yin, David Beaver, Junyi Jessy Li

**Abstract:** arXiv:2610.25586v1 Announce Type: new  Abstract: Moore's paradox \citep{moore1942} concerns sentences such as ``It is raining, but I don't believe that it is,'' which may well be true yet cannot be sensibly asserted. The absurdity lies not in the content but in the act, since to assert is to vouch for one's own belief. This makes the paradox a test of whether language models take the speech act into account when computing meaning. We present the first test of Moore's paradox in LLMs, evaluating 12 models on a curated set of Moorean sentences in varied contexts. We find that models largely recognize the infelicity of Moorean sentences, and that the judgement is not tied to a memorized form and tracks the speech act. Using the Rational Speech Act framework (RSA; \citealt{goodmanfrank2016}), we then test whether these judgements arise from a listener reasoning about a speaker. We find the models' own listeners do not reason about their own speakers, and their reasoning traces suggest that they instead recall knowledge about the paradox and apply rules therein. The models thus appear to have learned the paradox as a fact about sentences, not as a requirement on speakers.

**Comment:** Related to language model understanding of Moore's paradox and speech acts, but does not match criteria on RLHF, contamination, diffusion, evaluation paradigms, or interpretability.
**Relevance:** 3
**Novelty:** 5

## 214. [From Trace to Fix: Residual Task State in Long-Horizon Agents](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.25776) <a id="link214"></a>
**ArXiv ID:** 2610.25776
**Authors:** Zile Huang, Ser-nam Lim

**Abstract:** arXiv:2610.25776v1 Announce Type: new  Abstract: Recovering from failures in long-horizon, stateful language agents is challenging because the action introducing a defect, the symptom exposing it, and the persistent state requiring repair frequently diverge. Existing methods largely focus on retrospective failure attribution, leaving sequential diagnosis and state repair poorly integrated under constrained budgets. We introduce TRACE-FIX, a unified recovery framework centered on versioned residual task state. Trace transforms execution logs into a structured dependency-linked ledger, prioritizing unresolved task requirements rather than salient surface errors. Fix formulates recovery as budgeted planning over admissible actions, incorporating outcome forecasting and explicit repair-reserve constraints to prevent premature budget exhaustion. To rigorously decouple localization accuracy from downstream repair capability, we present LongH-Debug, evaluating root-cause attribution, verified-fault recovery, and end-to-end closed-loop execution independently. Our empirical analysis demonstrates that structured residual tracking substantially improves causal localization over raw-trace baselines, while budget-aware sequential planning converts verified fault signals into more reliable task recovery without destructive trial-and-error. Code is available at https://github.com/toffeecat/tracedp.

**Comment:** Related to long-horizon agent recovery and debugging, but does not match any specific criteria related to RLHF, contamination, diffusion LMs, evaluation paradigms, or interpretability.
**Relevance:** 3

## 215. [HoloCosm: Beyond Single-Metric Benchmark Compression Across Multiple Metrics](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.26714) <a id="link215"></a>
**ArXiv ID:** 2610.26714
**Authors:** Yupei Lin, Zhongzhan Huang, Junxin Li, Guoming Ling, Shanshan Zhong, Hefeng Wu, Yukai Shi, Liang Lin

**Abstract:** arXiv:2610.26714v1 Announce Type: new  Abstract: Benchmark compression reduces evaluation cost by selecting a small set of samples. Existing methods typically guide selection using a single metric, although benchmarks often assess models along multiple dimensions. We find that model rankings are often weakly correlated across metrics, indicating that different metrics capture distinct aspects of model performance. Samples selected for one metric do not reliably preserve scores and rankings on others when used for compressed evaluation. At 80\% compression, each of the five single-metric baselines yields higher reconstruction error than equal-size random sampling in over 70\% of cross-metric comparisons. To address this challenge, we present HoloCosm, which jointly minimizes normalized score-reconstruction error across source models and metrics while constraining ranking distortion. We evaluate HoloCosm across multiple compression rates on 15 language, multimodal, VLA, and agent benchmark configurations with 85 component metrics. With 80\% compression, HoloCosm outperforms Random, the strongest baseline overall: average NMAE drops by 35.3\% to 0.236, worst-component NMAE drops by 39.5\% to 0.330, and average Spearman correlation rises from 0.884 to 0.915. In addition, HoloCosm remains competitive with existing baselines in single-metric evaluation. We will release our code and experimental results.

**Comment:** Related to evaluation methodology but does not fundamentally rethink open-ended text generation evaluation; focuses on benchmark compression across multiple metrics, not new paradigms for open-ended evaluation (criterion 4).

## 216. [Latent State Utilization in Probabilistic Circuits](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.24315) <a id="link216"></a>
**ArXiv ID:** 2610.24315
**Authors:** Zilei Shao, Anji Liu

**Abstract:** arXiv:2610.24315v1 Announce Type: new  Abstract: Probabilistic circuits (PCs) are generative models that support efficient probabilistic inference. Adding latent states to a PC increases its representational capacity, but yields diminishing gains in likelihood. We use mutual information to examine how the dependence represented by a trained PC changes with latent size. Experiments with Hidden Chow-Liu Trees (HCLTs) on ImageNet32 show that learned dependence grows slowly and remains well below both the latent-state upper bound and estimates of dependence in the data. To understand how learning uses these states, we examine how strongly each sample favors particular latent states and how broadly states are used across the dataset. Tracking these measurements during HCLT training distinguishes rapid early posterior concentration from the more gradual stabilization of sample-dependent state preferences. Early measurements also guide initialization selection in HCLTs and hidden Markov models (HMMs), as well as tree selection in HCLTs. The resulting choices improve the ultimate performance across the tested latent sizes, providing a practical benefit from our study of latent-state utilization.

**Comment:** Related to generative modeling and probabilistic circuits but does not match any specific criterion; focuses on latent state utilization and mutual information analysis in probabilistic circuits, which is relevant to statistical machine learning but not to RLHF, contamination, diffusion LMs, or interpretability of neural LMs.

## 217. [A Simple and General Approach to top-$k$ Selection](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.26407) <a id="link217"></a>
**ArXiv ID:** 2610.26407
**Authors:** Motti Goldberger, Nils Rudi

**Abstract:** arXiv:2610.26407v1 Announce Type: new  Abstract: This paper considers the Bayesian top-$k$ identification problem, wherein a decision maker is tasked with selecting $k$ items from a pool of items of unknown quality. To assess the items, the decision maker adaptively allocates a fixed sampling budget, where each sample of an item yields a noisy observation of its quality, with the objective of minimizing the expected opportunity cost of the selected set. We propose an approach that allocates each batch of samples by minimizing a surrogate objective derived from an upper bound on the expected opportunity cost. The approach is general: samples can be allocated in batches, the selection size $k$ is arbitrary, and it applies to many sampling distributions. It is also simple and intuitive. We prove that the method's posterior expected opportunity cost decays at an exponential rate of at least half the best rate achievable by any sampling rule. In simulations across a wide range of settings, the method is competitive with, and often improves upon, the best existing algorithm in each setting.

**Comment:** No direct match to any specific criterion. The paper proposes a Bayesian top-k selection method with theoretical guarantees and broad applicability, but it is not focused on language models, RLHF, contamination, diffusion models, or interpretability.

**Novelty:** 5

## 218. [TIRE: Top-Down Formula Instantiation with Reliable Execution for Physics Problem Synthesis](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.25134) <a id="link218"></a>
**ArXiv ID:** 2610.25134
**Authors:** Xiaoyu Wu, Zhaoxin Yu, Xinglin Xiao, Nan Xu

**Abstract:** arXiv:2610.25134v1 Announce Type: new  Abstract: Advancing physics reasoning in large language models requires scalable training data that connect physical laws, concrete problem scenarios, and verifiable numerical answers. Expert-designed problems offer top-down control over physical laws, conditions, and scenarios, but require costly human effort. LLM-based synthesis from texts scales efficiently, but model-generated answers and assessments leave supervision vulnerable to hallucinations. We introduce TIRE, a framework for Top-Down Formula Instantiation with Reliable Execution that brings knowledge-driven problem construction to large-scale data generation. Top-down instantiation uses reusable physical formulas and their applicability conditions to control the physical relations and numerical configurations of generated problems. Reliable execution establishes reference answers before problem generation, providing a common numerical foundation for problem construction and teacher distillation. Together, these mechanisms combine control over problem content with execution-grounded supervision for both supervised fine-tuning and reinforcement learning. Using TIRE, we construct TIRE-19k, spanning nine subfields of physics. Across 3 backbone LLMs and 4 benchmarks, TIRE-19k outperforms 5 representative datasets with equal amounts of SFT data, exceeding the strongest baseline by 2.86 percentage points on average. Further GRPO raises Qwen3-8B's average accuracy by 1.52 percentage points over its TIRE-19k SFT checkpoint. These results demonstrate the value of generating physics training data from explicit physical knowledge with reliable execution.

**Comment:** This paper proposes a new method combining top-down formula instantiation with reliable execution for generating physics problem data, used for supervised fine-tuning and reinforcement learning. It relates to instruction-following improvements via data generation and RL fine-tuning, but does not explicitly mention RLHF or instruction-tuning methods for language models in general.
**Relevance:** 3
**Novelty:** 5

## 219. [Variance-Aware Non-Stationary Bandits:All-Budget Regret Bounds and Adaptation Costs](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.26116) <a id="link219"></a>
**ArXiv ID:** 2610.26116
**Authors:** Xiaojin Zhang

**Abstract:** arXiv:2610.26116v1 Announce Type: new  Abstract: Low reward variance reduces estimation cost, but does not eliminate monitoring of arms that may improve while unobserved. We refine the variance--variation tradeoff and characterize how both costs depend on supplied variance and variation budgets in bounded, non-stationary multi-armed bandits. Let $A$ be the number of arms, $T$ the horizon, $B$ a bound on consecutive mean variation excluding the initial vector, and $Q$ a bound on the sum of the largest per-round reward variances. For every admissible pair $Q,B$, the minimax expected dynamic regret is, within a single logarithmic factor, \[   \min\{T,\;A+\sqrt{AQ}+(AQBT)^{1/3}   +\sqrt{AT}\min\{B,\sqrt B\}\}. \] The deterministic boundary $Q=0$ is characterized without logarithms. For small positive budgets, a safe calibration phase identifies a common interval containing all future means of surviving arms. Clipping observations to a wider interval reduces the cost of restarting a prior hybrid static-regret algorithm; an original-mean analytical reference controls its residuals without charging the variation of clipped means. Balancing clipping bias and monitoring closes the small-budget noisy regime. Fixed oblivious constructions give the matching lower bound. We also quantify adaptation costs: on a disclosed deterministic one-improvement family, a stationary-regret budget has a matching worst-case change-regret frontier, including a finite improvement pulse. This implies an $\Omega((T/A)^{1/4})$ loss when one policy competes with all supplied-budget deterministic oracle rates. The budget-free low-noise scheduling problem remains separate.

**Comment:** This paper studies variance-aware regret bounds and adaptation costs in non-stationary bandits, a statistical learning topic but unrelated to language models, RLHF, instruction-following, test contamination, or diffusion LMs. It is relevant to statistical ML but not to the specific criteria.
**Relevance:** 3
**Novelty:** 5

## 220. [On Learning Expand-and-Sparsify Representations](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.26470) <a id="link220"></a>
**ArXiv ID:** 2610.26470
**Authors:** Alireza Asghari, Parikshit Ram, Kaushik Sinha

**Abstract:** arXiv:2610.26470v1 Announce Type: new  Abstract: Expand-and-sparsify ($\texttt{EaS}$) representations lift an input to a much higher dimension through a linear map and keep only the $k$ largest coordinates. Existing theory builds that map at random and needs the expanded dimension to grow exponentially in the input dimension, which puts the construction out of practical reach. We ask what is gained by learning the map by gradient descent instead, and how that gain depends on how wide the expansion is. We sweep $73$ OpenML classification tasks and $48$ regression tasks under a protocol that holds the datasets, expanded dimensions, sparsity levels and splits fixed across every method, and we summarise each comparison by a median over datasets and a Wilcoxon signed rank test. We report four findings. First, learning the expansion helps most when the expansion is narrowest, and its advantage over a random map decays to nothing by the widest expansions we run, so a learned $\texttt{EaS}$ layer can be made small. Second, with the map learned, a top-$k$ representation that keeps at most a tenth of its coordinates is indistinguishable from a dense $\texttt{ReLU}$ layer of the same expanded dimension at every expansion factor we test, while GELU and SiLU are not. Third, when the representation is forced to be binary the sparse code is the better of the two, and the reason is that binarization costs it nothing while it costs the dense layer accuracy. Fourth, how sparse the representation can be is governed by how wide it is: the sparsity level that is free rises as the expansion grows, so there is no fixed limit on how sparse it can be. The regression tasks reproduce the first finding and qualify the second and third.

**Comment:** No match to any specific criteria. The paper studies expand-and-sparsify representations and their learning, which is a general representation learning topic but does not explicitly relate to sparse autoencoders for interpretability or language models or RLHF or test contamination.
**Relevance:** 3
**Novelty:** 5

## 221. [Training with Ciphers to Enhance In-Context Terminology Learning For Translation from Rare Languages](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.24593) <a id="link221"></a>
**ArXiv ID:** 2610.24593
**Authors:** Deevyanshu Malu, Deeptanshu Malu, Sunita Sarawagi

**Abstract:** arXiv:2610.24593v1 Announce Type: new  Abstract: Fine-tuning LLMs with in-context examples (IC-Train) lets translation models adapt to new parallel data at deployment, but it is unclear whether they actually learn the translations of rare terms from those examples. We formalize this capability as in-context term learning (ICTL). We show that standard IC-Train develops this capability only weakly, and propose RareCipherFT, which strengthens it without any lexical resources. RareCipherFT pairs each training target with in-context examples that share a rare term with it. It then replaces that term with a character-shuffled cipher in both the context and the reference, so the term can only be recovered by copying from context. The ciphered fraction of training data is annealed to preserve translation quality. Across seven translation directions between English and Tamil, Assamese, Mizo, Nepali and Hausa, and four models from 1B to 7B parameters, RareCipherFT raises term recall over standard IC-Train by 5-15 absolute points without lowering COMET. Probes using synonyms, nonce words and controls for shared surface context show that these gains come from linking source terms to their in-context translations rather than from shortcuts.

**Comment:** No direct match to criteria; focuses on improving in-context terminology learning for translation from rare languages via cipher-based fine-tuning, not about RLHF, diffusion LMs, or contamination/membership inference.

## 222. [SpecMRL: Redesign Matryoshka Representation Learning within Parametric Spectrum](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.26824) <a id="link222"></a>

**Authors:** Zhongxin Chen, Songhao Wu, Heng Cui, Cong Li, Xiao Zhou, Rui Yan

**Abstract:** arXiv:2610.26824v1 Announce Type: new  Abstract: Matryoshka Representation Learning (MRL) is designed to support flexible representation sizes by jointly optimizing nested prefixes. While effective, this design lacks an explicit constraint to ensure that the leading portion provides a suitable compact encoding. To address this, we propose SpecMRL, which reorganizes the feature space to enable more effective prefix readout. Specifically, we extend MRL with a parameter-derived spectral basis organized with feature importance. SpecMRL otherwise follows standard MRL, with prefix readout performed in the learned spectral space. Experiments on MTEB show that SpecMRL outperforms MRL at all evaluated reduced dimensions, with larger gains under more aggressive compression. These results suggest that the learned spectral basis better prioritizes informative directions, enabling more effective representation compression. Notably, SpecMRL extends the performance frontier of MRL, surpassing full-dimensional MRL with only half the embedding dimensions.

**Comment:** No direct match to any criterion. The paper proposes a spectral basis for Matryoshka Representation Learning to improve representation compression, but does not address instruction-following, contamination, diffusion LMs, or evaluation paradigms.

## 223. [What Can't Jev Decide? Probing the Limits of One-Pass Decision Models](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.24929) <a id="link223"></a>
**ArXiv ID:** 2610.24929
**Authors:** Wang Yang, Xinpeng Li, Yanyan Zhang, Chuang Ma, Shouren Wang, Jianzhe Wei, Liqin Ye, Xiaoran Cai, Mohsen Hariri, Vipin Chaudhary, Xiaotian Han

**Abstract:** arXiv:2610.24929v1 Announce Type: new  Abstract: Jev and its open replicas choose among the options a caller supplies with a single forward pass, fast enough to decide inside an agent loop. We ask what such a model cannot decide, and find a simple limit: Jev cannot make a decision that an LLM makes only by writing out intermediate steps. On a benchmark that varies one factor at a time, Jev finds and selects records without error up to 20k tokens and 64 options, but once the answer depends on two or more updates of one user, accuracy falls, to 53-80% at sixteen updates, because similar records interfere. Given a stale summary of the state, Jev follows it. On real decision items graded by how many thinking tokens a reference LLM needs, Jev answers 97.5% of the items that one forward pass of that LLM solves, but only 74% of the others. Jev also does not know its limit: its confidence flags the errors it makes itself but not those it inherits from a stale input, and neither an explicit nor a trained hand-off option catches the latter. The open models we evaluate share these limits. Checking the state such models rely on requires a model that reasons.

**Comment:** No direct match to any specific criterion. The paper studies limits of one-pass decision models compared to LLMs with intermediate reasoning steps, but does not focus on instruction-following fine-tuning, contamination, diffusion LMs, or evaluation paradigms.
**Relevance:** 3
**Novelty:** 5

## 224. [What "Form'' of Uncertainty Does Conformal Prediction Quantify?](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.24510) <a id="link224"></a>
**ArXiv ID:** 2610.24510
**Authors:** Soroush H. Zargarbashi, Mohammad Sadegh Akhondzadeh, Rebekka Burkholz, Aleksandar Bojchevski

**Abstract:** arXiv:2610.24510v1 Announce Type: new  Abstract: Conformal prediction (CP) is a post-hoc calibration method that returns prediction sets with distribution-free coverage guarantees, and the size of the output set is often interpreted as a notion of uncertainty. We show that the difference in set size between full and split CP is directly connected to Bayesian posterior variance, which is the epistemic uncertainty of the model. Essentially, this difference in set size is an aggregation of two opposite effects: (1) better model accuracy and therefore smaller set size achieved by training over extra calibration points, and (2) the "formability" of the model at test point towards every possible label. The latter effect vanishes with stronger models or more calibration data (as epistemic uncertainty is known for that behavior). It turns out that the model's formability itself (without the quantization effect of prediction sets, and through estimating the posterior variance) is a useful tool to quantify the epistemic uncertainty. In our empirical evaluation it performs on par with the state of the art in many setups.

**Comment:** Matches criterion 2: The paper studies conformal prediction uncertainty quantification related to Bayesian posterior variance, which is a statistical method relevant to uncertainty and potentially contamination detection, but does not explicitly address test set contamination or membership inference for language models.
**Relevance:** 3
**Novelty:** 5

---

## 225. [HABIT-Bench: Evaluating Habit Induction from Longitudinal Weak Evidence in Agent Memory](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.24520) <a id="link225"></a>
**ArXiv ID:** 2610.24520
**Authors:** Junming Zhang, Rubin Wei, Xu Cheng, Hao Sun, Ruoxi Cheng, Xiquan Wang, Peisong Wu, Mingsong Chen, Zhouhan Lin

**Abstract:** arXiv:2610.24520v1 Announce Type: new  Abstract: In long-term interactions with large language model (LLM) agents, users reveal habits through their choices and actions. These habits capture implicit user information that agents must infer across conversations. However, individual observations may provide only partial evidence about a habit or when it applies. Retaining past interactions alone does not ensure that agents can infer habits and apply them appropriately. We introduce HABIT-Bench to evaluate how well agent memory systems infer habits from weak evidence and apply them to later requests. We construct synthetic user histories from task-oriented dialogue data and controlled habit graphs. The histories span hundreds of sessions across food, finance, software, and travel. Four-choice tasks assess agents' ability to infer user habits and apply them appropriately. They include temporary exceptions, changes in habits, and suggestions that users have not accepted. Sampled tasks undergo independent human review. We evaluate eight memory methods with Qwen3-235B-A22B and GLM-5.3-Flash. The best-performing memory method achieves 33.61\% macro accuracy across the four domains. Performance remains limited even when annotated evidence is supplied directly. These results expose a persistent gap between access to past interactions and the ability to infer and appropriately use user habits.

**Comment:** This paper evaluates agent memory systems for habit induction from longitudinal weak evidence. It focuses on agent memory and user habit inference rather than instruction-following fine-tuning or contamination detection. No direct match to criteria.
**Relevance:** 3
**Novelty:** 5

## 226. [System X: An LLM Decision Model That Replies from Its Own KV Cache](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.25456) <a id="link226"></a>
**ArXiv ID:** 2610.25456
**Authors:** Syed Usama Khalid Bukhari, Syed Safwan Khalid, Muhammad Aamir

**Abstract:** arXiv:2610.25456v1 Announce Type: new  Abstract: Decision models return probabilities over supplied options, but a deployed agent may also need to reply in words. The present study introduces System X, a large language model (LLM) decision model that adds a text-reply option to the LLM-as-Jev readout. When this option wins, or confidence is low, the same model continues from the decision's key-value (KV) cache, avoiding a repeated prompt prefill. Qwen3-0.6B has been fine-tuned with LoRA on Reason-9k and Bitext reply rows. JevBench accuracy shows no statistically significant change (55.4% to 54.5%, p=0.87), while expected calibration error falls from 0.292 to 0.169. Held-out Bitext routing accuracy reaches 98.3%. Cache reuse saves 15-37% of latency on an Apple M4 Pro, with token-identical replies in the FP32 demonstration cases. Scoring options on copies of the prompt cache matches full-sequence scoring to within 1.4e-6 in probability. Adding dialogue, industry and complaint data (System X+) improves support-task results but lowers IFEval accuracy from 55.5% to 45.0%. System X v4 uses Qwen3-1.7B, rebalanced data and teacher-rewritten reply targets. Against its base LLM, JevBench accuracy rises from 55.0% to 61.0% (p=0.04), with no statistically significant IFEval change (p=0.71). A matched latency test on unadapted Qwen3-1.7B saves 17-39%. One LLM judge prefers v4 replies to an unadapted 1.7B second call (31 to 11 pairs, p=0.003); the other's preference is not significant. These reply comparisons also differ in prompts and generation limits. State conflicts and unsupported reply details remain failure modes. Further studies with several seeds, human raters and real support data are recommended.

**Comment:** Related to improving LLM decision models and latency via KV cache reuse, but does not match criteria on RLHF, contamination, diffusion LMs, or interpretability.
**Relevance:** 3
**Novelty:** 5

## 227. [Agents' Last Doubt: Knowing When to Act and When to Step Back in Tool-Using Agents](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.24194) <a id="link227"></a>
**ArXiv ID:** 2610.24194
**Authors:** Qisai Liu, Qingyue Jiao, Bangwei Guo, Wenxi Dai, Changyi Yang, Jiahe Liu, Dimitris N. Metaxas, Yiyu Shi, Zhanhong Jiang, Minghao Guo

**Abstract:** arXiv:2610.24194v1 Announce Type: new  Abstract: Tool-using LLM agents repeatedly face decision points under uncertainty, choosing between autonomous action, additional information gathering, and human escalation. These decisions require balancing task success against interaction cost and the risk of irreversible errors. Learning to make these trade-offs is challenging because existing interaction trajectories record only the outcomes of actions taken, leaving the consequences of alternative actions unobserved. To address this gap, we construct a counterfactual dataset containing 3,115 action outcomes from 1,036 decision points in ToolSandbox. At each decision point, we execute each legal action and evaluate its downstream outcomes based on task completion, interaction cost, and irreversible errors. Using this dataset, we train SURE-Act, a controller that selects actions for a frozen backbone agent at inference time. Our experiments show that learning to rank alternative actions improves offline action-selection utility by 0.0309 over a regression-only baseline. We further show that adding SURE-Act enables end-to-end gains of up to 0.0575 in ToolSandbox's Overall Score with the Qwen3-4B agent. Moreover, SURE-Act outperforms adapted decision rules from SAGE-Agent and Calibrate-Then-Act by 0.1506 and 0.1438, respectively. This work establishes the first counterfactual dataset for ToolSandbox and provides actionable insights into decision-making under uncertainty for tool-using agents.

**Comment:** Does not match any specific criteria; focuses on decision-making in tool-using agents with counterfactual datasets, not on RLHF, contamination, diffusion LMs, or interpretability.
**Relevance:** 3
**Novelty:** 5

## 228. [TokenGate: Adapting LLMs for Fast and Memory-Efficient Prefill with Learned Token Pruning](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.26754) <a id="link228"></a>
**ArXiv ID:** 2610.26754
**Authors:** Liana Patel, Rishabh Ranjan, Ion Stoica, Matei Zaharia, Carlos Guestrin

**Abstract:** arXiv:2610.26754v1 Announce Type: new  Abstract: Expert human readers process large inputs (e.g., code repositories and long documents) by skimming to closely read only what matters. On the other hand, language models process all input tokens through each model layer, resulting in significant processing costs for long-context inputs. We introduce TokenGate, a small, drop-in component that extends existing trained models to enable end-to-end learnable token pruning over long-context inputs for fast and memory-efficient prefill. To enable end-to-end learning despite the non-differentiable top-K operator for token pruning, we introduce token-gated attention, a mechanism inspired by Mixture-of-Experts that re-weights attention logits using token importance scores. Across diverse tasks, TokenGate achieves $1.4$-$11.3\times$ faster time-to-first token (TTFT) and $1.5$-$3.4\times$ lower peak memory at no accuracy degradation for prefill in transformers. Compared to the best-performing prior methods for token-pruning, TokenGate gives up to $6.4\times$ faster prefill and $2\times$ lower peak memory. TokenGate natively extends both standard transformers (e.g., Qwen3) and hybrid-attention models (e.g., Qwen3.5) with simple lightweight adaptation comparable to standard parameter-efficient fine-tuning. We make our code available at: https://github.com/tokengate-models/TokenGate.

**Comment:** No direct match to any specific criterion. The paper proposes a token pruning method for efficient prefill in transformers, which is a model efficiency improvement but does not relate to RLHF, instruction-following fine-tuning, test contamination, diffusion LMs, evaluation paradigms, or interpretability.

**Novelty:** 5
