## 229. [Towards Human-AI Complementarity for AI Safety](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.24969) <a id="link229"></a>
**ArXiv ID:** 2610.24969
**Authors:** Sander Lanbo Shi, Miranda Anna Christ, Eric Modesitt, Andrew Louis, Konrad Cheng, Brandon Thai Tran, Sewoong Lee, Ratnakar Pawar, Gaia Molinaro, Juliana Bain, Nandika Donthi, Maja Pavlovic, Annya Dahmani, Francesca Bianco, Abhishek Shah, Tyler Crosse, Arth Singh, Karissa Huang, Sukanya Krishna, Konstantinos Voudouris, David Demitri Africa, Joshua Jacob, Rishub Jain

**Abstract:** arXiv:2610.24969v1 Announce Type: new  Abstract: Safely overseeing AI is increasingly challenging for the humans doing it, as they must evaluate longer and more complex outputs with every new model generation. To mitigate this challenge, we could leverage complementarity: where humans and models make different mistakes, combining their judgements could yield an oversight signal stronger than either produces alone. We study this opportunity in realistic, safety-relevant settings, contributing the largest known suite of such tasks (3,957 instances across 8 datasets and 6 domains) as well as a platform and data pipeline for measuring human, AI, and human-AI performance on matched items. We collected 35,613 judgements from 416 human raters, finding a considerable opportunity for a complementarity effect. We also found that with the assistance methods we tested, human raters corrected the AI's errors less often, even when overall human accuracy rose, and the hybridisation methods we tested added little over the better single source on average. While complementarity is an important and feasible engineering target for safe oversight, achieving it remains an open challenge for future work.

**Comment:** No direct match to any specific criterion. The paper studies human-AI complementarity for AI safety oversight, collecting large-scale human and AI judgments, but does not focus on RLHF, contamination, diffusion LMs, or mechanistic interpretability.
**Relevance:** 3
**Novelty:** 5

## 230. [Think, Compress, Answer: Reinforcement Learning for Multimodal Grounding via Reasoning Compression](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.25640) <a id="link230"></a>
**ArXiv ID:** 2610.25640
**Authors:** Jinbae Seo, Hyeongjun Kwon, Jiyoung Lee, Hyunsung Jang, Namkoo Ha, Jungin Park, Kwanghoon Sohn

**Abstract:** arXiv:2610.25640v1 Announce Type: new  Abstract: Extended reasoning has substantially improved multimodal large language models (MLLMs) on tasks requiring compositional and relational inference. However, multimodal grounding imposes a distinct requirement: reasoning must ultimately converge on a target-specific compact representation that supports precise localization. As reasoning trajectories grow longer, they often expand into broad scene narratives and peripheral relational details, which can dilute grounded perceptual evidence and lead to ambiguous or incorrect predictions. Motivated by the information bottleneck principle, we propose Think-Compress-Answer (TCA), a reinforcement learning framework that decomposes multimodal grounding into three phases: free-form reasoning, target-aware context compression, and answer generation conditioned on the compressed context. TCA is optimized through a multi-stage Group Relative Policy Optimization (GRPO) with progressive rewards that establish the structured output protocol, minimize reasoning-specific information while preserving target-relevant content, and directly improve localization accuracy. This staged design enables MLLMs to retain the benefits of extended reasoning while distilling it into the minimal context required for grounding. Experiments across six multimodal grounding benchmarks show that TCA attains substantial zero-shot performance gains, demonstrating the effectiveness of reasoning compression.

**Comment:** Not relevant to any specific criterion: proposes reinforcement learning for multimodal grounding and reasoning compression, but does not address instruction-following fine-tuning, contamination, diffusion LMs, or evaluation paradigms.
**Relevance:** 3
**Novelty:** 5

## 231. [Table Serialization Determines Which Relations Language Models Preserve](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.24798) <a id="link231"></a>
**ArXiv ID:** 2610.24798
**Authors:** Junwei Ma, Arun Kumar Chithanar, Palash Goyal, Chul Lee

**Abstract:** arXiv:2610.24798v1 Announce Type: new  Abstract: We study how text serialization affects table lookup in instruction-tuned language models. Our diagnostic asks the model to find the row containing a specified value under one named column, then return the value under another named column in that same row. We render the same synthetic 256-cell tables in row-major form and in column-major form with each header placed beside its values, while varying the table aspect ratio across five shapes. Across three models, the better format changes with table shape: row-major performs better on tall tables, whereas column-major performs better on wide tables. At 64 x 4, row-major leads by 42.3-54.0 percentage points; at 4 x 64, column-major leads by 35.0-59.0 points. Mapping wrong answers back to their source cells reveals a complementary error pattern: row-major tends to preserve the correct row but lose the target column, while column-major tends to preserve the correct column but lose the row. We use the term relational locality to describe this pattern: serialization keeps one relation locally available while making the other depend on positional alignment. A separate non-tabular ordering experiment supports this interpretation. Thus, apparent width-related failures in text-serialized tables depend on linearization, not table shape alone.

**Comment:** No direct match to any criterion. The paper studies how table serialization affects relational reasoning in instruction-tuned LMs, but this is an empirical analysis of input formatting effects, not about instruction tuning methods, contamination, diffusion LMs, or interpretability.
**Relevance:** 3
**Novelty:** 5

## 232. [Cognitively Decoupled Meta-Induction for Out-of-Distribution Generalization](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.25749) <a id="link232"></a>
**ArXiv ID:** 2610.25749
**Authors:** Yuqian Wang, Yuanzheng Wang, Chaojun Xiao, Ke Xu, Bingxiang He, Jinghan Tan, Zijun Chen, Lu Chen, Yi-Xin Huo, Maosong Sun

**Abstract:** arXiv:2610.25749v1 Announce Type: new  Abstract: In-context learning (ICL) tackles downstream tasks conditioned on demonstrations. This paradigm relies heavily on neighbor-based matching between queries and demonstrations, limiting out-of-distribution (OOD) performance. For better generalization, rule-based inductive reasoning derives task-level regularities, which we term task-level inductive representations (TIR), before directly answering. However, existing methods lack generalizable inductive ability across diverse tasks. Inspired by meta-learning, we propose Cognitive-Decoupling meta-Induction (CoDI) for TIR-based inductive reasoning on general tasks. CoDI comprises a Cognitive-Decoupling Framework for query-blind TIR induction, and a Dual-Loop Meta-Inductive Learning method for cross-task inducer optimization. Empirically, CoDI achieves substantial performance gains on five ID and OOD benchmarks, and preserves OOD gains without explicit induction at inference. Code is available at https://github.com/Yuqian-Wang2277/codi-meta-induction.

**Comment:** Related to generalization and meta-induction methods for in-context learning, but does not specifically address RLHF, instruction-following fine-tuning, or test contamination.

## 233. [AdaExploreBench: Benchmarking LLM Agents for Proactive Exploration and Test-time Adaptation in Evolving Environments](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.25950) <a id="link233"></a>
**ArXiv ID:** 2610.25950
**Authors:** Rui Wang, Zishen Lai, Jiayu Liu, Cheng Qian, Jeonghwan Kim, Heng Wang, Xuejun Zhang, Jiateng Liu, Bingxuan Li, Shujin Wu, Qing Zong, Tianshi Zheng, Chunyang Li, Qihan Lin, Chen Li, Dadi Guo, Xiusi Chen, Yangqiu Song

**Abstract:** arXiv:2610.25950v1 Announce Type: new  Abstract: Large Language Model (LLM) agents are increasingly deployed in unfamiliar and evolving environments, requiring them to proactively explore, understand, and adapt to environmental dynamics. However, existing benchmarks rarely test whether agents can proactively discover how an environment works and adapt as it evolves. To address this gap, we introduce AdaExploreBench, a benchmark for proactive exploration and test-time adaptation in evolving environments. By instantiating environmental dynamics as hidden rules, AdaExploreBench requires agents to actively acquire evidence and infer the rules. Under implicit rule drift, the latent rule changes without announcement, requiring agents to detect from degraded performance that prior knowledge is stale and re-explore. Experiments on eight leading LLMs show that proactive and adaptive exploration remain challenging: GPT-6 Sol achieves only 61.9% calibrated accuracy, while providing sufficient evidence or removing rule drift improves average performance significantly by close to 10%. These results reveal a critical bottleneck in current LLM agents: they can often reason effectively from informative evidence once it is available, but struggle to actively acquire the right evidence and recognize when previously acquired evidence is no longer valid.

**Comment:** No direct match to any criterion. The paper introduces a benchmark for proactive exploration and test-time adaptation of LLM agents in evolving environments, focusing on agent behavior and adaptation rather than instruction-following fine-tuning or contamination detection.
**Relevance:** 3
**Novelty:** 5

## 234. [How Much Does One Wrong Answer Cost? Error Propagation in Constraint Pruning with Noisy LLM Oracles](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.25739) <a id="link234"></a>
**ArXiv ID:** 2610.25739
**Authors:** Zhengtao Gui (University of California, Irvine), Weining Shen (University of California, Irvine)

**Abstract:** arXiv:2610.25739v1 Announce Type: new  Abstract: Constraint pruning saves queries to a large language model (LLM) or another noisy oracle by skipping each yes/no question implied by answers accepted earlier, for example through transitivity. Entity-resolution systems and LLM-based rankers use this idea. However, their analyses typically assume a correct oracle. We argue that pruning should be judged by its risk when the oracle errs, since an accepted wrong answer spreads to every question it implies. When each answer is wrong independently with small probability $\varepsilon$, the first-order risk is set by how many facts one wrong answer corrupts on average. We consider two kinds of constraints. For implication rules, as in taxonomies, pruning in a random order adds no first-order risk: a wrong answer that would change $k$ facts is accepted with probability $1/k$, so it costs one fact on average. Rules with $s$ premises break this balance: a wrong answer at a premise shared by $r$ rules can cost order $r^{1-1/s}$ facts. For entity resolution with $m$ records of one entity, the first-order risk equals $\varepsilon$ times the Wiener index (the total pairwise distance) of the spanning tree built by the query order. Pivot orders, which first compare one record with all others, amplify errors by less than 2 and are optimal; random orders by order $m^{1/3}$; the common most-similar-first order is the worst in one dimension and, for large $m$, worse than random in two. With several entities, the cost of wrongly merging two does not depend on how many others exist. Simulations agree with every predicted constant within sampling error.

**Comment:** No direct match to any criteria. The paper studies error propagation in constraint pruning with noisy LLM oracles, which relates to LLM usage but does not address RLHF, instruction-following fine-tuning, test contamination, diffusion LMs, or interpretability. It is more about error analysis in querying noisy oracles.
**Relevance:** 3
**Novelty:** 4

---

## 235. [FracTS: Hierarchical and Autoregressive Time Series Generation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.25523) <a id="link235"></a>
**ArXiv ID:** 2610.25523
**Authors:** Jiayu Li, Umair Afzal, Zilong Zhao, Milad Abdollahzadeh, Uzair Javaid, Biplab Sikdar

**Abstract:** arXiv:2610.25523v1 Announce Type: new  Abstract: Time series are ubiquitous across different domains, and the growing use of synthetic data for privacy-preserving sharing, data augmentation, and stress testing makes time series generation increasingly important. Early generative approaches are predominantly autoregressive, which can suffer from low sample diversity, error accumulation, and difficulty modeling long-range dependencies. In this paper, we introduce FracTS, a hierarchical, coarse-to-fine autoregressive model for multivariate time series generation. Inspired by fractal generative modeling developed for images, FracTS adapts the paradigm from 2D to 1D sequences by learning a multi-scale representation that captures global trajectory structure at coarse scales and local dynamics at fine scales while following the autoregressive nature of time series. In contrast to unstructured images, time series are structured, allowing easy automated or designated temporal feature processing optimized for the model, and global (static) feature extraction describing the instances that generate the corresponding time series in the collection. We incorporate them in the generation process to further improve the generation quality. The design naturally handles multivariate dependencies and improves long-horizon temporal coherence without requiring fixed-length inputs. Experiments on five datasets (two conditional, three unconditional) conducted against seven baselines show that FracTS consistently outperforms baselines across marginal distribution and predictive ML utility, and correlation preservation in the unconditional setting. In particular, the predictive ML utility is averagely improved by 100.0% and 75.0-81.0% for unconditional and conditional time series datasets compared to the best baseline (relative error computed on dataset-averaged raw values). Code is available at: https://github.com/BetterdataLabs/fracts.

**Comment:** No direct match to any specific criterion. This paper focuses on hierarchical autoregressive time series generation, which is a generative modeling topic but not about language models or RLHF or evaluation paradigms.
**Relevance:** 3
**Novelty:** 4

## 236. [Fine-Grained Units, Flat Decisions: Hierarchical Micro-Expert Pruning for Mixture-of-Experts Models](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.25717) <a id="link236"></a>
**ArXiv ID:** 2610.25717
**Authors:** Zukang Xu, Xinpei Gao, Xing Hu, Zhe Jiang, Dawei Yang

**Abstract:** arXiv:2610.25717v1 Announce Type: new  Abstract: Micro-expert pruning reduces the capacity of mixture-of-experts (MoE) models by removing coupled channels within routed experts. However, finer pruning granularity alone does not guarantee the preservation of downstream capabilities, as similar language-modeling perplexity can accompany substantially different task performance. We revisit micro-expert mask construction as two distinct decisions: allocating retained capacity across experts and selecting channels within that capacity. We propose UC-RAMP, a two-stage post-training pruning framework that explicitly separates these decisions. CAPE uses cross-domain calibration evidence to redistribute expert widths under an exact layer-wise budget, while RAMP refines channel composition at fixed widths through one-out, one-in exchanges guided by a channel-interaction surrogate. Across three MoE models at 25% and 50% routed-expert channel pruning, UC-RAMP achieves the highest ten-task mean accuracy among evaluated pruning methods in five of six settings. At 50% pruning, it outperforms the strongest evaluated baseline by 8.19, 3.96, and 6.14 percentage points across the three models, respectively. In a single-A100 deployment at 25% pruning, UC-RAMP reduces measured time to first token by 19.7--23.9% and time per output token by 20.2--22.3% relative to the unpruned model. The code is available at https://github.com/xzktx003/UC_RAMP.

**Comment:** Related to pruning methods for mixture-of-experts models, but does not discuss RLHF, instruction-following fine-tuning, or any of the specified criteria. No match to criteria 1-19.

## 237. [The World as Viewed by 136 Countries: An Open, Curated, Annotated, and Multilingual Dataset of 118 Million State Media Articles](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.24990) <a id="link237"></a>
**ArXiv ID:** 2610.24990
**Authors:** Bryan Li, Samar Haider, Grace Kim, Fiona Luo, Jinqi Luo, Yuan Yuan, Duncan Watts, Ren\'e Vidal, Chris Callison-Burch

**Abstract:** arXiv:2610.24990v1 Announce Type: new  Abstract: State media outlets often project their governments' narratives to target both domestic and foreign audiences. Because this content is plentiful and free to access, it appears widely in the web corpora that large language models (LLMs) are trained on and that retrieval systems draw from. However, the prevalence of state media in these corpora and its potential influence on LLM outputs remain largely unexplored. In this paper, we introduce WorldStateMedia, an open dataset of 118 million state media articles from 136 countries in 114 languages. We extract these articles from existing pretraining corpora and annotate each outlet with level of state control from State Media Monitor. Using WorldStateMedia to audit these corpora, we find that, in English web crawls, the share of state media pages from editorially controlled (vs. independent) outlets rose from 39% in 2013 to 93% in 2025. In several low-resource languages, state media make up a substantial share of all web data, such as 17.8% for Uyghur and 7.8% for Belarusian. To characterize the differing narratives of news outlets, we compare Russian and Ukrainian state media in English and in their native languages, revealing asymmetric topical focus and opposing framings of contested issues such as Crimea. Furthermore, in a retrieval-augmented generation (RAG) experiment on territorial disputes, retrieving a country's state media shifts model answers toward that country's claims, most strongly for English-language (foreign-targeted) media. We release WorldStateMedia to support multilingual, cross-national insights on state media at https://huggingface.co/collections/manestay/worldstatemedia

**Comment:** This paper provides a large multilingual dataset of state media articles and analyzes their prevalence in pretraining corpora, but does not focus on RLHF, instruction-following fine-tuning, test set contamination detection methods, diffusion language models, or evaluation paradigms. It is relevant to real-world usage data but does not meet the specific criteria for surveys or safety datasets.

**Novelty:** 4

## 238. [Hippo: Training-Free Agent Self-Evolution via Hippocampal Replay](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.24453) <a id="link238"></a>
**ArXiv ID:** 2610.24453
**Authors:** Yutong Song, Jiang Wu, Yuxuan Ren, Tony Qin, Baoyuan Wang

**Abstract:** arXiv:2610.24453v1 Announce Type: new  Abstract: Motivated by recursive self-improvement (RSI), we study how LLM agents can learn through external memory without updating model parameters. Extracting useful experience from a single attempt is difficult: flawed reasoning can produce success, while poor execution can obscure a sound strategy. Preserving its value over time is also difficult: our accumulation baseline without consolidation initially improves but eventually performs below a no-memory baseline. Our key idea is to compare attempts at the same task to identify reusable decisions and refine when they apply. We introduce Hippo, a memory framework inspired by selective hippocampal encoding and replay that learns from these comparisons when shortcomings are diagnosed. It compares the resulting experience with existing memory to merge overlaps and resolve conflicts through explicit applicability conditions. This allows prior guidance to be revised without storing every lesson as a separate entry. Experiments on SWE-bench Verified, WebArena, and Mind2Web show performance gains in all three settings and substantially fewer retained experiences than the accumulation baseline. The gains extend across the evaluated base models of varying capabilities, indicating that memory-based adaptation remains useful even for highly capable models.

**Comment:** No direct match to any specific criterion. The paper proposes a memory framework for LLM agents to improve self-evolution without parameter updates, which is interesting but not about RLHF, contamination, diffusion LMs, or evaluation paradigms.
**Relevance:** 3
**Novelty:** 4

## 239. [Composable Parametric Demographic Injection for Values Survey Simulation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.23867) <a id="link239"></a>
**ArXiv ID:** 2610.23867
**Authors:** Xinyuan Cao, Qingyi Pan, Bangde Du, Yujia Zhou, Hexi Wang, Weihang Su, Yiqun Liu, Yueyue Wu, Min Zhang, Qingyao Ai

**Abstract:** arXiv:2610.23867v1 Announce Type: new  Abstract: Large language models (LLMs) are increasingly used as simulated respondents in computational social science, yet accurately reproducing population-specific heterogeneous response distributions remains challenging. Existing methods such as demographic prompting are sensitive to prompt design and increase inference overhead, while parameter-based methods struggle to reuse demographic information efficiently, limiting generalization to unseen combinations. In this regard, we propose PARADE, a composable PARAmetric DEmographic injection framework that represents each demographic attribute value with a reusable and composable LoRA module. Specifically, PARADE separates task-common survey capability from demographic-specific value shifts via a shared Task LoRA and attribute-level Demographic LoRAs, with composition calibration to reduce parameter interference. At inference time, target population models can be constructed on demand by recombining the relevant modules, including demographic combinations not observed during training. Extensive experiments on the World Values Survey and SocioBench across three backbone models show that PARADE achieves strong population-level distribution alignment while maintaining competitive predictive performance, reducing KL divergence by up to 52.8% relative to the strongest baseline.

**Comment:** No direct match to any specified criteria. Focuses on demographic injection for survey simulation using LoRA modules, which is a parameter-efficient adaptation method but not about instruction-following fine-tuning or RLHF.

**Novelty:** 4

## 240. [StopLoss: Teaching Agents to Predict Failure and Stop Early](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.25525) <a id="link240"></a>
**ArXiv ID:** 2610.25525
**Authors:** Jiarong Liang, Xueguang Ma, Dongfu Jiang, Yuxuan Zhang, Ping Nie, Wenhu Chen

**Abstract:** arXiv:2610.25525v1 Announce Type: new  Abstract: Coding agents keep working on runs that will fail verification, and asking a frontier model to watch for this is expensive in its own right. We introduce StopLoss, a small external monitor that learns failure recognition from completed trajectories and their verifier outcomes. The name is inspired by the stop-loss order in finance: StopLoss ends a run early when the run is likely to fail, which saves resources. Final outcomes label earlier local windows, so a fine-tuned language model can score the most recent tool steps in a single forward pass, and a threshold calibrated on successful runs turns that score into an abort decision without changing the executor. Trained on GPT-family SWE-rebench trajectories and evaluated on SWE-bench Verified and a SWE-bench Pro subset with GPT and Claude executors, StopLoss is compared against six general-purpose judges reading identical windows. Outcome supervision lifts failure prediction from near chance to a useful signal at the first check, at one to two orders of magnitude lower cost per check than any tested judge. This translates into savings: relative to completing every task, StopLoss lowers cost per retained solve by 4.5\% on Verified while preserving 99\% of successful runs, and by up to 31\% on the costlier Pro subset, reaching 48\% with a Qwen3-8B backbone, and in all four settings its cost per retained solve is below that of every judge policy. Outcome-trained small monitors can make failure recognition cheap enough for early stopping to pay for itself.

**Comment:** No direct match to any specific criterion. The paper focuses on failure prediction and early stopping in agent runs, which is related to efficiency and monitoring but does not explicitly address RLHF, instruction-following fine-tuning, test contamination, diffusion LMs, or other listed criteria.
**Relevance:** 3
**Novelty:** 4

## 241. [FlowMaker: From Tasks to Cost-Efficient Generative AI Workflow Deployments](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.24861) <a id="link241"></a>
**ArXiv ID:** 2610.24861
**Authors:** Dimitrios Liakopoulos, Prasoon Sinha, Neeraja J. Yadwadkar

**Abstract:** arXiv:2610.24861v1 Announce Type: new  Abstract: Generative AI workflows execute user tasks by composing sequences of LLM calls, where each call performs a sub-task (e.g., summarization or translation), forming a workflow. Executing such workflows requires decisions over workflow structure, model assignments, and deployment configurations, which jointly determine latency, cost, and accuracy under dynamic workloads and resource constraints. Existing systems optimize these decisions in isolation or fix them early, leading to suboptimal cost and goodput. We make two key observations. First, efficient serving requires jointly exploring all combinations of workflows, model choices, and deployment configurations under dynamic cluster state. Second, reuse of deployed LLMs is critical for reducing cost, but must be performed on-the-fly, as reuse depends on current load and resource availability. Together, these make selecting a workflow-variant-a combination of workflow, models, and deployment configuration-a time-critical problem. We present FlowMaker, which exposes a task-level interface and selects workflow-variants on behalf of users. To realize this, we design a constraint-guided load-aware pruning algorithm for fast, on-the-fly decisions. FlowMaker improves goodput by up to 10.14$\times$ and reduces cost by up to 2.77$\times$ compared to state-of-the-art baselines, including Murakkab.

**Comment:** No direct match to any specific criterion. The paper focuses on optimizing generative AI workflow deployments for cost and latency, which is a systems engineering contribution rather than a methodological advance in RLHF, contamination detection, diffusion LMs, or evaluation paradigms.
**Relevance:** 3
**Novelty:** 4

## 242. [IF-MAP: Influence-Function-Inspired Agent Prioritization in Multi-Agent Systems](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.24383) <a id="link242"></a>
**ArXiv ID:** 2610.24383
**Authors:** Jie Mao, Pingchen Lu, Zikun Qu, Jiahang Sun, Xiangyi Wang, Zhiwei Shang, Zhiyong Wang, Guojun Xiong, Zhongxiang Dai

**Abstract:** arXiv:2610.24383v1 Announce Type: new  Abstract: Improving every agent in an LLM-based multi-agent system can be prohibitively costly, making it important to identify which agent should be prioritized to achieve the greatest system-level gain with limited resources. Failure attribution can inform this decision, but an agent's responsibility for failures does not directly reveal the system-level gains achievable by improving it. We therefore introduce IF-MAP, an influence-function-inspired method for identifying agents with high potential for system-level improvement. For each agent, IF-MAP measures end-to-end loss changes under controlled semantic prompt perturbations, fits a regularized local response model in a low-dimensional prompt-embedding subspace, and uses the estimated response norm as a priority score. This black-box procedure requires no access to model parameters or gradients and produces agent priorities that can guide subsequent improvement effort. We evaluate IF-MAP across six multi-agent workflows spanning code generation, question answering, and mathematical reasoning, using the same priorities for prompt optimization and model upgrades. Optimizing the selected agents' prompts achieves a macro-average gain of 3.81 percentage points, compared with 1.85 for uniform agent selection. Across five target models, upgrading the selected agents yields macro-average gains of 7.62-16.42 percentage points, compared with 3.81-8.62 for uniform agent selection. Despite its probing overhead, IF-MAP reduces Qwen3-8B and DeepSeek-V3.2 token use by 74.2% and 35.2%, respectively, relative to optimizing every agent, and reduces target-model tokens by 63.6-64.9% relative to upgrading every agent. These results support the use of IF-MAP to identify promising agents and direct improvement effort toward greater end-to-end performance gains.

**Comment:** No direct match to any specific criterion. The paper proposes an influence-function-inspired method for prioritizing agents in multi-agent LLM workflows, which is an optimization and resource allocation technique rather than a fine-tuning or instruction-following improvement (criterion 1), nor does it address contamination, diffusion LMs, evaluation paradigms, or interpretability.

**Novelty:** 4

## 243. [Same Simulation, Different Evidence: Where Retrieved Tool Output Costs LLM Agents Accuracy](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.26836) <a id="link243"></a>
**ArXiv ID:** 2610.26836
**Authors:** Ke Zhang, Sahchit Chundur, Jerry Peprah Owusu, Mohammad Javad Abdolhosseini Qomi, Maziar Raissi

**Abstract:** arXiv:2610.26836v1 Announce Type: new  Abstract: LLM agents that compute the right answer can still answer wrongly. Agent harnesses increasingly keep long tool outputs out of the context window, saving them to files that the agent searches or reads on demand. We ask where this costs accuracy with a real scientific tool: PHREEQC, a widely used geochemistry simulator from the USGS, whose reports reach 846.8k characters and whose determinism lets every printed value be traced. Agents use it to answer 158 execution-verified multiple-choice items. When agents read the simulator's report through a section index rather than in context, GPT-5.1 and GPT-5.2 lose 11.3 and 7.9 points of accuracy, while Claude Opus 4.6 and Claude Sonnet 4.6 show no detectable cost. Re-running all 11,007 logged simulator calls shows that both GPT models compute the keyed value about as often as before; what changes is the evidence they answer from. Under retrieval they often skip the report, answer from their own higher-precision output files, and misround to a neighboring option, a failure we call evidence substitution. With the simulation held fixed, retrieval still costs GPT-5.2 9.4 points. For GPT-5.4, more frequent correct simulations mask an evidence-use loss of about ten points, leaving a net change indistinguishable from zero. Naming the authoritative output raises accuracy by 6.3-7.9 points over plain retrieval for the three GPT models; for GPT-5.1 and GPT-5.2, inlining outputs that fit works as well, while asking for correct rounding does not. Context-management choices in agent harnesses should be judged by where accuracy is lost, not only by final accuracy.

**Comment:** No direct match to any specific criterion. The paper studies accuracy loss in LLM agents using tool outputs and retrieval strategies, but does not focus on RLHF, instruction-following fine-tuning, test contamination, diffusion LMs, or evaluation paradigms.
**Relevance:** 3
**Novelty:** 4

## 244. [Beyond Clean Accuracy: The Practical Challenges and Benefits of Pixel-Based Language Models](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.24290) <a id="link244"></a>
**ArXiv ID:** 2610.24290
**Authors:** Haowen Tang, Wei Xia, Raoyuan Zhao, Hinrich Sch\"utze, Yihong Liu

**Abstract:** arXiv:2610.24290v1 Announce Type: new  Abstract: Pixel-based language models process rendered text without relying on a fixed subword vocabulary, offering a promising approach to noisy and multilingual inputs. However, their practical value depends on whether they can be reliably adapted to downstream tasks. To this end, we systematically examine this question through fine-tuning behavior, typographical robustness, and zero-shot crosslingual transfer. Under our tested settings, pixel-based models require longer training and broader labelled-data coverage than text-based models, while delayed learning and learning-rate-dependent failures make adaptation less reliable. Our analyses further suggest that task-relevant information is less readily accessible in pretrained pixel representations, placing greater demands on fine-tuning. Once successfully adapted, however, pixel-based models lose substantially less accuracy under typographical noise and achieve higher target-language accuracy than English-pretrained text-based counterparts on two crosslingual benchmarks, despite weaker clean English performance. Together, these findings reveal a central trade-off: replacing tokenization with rendering reduces sensitivity to corrupted and unfamiliar inputs, but shifts a central bottleneck to downstream adaptation.

**Comment:** This paper studies pixel-based language models and their fine-tuning behavior and robustness, but does not discuss RLHF, instruction-following improvements, test contamination, diffusion LMs, or evaluation paradigms. It is about adaptation and robustness rather than new methodological improvements to instruction-following.

## 245. [Reranking Answers of Large Language Models with Knowledge Graphs](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.25185) <a id="link245"></a>

**Authors:** Mikhail Salnikov, Hai Le, Olga Tsymboi, Ivan Lazichny, Dmitrii Iarosh, Egor Cheremiskin, Andrey Savchenko, Dmitry Simakov, Alexander Panchenko

**Abstract:** arXiv:2610.25185v1 Announce Type: new  Abstract: Answering natural language questions over knowledge graph data is challenging due to the vast number of facts, which can be difficult to process and navigate. One potential solution for this issue is to use mined subgraphs related to the query, although this process still requires extracting these subgraphs. This research presents a solution for extracting subgraphs related to entity candidates from a question-and-answer set, which can be obtained by inferring a large language model by calculating the shortest paths between entities. The proposed approaches detail various features that can be extracted from the subgraphs and reranking models to select the most probable answers from a list of candidates. Experiments were conducted on Wikidata to evaluate the effectiveness of the proposed approaches. This involved enumerating all the main feature types that can be extracted from mined subgraphs and a detailed analysis of the proposed features and reranking method combinations. In addition, a public web application that provides a useful web tool for studying the graph space between question and answer entities has been developed to work with subgraphs. This includes visualization of the extracted subgraph and automatic generation of natural language text to describe it.

**Comment:** No match to any specific criteria. The paper proposes reranking answers of large language models using knowledge graphs, which is an application of LLMs to QA over knowledge graphs, but does not discuss RLHF, instruction tuning, test contamination, diffusion LMs, or interpretability methods.

## 246. [Whose Credential Is It? How Owner Attribution in Agent MemoryShapes Cross-User Account Misuse](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.25707) <a id="link246"></a>
**ArXiv ID:** 2610.25707
**Authors:** Hau-Sing So, Daixian Liu, Carl Hou, Yuhaohang He, Mengzhu Wang, Hai-Tao Zheng

**Abstract:** arXiv:2610.25707v1 Announce Type: new  Abstract: Shared long-term memory lets an LLM agent serving one user inadvertently retrieve another user's credentials, but it is unclear when such exposure leads to actual harm. We introduce a three-stage audit that separates three events: a foreign credential reaches the prompt (exposure), the agent acts with it (behavior), and the other user's account changes (state). In AppWorld, a store that mixes 763 past logins from all users retrieves another user's credential for 84.7 percent of required apps, yet agents rarely act on it. Misuse appears in a stress test where memory lacks the user's own credential and presents a single foreign one as the user's record: seven agents then use it in 80 to 94 percent of runs and change the victim's account in up to 43 percent, sometimes passing the official check. Naming the owner before or inside each credential line prevents most of this: with all else fixed, misuse falls from 92 to 1 of 305 paired runs, also with name-free usernames, though owner-bound notes cost 3 to 5 successful tasks per model on clean memory. A wrongly written owner largely restores misuse; trailing owner tags and added safety instructions help only some models. Agent memory should consistently record whose credential it stores, and evaluations should measure behavior, not only exposure.

**Comment:** No direct match to any criterion. The paper studies cross-user credential misuse in LLM agent memory, focusing on security and behavior auditing rather than RLHF, contamination, diffusion LMs, or interpretability.

## 247. [Training Negatives Still Matter After Calibrating Answerability Readouts](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.26461) <a id="link247"></a>
**ArXiv ID:** 2610.26461
**Authors:** Jinchen Zhang, Zheyuan Liu, Yihan Zhu, Meng Jiang

**Abstract:** arXiv:2610.26461v1 Announce Type: new  Abstract: Answerability readouts predict whether a passage supplies an answer, but whether calibration can compensate for removing negative passages during training remains unclear. We present a controlled study of how training-negative construction affects calibrated prediction, ranking, and downstream answer retention. We compare native training, which retains negative passages, with empty training, which removes them, holding questions, labels, positive examples, and model weights fixed. Both are calibrated on the same labeled native examples. On SQuAD 2.0 and RepLiQA, native-trained logistic readouts at selected depths achieve lower Brier loss for Qwen2.5-7B-Instruct and Gemma-2-9B-it, with or without output scores. Native fusion also outperforms calibrated output alone. Post-hoc AUROC analysis shows better ranking under native training, beyond what threshold adjustment alone can explain. On the official SQuAD 2.0 development set, native readout policies improve F1 over empty policies but retain more answers to unanswerable questions. The advantage varies by estimator and depth, while RepLiQA's text shortcuts limit claims about contextual understanding. Unfinished generations prevent complete answer-level evaluation, although worst-case bounds preserve the native-empty F1 gains. Our findings show that training negatives affect ranking and downstream decisions even after common calibration, without establishing a universal advantage for native training.

**Comment:** No direct match to any specific criterion. The paper studies the effect of training negatives on calibrated answerability readouts in QA models, which is related to calibration and ranking but not to RLHF, contamination detection, diffusion LMs, or interpretability.

## 248. [FinHopper: A Progressive Multi-Hop Benchmark for Complex Financial Agents with Step-wise Evaluation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.26036) <a id="link248"></a>
**ArXiv ID:** 2610.26036
**Authors:** Yuxuan Wu, Miaopei Lin, Qiufang Ying, Lin Dan, Hao Zhao, Qirui Liu, Yuteng Shen, Shaohui Wu, Weihong Luo, Xiku Du, Xing Tang, Hao Chen

**Abstract:** arXiv:2610.26036v1 Announce Type: new  Abstract: Financial agents are expected to answer factual investment queries through open search, but financial multi-hop retrieval differs from general multi-hop QA in a crucial way: each intermediate answer is an exact typed constraint, such as an entity, date, event, or numerical indicator, that determines the next query. This strict answer-conditioned dependency makes small intermediate errors cascade into plausible but wrong search trajectories. We introduce FinHopper, a benchmark for complex financial agents with controlled reasoning depth from 1 to 6 hops, covering stocks, funds, and macroeconomic facts, with gold intermediate answers for process-level evaluation. To construct FinHopper at scale, we design a state-transition-based synthesis framework that composes verified atomic financial QA patterns into dependency-preserving chains, followed by targeted in-synthesis verification, model-ensemble checking, and public-search validation by financial experts. Experiments with 20 LLM-agent configurations show that public search tools are necessary but insufficient: performance drops sharply as hop depth increases, the best model remains far below human experts, and process-aware scoring reveals partial reasoning progress hidden by final-answer-only evaluation. The benchmark is available at https://github.com/justajustin/FinHopper/.

**Comment:** No direct match to any specific criterion. The paper introduces a new benchmark for multi-hop financial QA agents with step-wise evaluation, but this is an application-focused dataset and evaluation, not about instruction-following improvements, contamination, diffusion LMs, or other criteria.

## 249. [AnyGoal: On World Model Generalization Beyond Expert Rollouts](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.26844) <a id="link249"></a>
**ArXiv ID:** 2610.26844
**Authors:** Heejeong Nam, Divyat Mahajan, Quentin Le Lidec, Randall Balestriero, David Klindt

**Abstract:** arXiv:2610.26844v1 Announce Type: new  Abstract: The goal of a world model is to capture the dynamics of an environment. This has the great appeal that it allows agents to plan and simulate a course of actions with the help of a world model. For these uses to be reliable, however, the learned dynamics must behave well beyond the specific state-action combinations observed in expert rollouts. Despite this, the relationship between data coverage, dynamics generalization, and downstream planning performance remains underexplored. We study this relationship through the lens of compositional generalization: can a world model recombine familiar state and action factors into valid dynamics? To answer this, we introduce AnyGoal, a benchmark of goal-shifted task variants built from widely used world-model environments, together with an open-source library. Each task is designed to be within reach of a world model that has learned the underlying dynamics: individual state factors and actions remain within their training support, but their combinations are underrepresented in expert trajectories. Across multiple environments and world model families, we find that current models consistently exhibit failures under these recombinations. By examining dynamics and representation failures across model families, interaction regimes, and evaluation metrics, we study where current world models generalize and where challenges remain for goal-shifted prediction and planning. Together, this framework, benchmark, and analysis provide a basis for evaluating progress toward world models that generalize to new planning tasks.

**Comment:** No direct match to any specific criterion. The paper studies world model generalization and compositional generalization in reinforcement learning environments, which is not about RLHF, instruction-following fine-tuning, test contamination, diffusion LMs, or other listed criteria.
**Relevance:** 3
**Novelty:** 4

## 250. [Do Conversational Agents Need Explicit Memory? A Controlled Comparison with Raw-History Retrieval](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.24671) <a id="link250"></a>
**ArXiv ID:** 2610.24671
**Authors:** Yongjie Zhou, Shuai Wang, Bevan Koopman, Guido Zuccon

**Abstract:** arXiv:2610.24671v1 Announce Type: new  Abstract: Explicit memory systems transform conversation history into persistent representations intended to improve future access, often at substantial construction cost. Yet it remains unclear whether this added complexity yields better answer accuracy than retrieving directly from raw history. This study compares six state-of-the-art explicit memory systems with raw-history RAG on LongMemEval-S and BEAM-100K under original and controlled retrieval settings. We find only localized gains for particular question types and tight run-time context budgets, with no consistent advantage over strong raw-history RAG baselines. Oracle retrieval shows that some systems fail to preserve answer-supporting information during explicit memory construction, while others preserve it but fail to retrieve it effectively, despite construction costs reaching hours per conversation.   Our findings suggest that while many existing memory systems for AI agents emphasize increasingly elaborate explicit memory construction, they are often evaluated without strong controlled comparisons to raw-history RAG and may underemphasize retrieval. Retrieval remains a major bottleneck when useful information is preserved, suggesting that improving access to existing information may be at least as important as constructing richer explicit memory.

**Comment:** No direct match to any criterion. The paper compares explicit memory systems versus raw-history retrieval for conversational agents, focusing on retrieval effectiveness and memory construction cost. This is about architectural design and retrieval, not instruction-following improvements or evaluation paradigms.

## 251. [Self-Consistency of Entity Sets and Citations in a Deep Research System under Repeated Prompting](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.26421) <a id="link251"></a>
**ArXiv ID:** 2610.26421
**Authors:** Lauri Pyk\"al\"a, Kimmo K. Kaski

**Abstract:** arXiv:2610.26421v1 Announce Type: new  Abstract: Deep research agents (DRA) are commonly used for open-ended synthesis tasks where closed-world QA benchmarks are poorly suited to characterize relevant aspects of performance. We study the self-consistency of one commercial deep research system under repeated identical prompting, representing each report with the set of named entities it mentions, categorised by a custom schema, and the set of citations it retrieves. Across repeated runs of a single open-ended cybersecurity related prompt, we quantify pairwise overlap to characterize how stable the retrieved and synthesized output structure is under repeated identical prompts. In ten runs, the pairwise entity Jaccard results in a median of 0.12 and the pairwise citation Jaccard is below 0.02, with discovery curves that do not visibly saturate. The magnitude and structure of (dis)agreement between entity sets varies by entity class, which suggests that self-consistency in a DRA is better understood as a function of the underlying entity space than as a stable property of the system.

## 252. [Quality-Controlled Reply and Summary Data for Post-Training Small Language Models in AI Call-Screening Agents](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.25458) <a id="link252"></a>
**ArXiv ID:** 2610.25458
**Authors:** Jiaqi Gan, Tsang Ng, Ankit Raj, Tommy Duong, Xingyu Shen, Kidus Zewde, Huaiyu Guo, Simiao Ren

**Abstract:** arXiv:2610.25458v1 Announce Type: new  Abstract: Small language models have improved to the point where a phone can plausibly screen its own calls, answering on an absent owner's behalf without sending call content off the device, a privacy-respecting alternative to cloud call handling. Realizing that promise takes a model that can do two things well: reply within the owner's authority during the call, and afterward tell the owner what happened and what needs attention. Released small models do neither reliably - they raise unwarranted alarms about ordinary callers and write summaries that drop material conditions or report a caller's claims as fact - and real calls supply no reference replies or summaries to learn from. We construct supervision for both tasks from synthetic calls under an information-boundary constraint: a caller writer works from a private scenario brief, a reply writer sees only the conversation up to the current turn, and a summary writer sees the completed transcript. Separate model-based reviews of the full dialogue and the summary decide which calls enter training, yielding a corpus of 3,395 calls with 15,102 targets (11,707 reply, 3,395 summary). Training a single shared LoRA adapter on both tasks, we evaluate six models (0.6-4B parameters) before and after adaptation on 192 held-out calls. Reply qualification rises from 21-54% to 94-97% and summary qualification from 0-6% to 43-72%; all twelve paired gains (six models x two tasks) have bootstrap intervals above zero, and the summary gains hold in every source family. Because one model both screened the training data and scored the outputs, these rates measure agreement with that evaluator's standard rather than human acceptance, which a planned human study will test. After adaptation, coherence and naturalness failures nearly vanish, leaving grounding and usefulness - faithfully tracking who said what, under which conditions - as the remaining challenge.

**Comment:** No direct match to any specific criterion. The paper constructs synthetic supervision data for small LMs in call screening tasks and adapts models with LoRA, but this is an application-focused data and model adaptation work, not about instruction-following improvements or contamination detection.
**Relevance:** 3
**Novelty:** 4

## 253. [Standing on the Shoulders of Giants: Enhancing LLM Agents for Automated Fact-Checking with Human Expert Skills](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.25777) <a id="link253"></a>
**ArXiv ID:** 2610.25777
**Authors:** Xinwen Chen, Haorui He, Yupeng Li

**Abstract:** arXiv:2610.25777v1 Announce Type: new  Abstract: Automated fact-checking (AFC) systems powered by large language model (LLM) agents still face challenges in evidence retrieval and claim verification. Existing approaches improve their capabilities through computationally costly post-training or human-in-the-loop feedback that is difficult to scale. To transfer professional fact-checking expertise to LLM agents efficiently, we distill publicly available guidelines published by Agence France-Presse (AFP) Fact Check into a reusable, modular Professional Fact-Checking Skill (PFC-Skill). We then present SkillCheck, the first AFC system that gives LLM agents dynamic access to PFC-Skill to guide evidence retrieval and claim verification at inference time. Extensive experiments demonstrate that SkillCheck improves upon skill-free DEFAME baselines across multiple LLM backbones, with gains of 2.2--4.8 pp. in Accuracy and 1.5--6.3 pp. in Macro-F1 on the AVeriTeC benchmark. Notably, SkillCheck with GPT-4o-Mini exceeds DEFAME with GPT-4o by 3.4 pp. in Accuracy while cutting inference cost by roughly 61%. The performance gains also extend to open-web retrieval. Ablations show that incorporating the skill during evidence retrieval provides most of the gains, while incorporation into both stages yields the best overall performance. Case studies show that PFC-Skill enables more targeted search and closer scrutiny of evidence. To conclude, our work shows that LLM agents can "stand on the shoulders of giants" by drawing on the best practices of professional fact-checkers, substantially enhancing AFC performance without extra training or continuous human supervision.

**Comment:** No direct match to any specific criterion. The paper focuses on improving fact-checking LLM agents by distilling expert skills into modular components used at inference time, which is an application of LLMs rather than a new methodological improvement to RLHF or instruction-following (criterion 1).

**Novelty:** 4

## 254. [Can LLM User-Proxies Stand In for Humans in Reliance Measurement? Replicating Rel-A.I. with Simulated Users](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.25788) <a id="link254"></a>
**ArXiv ID:** 2610.25788
**Authors:** Shuai Wang, Rui Hu

**Abstract:** arXiv:2610.25788v1 Announce Type: new  Abstract: Can language-model user proxies reproduce human reliance on an AI answer across contexts? Published simulated-user studies do not establish validity for this partial-answer decision, and Rel-A.I.'s released human record omits trial-level choices. We audit its 90 printed questions and 15 domain-by-marker reliance rates using sampled decisions and counterbalanced A/B probabilities from open models through 72B parameters. A matched crossover varies the printed agent specialty within each question, separating question content, label averages, and their interaction. Mistral Small's strong-marker math-minus-law label marginal is -0.4 percentage points, yet matching the agent label to the question domain raises reliance by 13.3 points on average across the five domains. The best A/B proxy has 22.5-point absolute-rate error and 8.3-point domain-effect error against the human table. A two-sample independent-binomial human replication has 3.5-point expected domain-effect error under the stated working model. The interaction is model dependent, and inference layout changes identical-prompt probabilities. This single-study audit does not establish proxy validity for human effect sizes or general directional screening.

**Comment:** Studies simulated user proxies for measuring human reliance on AI answers, but does not address RLHF, instruction-following fine-tuning, test set contamination, diffusion LMs, or other criteria. No direct match.

## 255. [ConCI: A Controlled Counterfactual Benchmark for Contextual Integrity in LLMs](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.24494) <a id="link255"></a>
**ArXiv ID:** 2610.24494
**Authors:** Juhyeon Park, Sumin Yu, Taesup Moon

**Abstract:** arXiv:2610.24494v1 Announce Type: new  Abstract: Contextual integrity (CI) holds that the appropriateness of sharing personal information depends on the context of the information flow, not on the information alone. However, existing CI benchmarks often use independent scenarios that vary multiple CI parameters simultaneously and are dominated by inappropriate flows, making it unclear whether correct predictions reflect sensitivity to context or a general refusal tendency. We present ConCI, a controlled-counterfactual benchmark in which an anchor flow is paired with two counterfactuals that each modify a single CI parameter. The resulting triplet is scored jointly, with credit given only when all three flows are judged correctly. Built from six existing benchmarks, ConCI contains 1,592 flows in 556 triplets, is approximately label-balanced, and is validated by seven human annotators per flow. Across seven LLMs, many correct anchor predictions fail on at least one counterfactual, and even the strongest model errs on about a fifth of triplets. Furthermore, models perform near ceiling on inappropriate flows but weaker on appropriate ones, often continuing to judge a flow inappropriate even when a contextual change should make it appropriate. Larger models narrow this bias toward non-disclosure but do not eliminate it, and reasoning yields only modest gains.

**Comment:** No direct match to any specific criterion. The paper focuses on contextual integrity evaluation of LLMs, which is about model behavior on privacy-sensitive flows, not about instruction-following improvements, contamination detection, diffusion LMs, or evaluation paradigms as defined.
**Relevance:** 3
**Novelty:** 3

## 256. [Assessing the AI Readiness of Construction Datasets: A Multidimensional Operational Framework](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.25499) <a id="link256"></a>
**ArXiv ID:** 2610.25499
**Authors:** Xiaoyu Hou, Bo Xiao, Abdolmajid Erfani, Ronghua Xu, Yu Cai

**Abstract:** arXiv:2610.25499v1 Announce Type: new  Abstract: The rapid growth of artificial intelligence (AI) applications in construction has increased the demand for datasets suitable for reliable model development, evaluation, and reuse. However, construction datasets vary widely in documentation, quality, governance, and evaluation support, and consistent criteria for assessing AI readiness remain limited. This study develops a Construction Dataset AI Readiness Framework with 30 attributes across four dimensions: Metadata Completeness, Data Quality and Representativeness, Data Governance and Accessibility, and AI Utility. The framework uses attribute-specific scoring, applicability screening, and automated and manual verification. It was evaluated using 21 publicly available datasets across seven data-type groups. Overall readiness scores ranged from 0.720 to 0.964, with a mean of 0.879. Hybrid verification achieved 90.5% agreement between automated suggestions and manually verified scores. The framework supports consistent assessment of heterogeneous construction datasets and identification of readiness gaps.

**Comment:** No direct match to any specific criterion. The paper proposes a framework for assessing AI readiness of construction datasets, focusing on data quality and governance, which is data-centric but domain-specific and not about data-centric AI methodology broadly.
**Relevance:** 3
**Novelty:** 3

## 257. [Analyzing Repetition and Interference Effects in Memory-Constrained User Simulators](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.24232) <a id="link257"></a>
**ArXiv ID:** 2610.24232
**Authors:** Cara Su-Yi Leong, Nicholas Tomlin, Tal Linzen

**Abstract:** arXiv:2610.24232v1 Announce Type: new  Abstract: User simulators can help evaluate and train AI systems to interact with humans at scale. To do so, evaluation of user simulators must measure behavioral parity with people. We evaluate whether memory-constrained user simulators are sensitive to two factors known to affect human information retention: repetition of a fact and interference from competing information. We find that some simulators achieve human-like overall accuracy, and replicate the magnitude of repetition and interference effects, but none match people's accuracy on particular questions: while humans misremember facts, models forget them, leading to different error patterns. We release this data as a challenge benchmark for future work on user simulators.

**Comment:** No direct match to any specific criterion. The paper studies memory-constrained user simulators and human-like memory effects, which is related to behavioral modeling but not to RLHF, contamination detection, diffusion LMs, or interpretability.
**Relevance:** 3
**Novelty:** 3

## 258. [EpiLens: Lightweight RAG for Epidemiological Knowledge Acquisition](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.26730) <a id="link258"></a>
**ArXiv ID:** 2610.26730
**Authors:** Vincenzo Perri, Ciro Cattuto, Daniela Paolotti

**Abstract:** arXiv:2610.26730v1 Announce Type: new  Abstract: To prepare for future outbreaks, researchers need to know what studies have been done and what data they use. Mapping a large literature by hand is slow, while large language models alone can misstate or conflate sources. We present EpiLens, a lightweight, open-source retrieval-augmented generation package that applies a fixed retrieval template and output schema to every paper in a corpus and links outputs to source passages. We build a corpus of 2,297 papers from the sources of a multi-pathogen parameter study and evaluate EpiLens's paper-classification workflow against 214 manually labelled papers. Without task-specific training data, the classifier matches or exceeds the tested supervised and zero-shot baselines across four tasks. The study's early extraction sheets cite published sources for 327 estimates. We link each estimate to a cited document or to corpus studies included by a review. Then, we use the classifier's labels to compare the papers associated with different parameters and pathogens. EpiLens labels more papers linked to reproduction numbers than fatality risks as model-based (54% versus 22%, compared with 62% versus 24% in the manual labels). The gap is consistent with the frequent use of transmission models to estimate reproduction numbers and persists within each of five pathogens compared. Beyond the contrast between parameters, the model-based share of papers linked to incubation-period estimates differs by pathogen: 55% for SARS-CoV-2 versus 6% for mpox.

**Comment:** No direct match to any specific criterion. This paper presents a retrieval-augmented generation system for epidemiological literature classification and knowledge extraction, which is an application rather than a methodological advance in instruction-following, contamination detection, diffusion LMs, or evaluation paradigms.
**Relevance:** 3
**Novelty:** 3

## 259. [Modal Sense Disambiguation in Language Models: The Role of Context, Training Domain, and Input Frequency](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.25816) <a id="link259"></a>
**ArXiv ID:** 2610.25816
**Authors:** Ezgi Ba\c{s}ar, Yevgen Matusevych, Annemarie van Dooren, Arianna Bisazza

**Abstract:** arXiv:2610.25816v1 Announce Type: new  Abstract: Modals such as may and must can take on different senses depending on discourse context, alternating between root interpretations (e.g., permission, obligation) and epistemic interpretations (e.g., knowledge, belief). While modals are known to be notoriously difficult for children to acquire, limited research exists on whether language models (LMs) can reliably distinguish between different modal senses. We evaluate seven LMs that vary in architecture, size, and training data type using vector-based similarity and surprisal measures across four datasets. Specifically, we investigate whether LMs can leverage contextual information to disambiguate modal senses and whether their performance reflects the distribution of modal senses in their training data. We show that larger LMs trained on diverse text successfully leverage additional contextual information when available. In the absence of context, they tend to favor the dominant sense based on corpus statistics. Smaller developmentally plausible LMs, including those trained on child-directed language (CDL), fail to show consistent sensitivity to modal sense distinctions and do not benefit from context to the same degree.

**Comment:** No direct match to any criteria. Focuses on modal sense disambiguation in language models, analyzing contextual effects but not on instruction-following improvements or contamination or evaluation paradigms.
**Relevance:** 3
**Novelty:** 3

---

## 260. [Which Model for Which Role? Cost-Efficient Pareto Recommendation for LLM Agentic Workflows](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.26676) <a id="link260"></a>
**ArXiv ID:** 2610.26676
**Authors:** Qian Xie, Wenyue Hua, Yueli He, Sripad Karne, Armaan Agrawal, Nikos Pagonas, Eugene Wu, Kostis Kaffes, Tianyi Peng, Nairen Cao

**Abstract:** arXiv:2610.26676v1 Announce Type: new  Abstract: LLM agentic workflows increasingly employ different models for specialized roles. Users may prefer different trade-offs between task quality and deployment cost, sometimes also considering latency. Finding good assignments incurs a separate search cost from evaluating multiple candidates. We introduce Cost-Coupled Gittins (CC-Gittins), a multi-objective extension of single-objective cost-aware Gittins search for Pareto recommendation. It exploits the coupling between evaluation and deployment costs: assignments that cost more to deploy also cost more to evaluate. The search alternates between seeking higher quality and lower deployment cost, reusing each evaluation to inform both searches. After each batch, CC-Gittins recommends assignments that are nondominated under uncertainty-adjusted estimates of quality and deployment cost. We evaluate CC-Gittins on eight quality--cost matrices from workflows with two to five roles and up to 1,681 candidate assignments. We also evaluate the method with latency as a third objective. Across the eight benchmarks, CC-Gittins recovers a substantial fraction of the true Pareto frontier at roughly 10% of the exhaustive-evaluation cost, and recovers most of the frontier with few false positives by 30%.

**Comment:** No direct match to any specific criterion. The paper focuses on cost-efficient model assignment in LLM agent workflows, which is an application/optimization problem rather than methodological improvements to RLHF or instruction-following, contamination detection, diffusion LMs, or interpretability.
**Relevance:** 3
**Novelty:** 3

## Paper selection prompt
 1. New methodological improvements to RLHF or instruction-following which are specific fine-tuning steps that are taken to make language models better at following user instructions across a range of tasks.
    - Relevant: papers that discuss specific methods like RLHF, or instruction-tuning datasets, improving these methods, or analyzing them. Usually these papers will explicitly mention RLHF, instruction-following or instruction-tuning.
    - Not relevant: papers about adaptation to some task. Simply following instructions or inputs are not sufficient.
 2. Shows new powerful test set contamination or membership inference methods for language models. Test set contamination is the phenomenon where a language model observes a benchmark dataset during pretraining.
    - Relevant: test statistics that can detect contamination of benchmarks in language models. statistics that can provide guarantees are more interesting. membership inference methods that are general enough to apply to language models are also relevant.
    - Not relevant: any papers that do not consider language models, or that do not consider test set contamination.
 3. Shows a significant advance in the performance of diffusion language models.
    - Relevant: papers that study language models that are also diffusion models. Continuous diffusions are even more relevant, while discrete diffusions are less so.
    - Not relevant: papers about image diffusions like DALL-E or Stable Diffusion, or papers that do not explicitly mention language models or applications to text.
 4. Describes new paradigms to evaluating open-ended text generation. Evaluating the outputs of language models is hard, especially in open-ended settings like for chatbots.
    - Relevant: papers that fundamentally rethink language model evaluation -- especially by accounting for subjectivity or using adversaries.
    - Not relevant: specific evaluations for specific tasks, identifying new properties or flaws of language models, or simply collecting new data.
 5. Conducts surveys or provides data into real-world usage and safety properties of language models.
    - Relevant: papers that create new datasets or surveys on real-world usage of language models.
    - Not relevant: papers that apply language models to new real-world tasks.
 6. Studies 'scaling laws' in the context of neural networks. Scaling laws refer to the very clear power-law relationship between the size or computational power used to train a model and the performance of that model.
    - Relevant: theoretical or conceptual explanation behind scaling laws for language models.
    - Not relevant: papers that have experiments at different model scales (but do not explicitly fit a scaling law) or papers that mention scaling laws, but the scaling laws are not the central subject of the paper
 7. Presents new methods or improvements for sparse autoencoders in neural network interpretability. Sparse autoencoders are unsupervised learning techniques that learn sparse representations of neural network activations to understand what features networks detect.
    - Relevant: papers that explicitly mention sparse autoencoders, sparse coding, or sparse feature extraction applied to understanding neural networks, language models, or other AI systems.
    - Not relevant: papers about general autoencoders without sparsity constraints, or sparse methods not applied to interpretability.
 8. Demonstrates new mechanistic interpretability techniques or discoveries about how neural networks work internally. Mechanistic interpretability seeks to understand the algorithms and circuits that neural networks learn.
    - Relevant: papers about circuit analysis, feature visualization, activation patching, causal interventions on neural networks, or reverse-engineering specific algorithms learned by models.
    - Not relevant: papers that only do black-box analysis or correlation studies without mechanistic understanding.
 9. Shows advances in machine unlearning techniques for removing specific knowledge or behaviors from trained models.
    - Relevant: papers on forgetting specific data, removing harmful capabilities, or selectively editing model knowledge while preserving other capabilities.
    - Not relevant: papers about general model editing or fine-tuning without specific unlearning focus.
 10. Presents methods for tamper resistance, robustness against adversarial modifications, or secure AI systems.
    - Relevant: papers on preventing model poisoning, backdoor attacks, or making AI systems resistant to tampering or manipulation.
    - Not relevant: papers only about general adversarial robustness without security/tamper resistance focus.
 11. Studies AI alignment techniques, value learning, or methods to ensure AI systems behave according to human values and intentions.
    - Relevant: papers on reward modeling, constitutional AI, value alignment, or techniques to make AI systems more aligned with human preferences.
    - Not relevant: papers focused only on performance improvement without alignment considerations.
 12. Advances in LLM reasoning capabilities, chain-of-thought reasoning, or understanding how language models perform complex reasoning tasks.
    - Relevant: papers on step-by-step reasoning, logical inference in LLMs, reasoning chain analysis, or methods to improve reasoning capabilities.
    - Not relevant: papers about reasoning in non-LLM contexts or that don't focus on reasoning mechanisms.
 13. Presents new methods for AI control, oversight, or monitoring of AI system behavior during deployment.
    - Relevant: papers on AI governance techniques, monitoring systems, control mechanisms, or methods to maintain oversight of deployed AI systems.
    - Not relevant: papers focused only on training-time controls without deployment considerations.
 14. Shows advances in chain-of-thought monitoring, reasoning transparency, or methods to understand AI decision-making processes.
    - Relevant: papers on monitoring reasoning chains, making AI reasoning more transparent, or techniques to track how models arrive at conclusions.
    - Not relevant: papers about general explainability without specific focus on reasoning processes.
 15. Studies ControlArena, Inspect framework, or similar evaluation platforms for AI safety and control.
    - Relevant: papers using or extending these evaluation frameworks, comparative studies using these platforms, or methodological improvements to these tools.
    - Not relevant: papers that mention these tools only in passing without substantial use or analysis.
 16. Advances in latent reasoning, implicit reasoning processes, or understanding hidden computational processes in neural networks.
    - Relevant: papers on uncovering implicit reasoning, analyzing hidden computational steps, or methods to make latent reasoning processes more explicit.
    - Not relevant: papers about explicit reasoning without focus on hidden or latent processes.
 17. Studies data poisoning attacks or defenses in machine learning systems.
    - Relevant: papers on poisoning training data to manipulate model behavior, detecting poisoned data, or defending against data poisoning attacks.
    - Not relevant: papers about general adversarial examples at inference time without training data manipulation.
 18. Advances in data-centric AI approaches that focus on improving data quality, curation, or data-driven methods for ML.
    - Relevant: papers on data quality assessment, data selection, data augmentation strategies, curriculum learning based on data properties, or systematic approaches to dataset creation and curation.
    - Not relevant: papers that simply use standard datasets without focusing on data-centric methodology.
 19. Studies security of LLMs and methods to protect language models from attacks or exploitation.
    - Relevant: papers on prompt injection defenses, jailbreak prevention, securing LLM deployments, protecting against extraction attacks, or hardening LLM systems against adversarial users.
    - Not relevant: papers about using LLMs for security applications, or general robustness without security focus.

 In suggesting papers to your friend, remember that he enjoys papers on statistical machine learning, and generative modeling in natural language processing.
 Your friend also likes learning about surprising empirical results in language models, as well as clever statistical tricks.
 He does not want to read papers that are about primarily applications of methods to specific domains.
